Chapter 7. The signed positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


7.2     The adjunction and its actual maps
Proposition 7.1 (Twisting adjunction). There are natural bijections between dg module maps
L(N ) → M and dg comodule maps N → K(M ). The counit and unit are

                                                                               c ⊗ 1 ⊗ n′ .
                                                                           X
                      εM (a ⊗ c ⊗ m) = aεC (c)m,         ηN (n) =

Proof. A free-module map is determined by its restriction f : N → M . Equation (7.1) says it is a
chain map precisely when
                               dM f − f dN + µM (τ ⊗ f )∆N = 0.
A map into the cofree comodule is determined by its counit component f and is (1 ⊗ f )∆N .
Projecting its chain-map defect to the cogenerators gives the same equation. The defect is again
determined by that projection, proving sufficiency as well as necessity. The formulas for the unit
and counit follow by choosing the identity maps. Their two triangle identities are the ordinary
coalgebra counit and algebra unit identities applied to the coaction and action. Thus no existence
theorem is being substituted for the maps.


7.3     The counit is a normalized module resolution
The complex LK(M ) = A ⊗ BA ⊗ M has the internal differential, bar multiplication, a negative first
action and a positive last action in length one. To identify it with Theorem 6.1, use the following
signed map on homogeneous inputs of simplicial degree n:
                                                   Pn
                                                             i|ai |+n|m|
               a0 ⊗ a1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ an ⊗ m 7−→ (−1)n+     i=1                 a0 ⊗ [a1 | · · · |an ] ⊗ m.   (7.2)

The total differential on the unsuspended side is dint + (−1)q ∂, where q is internal total degree.
Increasing |ai | by one changes the exponent by i, and increasing |m| changes it by n; these give
exactly the internal suspension signs. For the first face the exponent difference converts (−1)q into
−(−1)|a0 | . For an interior face i it gives ij=0 |aj | + i − 1, the bar prefix and binary suspension
                                              P

signs. For the last face it gives n−1
                                    j=0 |aj | + n − 1, the last-slot action sign. This checks every face
                                 P

and every internal differential.
  Therefore the counit LK(M ) → M is a weightwise quasi-isomorphism. This step uses no
quadratic purity or finite dimension of the weight spaces. The unit-insertion contraction and
normalization are the actual proof.


7.4     Why the unit is an equivalence
Both L and K preserve weightwise quasi-isomorphisms. For K in total weight w, filter increasingly
by the weight of C. Its twist lowers that weight. The finite associated-graded pieces are tensor
products of a C weight piece and an M weight piece with only their internal differential. Tensoring
over a field preserves quasi-isomorphisms. For L, filter by the weight of N ; its twist lowers that
weight, giving the same argument.
   The functor L is conservative in this category. Start at the lower bound b of the weights of
N . The weight-b part of L(N ) is Nb . Inductively assume Nj acyclic for j < w. In (A ⊗ N )w , the
terms of positive A weight form a subcomplex, since twisting raises A weight. Their finite filtration


                                                  25
Chapter 7. The signed positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


has acyclic tensor quotients by induction. The quotient is Nw . Hence acyclicity of L(N ) implies
acyclicity of Nw . Applying the same argument to cones detects quasi-isomorphisms.

Theorem 7.2 (Positive-weight module–comodule equivalence). After inverting weightwise quasi-
isomorphisms, L and K are inverse equivalences between the bounded-below weight module and
comodule categories just specified. The same holds after product completion in auxiliary weight,
using tensor products completed in that same weight.

Proof. The counit is an equivalence. The triangle identity says

                                         εL(N ) L(ηN ) = 1L(N ) .

Thus L(ηN ) is an equivalence, and conservativity makes ηN an equivalence. The functors preserve
the equivalences being inverted, so this is an equivalence of the enhanced localized categories, not
only of cohomology groups. In the completed case the same finite proof holds in every weight and
products of complexes of vector spaces preserve weightwise equivalences. The completed coaction
lands in the completed tensor; no claim of ordinary elementwise conilpotence of an infinite product
is made.

   The lower bound is essential. For A = k[x] of positive generator weight, M = k[x, x−1 ] is nonzero
but k ⊗LA M = 0. Its weights are unbounded below. The induction above has no starting point, and
extending the theorem to this module would be false.


7.5     The two-sided transform and the regular diagonal
For an A–B bimodule define
                                     KA,B (M ) = BA ⊗ M ⊗ BB.
The left and right twists are the preceding formulas and their graded-opposite versions. Their cross
terms anticommute because the two module actions commute before totalization. Applying the
one-sided equivalence twice gives the bimodule–bicomodule equivalence in the same weight category.
   For the regular bimodule, the map
                                                                X
                         BA −→ BA ⊗ A ⊗ BA,             c 7−→       c(1) ⊗ 1 ⊗ c(2)                 (7.3)

is a chain map of bicomodules. Its two twists cancel by coassociativity and the opposite endpoint
signs. It is the unit of the twisting adjunction applied to the regular comodule, so it is an equivalence.
This constructs the diagonal identification rather than stipulating it.


7.6     Derived tensor and derived cotensor
For a right C-comodule P and left C-comodule Q, take the two-sided cosimplicial cobar with
terms P ⊗ C ⊗r ⊗ Q. The cofaces are the endpoint coactions and the coproduct in the intervening
factors. For internal degree q, its total differential is dint + (−1)q i (−1)i di . Normalize by the
                                                                      P

counit codegeneracies. The length-r normalized term inserts r reduced coalgebra factors, with their
desuspensions. Positive weight makes the number of these factors finite in each total weight. This
totalization is the derived cotensor used in the next theorem.


                                                   26
Chapter 7. The signed positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


Theorem 7.3 (The tensor–cotensor law). For composable bounded-below weight bimodules M, N ,
there is a natural equivalence

                            KA,D (M ⊗LB N ) ≃ KA,B (M )□R
                                                        BB KB,D (N ).

It respects triple composition and the diagonal maps (7.3).

Proof. The graded endpoints on the right are cofree over BB. Their ordinary cotensor is

                                     BA ⊗ M ⊗ BB ⊗ N ⊗ BD,

embedded into the degree-zero cobar term by the coproduct of the middle BB. The induced
differential has the bar differential of that middle factor, the right B-action on M , the left B-action
on N , and the two outer differentials. Coassociativity makes its horizontal cobar differential zero.
Separating a cut from a bar multiplication or an endpoint action checks vertical compatibility. Thus
this is a chain map into the derived cotensor.
   Filter by the number of original B letters across all the middle bars. Multiplication and endpoint
twists lower that number; coproduct preserves it. The filtration is finite at fixed weight. On its
associated graded the endpoints are genuine cofree dg comodules without the lowering twists. The
augmented cosimplicial cobar is contracted by the counit on the first cofree factor: the split of that
factor gives the identity and every other split occurs in a cancelling pair. At augmented degree the
counit followed by the coproduct is the identity on the single retained BB. Thus the map is an
associated-graded equivalence and hence an equivalence.
   The retained complex M ⊗ BB ⊗ N is exactly the module two-sided bar for M ⊗LB N , with the
signs of (7.2) and its opposite. This identifies the left side. For three bimodules, both comparisons
retain the same two middle bars. Coassociativity identifies their inclusions and finite weightwise
Fubini identifies the two orders of contraction. The diagonal case is the preceding unit comparison.
This proves all the composition and unit assertions.

   Consequently the endomorphisms of the regular diagonal are identified with those of the regular
bicomodule, preserving the ordinary internal E2 structure of the monoidal unit. Initially this is
the weight-preserving centre. Applying the same theorem to fixed auxiliary weight shifts gives
every homogeneous operation weight. An unrestricted product over operation weights, or a Hom
containing arbitrary weight-mixing maps, requires a separately specified topology and continuity
theorem. This qualification preserves the completed-centre distinctions rather than erasing them at
the end of a valid weightwise proof.




                                                  27
Chapter 8

Incidence, signs, and what a boundary
identity actually proves

8.1     The interval is already a decisive test
There are no codimension-two corners in an interval. This simple fact is enough to test a frequently
used argument: attach an odd endomorphism ρF of one complex to every face of a manifold with
corners, and declare that the sum of those endomorphisms squares to zero because corners have
opposite orientations. The argument is false without an incidence grading. Its expansion contains
products which are not attached to corners at all.
    Take E = ke0 ⊕ ke1 ⊕ ke2 , with |ei | = i and zero differential. Attach to the two endpoints of
[0, 1] the maps
                                   ρ0 (e0 ) = e1 ,   ρ1 (e1 ) = e2 ,
and set their other values to zero. Each map is odd, each squares to zero, and every requirement
imposed only at a codimension-two corner is vacuous. Nevertheless

                                            (ρ0 + ρ1 )2 e0 = e2 .

An orientation sign on an absent corner cannot cancel this term. The correction is not an extra
sign; it is the source and target of every face map.


8.2     A coefficient system on a face poset
Let F be a finite face poset with a rank r(F ), increasing by one at a covering relation F ≺ G. For
a manifold with corners one may take the rank to be codimension, but we require the individual
connected faces and incidence occurrences to be part of the data. In particular self-identifications in
a boundary cannot be forgotten. Choose incidence numbers [G : F ] ∈ {0, 1, −1} such that
                         X
                                [H : G][G : F ] = 0    whenever r(H) = r(F ) + 2.                  (8.1)
                       F ≺G≺H

For an oriented regular cell structure these are the familiar coefficients of the square of its boundary.
Our use of the equation is algebraic and does not require a compactification whose strata have not
been constructed.

                                                      28
Chapter 8. Incidence, signs, and what a boundary identity actually proves


   Assign a cochain complex EF to every face, and a degree-zero chain map

                                           rGF : EF −→ EG

to every covering relation. A strict coefficient system supplies maps for all comparable pairs,
satisfying rHG rGF = rHF . Form            M
                                     V =       EF [−r(F )].
                                                 F ∈F

An element of internal degree a in EF has total degree a + r(F ). Put
                                                                     X
                         D0 |EF = (−1)r(F ) dF ,          D1 |EF =       [G : F ]rGF .             (8.2)
                                                                     F ≺G

Both terms have total degree one. The G, F matrix block of D1 is zero unless F ≺ G. Thus a
product of two blocks can be nonzero only along an actual flag.
Theorem 8.1 (The coefficient incidence differential). For the data above, D0 + D1 squares to zero
if and only if                   X
                                     [H : G][G : F ]rHG rGF = 0
                                  F ≺G≺H

for every rank-two interval. A strict coefficient system satisfies these identities whenever (8.1) holds.

Proof. The internal square is zero. In the G, F covering block the mixed part is

                          [G : F ] (−1)r(F )+1 dG rGF + (−1)r(F ) rGF dF = 0,
                                                                              


since rGF is a chain map. The only remaining nonzero blocks have rank difference two, and matrix
multiplication gives exactly the sum in the statement. This proves necessity and sufficiency. For
a strict system, every composite in that sum is the same map rHF . Factoring it out leaves (8.1).
Notice the two separate ingredients: orientation cancels the incidence numbers, whereas functoriality
identifies the coefficient maps. Neither ingredient proves the other.

   On the interval the corrected object has one summand for the interval and one for each endpoint.
The endpoint maps leave the interval summand and enter endpoint summands. There is no map
from one endpoint to the other, so their product is zero for a reason visible in the matrix blocks.
This is exactly what the counterexample omitted.


8.3     A homotopy is a new component of the differential
Suppose the two composites around a rank-two interval are only chain homotopic. They cannot
be inserted into the preceding theorem as equal maps. Instead add a map of internal degree −1
between its endpoints. More generally let Dj have matrix blocks only when the rank difference is j;
its block then has internal degree 1 − j, so it again has total degree one. The complete equation is
                                               X
                                [D0 , Dj ] +           Da Db = 0,      j ≥ 1,                      (8.3)
                                               a+b=j
                                               a,b≥1


where the bracket is the graded commutator. At j = 2, D2 is the actual homotopy for D12 . At j = 3,
D3 is a homotopy for the compatibility of those homotopies. The equation displays the required
maps, their degrees, and their obstruction complexes.

                                                        29
Chapter 8. Incidence, signs, and what a boundary identity actually proves


Theorem 8.2 (Finite coherent incidence realization). Let D1 , D2 , . . . have the stated rank supports.
                P
Then D = D0 + j≥1 Dj is a differential precisely when (8.3) holds. The sum is finite. If its
components through rank difference n − 1 have been constructed, the next curvature
                                                    X
                                           Rn =            Da Db
                                                  a+b=n
                                                   a,b≥1


is closed for [D0 , −]. A choice of Dn exists exactly when its class vanishes in the degree-two
cohomology of the corresponding rank-n mapping complex.

Proof. Expand D2 and group its blocks by rank difference. This gives (8.3). The strictly increasing
rank bounds the number of factors in any nonzero composition, so no completion is needed. To
check the obstruction, apply [D0 , −] to Rn , substitute the already satisfied lower equations, and
pair the terms Da Db Dc from the two possible adjacent parenthesizations. Their signs are opposite
because each Di has degree one. Thus [D0 , Rn ] = 0. A correction changes the curvature by [D0 , Dn ],
proving the criterion.

   This criterion does not assert vanishing. For example, if the relevant mapping complex has
zero differential and Rn is nonzero, there is no correction on that carrier. A contractible simplex
of geometric parameters gives a primitive only after the parameter-dependent coefficient maps
and their differential have been constructed. The cyclic trace construction below will supply such
primitives explicitly.


8.4     Two colours and two genuinely different sewing operations
Take two face systems with ranks rX , r⊥ . On their product use the normal orientation in the order
X, then ⊥. The total boundary is

                                      ∂ = ∂X ⊗ 1 + (−1)rX 1 ⊗ ∂⊥ .

If the coefficient maps in the two directions commute, then
                             2             2
                            ∂X = 0,       ∂⊥ = 0,          ∂X ∂⊥ + ∂⊥ ∂X = 0.

For the mixed identity, apply both composites to a face of bidegree (rX , r⊥ ). In one order the
perpendicular sign is (−1)rX and in the other it is (−1)rX +1 . The remaining coefficient composites
are equal by the stated hypothesis. Hence they cancel.
   The two ranks need not be identified. In the product of a stable-curve sewing system and a
ribbon-graph system, the first and second genera remain independent. Their mixed anticommutator
is proved on the product system, not on an unspecified common moduli space. A proposed
identification with a physical or analytic sewing theory must supply that moduli space and its
coefficient maps separately.

Chapter 8. Incidence, signs, and what a boundary identity actually proves


8.5     Integration gives the incidence identities, not arbitrary endo-
        morphisms
Let Ω(∆q ) be polynomial forms on the standard simplex over a characteristic-zero field, with
barycentric coordinates t0 , . . . , tq . The formula

                                                                           a0 ! · · · aq !
                             Z
                                   ta00 · · · taq q dt1 · · · dtq =
                              ∆q                                      (a0 + · · · + aq + q)!

follows by induction from the elementary beta-integral, or by polynomial antiderivatives and the
binomial theorem. It determines an algebraic functional on polynomial top forms. Direct integration
of monomials proves Stokes’ formula
                                         Z             q              Z
                                                                               ∂i∗ ω.
                                                       X
                                               dω =           (−1)i
                                          ∆q            i=0             ∆q−1

Each term has the face as its actual domain. If a differential-graded coefficient map has been defined
on the whole simplex and restricts to the given face maps, integration supplies a homotopy between
their oriented sum. It supplies every higher homotopy in the same way. Conversely, a collection of
unrelated endomorphisms does not become such a map by attaching face names to its members.
   The distinction is elementary, but it is essential to every later use of collision forests, associahedra,
ribbon graphs, and renormalization faces. The geometry dictates where an operation can compose;
the operation must still satisfy its own coefficient equation.




                                                              31
Chapter 9

Locality, the two products, and their
units

9.1    The species calculation
For a species F , define the Hadamard and Cauchy products by
                                                                  M
                (F ⊙ G)(I) = F (I) ⊗ G(I),      (F ∗ G)(I) =             F (S) ⊗ G(T ).
                                                                 I=S⊔T

The decomposition is ordered and may have empty blocks. The units differ: the Hadamard unit is
k on every finite set; the Cauchy unit is k only at the empty set.
   Expanding (A∗B)⊙(C∗D) gives two independent decompositions of I. Expanding (A⊙C)∗(B⊙D)
gives one. Including the summands where the decompositions coincide gives a natural map ι from
the latter to the former, after the middle tensor swap. Projection to those summands gives π in the
reverse direction. Thus
                                    πι = 1,     e = ιπ,    e2 = e.
The image is the aligned sector. On three iterated decompositions the two possible successive
projectors both retain exactly the triple-equal sector. This proves the elementary coherence of the
projector. It does not make e the identity.
   If F is nonzero on an r-element set with r > 0, choose disjoint such sets S, T . The summand
indexed by (S, T ) in one factor and (T, S) in the other is nonzero and is killed by e. No full
subcategory containing the constant Hadamard unit can turn every such comparison into an
isomorphism. Restriction of one comparison to the image of its projector is a different statement.
  For the singleton-supported species F ({∗}) = k, F (I) = 0 otherwise,

                                     (F ∗ F )({1, 2}) = k ⊕ k.

The two summands are the nonempty decompositions ({1}, {2}) and its reverse. They are not
empty-block terms. No quotient or no-go theorem may be deduced from the opposite count.




                                                32
Chapter 9. Locality, the two products, and their units


9.2     The direction of interchange
A useful double-monoid compatibility uses the inclusion direction:
                                  m⊥ (mX ⊙ mX )ι = mX (m⊥ ∗ m⊥ ).
Its unit diagrams must also be imposed. The projection-direction analogue has a severe degeneracy
with the two units. In the double sum use decompositions (I, ∅) and (∅, I). Cauchy multiplication
by its unit produces arbitrary elements x, y ∈ A(I), while projection kills their mismatched pair.
The law would force m⊥ (x, y) = 0. The Hadamard unit then forces A(I) = 0 in positive arity.
   This is a refutation of that particular direction with those unit axioms. It is not a refutation
of every homotopy-coherent mixed structure. For a nondegenerate combinatorial example use
A(I) = End(V ⊗I ), composition as the Hadamard product, and tensor product on aligned disjoint
decompositions. The middle-factor interchange is ordinary functoriality of tensor products of
operators. This example verifies the combinatorics and does not manufacture a chiral collision map.


9.3     Factorization is not a stable subcategory
A factorization condition identifies the value at a disjoint union with a tensor product of the values.
For two such objects F, G, the pointwise direct sum at two points has only the two summands
F12 ⊕ G12 . Factorization of the one-point sum would require
                                        (F1 ⊕ G1 ) ⊗ (F2 ⊕ G2 ),
which has two additional cross terms. Thus pointwise direct sums do not in general remain factorizing.
A category defined by these equivalences cannot simply be called a stable linear ambient category.
    This does not prohibit every geometric realization in a factorization category. A preservation
theorem can hold for specified sifted colimits when tensor commutes with those colimits. The point
is that tensor coalgebras, kernels, cones and normalization must first be formed in an actual stable
category, or be justified by the appropriate nonlinear universal property. The failure of direct sums
is not a proof that all realizations fail.
   Compatibility must include every operation. A binary equivariant product need not make an
A∞ structure equivariant. For example let a reduced graded space have a in degree zero and b in
degree −1, with m2 = 0, m3 (a, a, a) = b, and every operation involving b zero. All composites of
two nonzero higher products vanish, so this is a minimal reduced A∞ algebra. The involution fixing
a and negating b preserves m2 but not m3 . The same logical issue applies to a proposed locality
test checking only binary multiplication squares.


9.4     The actual chiral tensor
Here is the geometric input used for the native associative construction. Work in the enhanced
stable category of right D-modules on the nonempty Ran space of a smooth characteristic-zero curve.
For r objects its chiral tensor on a finite-set chart is described by the finite sum over surjections
π : I ↠ {1, . . . , r}, taking external products of the block components, restricting to the locus
where different blocks are disjoint, and extending by the chiral direct-image convention. This is
the standard Ran chiral tensor of [1, 2]. Its composition and transition coherences are part of that
construction, not supplied merely by the species count.

                                                   33
Chapter 9. Locality, the two products, and their units


   No surjection exists when r > |I|. Consequently a chiral word of length r vanishes on charts of
cardinality less than r. At a finite support-cardinality stage, sufficiently long tensor products vanish.
This is the pro-nilpotent mechanism behind the operadic theorem, and it differs from an assumed
positive conformal weight.
   The nonempty Ran tensor is nonunital. A unit for an augmented associative problem is adjoined
externally when required. The pointwise tensor and the chiral tensor are different monoidal
structures, with different support behavior. In particular the finite-cardinality word bound for the
latter is not a word bound for the former.


9.5     Three bars, with their domains
The following notation fixes the distinction.
   For an augmented associative algebra for a specified transverse or pointwise tensor, B ⊥ is the
ordinary associative word bar for that tensor. For an associative algebra for the actual chiral
           ch is the associative operadic bar for that tensor. Its finite-cardinality stages admit
tensor, BAss
the cut-complex reconstruction of Chapter 2, using the pro-nilpotent framework. For a chiral Lie
            ch is the chiral Chevalley construction, with symmetric suspended chains and the Jacobi
algebra, BLie
differential.
    There is a commutator morphism from associative to Lie operations in any suitable symmetric
monoidal category. There is also a bar–Chevalley comparison for an actual enveloping algebra with
its PBW and convergence hypotheses. These facts do not identify the three displayed bars on an
arbitrary object. Even over a point the comparison from the Chevalley chains of the augmentation-
ideal commutator algebra to the associative bar need not be an equivalence. For A = k[x], the
commutator Lie algebra xk[x] is abelian. Its nonzero degree-one class x2 maps to [x2 ] = b[x|x], a
boundary in BA.
   A formal-disc realization of a chiral Lie operation uses the full singular distribution and yields
a vertex-type bracket. An associative chiral algebra and a nonlocal vertex algebra have different
a priori operation domains. An equivalence requires vacuum, truncation, expansion and descent
comparisons; it does not follow from writing both products in a spectral parameter. This distinction
remains in force in the explicit field examples later.




                                                   34
Chapter 10

The associative Ran theorem and its
polarized dual

10.1     The stable coefficient category comes first
Let X be a smooth characteristic-zero curve. The Ran coefficient category is the enhanced
category of right D-modules specified by compatible objects on finite powers X I , with the diagonal
transition functors of the Ran presentation. We use extraordinary restriction for its finite-cardinality
truncations. This stable category must not be confused with the nonlinear subcategory of objects
satisfying a factorization-isomorphism condition.
   The six-functor construction of this category and of the chiral tensor is a foundational input from
Beilinson–Drinfeld and Francis–Gaitsgory [1, 2]. Its features used below are precise. On a finite
labelled chart the tensor is assembled from the disjoint-decomposition opens by ordinary direct
image j∗ . For an ordered decomposition into nonempty blocks, the corresponding term is the direct
image of the external product restricted to the locus of disjoint block supports. Union of supports
supplies the map back to Ran. Refining a decomposition gives the same open set and direct-image
composite in either parenthesization. These identifications are the associativity constraints of the
tensor, not an extra multiplication on an arbitrary coefficient object.
   Write D≤n for the category on configurations of cardinality at most n. The chiral product on
this truncation is tensor-nilpotent:

                                      M1 ⊗ch · · · ⊗ch Mn+1 = 0.

The reason is disjoint support cardinality. Every factor has nonempty support, so a disjoint union
of n + 1 of them cannot lie in this stage. Ordinary direct image can retain singular boundary data
on larger charts, but its extraordinary restriction to the forbidden closed support locus is zero.
Confusing this extraordinary restriction with an ordinary stalk would invalidate the argument.
   The nonempty Ran tensor has no internal unit. Augmented algebra notation means external
unitization: adjoining a formal coefficient unit and taking an augmentation ideal in D. The
actual theorem below is equally a theorem about nonunital associative algebras and noncounital
coassociative coalgebras in D. It does not declare the vertex vacuum to be an internal unit for the
nonempty chiral tensor.



                                                  35
Chapter 10. The associative Ran theorem and its polarized dual


10.2      A finite nilpotent theorem with an elementary proof
Let V be a stable enhanced linear monoidal category with exact tensor, and suppose every N -fold
tensor is zero. The finite associative bar of a multiplication object has terms

                                                       (A[1])⊗m
                                                 M
                                        BA =
                                               1≤m<N

with the internal and adjacent multiplication differentials. The finite cobar of a coproduct object
has terms with the opposite suspension. Their faces are well defined with the tensor signs fixed in
Chapter 1.
Theorem 10.1 (Finite tensor-nilpotent reconstruction). Associative bar and cobar are inverse
derived constructions in V, with coassociative coalgebra output and the actual twisting-cochain unit
and counit.

Proof. Expand a word of ΩBA into its original A-letters. No term has N letters, by tensor nilpotence.
Filter by their number. Adjacent multiplication lowers the filtration. In its associated graded, each
gap has the two possible statuses “inside one bar word” and “between two cobar factors”, joined by
an isomorphism with the suspension sign. Their tensor product is contractible whenever there is a
gap. Contract the leftmost gap, carrying the tensor signs of all preceding letters. Only the single
original letter remains. The finite filtration proves the counit is an equivalence.
   For BΩC, use the same original-letter expansion in C. The cobar coproduct can increase the
number of original letters, so use the finite descending filtration. The associated graded is again
the gap complex, now with concatenation removing the complementary gap status. It contracts to
the one-letter term. This proves the unit. All sums and filtrations are finite. The maps are the
twisting-cochain unit and counit, so their naturality and triangle identities are inherited from the
explicit adjunction. This proves the equivalence, not merely a matching of homology objects.


10.3      Applying the theorem stage by stage
Theorem 10.2 (Associative chiral reconstruction). The associative bar and cobar give inverse con-
structions on the compatible finite-cardinality systems of associative chiral algebras and coassociative
chiral coalgebras, with the derived transition maps supplied by the Ran coefficient category. The
finite-stage construction uses the associative operad throughout.

Proof. Apply Theorem ?? to each D≤n . Restriction to a smaller cardinality stage is monoidal for
the stated tensor and carries each word and its face maps to the corresponding word and faces
there. The units, counits and cut contractions therefore define compatible equivalences of the towers.
Taking their enhanced limit gives the asserted equivalence on compatible systems. Limits preserve a
diagram of equivalences; no assertion that an arbitrary product totalization is exact has been used.
   The identification of the full Ran category with this support-cardinality limit is the cited Ran-
space construction. It is not a claim that an arbitrary filtration by conformal weight is complete,
that every inverse system has surjective cohomology transitions, or that scalar extension commutes
with its totalized Hochschild centre.

   This is separate from the chiral Lie–commutative theorem. Applying a different operad changes the
coalgebra output and, in the Francis–Gaitsgory theorem, the factorization condition. A coassociative

                                                  36
Chapter 10. The associative Ran theorem and its polarized dual


coalgebra from the associative bar does not become a commutative factorization coalgebra by
changing its symbol.


10.4     A nontrivial finite-stage example
In a two-cardinality stage take any coefficient M and the free nonunital associative algebra
                                          A = M ⊕ (M ⊗ch M ).
Multiply two elements of M by their inclusion into the second summand and set every product
involving that second summand to zero. Triple products vanish because the triple chiral tensor is
zero in this stage. Thus this is an actual associative chiral algebra for the given monoidal product.
Its bar has the two-letter term mapping isomorphically to the suspended second summand; that pair
cancels. Its remaining primitive coalgebra is M [1]. The contraction is the two-letter cut calculation,
now in the geometric coefficient category.
   The example does not require M to be a vector space or have regular coefficients. Its disjoint-open
direct image can retain a meromorphic collision extension. It exhibits the geometric associative
theorem without identifying a nonlocal state-field algebra with it.


10.5     Verdier duality changes both the extension and the variance
On finite charts restrict to holonomic objects for which Verdier duality is involutive and the stated
external products remain in the duality domain. Then
                            Dj∗ = j! D,       D∆∗ = ∆∗ D,        Di! = i∗ D.
The proper diagonal has the same direct image, but the open extension and the diagonal transition
variance change. Therefore a chiral operation
                                   j∗ j ! (M1 ⊠ · · · ⊠ Mr ) −→ ∆∗ N
transposes to a co-operation
                                ∆∗ DN −→ j! j ∗ (DM1 ⊠ · · · ⊠ DMr ),
with the duality shifts and tensor signs. Its codomain is the opposite-polarity coefficient problem.
It is not another multiplication of the same ordinary vertex-algebra type.
   Finite bar windows can be dualized term by term. A shift [1] becomes [−1], deconcatenation
becomes concatenation, and the transpose of multiplication becomes the coproduct contribution to
the cobar differential. If the chosen ordered tensor-duality convention reverses factors, the resulting
algebra is taken with the corresponding opposite product. This gives the usual bar-dual/cobar
comparison on those finite windows. Applying involutive duality twice restores the original operations
and their diagrams.
   An infinite direct sum dualizes to a product. Thus weightwise finite duality extends only with
the displayed product topology and compatible transition variance. Neither involutivity on a finite
holonomic chart nor the ordinary polynomial dual-number example justifies an undeclared infinite-
dimensional Verdier endofunctor on every vertex algebra. In particular no reflected central-charge
formula is implied by these geometric identities. Such a formula requires a separate identification of
the dual object and its conformal structure.

                                                  37
Chapter 11

A collision is a derived algebra

11.1     Two charts and one overlap
Let R = k[z], or k[[z]] when a formal base is explicitly intended, and put K = R[z −1 ]. Glue two
copies of Spec R along Spec K. The finite Cech complex for derived functions is

                            C 0 = R ⊕ R,       C 1 = K,     d(a, b) = b − a.

Its Alexander–Whitney multiplication is

               (a, b)(c, d) = (ac, bd),    (a, b)v = av,     v(c, d) = vd,     vw = 0.

These formulas are associative. For example ((a, b)v)(c, d) = avd = (a, b)(v(c, d)). The Leibniz
identity on two degree-zero inputs is

                                     bd − ac = (b − a)d + a(d − c),

and the remaining cases either reduce to this scalar action or have degree-two target zero. Thus
this is a dg associative algebra, with unit (1, 1).
  Its cohomology is H 0 = R and H 1 = J = K/R. The induced algebra is the square-zero extension
H = R ⊕ J[−1]. This does not make C quasi-isomorphic to H as an R-algebra.
Theorem 11.1 (Relative nonformality detected by idempotents). There is no zigzag of R-linear dg
algebra quasi-isomorphisms between C and its cohomology algebra H. Their derived special fibres
have respectively the degree-zero algebras

                            C ⊗LR k ≃ k × k,       H 0 (H ⊗LR k) ∼
                                                                 = k[θ]/θ2 .

Proof. Both terms of C are flat and the complex is bounded. Hence ordinary tensor computes
its derived fibre. Localization K ⊗R k is zero, and the remaining degree-zero multiplication is
componentwise on k2 .
   For the cohomology algebra resolve J by [R ,→ K] in degrees −1, 0. Form the square-zero
dg algebra R ⊕ [R ,→ K][−1]. It is a bounded flat R-complex and maps multiplicatively by a
quasi-isomorphism to H. Its fibre is k ⊕ kθ in degree zero, with θ2 = 0. Derived extension of scalars
preserves algebra equivalences, so a zigzag would identify these two fibre algebras. The first has
two nontrivial orthogonal branch idempotents and no nonzero nilpotent; the second has a nonzero
nilpotent and no such idempotent decomposition. They are not isomorphic.

                                                   38
Chapter 11. A collision is a derived algebra


    The underlying cohomological dimensions of these fibres are equal. The test detects multiplication,
not a missing vector-space dimension. It also explains why replacing a derived coefficient algebra by
its cohomology before specialization can corrupt an otherwise exact calculation.


11.2      A commutative coefficient model
In characteristic zero the same derived functions admit a commutative model

                T 0 = {f (s) ∈ K[s] : f (0), f (1) ∈ R},         T 1 = K[s]ds,   df = f ′ (s)ds.

Products are polynomial products with ds2 = 0. Define
                                                                                     Z 1
                   I(f ) = (f (0), f (1)),                                I(gds) =         g(s)ds,
                                                                                      0
                W (a, b) = (1 − s)a + sb,                                  W (v) = vds,
                            Z s                Z 1
                 h(gds) =         g(t)dt − s         g(t)dt,                h(f ) = 0.
                             0                  0

Then IW = 1 and dh + hd = 1 − W I. The endpoint conditions for h hold because its values at
both endpoints are zero.
                      R1
                           On a polynomial f , the last identity is f − f (0) − s(f (1) − f (0)); on a
one-form it is gds − ( 0 g)ds.
   Integration is not multiplicative for the ordinary Cech cup product. In fact

                       W (a, b)W (c, d) − W (ac, bd) = −s(1 − s)(b − a)(d − c).

The comparison preserves the commutative structure homotopy coherently, or one retains the
commutative model itself. A linear contraction is not a strict algebra map. None of this changes
Theorem 10.1, which concerns the further replacement by the cohomology algebra.


11.3      Principal parts and supported derivatives
The quotient J = K/R has basis z −n−1 for n ≥ 0 in the polynomial case. Multiplication by z is
surjective on J and kills only the line kz −1 . Consequently

                                                     J ⊗LR k ≃ k[1].

The nonzero class is a Tor class, not the ordinary tensor J/zJ, which is zero.
   Let D = k[z]⟨∂⟩/([∂, z] − 1). The left module D/Dz identifies with J by

                                             ∂ n δ 7−→ (−1)n n!z −n−1 .

The relation zδ = 0 is respected, and the commutator gives z∂ n δ = −n∂ n−1 δ, exactly the same
action on principal parts. Both modules have the displayed bases, so the map is an isomorphism.
This is the derivative-supported target that a scalar residue discards.
   Indeed Res(f (z)dz) is k-linear on J, but is not R-linear to k = R/(z). The residue of z · z −2 is
one, whereas z acts by zero on the target. Replacing the supported D-module by that scalar target
therefore breaks the module type of the operation.

                                                           39
Chapter 11. A collision is a derived algebra


11.4      A boundary transgression, not a projection
Restriction to the first chart is the dg algebra map ρ : C → R, given by (a, b) 7→ a and v 7→ 0. Its
kernel is [R ,→ K] in degrees 0, 1. Thus

                                               ker(ρ)[1] ≃ K/R

with the latter in degree zero. The shift records that a bulk principal-part class was in degree one.
Projection alone kills that class. A boundary operation using it must include a relative transgression,
residue or duality of the indicated degree, rather than treat projection as if it preserved a degree-zero
singular field.
   This construction supplies a coefficient-level map. To apply it to a bulk–boundary theory, a
state-space action compatible with the coefficient multiplication is still needed. The distinction is
especially important in arity three, where two routes to the boundary can differ by a nontrivial
homotopy.


11.5      The whole pole filtration
For a smooth divisor D ⊂ X 2 , the pole filtration has

                grm OX 2 (∗D) = OX 2 (mD)/OX 2 ((m − 1)D) ∼
                                                          = OD (mD),             m ≥ 1.

The last line is the mth tensor power of the normal bundle. It transforms nontrivially under a
change of transverse coordinate. For a meromorphic function am (z − w)−m , a logarithmic residue
                                                             P

of a one-form detects one normal symbol, not all of the higher symbols. Recovering higher modes
requires jets or the full localized operation. A coordinate-free inverse from a single logarithmic
generator to every higher principal part has not been defined by the residue identity.
   The same distinction appears under derived restriction. The localization K has zero derived fibre
at z = 0. The quotient K/R has a nonzero shifted fibre. A construction localizing first and then
taking a closed fibre cannot be identified with one retaining principal parts and then specializing.
The two operations have different exact triangles.


11.6      Duality changes the coefficient problem
On finite holonomic charts, Verdier duality changes direct-image extension across an open set
into proper-support extension and changes extraordinary inverse-image compatibility into ordinary
inverse-image compatibility. Transporting a chiral tensor through this duality therefore gives an
opposite-polarity tensor. It does not give an endofunctor of an unchanged coefficient category by
notation alone.
    For a finite dualizable word window the elementary identity is that the dual of the bar coalgebra
is the cobar of the dual coalgebra. The dual deconcatenation is multiplication. In the suspension
convention used here, the shift map is (tf )(sa) = (−1)|f | f (a). Transposing the two bar cogenerator
maps then gives exactly the two cobar generator maps. Finite charts allow this identity to assemble
when their duality transformations and transitions agree. The dual of a bar is already an algebra;
applying a coalgebra cobar functor to that algebra would be a type error.


                                                     40
Chapter 11. A collision is a derived algebra


  Neither this identity nor Verdier biduality identifies a reflected-level vertex algebra with an
augmentation dual. That requires a separate representing pairing and a comparison with the relative
endomorphism algebra. The polarized construction and the diagonal centre of the later chapters
remain distinct.

Chapter 12

All orders, cyclic branches, and
excision

12.1     The order-chart scheme
For a finite label set I put RI = k[zi | i ∈ I] and SI = Spec RI . For each total order σ take an affine
chart Uσ = SI . If σ, τ disagree on some pair orders, put
                                                   Y

   For three orders, the union of inversion sets from any anchor is the set of pairs on which the three

The projection pI : XI → SI is etale, since its restriction to every chart is the identity. It is
quasi-compact and quasi-separated. It is not generally separated. This geometry is the order-chart
construction used in the cochain raviolo formalism [3].

Proposition 12.1 (Every residue fibre). Let p ⊂ RI be a prime. Partition I into blocks B by
equality of the coordinate images in κ(p). Then the fibre is the disjoint union of B |B|! copies of
                                                                                  Q

Spec κ(p).

Proof. A chart overlap survives precisely when all its inverted differences are nonzero in the residue
field. Thus two total orders give the same point precisely when their restrictions to each equality
block agree. Every tuple of block orders extends to a total order. Distinct tuples are not identified.
Chart fibres are reduced affine points and every overlap is either the whole point or empty, so this
proves the fibre as a scheme, not only its set of points.

Chapter 12. All orders, cyclic branches, and excision


12.2      Binary choices are too many
For each pair {i, j} let Dij be the two-chart double of SI along D(zi − zj ), and form

                                           PI =                   Dij .
                                                           Y

                                                     {i,j}⊂I,SI

Its charts are indexed by tournaments: one orientation of each pair. The total-order charts are those
indexed by transitive tournaments. At a total three-point collision the product has eight branches,
including the two directed three-cycles, whereas the order scheme has six.
   For a chosen directed triangle, take the closed locus where its three coordinates agree and its
three orientations have that cyclic value. It is closed: in the product of the three corresponding
doubles over the triple-equality locus it is a closed-and-open component, and the equality locus is
closed in the base. Pull back to PI . Let ZI be the finite union of all these directed-triangle loci.
Theorem 12.2 (Acyclic binary reconstruction). The inclusion of the total-order charts induces an
isomorphism
                                      XI ∼= PI \ ZI .
It respects relabelling and the given affine chart coordinates.

Proof. The intersections of tournament charts invert exactly the pair differences on which their
orientations disagree. Hence the intersections of total-order charts are precisely those defining XI .
Every such chart avoids the cyclic equality loci.
   Conversely take a point of the complement and form its equality blocks in the residue field.
Within a block, the retained orientations give a tournament with no directed triangle. Such a
tournament is transitive: if a ≺ b and b ≺ c but a ̸≺ c, there is a directed triangle. Thus it is a total
order on each block. Extend those block orders to a total order on I. Every orientation changed
during this extension joins different blocks, so the corresponding difference is invertible near the
point. The point therefore belongs to that total-order chart. These charts cover the complement
with exactly the already identified gluing, proving the isomorphism. Relabelling preserves all the
constructions.

   The fibre counts are now forced:
                                                  (|B|
                                          P
                                                    2 ),
                                                                               Y
                            #(PI,p ) = 2      B                 #(XI,p ) =         |B|!.
                                                                               B

A spanning tree of binary comparisons is also insufficient. With three coincident labels it gives four
binary branches, rather than six total orders. The correct geometry is neither all independent pair
choices nor a choice of tree edges alone.


12.3      The first derived excision term
For three labels remove the common translation coordinate and put R = k[x, y], so the pair
differences are x, y, x + y. Let P = P3 , U = X3 , and
                                                                                 R[x−1 , y −1 ]
                 B = RΓ(P, OP ),        C = RΓ(U, OU ),                   M=                      .
                                                                               R[x−1 ] + R[y −1 ]
The quotient has basis x−a y −b , a, b ≥ 1.

Theorem 12.3 (Cyclic branches are removed by local cohomology). There is a natural distinguished
triangle of R-module complexes
                            (M ⊕ M )[−2] −→ B −→ C −→ (M ⊕ M )[−1].
At the total collision its derived specialization is the triangle
                                             k2 −→ k8 −→ k6 ,
where the last map forgets the two cyclic branch coordinates.

Proof. The complement consists of two disjoint cyclic branch points. Excision identifies the support
complex at either point with the affine local-cohomology complex
                               [R −→ R[x−1 ] ⊕ R[y −1 ] −→ R[x−1 , y −1 ]],
in degrees 0, 1, 2. The first map is restriction and the second is the signed difference. Its only
cohomology is M in degree two. The localization triangle for the union of the two disjoint supports
gives the first assertion.
   The finite chart covers of P and U have respectively eight and six charts, and flat affine
intersections. Derived tensor with k = R/(x, y) can be applied to their bounded Cech complexes.
Every intersection of distinct charts inverts at least one difference that vanishes in this fibre. Those
terms become zero. Hence B ⊗LR k = k8 and C ⊗LR k = k6 , with the stated restriction map.
   For completeness compute the support term independently. Tensoring the Koszul resolution of k
with M gives
                                     w7→(−yw,xw)          (u,v)7→xu+yv
                                 M −−−−−−−−→ M 2 −−−−−−−−→ M.
Multiplication by x is surjective, and the common kernel of x, y is kx−1 y −1 . The middle term is
exact: for xu + yv = 0, choose w with xw = v. Then u + yw ∈ ker x. Multiplication by y is surjective
on ker x, so choose a ∈ ker x with ya = u + yw. Replacing w by w − a gives (u, v) = (−yw, xw).
Therefore M ⊗LR k = k[2], and each shifted support term contributes one degree-zero line. This
proves the specialized triangle including its degrees.

   The map B → C is a map of derived coefficient algebras. The distinguished triangle is asserted
in modules, not as a direct-sum splitting of algebras. The removed terms become genuine branch
idempotents after derived specialization. Before specialization they are local-cohomology classes
two degrees higher. A dimension count alone sees neither the support map nor this degree change.


12.4      An all-arity correction complex
The same method gives a finite constructive correction in every arity. Index the directed-triangle
closed loci by a finite set TI . For a nonempty subset J ⊂ TI , write ZJ = α∈J Zα . The standard
                                                                          T

support Mayer–Vietoris complex has terms
                                  M
                                         RΓZJ (PI , O)[r − 1],      r ≥ 1,
                                 |J|=r

with alternating maps deleting a support condition. Its totalization is RΓZI (PI , O). This follows
inductively from the two-support triangle
                              RΓZ1 ∩Z2 −→ RΓZ1 ⊕ RΓZ2 −→ RΓZ1 ∪Z2 .

                                                     44
Chapter 12. All orders, cyclic branches, and excision


The latter is obtained by the Cech complex of the two complementary opens. Finite induction
proves the formula, with no infinite convergence issue.
   This is an explicit algorithm rather than a new unnamed support object. On each tournament
chart a directed-triangle condition is either empty or the equality ideal for that triple. Intersections
of nonempty conditions identify all coordinates in the connected components of the resulting equality
graph. Choose one representative in each component. The differences to that representative form
an independent regular sequence in RI . The support complex is the finite tensor of the two-term
localization complexes for those differences. Use the finite affine chart Cech complex to glue them
and the preceding alternating support complex to form their union. Finally the cone of support
sections mapping to ordinary derived sections gives RΓ(XI , O).
   Thus every term, differential and restriction map of the correction can be written in localizations
of polynomial rings. At four or more labels, simply summing one term per triangle is wrong because
cyclic support loci can intersect. The intersection terms are exactly what the Mayer–Vietoris
complex retains.
