13.1      The map which an order chart has to receive
There is a difference between inventing coordinates which resemble a mixed configuration and
constructing a map from that configuration. We do the latter. The target will remember precisely
the real order which cannot be changed while keeping the holomorphic coordinates fixed. It will not
remember an order between points which can pass at distinct complex positions.
   Let P and Q be disjoint finite label sets. Define
                   n                                                                              o
          FP,Q = (zi , ti ; wa ) ∈ (C × R>0 )P × CQ : (zi , ti ) ̸= (zj , tj ),   wa ̸= wb (a ̸= b) .

The labels in P are interior points of the half-space and those in Q are boundary points. There is
no exclusion zi ̸= wa : a bulk point above a boundary point is not a collision. For the interior theory
alone one may replace R>0 by R. The distinction will be retained whenever a contraction is chosen.
   Put
                               SP,Q = Spec C[zi , wa ][(wa − wb )−1 : a ̸= b].
For every total order σ of P take one copy of SP,Q , and glue two such copies by inverting the differences
zi − zj on which their orders disagree. Denote the result by XP,Q . Thus XP,Q = XP × Conf Q (A1 )
as a scheme; for P = ∅ there is one chart, not an empty space. This coefficient scheme is the
two-coloured extension constructed in the preceding programme sources. The new point is the
geometric comparison below.
   A mixed configuration determines a point of XP,Q    an : on each group of equal z coordinates, use

the strict order of its real coordinates ti , and extend these orders arbitrarily to a total order of P .
Different extensions differ only on pairs with distinct z coordinates and hence describe the same
point of the glued target. We have therefore defined a map
                                                            an
                                            pP,Q : FP,Q −→ XP,Q .                                       (13.1)

Relabelling and forgetting some labels commute with this map.




                                                       46
Chapter 13. The actual mixed configuration space, with both colours


13.2      Every multiple overlap has a vertical contraction
Let J be a nonempty finite collection of order charts and UJ their intersection. In its coordinate
description all differences on which two orders in J disagree have been inverted. The inverse image
p−1 UJ is characterized as follows. If two bulk holomorphic coordinates coincide, every order in J
orients that pair in the same direction, and their real coordinates must have that direction. There
are no other inequalities.
   Choose an order σ0 ∈ J and real numbers ai strictly increasing in that order. For the half-space
choose all ai > 0. The formula

                          Hu (z, t; w) = (z, (1 − u)t + ua; w),      0 ≤ u ≤ 1,                    (13.2)

is defined on all of p−1 UJ . Indeed a required difference tj − ti is positive at both ends of this
convex combination, so remains positive. Differences of holomorphic coordinates do not change.
Positivity of each real height is preserved in the half-space. At u = 1 this map lands in the section
sa (z; w) = (z, a; w), and the section is fixed throughout the homotopy. The same argument works
after restricting UJ to any analytic open subset.
   This is stronger than a contractible-fibre assertion. It gives one contraction over the entire
overlap, compatible with restriction in the base. A different choice of constants gives a different
contraction, but not a different projection or a different pullback map of functions.


13.3      The partial de Rham–Dolbeault theorem
On FP,Q let
                               AHT = A0,∗                    ¯     
                                      z,w ⊗ Λ(dti : i ∈ P ), ∂ + dt .

Here the coefficients are smooth functions on the displayed open manifold. Holomorphic differentials
dzi , dwa are not yet included. The total product is the wedge product, with its ordinary graded
signs. For P = ∅ this is exactly the boundary Dolbeault complex.

Theorem 13.1 (Mixed coefficient comparison in every colour profile). Holomorphic pullback along
(12.1) defines a multiplicative quasi-isomorphism of derived sheaves
                                                 ∼
                                       OXP,Q
                                         an −−→ RpP,Q∗ AHT .

It is compatible with restriction, relabelling, and forgetting labels. The assertion includes empty label
sets and both the full real-line and positive-half-line versions.

Proof. Work first over one multiple overlap UJ . Pull a form back by H : [0, 1] × p−1 UJ → p−1 UJ ,
take its coefficient containing du, and integrate that coefficient from zero to one. Call the resulting
operator K. The elementary fundamental theorem of calculus on each smooth coefficient gives

                                  (∂¯ + dt )K + K(∂¯ + dt ) = s∗a π ∗ − 1

up to replacing K by its negative according to the endpoint convention. Fix the sign by this
displayed identity. The reason ∂¯ introduces no defect is that the homotopy changes only real
variables and is independent of z, w. More explicitly, the derivative with respect to every z̄i or w̄a
passes through the integral, and the total wedge sign is the sign needed in the anticommutator.


                                                     47
Chapter 13. The actual mixed configuration space, with both colours


   Thus the partial complex on p−1 UJ is homotopy equivalent, through a base-preserving homotopy,
to the Dolbeault complex on UJ . The same is true over every smaller open in UJ . The local
Dolbeault lemma identifies the latter complex with holomorphic functions. This proves the assertion
as a sheaf quasi-isomorphism on each chart. The terms of the smooth form complex are acyclic for
sections on the preimages of analytic opens: those preimages are open manifolds and the sheaves
are fine. Consequently this computation represents derived direct image, not just underived direct
image.
   The map which is a quasi-isomorphism is the canonical holomorphic pullback. It is multiplicative.
The auxiliary section and homotopy need not preserve multiplication and are used only to prove
that this canonical map is an equivalence. On overlaps the canonical pullbacks agree, so descent
gives the global derived equivalence. Naturality of that map under the three stated operations
proves the final assertions without choosing contractions simultaneously for all label sets.

  The theorem does not identify algebraic global functions with analytic global functions. Even on
one copy of A1 , polynomials and entire functions differ. Algebraic CP,Q = RΓ(XP,Q , O) and analytic
      an , O) are separate coefficients connected by analytification. An algebraization theorem needs
RΓ(XP,Q
additional hypotheses on the operations and their growth.


13.4       Topologization is a further differential
Add the holomorphic differentials to the partial complex. Its differential becomes the full de Rham
differential. The local Poincare lemma identifies the result with the constant sheaf on FP,Q . On the
target this operation is holomorphic de Rham hypercohomology, not ordinary cohomology of its
structure sheaf.
     For Q = ∅ and the full real-line version, FP,∅ = Conf P (R3 ). The resulting Poincare polynomial
is
                                                               |P |−1
                               X                                Y
                                     dim H j (FP,∅ ; C)q j =         (1 + aq 2 ).               (13.3)
                                 j                             a=1

Here is the geometric calculation. Forget the last labelled point. Locally move the remaining points
by disjoint small ambient translations with bump functions; this constructs a local trivialization of
the forgetting map. Its fibre is R3 minus n − 1 points, which retracts to a wedge of n − 1 two-spheres
by joining small enclosing spheres along arcs and contracting the complement. The fibre has only
degrees zero and two. The base configuration space is simply connected: a disk filling a loop
in Euclidean configuration coordinates can be perturbed off the collision subspaces, each of real
codimension three. Hence the fibre cohomology local system is constant.
   For each pair of labels the direction map to S 2 gives a global degree-two class. The n − 1 classes
involving the last label restrict to the basis of the punctured-fibre cohomology. Leray–Hirsch, proved
here by trivializing and then gluing the fibre basis, identifies the total cohomology as a free module
over that of the base with these n basis elements. Induction gives (12.3). This calculation is the
classical configuration-space argument; it is not a consequence of the number of order charts.
    The oriented generators satisfy ωji = −ωij , since the antipodal map on S 2 has degree −1, and
 2 = 0. Three-point restriction gives
ωij

                                      ωij ωjk + ωjk ωki + ωki ωij = 0.



                                                     48
Chapter 13. The actual mixed configuration space, with both colours


One can normalize the signs by using the three outward direction maps on the boundary of a
small three-point collision. The relation is the oriented boundary relation of that configuration.
Together with the preceding induction, it reduces every monomial to a product with distinct larger
endpoints and gives the same dimension as (12.3). Thus no additional linear relation occurs in this
presentation.


13.5     The two-point transgression is not a lost residue
For two points remove the common translation and use the separation coordinate z. The algebraic
two-chart complex is [C[z] ⊕ C[z] → C[z, z −1 ]]. After adjoining dz and the holomorphic differential,
its degree-one principal part z −1 becomes the degree-two class represented by z −1 dz. Polynomial
primitives remove higher Laurent powers; the simple pole survives. Under
                                                                       H
                                                                              the mixed comparison
it is the sphere class of C × R \ {0}, with the scalar factor fixed by dz/z = 2πi and the chosen
sphere orientation. This is a transgression between two specified complexes, not the assertion that a
degree-one coefficient was already a degree-zero boundary product.


13.6     What the two colours do and do not provide
The boundary factor in XP,Q requires its points to remain distinct. Approaching a boundary
collision therefore needs a singular extension, supported target, or residue construction; it is not an
ordinary closed fibre of a localization. Bulk–boundary equality of complex coordinates, in contrast,
is allowed at positive height. These facts fix the domains of mixed insertion maps before any state
space is attached.
   The maps in this chapter prove a coefficient comparison. They do not manufacture state
operations Z ⊗P ⊗ A⊗Q → A or identify a derived chiral centre. The next chapters construct
nested coefficient substitutions and their choice homotopies. Only after operations compatible with
those maps have been constructed can one assert a mixed algebra structure. That separation is
mathematical: the half-space configuration exists independently of a chosen boundary algebra.




                                                  49
Chapter 14

A coloured configuration geometry for
a half-space

14.1     Bulk labels and boundary labels are different
Let P and Q be disjoint finite sets. Elements of P label bulk inputs; elements of Q label boundary
inputs. Their holomorphic coordinates are zi and wa . In a half-space the bulk has a positive
normal coordinate, while all boundary inputs have normal coordinate zero. Consequently boundary
inputs with the same holomorphic coordinate would collide, whereas bulk inputs may have the same
holomorphic coordinate while remaining normally separated.
  This distinction suggests the following algebraic coefficient geometry. It is a construction in its
own right. Its comparison with an analytic or field-theoretic model is not built into its definition.
Definition 14.1. Put
                                                       h                     i
                       RP,Q = k[zi , wa : i ∈ P, a ∈ Q] (wa − wb )−1 : a ̸= b .
For every total order σ of P , take a chart Uσ = Spec RP,Q . For a nonempty set J of such orders, let
DP (J) be the unordered bulk pairs whose relative order varies in J, and put
                                          h                              i
                           RP,Q;J = RP,Q (zi − zj )−1 : {i, j} ∈ DP (J) .
Glue the charts by the identity on these localizations. Denote the resulting scheme by RoP,Q .

   If P is empty there is one chart, not zero charts: there is one total order on the empty set. Thus
Ro∅,Q is the ordinary ordered configuration scheme of boundary points, with all boundary diagonals
removed. If Q is empty, the same construction has the coefficient geometry of the closed ravioli
configurations. The superscript o records that the output is on the boundary; it does not assert
that the two output types have already been identified as an operad.
Proposition 14.2 (The mixed charts glue). The specified localizations are precisely the finite
intersections of the charts. The construction is equivariant under independent permutations of P
and Q.

Proof. For any three bulk orders σ, τ, ρ, a pair disagrees with at least one of τ, ρ when compared
with σ precisely when its order is not constant among the three. Therefore
                                DP (σ, τ ) ∪ DP (σ, ρ) = DP (σ, τ, ρ).

                                                 50
Chapter 14. A coloured configuration geometry for a half-space


The same equation holds from either other chart. Identity maps on the common localizations satisfy
the cocycle condition. Iteration proves the formula for every finite intersection. Boundary differences
are inverted in every chart, so they introduce no further gluing condition. Relabelling variables and
orders sends every inverted pair to the corresponding inverted pair, which proves equivariance.


14.2      A bounded flat complex
Choose an auxiliary order on Ord(P ) and set
                                                M
                                     r
                                    CP,Q =                RP,Q;{σ0 ,...,σr } .
                                             σ0 <···<σr

The alternating restriction differential and the Alexander–Whitney product are exactly those of
Chapter 11. They make this a bounded dg associative algebra over the polynomial coordinate ring.
Every term is flat, being a finite sum of localizations.

Theorem 14.3 (Derived mixed coefficients). The complex CP,Q computes RΓ(RoP,Q , O). In char-
acteristic zero a strictly permutation-equivariant commutative model is obtained from compatible
polynomial differential forms on the simplices of the same cover. The comparison with the or-
dered complex is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes; integration alone is not asserted to preserve
multiplication strictly.

Proof. The cover and all its intersections are affine. The augmented sheaf Čech resolution is exact:
on a stalk choose a chart containing the point and contract by insertion of its index. Resolve the
terms by injectives and take the finite Čech total complex. One filtration gives derived global
sections, while the other gives CP,Q because quasi-coherent cohomology on each affine intersection
vanishes in positive degree. Both filtrations have finite cover length.
   For the commutative presentation replace the coefficient on an intersection indexed by J by
                                                               X                 X
                            RP,Q;J ⊗ k[tσ , dtσ : σ ∈ J]/(          tσ − 1,          dtσ )

and impose compatibility under all faces. Differential and multiplication are coordinatewise;
permutation equivariance is literal permutation of the coordinates and simplex vertices. The standard
polynomial-simplex contraction in characteristic zero supplies the Thom–Sullivan comparison. This
is the same classical comparison used in the preceding coefficient construction, with the coefficient
localizations changed as specified. It is not a claim that the Alexander–Whitney presentation is
strictly symmetric.


14.3      Specialization, including the boundary exclusion
Let a be a point of the affine coordinate space. There are two cases. If two distinct boundary
coordinates coincide at a, a globally inverted difference specializes to zero. If all boundary coordinates
are distinct, only bulk pairs can destroy chart intersections.

Theorem 14.4 (All mixed coefficient fibers). If two boundary coordinates coincide, then

                                            CP,Q ⊗L ka ≃ 0.


                                                    51
Chapter 14. A coloured configuration geometry for a half-space


Otherwise let πP be the partition of P by equality of the bulk coordinates at a. Then the derived
fiber has cohomology only in degree zero and
                                                           Q
                                                                     |B|!
                                   H 0 (CP,Q ⊗L ka ) ∼
                                                     =k       B∈πP
                                                                            .
A bulk coordinate is allowed to equal a boundary coordinate; that equality does not change this
formula.

Proof. Bounded flatness permits ordinary tensoring. A ring in which a function u is invertible
tensors to zero at a point where u = 0: the tensor product would satisfy both u = 0 and uv = 1.
This proves the first assertion. In the second case all globally inverted boundary differences specialize
to nonzero scalars.
   A bulk chart intersection survives exactly when its orders have the same restriction to every block
of πP . Partition the chart set by these restrictions. Each class gives the full simplex complex on the
orders belonging to it, with every nonzero coefficient equal to k. The insertion contraction leaves
one copy of k per class in degree zero. There are |B|! classes, and degree-zero multiplication is
                                                      Q

coordinatewise. No bulk–boundary difference occurs in any of the localization sets, which proves
the last assertion.

   For example, one bulk and two boundary inputs have a single coefficient chart
                                       k[z, w1 , w2 , (w1 − w2 )−1 ].
Its specialization is k when w1 ̸= w2 and zero when w1 = w2 , even when the bulk coordinate is
distinct. Two bulk inputs and one boundary input have two charts. Their coefficient fiber is k 2
when the bulk holomorphic coordinates coincide, regardless of whether that common coordinate
also equals the boundary coordinate.


14.4      An operator-product expansion is not a fiber at an excluded
          point
The zero fiber in the theorem is not a failure of a chiral operator-product expansion. It says that an
ordinary algebraic pullback to an excluded diagonal is zero. A Laurent expansion approaches that
diagonal from its complement and is a different map.
Example 14.5. The ring k[u, u−1 ] has zero tensor product over k[u] with k[u]/(u). Nevertheless
the function u−2 has the nonzero Laurent expansion u−2 in k((u)). A rule that replaced every
meromorphic collision expansion by a derived fiber at u = 0 would erase this coefficient altogether.

   This example separates two tasks in a native mixed theorem. Closed ordering fibers can detect
extension data such as the idempotents of the two-chart raviolo. Boundary poles instead require
punctured formal expansions and residues. One cannot use the first calculation as a substitute for
the second. Both occur in the coloured geometry, but through different functors.


14.5      The singular degree follows from the coefficient degree
At two closed points, the principal-part coefficient lives in cohomological degree one. At two
boundary points, a Laurent coefficient lives in degree zero. This difference forces the two possible
degrees of a singular operation, without proving the corresponding Jacobi identity.

                                                    52
Chapter 14. A coloured configuration geometry for a half-space


Proposition 14.6 (A necessary degree calculation). Suppose a degree-zero binary coefficient-valued
operation on a graded complex Z induces, on cohomology, a principal-part expansion

                                                       βr (a, b) ⊗ [z −r−1 ],
                                                 X
                                 µ(a, b)sing =
                                                 r≥0

where the coefficient classes have degree one and the sum is finite for each input pair. Then
|βr (a, b)| = |a| + |b| − 1. Consequently the formal polynomial r λr βr (a, b)/r! has cohomological
                                                               P

degree −1. If its Jacobi and skew identities are separately proved, it becomes a degree-zero Lie
operation on H(Z)[1] under the shift convention of this book.
   For a boundary operation with Laurent coefficients in degree zero, the analogous singular coeffi-
cients instead have degree |a| + |b|.

Proof. The total degree of each tensor term equals the sum of the output-state degree and the
coefficient degree. Equality with the input degree gives the first assertion. The formal parameter
has degree zero, so it does not change that degree. An element of original degree p has degree p − 1
in H(Z)[1]; the output of the proposed bracket therefore has shifted degree p + q − 2, equal to the
sum of the two shifted input degrees. Repeating the first calculation with coefficient degree zero
gives the boundary assertion.

   This is not a proof of a Gerstenhaber or vertex identity. The identity requires the actual three-
input operations and their comparison under the tree expansions. A degree calculation may force
the shape of a possible theorem, but it cannot supply the missing operations.


14.6     The construction on a smooth curve
The chart gluing itself does not require a preferred coordinate. For a smooth separated curve X,
begin with the open subset of X P ⊔Q on which the boundary coordinates are pairwise distinct. Take
one copy for each order of P , and glue two copies on the complement of every bulk diagonal on
which the two orders disagree. The pairwise gluing proof is unchanged because it uses only the sets
of inverted diagonals.
   On coordinate neighbourhoods the construction restricts to the preceding formulas. A change of
coordinate sends the ideal of a pairwise diagonal to the same ideal. In completed local coordinates,
the ratio of the two defining equations is a unit. Thus the chart gluing and its restriction to formal
neighbourhoods are intrinsic. The global derived coefficient object is the derived pushforward of the
structure sheaf along this glued scheme’s map to X P ⊔Q .
    No assertion of proper base change is hidden here. The explicit fiber calculation is local on
the coordinate base and follows from its bounded localization model. On a general curve a global
computation of derived sections additionally uses an affine cover of the curve and its intersections.
The finite chart gluing and the sheaf-level construction exist independently of a choice of global
affine coordinates.




                                                       53
Chapter 15

Formal insertion maps at every tree

15.1      The ring in which an expansion lives
Let D be a commutative k-algebra and let t1 , . . . , ts be a finite set of formal parameters. Write

                                       D[[t1 , . . . , ts ]][t−1           −1
                                                              1 , . . . , ts ]

for the localization of the power-series ring obtained by inverting the parameters. Every individual
element has a lower bound on the exponent of each parameter. This ring is smaller than a collection
of arbitrary arrays with unrelated negative tails. The distinction matters for substitution.

Lemma 15.1 (The formal inverse used below). If u ∈ D× and v ∈ (t1 , . . . , ts )D[[t1 , . . . , ts ]], then

                                     (u + v)−1 = u−1              (−u−1 v)r
                                                             X

                                                             r≥0

is a well-defined element of D[[t1 , . . . , ts ]]. It is the unique inverse, and its coefficient in total
parameter degree at most N uses only the first N + 1 summands and the coefficients of v up to that
degree.

Proof. The rth summand lies in the rth power of the parameter ideal. Therefore only finitely many
summands contribute modulo any fixed power. Their compatible partial sums define an element
of the complete ring. Multiplication by u + v telescopes to 1 modulo every power of the ideal,
hence equals 1. Uniqueness follows by multiplying any proposed second inverse by u + v. The
finite-coefficient assertion follows from the same filtration estimate.

   The result does not say that u + v can be inverted when u is not a unit. Which leading coordinate
differences are inverted is decided by chart intersections. We now check that decision at every
internal vertex.


15.2      Closed rooted trees and their coordinates
A rooted tree T has a finite nonempty set of leaves I. Every internal vertex v has a finite nonempty
set of incoming edges In(v). Unary vertices are permitted as coordinate substitutions; they are not
silently identified with operadic identity vertices. Nullary vacuum insertions and unital operadic

                                                        54
Chapter 15. Formal insertion maps at every tree


identities are not asserted by the expansion theorem. Assign a coordinate uv,a to each incoming
edge a. Assign a scale tv to each nonroot internal vertex. A total order on In(v) is a chart choice at
v.
  For a leaf i, define its global holomorphic coordinate recursively. At a vertex v, a leaf in child a
has relative coordinate
                                (
                       (v)          uv,a ,           a is the leaf i,
                      Zi =                     (w)
                                    uv,a + tw Zi ,   a leads to an internal vertex w.

The root coordinate is Zi . Expanding the recursion writes Zi as a finite sum of local coordinates
multiplied by products of scales along a path.
   The local chart orders determine a global leaf order by lexicographic comparison at the first
vertex where two leaf paths separate. Equivalently, concatenate the leaf orders of the child subtrees
in the order specified at their parent.
Lemma 15.2 (Least-common-ancestor factorization). Let i, j be distinct leaves and let v be their
least common ancestor. Let a, b be the two incoming edges of v containing them. Then
                                                                        
                                         Zi − Zj = sv uv,a − uv,b + rij ,

where sv is the product of the scales on the path from the root to v, and rij belongs to the ideal
generated by the scales strictly below v. The global order of i, j is the local order of a, b at v.

Proof. All coordinate contributions above v are identical for i and j and cancel. Their common
multiplicative scale is sv . At v the first different contributions are uv,a and uv,b . Every later
contribution includes the scale of a vertex below v, giving the asserted remainder ideal. The rule for
lexicographic order is exactly comparison at this first separation.


15.3      Pullback on every chart intersection
Consider a nonempty finite collection L of tuples of local chart orders, one order at each vertex of
T . Its projection to the order set at v is Lv . Put
                                                      h                                 i
                                k[uv,a : a ∈ In(v)] (uv,a − uv,b )−1 : {a, b} ∈ D(Lv ) .
                           O
                  DT,L =
                            v

Let ET,L be its power-series ring in all scales, localized by the scales themselves. The tuple-to-leaf-
order map sends L to a collection ℓ(L) of global orders.
Theorem 15.3 (All closed expansion maps). The assignment zi 7→ Zi extends uniquely to a
coefficient-ring map
                                   RI,ℓ(L) −→ ET,L .
These maps commute with restrictions of chart intersections. The inverse of every inverted difference
is given by the formal inverse lemma at its least common ancestor.

Proof. A generator zi − zj is inverted in RI,ℓ(L) precisely when the global order of i, j differs between
two tuples in L. By the least-common-ancestor lemma, the local order of the two corresponding
incoming edges at their least common ancestor differs in Lv . Therefore uv,a − uv,b is inverted in
DT,L .

                                                          55
Chapter 15. Formal insertion maps at every tree


   The same lemma writes its substituted global difference as an invertible scale monomial times
a unit plus a parameter-adically small remainder. The formal inverse lemma gives its inverse in
ET,L . Hence the polynomial coordinate map extends to the localization by its universal property.
Uniqueness follows from that same universal property. Enlarging L merely inverts further local
differences. The two formulas for each inverse agree after this localization, so the maps commute
with restriction.

    This is an all-arity result: the proof applies to every finite rooted tree and every chart intersection.
It is not a claim that a map to an uncompleted tensor product exists. A single denominator can
produce infinitely many nonnegative powers of an insertion scale.


15.4      A four-point calculation
Take two pairs, with coordinates

                  z1 = w1 + ϵx1 ,   z2 = w1 + ϵx2 ,        z3 = w2 + ϵy1 ,   z4 = w2 + ϵy2 .

An internal pair difference is
                                     (z1 − z2 )−1 = ϵ−1 (x1 − x2 )−1 .
On an intersection where w1 − w2 is invertible, a cross-pair difference expands as

                                        1     X (−ϵ)r (x1 − y1 )r
                                            =                     .
                                     z1 − z3 r≥0 (w1 − w2 )r+1

The internal inverse uses a punctured scale, while the cross inverse uses completion. Neither
operation can be erased from the target coefficient ring.
   For a further insertion x1 = v1 + δa and x2 = v2 + δb, one obtains

                                       1           X (−δ)r (a − b)r
                                             = ϵ−1                   .
                                    z1 − z 2       r≥0
                                                       (v1 − v2 )r+1

The same formula is obtained by first expanding the inner pair and then inserting the result, or by
substituting directly into the original difference and factoring at the least common ancestor. The
next theorem is the general version of this calculation.


15.5      Coherence under refinement
Refining a vertex replaces one local coordinate configuration by a smaller rooted tree, with fresh
scale parameters. If an old edge is replaced by a path of new edges, its scale is replaced by the
product of the scales on that path. Its attachment coordinate is replaced by the corresponding finite
path sum. These two substitutions must be made together.
    There is an important restriction. We compare the expansions of finite-pole rational coefficient
functions and polynomial-simplex forms with such coefficients. We do not assert that every vertex
refinement defines a map on an arbitrary completed series ring. The admissible coefficient subalgebra
is the image of these localized rational functions under the displayed expansion maps. A rational
presentation, or an equivalent pole-growth condition, is retained when it is refined.

                                                      56
Chapter 15. Formal insertion maps at every tree


Theorem 15.4 (Treewise coherence). For any finite sequence of vertex refinements, the expansions
of a finite-pole rational input obtained by iterated rational substitution and by direct substitution in
the final tree agree in the final expansion ring. Different orders of independent refinements give the
same result. Relabelling leaves and vertices commutes with this admissible expansion system. No
refinement map on all unrestricted formal series is claimed.

Proof. On a polynomial generator zi the two maps agree because recursively substituting the
coordinate formula is ordinary associativity of substitution in a finite polynomial expression. For
an inverted difference, first retain its rational expression through refinement. In the final tree the
least-common-ancestor factorization expresses its substituted denominator as a scale monomial
times a formal unit. Its inverse therefore has a fixed lower bound in each scale exponent. A finite
product of such inverses has the same property with the bounds added. Both rational substitution
orders yield this same denominator, so uniqueness of its inverse in the final expansion ring makes
their expansions equal. This proves existence in that ring as well as equality; it does not rely on
substituting arbitrary infinite coefficient series.
   This argument checks a generating set of the localized source ring. Hence the maps agree on
the whole ring. Independent refinements introduce disjoint coordinate and scale variables, so the
same argument applies in either order. Finally relabelling preserves paths, least common ancestors,
and the sets of inverted pairs; it sends every displayed formula to the corresponding relabelled
formula.

   Notice what the proof avoids. It does not set a formal scale equal to 1 inside an arbitrary power
series, an operation which need not exist. It compares all maps after introducing the parameters of
the final tree. Edge contraction at a numerical scale is a separate specialization problem, not part
of the formal-coherence assertion.


15.6      A completion counterexample that fixes the scope
Let u be invertible and consider the perfectly valid formal series
                                                            2
                                                    tn u−n ∈ k[u, u−1 ][[t]].
                                              X
                                 F (t, u) =
                                              n≥0

The substitutions u = δa and t = δϵ would give
                                                        2       2
                                                    δ n−n ϵn a−n .
                                              X

                                              n≥0

There is no uniform lower bound on the exponent of δ. Consequently this is not an element of

                                       k[a, a−1 ][[δ, ϵ]][δ −1 , ϵ−1 ].

Thus an unrestricted map between the completed rings does not follow from the coordinate
substitution.
   A finite-pole inverse behaves differently. For
                                       1
                                               (−t)n v n u−n−1 ,
                                             X
                                           =
                                     u + tv n≥0

Chapter 15. Formal insertion maps at every tree


the same scale-aware substitution gives

                                         δ −1        (−ϵ)n v n a−n−1 ,

which has the fixed lower bound −1 in δ. The preceding theorem uses precisely this finite-pole
property. Promoting the theorem to completed state-valued operations requires a stated growth
bound that replaces it; that promotion has not been assumed.


15.7      Derived coefficient maps
The chart maps induce maps of coefficient diagrams. To retain permutation symmetry, use the
polynomial-simplex model rather than treating an ordered Alexander–Whitney product as strictly
symmetric. If ℓ sends a tuple of local chart orders to its global order, the simplex map sends a
barycentric coordinate by
                                         X                                X
                              tσ 7−→              ta ,        dtσ 7−→              dta .
                                       a:ℓ(a)=σ                         a:ℓ(a)=σ

Together with the coefficient-ring maps this defines a dg commutative pullback on the compatible-
family models.

Proposition 15.5. For fixed atlas maps, the derived coefficient pullbacks respect differentials,
multiplication, all face restrictions, and admissible rational refinement. Their targets are the formal
expansion models of the product covers at the vertices. Identifying such a target with a tensor product
of vertex coefficient objects is an additional derived comparison, not equality of arbitrary ordered
presentations.
                                                                         P                 P
Proof. The barycentric substitution preserves the relations t = 1 and dt = 0, commutes with
d(t) = dt, and is multiplicative. Restricting to a face removes exactly the corresponding chart
indices on either side. The coefficient localization maps commute with those restrictions by the
closed expansion theorem. Thus compatible families map to compatible families, and the result is a
dg algebra map. Under refinement the barycentric maps compose as maps of finite vertex sets, while
the coefficient maps agree by treewise coherence. These two verifications prove the assertion.




                                                         58
Chapter 16

Mixed insertion and the boundary
pole problem

16.1      Colour-admissible rooted trees
Give every edge of a rooted tree one of two colours, closed or open. A vertex with closed output has
only closed inputs. A vertex with open output may have both sorts of input. The local coefficient
object at a closed vertex is the closed chart system of the preceding coefficient construction; at an
open vertex it is RoP,Q . In particular, distinct open incoming slots have their coordinate differences
inverted.
   Insertion into a closed slot uses the ordinary lexicographic order of a bulk cluster. Insertion of
an open-output configuration into a boundary slot is different. Its new bulk inputs lie close to the
boundary and precede the outer bulk inputs in the normal direction. Bulk inputs arising from two
different boundary slots may be interleaved. Their outer boundary centres are holomorphically
distinct, however, so changing such an interleaving changes only pairs whose differences are invertible
in the outer coefficient ring.
  This last fact permits the mixed insertion map to be canonical without pretending that the
boundary inputs themselves carry a topological total order.


16.2      The two possible leading denominators
                                                                 (w)
Use the same recursive coordinate formula Zi = uv,a + tw Zi along a coloured tree. At a least
common ancestor, two leaves can separate in two relevant ways.
   If they are separated by two closed slots, their relative bulk order is determined by the local bulk
chart order. A disagreement across charts inverts the corresponding leading bulk difference.
   If they arise from distinct open slots of an open-output vertex, the leading difference is inverted in
every chart, because boundary points at that vertex are required to be distinct. Thus any ordering
of the new bulk clusters associated with those two open slots gives the same glued image after
expansion.
   A pair of open leaves must separate at an open-output vertex through two open slots. Their
leading difference is therefore always invertible, exactly as required for a meromorphic boundary


                                                   59
Chapter 16. Mixed insertion and the boundary pole problem


operation. No choice of an order on the open leaves is needed.
Lemma 16.1 (Mixed denominator test). For a colour-admissible tree, every difference inverted in
a target chart intersection pulls back to a scale monomial times a unit plus terms in the parameter
ideal. Its inverse exists in the corresponding mixed expansion ring. This includes every open–open
difference in the output configuration.

Proof. For a bulk-order disagreement, follow the two leaf paths to their least common ancestor. If
the disagreement is determined by closed slots, the leading difference is inverted by the closed-order
chart-intersection rule. If it comes from a choice of interleaving between two open-slot clusters,
that leading boundary difference is globally inverted. These are the only sources of a change in the
induced bulk order: along a single closed slot the comparison is delegated to the subtree, and all
bulk clusters emerging from boundary slots lie before the outer bulk slots.
   For two open leaves, the paths cannot enter a closed-output vertex. At their first separation they
belong to distinct open slots, so the leading difference is globally inverted there. The least-common-
ancestor coordinate factorization then applies in every case. Invert the scale monomial and use the
formal inverse lemma for the remaining factor.


16.3      Independence of the auxiliary interleaving
Proposition 16.2. The local insertion maps obtained from different choices of interleaving of bulk
clusters attached to distinct boundary slots glue to the same morphism into the mixed configuration
scheme over the expansion ring.

Proof. Any two interleavings with fixed internal orders are connected by adjacent exchanges of
elements from different clusters. An exchanged pair has distinct outer boundary centres. Their
difference is a unit in the outer coefficient ring, and its substituted leaf difference remains a unit in
the expansion ring by the mixed denominator test.
   The target charts for the two exchanged orders are glued precisely on the open set where that
leaf difference is invertible. Thus the two local maps are identified by a target transition map.
Repeating the adjacent exchanges proves independence of the interleaving. The transition maps are
identities on their common localization, so these identifications obey their cocycle identity and do
not introduce a further choice of scalar or sign.

  For example, inserting one bulk point above each of two boundary points w1 , w2 gives the two
possible bulk orders 12 and 21. Their holomorphic difference is

                                        w1 − w2 + ϵ(x1 − x2 ).

Because w1 − w2 is inverted, this difference is invertible as a formal series. The two bulk-order charts
are therefore the same chart on the insertion domain. Replacing w1 − w2 by zero would invalidate
the argument, but that replacement is not allowed in the outer boundary configuration ring.


16.4      The full coloured expansion statement
Theorem 16.3 (Coloured tree expansion system). For every finite colour-admissible rooted tree
without nullary vertices there are coefficient pullbacks, on finite-pole rational coefficients and their

                                                   60
Chapter 16. Mixed insertion and the boundary pole problem


polynomial-simplex models, from its output configuration to the formal expansion model of its vertex
configurations. They have the following properties:

   1. every localization map is defined by the explicit inverse of a substituted coordinate difference;
   2. the underlying glued maps are independent of auxiliary interleavings of distinct boundary-slot
      clusters; choices of atlas lifts have coherent comparison homotopies as constructed below;
   3. admissible rational refinement, permutation of labels, and restriction of chart intersections
      commute at the glued geometric level, and through the specified comparison homotopies in
      chosen derived-section models;
   4. in the polynomial-simplex models they are dg commutative maps.

The theorem concerns a tree-indexed coefficient expansion system. It does not identify this system
with chains on a topological Swiss-cheese operad, and does not assert a representation on the centre
of every chiral algebra.

Proof. The mixed denominator test constructs each local ring map. Interleaving independence glues
it on the product chart cover. For refinement, the two candidate maps agree on all polynomial
coordinates by recursive substitution. Each inverted denominator maps to two inverses of the same
element, so the maps agree there as well. Any remaining discrepancy in an induced bulk chart order
is an exchange between two clusters whose leading boundary centres are distinct; the preceding
proposition identifies those charts. This proves refinement coherence at the scheme-and-expansion
level.
   Restriction of chart intersections is localization by further differences, and the same inverse
formulas apply after that localization. Permutations preserve the distinction between open and
closed colours, as well as paths and the inverted-diagonal rules. Finally the barycentric pullback
on the finite chart nerves is defined for each chosen atlas lift exactly as in the closed case. Its
compatibility with coefficients, differential, multiplication, and faces proves the dg assertion for
that lift. Equality of glued geometric maps does not make two different atlas lifts literally equal as
printed cochain maps; the next chapter supplies their comparison homotopies.


16.5      What a boundary operation has to preserve
Suppose a candidate representation assigns complexes Z and A to the closed and open colours. The
required operations have the form
                    µcP : Z ⊗P −→ Z b
                                   ⊗CPc ,       µoP,Q : Z ⊗P ⊗ A⊗Q −→ A b o
                                                                       ⊗CP,Q .
For each coloured tree, composing vertex operations and expanding coefficients must agree with
expanding the output operation, or must be joined by the homotopies prescribed by a chosen
cofibrant model. The target completion is the one supplied by that tree, not a guessed ordinary
tensor product.
   For P = ∅, the open operations must recover the boundary chiral operation with its poles,
translations, and residues. For one closed input they must recover the specified action of the derived
centre. For two or more closed inputs they must also preserve the centre compositions and their
coherences. Finally, on a curve the coordinate constructions must descend. These are concrete tests
of a proposed native mixed Deligne map. The coefficient theorem has now supplied the receiving
expansion rings and all the comparison maps among them, but has not supplied the operators µ.

                                                  61
Chapter 16. Mixed insertion and the boundary pole problem


16.6     Why constant coefficients do not produce the native repre-
         sentation
An ordinary Swiss-cheese algebra permits a scalar extension by a single commutative ring. The
preceding coefficient system is different. The coefficient ring changes with the arity and the colours;
open pair differences are inverted globally, bulk differences are inverted only on specified chart
intersections, and refinement uses punctured formal scales. Tensoring an ordinary action with
one holomorphic function ring does not construct those arity-changing pullbacks or the required
boundary Laurent operations.
   Conversely, the existence of the coloured expansion system does not force the centre of a boundary
algebra to be the Hamiltonian centre computed earlier. The latter is the derived endomorphism
algebra of one particular completed diagonal. A general chiral boundary has a different module
problem. Identifying the two would require a functor between the specified module categories and a
comparison of their diagonals or representing action objects.




                                                  62
Chapter 17

Chart choices and all their comparison
homotopies

17.1     Equality of maps is not equality of presentations
A geometric map may admit several maps between chosen covers. Their induced ordered Čech
maps need not coincide on the nose. This is not an obstruction to the geometric map, but it is an
obstruction to declaring a particular cochain presentation strictly natural without further work.
The mixed interleavings provide a concrete example.
   Let a source cover have index set S and a target cover have index set T . Suppose f0 , . . . , fm :
S → T are choices of atlas lifts for the same geometric coefficient map. We require the following
common-intersection condition: for every nonempty source intersection indexed by J ⊂ S, the image
of that intersection lies in all target charts indexed by
                                                            m
                                                            [
                                            F (J) =              fr (J).
                                                           r=0

The coefficient pullback to this common target intersection is the same geometric map for every
choice.
   For mixed insertion the condition holds. A change of interleaving reverses only pairs in distinct
boundary-slot clusters. Their differences are units on the insertion domain, so every chart occurring
in any of the chosen interleavings contains the same image. There are finitely many choices on a
finite tree, and hence a finite common intersection test.


17.2     An explicit cochain homotopy for two choices
Extend ordered Čech cochains alternatingly to arbitrary lists of chart indices, with repeated indices
interpreted as zero. If c has degree r ≥ 1, define
                                          r−1
                                          X
                       (Hc)a0 ...ar−1 =         (−1)j cf0 (a0 ),...,f0 (aj ),f1 (aj ),...,f1 (ar−1 ) ,
                                          j=0

with every coefficient pulled back to the common source intersection. Set H = 0 on degree zero.

                                                          63
Chapter 17. Chart choices and all their comparison homotopies


Proposition 17.1. The displayed map has degree −1 and satisfies

                                          dH + Hd = f1∗ − f0∗ .

Proof. Each summand has r + 1 target indices and is therefore a value of the degree-r cochain. The
common-intersection condition makes all coefficient restrictions legitimate. Expand dHc + Hdc. A
deletion before the repeated transition position aj is paired with the same deletion after moving the
transition position one place; their signs differ. The same cancellation occurs for deletions after that
position. The uncancelled end terms use f1 at every index and f0 at every index, with opposite
signs. These are f1∗ c − f0∗ c.
    In degree zero the formula is especially transparent: Hdc at a is (dc)f0 (a),f1 (a) = cf1 (a) − cf0 (a) .
The higher-degree cancellation is its prism version, with restrictions commuting because every map
is the same coefficient pullback on the common intersection.

   The homotopy is a homotopy of complexes. It is not claimed to be a multiplicative map between
Alexander–Whitney dg algebras. Multiplicative higher comparisons are more naturally written in
the polynomial-simplex presentation.


17.3      A polynomial-simplex family of multiplicative comparisons
For a nonempty J ⊂ S, use barycentric variables sa , dsa on ∆J . For the choices 0, . . . , m, use vr , dvr
on ∆m . Define, for each target chart index b,
                                                      X
                                          tb 7−→                s a vr .
                                                   a∈J, 0≤r≤m
                                                     fr (a)=b

The differential image is obtained by differentiating this polynomial. The sum of the images of all
       P     P
tb is ( sa )( vr ) = 1, so this substitution defines a map of polynomial differential-form algebras.
Theorem 17.2 (All comparison simplices). Together with the common coefficient pullback, the
barycentric formula defines a multiplicative dg comparison parametrized by ∆m between all the
chosen atlas lifts. Restricting to the vertex r gives the map associated with fr . Restricting to any
face of the choice simplex gives exactly the formula for the corresponding smaller list of choices.
Restriction in the source cover is compatible as well.

Proof. The source-intersection condition permits the target form on the intersection indexed by
F (J) to be pulled back. The barycentric polynomial formula preserves the simplex relation, and its
                                P
differentiated formula preserves dtb = 0. It is a map of dg commutative algebras by construction.
   On the vertex r, all v’s vanish except vr = 1, and the formula becomes tb 7→ a:fr (a)=b sa , the
                                                                                         P

atlas map for fr . On a face, setting the omitted v’s and their differentials to zero gives precisely
the smaller-choice formula. The same argument with the s’s proves compatibility with source-cover
faces. These verifications show that compatible families map to compatible families and prove every
asserted face identity.

   Thus two atlas lifts have a comparison interval; three have a comparison triangle whose boundary
is the three intervals; four have a tetrahedron filling the resulting boundary; and so on. This
is an explicit formula at every level. It does not rely on a claim that a binary homotopy must
automatically extend.

                                                      64
Chapter 17. Chart choices and all their comparison homotopies


17.4     A chain-level interpretation by shuffle prisms
The same coherence can be seen on normalized simplicial chains. Triangulate ∆r × ∆m by monotone
lattice paths from (0, 0) to (r, m). A path gives a simplex whose vertices are its successive grid
vertices. Give the path the sign (−1)ι , where ι counts the vertical steps occurring before horizontal
steps. Send a vertex (i, j) to the chart fj (ai ).
   The signed sum of these simplices is the comparison prism. In its boundary, an interior face
occurs twice, from the two paths differing by an adjacent horizontal–vertical exchange, and the
signs are opposite. The surviving boundary faces are the boundary of ∆r times ∆m , and (−1)r ∆r
times the boundary of ∆m . This proves the chain identity. Degenerate simplices vanish under
normalization. All sums are finite.
   Dualizing this finite combinatorial identity gives the higher cochain-homotopy identities. The
polynomial-simplex construction is preferable when multiplication must be retained, while the
prism formula makes the alternating signs and cancellations visible. Neither construction requires a
finite-dimensional algebraic dual of the entire Laurent coefficient complex.


17.5     Coherence that has been supplied and coherence that has not
The comparison simplices settle the dependence of the coefficient models on chart lifts and mixed
interleavings. Combined with the rational refinement theorem, they give a coherent tree-indexed
geometric coefficient system. They do not supply an action on an arbitrary boundary chiral algebra:
there is still no map from these coefficient-valued operations to the centre’s endomorphism system
until that representation is constructed. Distinguishing these two kinds of coherence is essential.
A complete proof for the coefficients is a genuine result, but it is not a proof about unspecified
operators.




                                                 65
Chapter 18

The diagonal of an order chart

18.1      Residue fields detect the whole complex
We first justify the unbounded categorical argument. Let R be a polynomial ring over a field. If
M ∈ D(R) satisfies M ⊗LR κ(p) = 0 for every prime, then M = 0.
   Here is a direct proof. The residue fields generate D(R) as a localizing subcategory. To see
this, prove R/p belongs to their localizing subcategory by induction on its Krull dimension. In
dimension zero it is its residue field. In positive dimension its fraction field is κ(p). The quotient of
that field by R/p is the filtered union of finitely generated torsion submodules. Each has a finite
prime filtration with prime quotients R/q for strictly larger primes. They belong by induction.
Closure under extensions and filtered colimits puts the quotient, and then R/p, in the subcategory.
In particular it contains R.
   For fixed M , the objects N such that N ⊗LR M = 0 form a localizing subcategory, because derived
tensor is exact and preserves colimits. The hypothesis puts every residue field in it, hence also R.
Therefore M = 0. This proof uses no boundedness of M .


18.2      A compact generator that is not an ordinary affine coordinate
          ring
Write X = XI , R = RI , and C = RΓ(X, OX ), retaining its full E∞ structure. All categories below
are enhanced derived categories. Derived quasi-coherent descent, tensor products and internal Homs
are the standard foundational framework; the specific generation and diagonal arguments are proved
here.
Theorem 18.1 (Monoidal reconstruction of order-chart coefficients). The structure sheaf is a
compact generator of Dqc (X), and derived global sections give a symmetric monoidal equivalence
                                                        ∼
                                       RΓ : Dqc (X) −
                                                    → ModC .

The tensors are ⊗LOX and ⊗LC , respectively.

Proof. The finite affine cover has affine intersections. Derived global sections are computed by its
bounded Cech totalization of affine sections. Each term commutes with direct sums, and a finite
totalization does too. Thus OX is compact.

                                                   66
Chapter 18. The diagonal of an order chart


   For generation suppose RΓ(F ) = 0. Derived base change to any residue field of R can be
performed on this finite Cech complex. On an affine intersection it is the ordinary associativity of
derived tensor, and the base scheme and its intersections are flat over R. Hence

                                   RΓ(F ) ⊗LR κ(p) ≃ RΓ(Xp , F |LXp ).

By Proposition 11.1 the fibre is a finite disjoint union of affine points, so the restriction to each point
is zero. On every original affine chart, the complex representing F consequently has zero derived
tensor with every residue field of R. The detection argument above makes that chart restriction
zero. Descent gives F = 0.
   For clarity, the compact-generator Morita proof is explicit. Put C = R End(OX ). The left adjoint
to R Hom(OX , −) sends C ⊗ V to OX ⊗ V and extends via free resolutions to N ⊗LC OX . The
adjunction unit is an equivalence on C and its equivalence locus is localizing, hence is all C-modules.
The counit is an equivalence on OX and its equivalence locus is localizing, hence all of Dqc (X) by
generation.
   Finally global sections have their natural lax symmetric monoidal map

                                RΓ(F ) ⊗LC RΓ(G) −→ RΓ(F ⊗LOX G).

For fixed G, both sides preserve colimits in F . The map is an equivalence at F = OX , where it
is the unit map. Generation makes it an equivalence for all F . Its associativity and symmetry
coherences are those of the lax structure. Thus the equivalence is strong symmetric monoidal.

   This does not assert that X = Spec H 0 (C) as an ordinary scheme. For the doubled line
H 1 (C) = K/R is indispensable. The nonconnective derived coefficient algebra is the input to the
equivalence. Replacing it by H(C) changes the monoidal category, as the idempotent test shows.
   The same proof applies to a finite product X ×S · · · ×S X. Its charts and intersections are
flat affine opens over S and its residue fibres are finite disjoint unions of points. A double Cech
computation gives
                                     RΓ(X ×S X, O) ≃ C ⊗LR C
with the coherent commutative structure. The two orders of finite totalization agree, and their
affine terms are precisely the tensor products of the two intersection localizations. This proves the
required Kunneth comparison here, rather than assuming it for an arbitrary nonproper scheme.


18.3      The relative diagonal is an affine open immersion
Let Y = X ×S X. On the chart Uσ ×S Uτ = S, the inverse image of the diagonal j : X → Y is
D(fστ ). Hence j is an affine open immersion. It need not be closed; the two origins are the basic
example.
  Put D = C ⊗LR C. Under the monoidal equivalence for Y , the algebra j∗ OX corresponds to the
D-algebra C with structure map multiplication D → C. Indeed its global sections are RΓ(X, OX ),
and pulling the two factors of a global function to the diagonal gives their product.
   On each chart of Y , j∗ OX is a flat localization algebra. Thus

                                       j∗ OX ⊗LOY j∗ OX ≃ j∗ OX .


                                                    67
Chapter 18. The diagonal of an order chart


Transporting through the monoidal equivalence gives the crucial identity

                                             C ⊗LD C ≃ C.                                         (18.1)

It is the diagonal multiplication D → C that is a homological epimorphism. The map R → C is not
being declared a homological epimorphism; in general C ⊗LR C ̸= C.


18.4      No additional relative Hochschild directions
Theorem 18.2 (Relative centre of order-chart coefficients). The canonical scalar action induces an
equivalence
                                                ∼
                                             C−
                                              → ZR (C).
It is an equivalence for the internal associative E2 structures, with the left side restricted from
its coherent commutative structure. In particular the statement retains the full derived coefficient
algebra, not only its cohomology.

Proof. The diagonal module C over D corresponds to j∗ OX . The derived direct image for an open
immersion is fully faithful, with j ∗ Rj∗ = 1. Therefore

                              RHomD (C, C) ≃ RHomY (j∗ OX , j∗ OX )
                                              ≃ RHomX (OX , OX ) = C.

Under this equivalence the scalar-action map is the identity. This proves the underlying equivalence
and preserves composition.
   For the higher assertion one can use the canonical central action of the commutative algebra C
on itself. It defines the standard E2 map to the associative centre. In a commutative dg model its
map is inclusion of nullary cochains; the multiplication, zero self-brackets and higher commutative
coherences give that same action. Alternatively use the monoidal fully faithful restriction argument
in the next theorem. The ordinary Deligne/centralizer framework supplies the E2 identification,
and its forgetful functor is conservative. Since the displayed underlying map is an equivalence, it is
an E2 equivalence.

   This is a relative statement over the separation-coordinate ring R. It does not erase absolute
tangent directions over k. For example Zk (k[z]) has the derivation class ∂z , while Zk[z] (k[z]) = k[z].
Nor does Theorem 17.2 prove that C is formal: Theorem 10.1 proves the contrary for the two-chart
coefficient.


18.5      Relative centralization for every coefficient algebra
Let A be an associative algebra in ModC . The coherent commutative action of C is central. Write

                                ER = A ⊗LR Aop ,         EC = A ⊗LC Aop .

There is a central scalar description EC ≃ ER ⊗LD C. The diagonal identity implies

                                          EC ⊗LER EC ≃ EC .

                                                    68
Chapter 18. The diagonal of an order chart


One may verify the latter by a two-sided bar resolution: move the central D-actions together,
identify the middle scalar bar with C ⊗LD C, and use (17.1). Thus restriction from EC -modules to
ER -modules is fully faithful. Indeed its extension–restriction counit is tensor with the displayed
multiplication equivalence.

Theorem 18.3 (Coefficient-relative centre comparison). For every associative C-algebra A, restric-
tion identifies
                                        ZR (A) ≃ ZC (A)
as internal associative E2 algebras. The right side is the centre relative to the full coefficient algebra
C.

Proof. The diagonal bimodule A belongs to the fully faithful image just described. Thus its derived
endomorphisms in the two bimodule categories agree. The image is closed under relative tensor over
A: tensors of C-linear bimodules are again C-linear, with the same tensor construction in the larger
category. Restriction is therefore a fully faithful monoidal functor preserving the unit bimodule
A. The E2 structure on endomorphisms of that unit is functorial for such monoidal functors. This
proves the higher comparison, not only its cup product.

   Transport through Theorem 17.1 gives the same result for algebra objects in Dqc (X). The word
“internal” is essential. Without a localization theorem, the internal quasi-coherent Hom is not
automatically the unrestricted sheaf of all local cochains. Infinite products of quasi-coherent sheaves
can differ from their naive sheafwise products. The next chapter supplies precise sufficient conditions
and a counterexample, so the centre comparison does not silently assert an invalid sheafification
theorem.


18.6      Absolute coordinate directions and finite resolutions
The preceding relative calculation has a useful absolute control. On S ×k S the base diagonal is
cut out by the regular sequence ziL − ziR . Pull this Koszul resolution to X ×k X. Inside its inverse
image, the order-chart diagonal is the affine open immersion already studied. Restricting to that
open and applying its fully faithful direct image retains the finite Koszul endomorphism calculation.
Thus the absolute diagonal calculation has the expected exterior coordinate directions in addition
to the derived functions.
   This paragraph describes the finite diagonal method, not an assertion of a chosen global E2
formality map. Identifying its underlying complex with polyvector fields uses the regular-sequence
Koszul calculation; identifying every higher operation with a strict Schouten model would require
a formality theorem. The distinction will become decisive for ramification, where even a relative
commutative refinement is obstructed by a special-fibre bracket.




                                                   69
Chapter 19

Absolute centres and the return of the
coordinates

19.1     The base ring was part of the preceding answer
For an order-chart scheme X = XI over R = k[zi : i ∈ I], the relative diagonal is open and
ZR (C) = C, where C = RΓ(X, O). This does not say that the absolute centre over k has no
coordinate derivations. In the absolute product, the two copies of every coordinate are independent.
Equating them is a regular equation, not an identity already imposed by the base.
   Let P = X ×k X. The finite chart construction and the compact-generator proof apply to P ,
with base R ⊗k R. Thus

                           RΓ(P, O) = C ⊗k C,            Dqc (P ) ≃ ModC⊗C .

The equality of global coefficient algebras follows by the finite affine-cover Kunneth calculation:
each pair of affine intersections has coordinate ring the tensor product of the two localization rings,
and finite totalization over a field commutes with that tensor product.


19.2     An open neighbourhood followed by a regular diagonal
Let W ⊂ P be the union of the matched pairs Uσ × Uσ . The diagonal of X lies in W . Inside this
open neighbourhood it is the common zero locus of

                                      ui = ziL − ziR ,      i ∈ I.

On a matched chart these form a regular sequence in a polynomial ring. On its overlaps they remain
regular after localization. Conversely, a point of W with equal left and right coordinates has the
same order-chart point on both sides, so is on the diagonal. Hence i : X → W is a regular closed
immersion of codimension n = |I|.
   The open immersion j : W → P need not be affine. For a doubled line its restriction on the pair
of opposite charts can be the complement of the origin in an affine plane. We therefore use derived
pushforward and its full faithfulness for open immersions; we do not invoke a false affine-intersection
assertion for this open neighbourhood. Since j ∗ Rj∗ = 1, derived endomorphisms of Rj∗ i∗ OX may
be calculated on W .

                                                  70
Chapter 19. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinates


19.3     A multiplicative small model, and what it forgets
Resolve i∗ OX by
                                                                                        X
                      K = OW ⊗ Λ(e1 , . . . , en ),              |ei | = −1,     dK =        ui ιi .
                                                                                         i

Exactness follows locally by tensoring the one-variable regular-element resolutions. The contractions
ιi are closed odd endomorphisms of K, anticommute and square to zero. Pullback along the first
projection gives the coefficient action of C. It commutes with these contractions in a coherent
commutative Cech or Thom–Whitney presentation.

Theorem 19.1 (Absolute diagonal cup model). There is a multiplicative quasi-isomorphism of
associative dg models
                                                ∼
                       C ⊗ Λ(η1 , . . . , ηn ) −−→ Zk (C), |ηi | = 1,
where ηi is represented by ιi in the Koszul endomorphism model. The differential on the left is the
coefficient differential of C. This assertion concerns the associative cup model, not a declaration
that its commutator computes the Gerstenhaber bracket.

Proof. The absolute diagonal object corresponds to C as a C ⊗ C-module. Full faithfulness of the
open pushforward identifies its derived endomorphisms with RΓ(W, End K). Compose the displayed
multiplicative map with postcomposition by K → i∗ OX . Because K is perfect, the map

                                            End(K) −→ Hom(K, i∗ OX )

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes. The target is i∗ (OX ⊗ Λ(ηi )) with zero Koszul differential:
every ui acts by zero there. On global sections the composite is the identity on C and on the exterior
contraction basis. It is therefore a quasi-isomorphism. The first map was multiplicative because
its generators act by coefficient multiplication and anticommuting contractions. This proves the
associative assertion without requiring postcomposition itself to be an algebra map.


19.4     The bracket acts on principal parts
The coordinate derivative ∂i acts on every order-chart localization ring and commutes with all
restriction maps. It is therefore a derivation of the coherent coefficient model C. As a one-input
Hochschild cochain it represents the exterior class ηi . The usual bar–Koszul comparison checks this
normalization by evaluating on the coordinate zj .
   In cohomology the bracket is

                       [ηi , zj ] = δij ,     [ηi , c] = ∂i c,   [c, c′ ] = 0,   [ηi , ηj ] = 0.       (19.1)

Here c, c′ are coefficient cohomology classes. The first and second formulas are the insertion of a
nullary cochain into a derivation; the third has no insertion slot, and the fourth is the commutator
of two commuting coordinate derivations. The Gerstenhaber biderivation rule then determines the
bracket on every exterior product. No formality theorem is needed for these cohomological formulas.
   On the doubled line, write M = k[z, z −1 ]/k[z]. Then H(C) = k[z] ⊕ M [−1]. Formula (??) gives

                                             [η, [z −m ]] = −m[z −m−1 ].

                                                           71
Chapter 19. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinates


The collision contribution is therefore acted on nontrivially by the absolute normal direction. Its cup
product can vanish while this bracket does not. Replacing the small cup model by a commutative
higher model with zero bracket would change the centre.
   The same formula proves that the absolute higher structure is not a structure over the coordinate
ring with central scalars in the relative sense: [η, z] = 1. A unital E2 augmentation to k is impossible,
since it would send that identity to 0 = 1. The boundary algebra can be augmented without its
absolute centre being augmented.


19.5      The parameter of a ramified family is a marking
For the doubled z-line, pull back along z = xm . The resulting scheme is the doubled x-line, because
its overlap is x ̸= 0. As an absolute geometric object it no longer distinguishes the integers m. As
an object over the original coordinate ring it remembers the map z = xm and its derivative

                                           [∂x , z] = mxm−1 .

Thus an absolute comparison can be simple while the relative centre has the ramification torsion
calculated in the following chapters. Forgetting the map to the base is a genuine forgetting operation,
not an equivalence of marked families.
