20.1      The diagonal cochain complex
For an ordinary algebra A projective over R, the unnormalized diagonal bar resolution has Pn =
A ⊗R A⊗n ⊗R A. Its augmented underlying complex contracts by inserting the unit. The terms are
projective as Ae -modules when the middle tensors are projective over R. Applying bimodule Hom
gives
                              n
                             CR (A, A) = HomR (A⊗n , A),      n ≥ 0.
The usual differential is

                     (bf )(a1 , . . . , an+1 ) = a1 f (a2 , . . . , an+1 )
                                                      n
                                                      X
                                                  +         (−1)i f (a1 , . . . , ai ai+1 , . . . , an+1 )
                                                      i=1
                                                  + (−1)n+1 f (a1 , . . . , an )an+1 .

For dg inputs one uses the internal differential and the suspension convention, retaining the product
totalization in Hom. Derived Hom need not be a direct sum over arities.
   For ordinary cochains the insertion product is
                            p−1
                                   (−1)i(q−1) f (1⊗i ⊗ g ⊗ 1⊗(p−i−1) ),
                            X
                  f ◦g =                                                             f ∈ C p, g ∈ C q .
                             i=0

It includes q = 0: a value is inserted into a slot. A nullary outer operation has no slots. An-
tisymmetrization in shifted degrees p − 1, q − 1 gives the Gerstenhaber bracket. Expanding the
associator of ◦, nested insertions cancel and disjoint insertions occur in opposite orders with the
Koszul interchange sign. This proves the graded pre-Lie identity and therefore the graded Lie
identity for the bracket. With m the multiplication, [m, m] = 0 is associativity, and a degreewise
sign change identifies [m, −] with b.
   Higher braces sum all disjoint ordered insertions. The same nested/disjoint partition proves their
identities in every arity. The cup product is induced by inserting two cochains into m. The ordinary
Deligne theorem identifies this full brace structure with a chain model of an E2 action [4, 5, 6]. The

                                                             73
Chapter 20. Centres, complete braces, and change of coefficients


insertion formulas and their algebraic identities are explicit here; the topological equivalence with
little disks is a foundational input. It is not the native holomorphic–topological mixed theorem.


20.2      Three different endomorphism problems
The augmentation dual RHomA (R, R) requires an actual augmentation. A probe algebra
RHomA (M, M ) requires a specified module and remembers only the subcategory detected by that
module. The diagonal centre ZR (A) resolves A as a bimodule. Its degree-zero part is the ordinary
centre, not an arbitrary endomorphism ring.
  For A = k[x], the diagonal resolution is the two-term Koszul complex on xL − xR . Applying
Hom gives
                            HH• (A, A) = k[x] ⊗ Λ(η),    |η| = 1,
where η is the derivation ∂x . Insertion gives [η, x] = 1. Consequently this E2 centre has no unital
E2 augmentation to k: a Gerstenhaber algebra morphism on cohomology would send the displayed
identity to 0 = 1. The boundary augmentation x 7→ 0 does not propagate to an augmentation of
the full centre. Forgetting the bracket can permit an augmentation of the cup algebra, which is a
different statement.


20.3      Base change with the correct finite object
Theorem 20.1 (Perfect-diagonal base change). Let A be a dg R-algebra whose diagonal is perfect
over AeR . For a derived scalar extension R → S, the canonical comparison is an equivalence
                                                   ∼
                                     S ⊗LR ZR (A) −
                                                  → ZS (A ⊗LR S).

It preserves the internal associative E2 structure.

Proof. For a diagonal module P , consider the comparison from scalar extension of RHomAeR (P, A)
to derived Hom from PS into AS . It is an equivalence at P = AeR , where both sides are AS . The
equivalence locus is closed under finite sums, shifts, cones and retracts, since all the functors involved
are exact. A perfect diagonal is a retract of a finite cell object built from this generator. Hence the
comparison is an equivalence at P = A. Scalar extension preserves the bimodule tensor and its unit,
so the induced centre comparison respects the E2 operations. No exchange of an infinite product
with tensor is used.

   The finite object is the diagonal bimodule. It need not be finite over R, as the enveloping
examples show. Conversely an algebra can be a bounded finite free R-complex and have an infinite
diagonal resolution whose cochains accumulate in a single degree.
Proposition 20.2 (A bounded-coefficient alternative). Let A be an ordinary algebra finite projective
over R. Let D be a commutative dg R-algebra whose underlying complex is bounded and termwise
flat. Then the complete Hochschild cochain comparison
                                    •            •
                              D ⊗R CR (A, A) −→ CD (D ⊗R A, D ⊗R A)

is an isomorphism of the derived cochain models, with their cup and brace operations. In particular
it computes the relative centre after this derived coefficient extension.

                                                   74
Chapter 20. Centres, complete braces, and change of coefficients


Proof. In arity n, finite projectivity identifies the target Hom with D ⊗R HomR (A⊗n , A). Suppose
D is supported in degrees [a, b]. A total cochain of degree q receives contributions only when
q − n ∈ [a, b]. Thus only finitely many arities occur in that degree. The product totalization can
therefore be interchanged with the displayed bounded tensor, degree by degree. Bar resolutions
remain projective/cofibrant relative resolutions after base change; their scalar factors are finite
projective and the bounded flat coefficient tensor is derived. The differential, cups and every fixed
brace are the same finite insertions on either side, with the coefficient Koszul signs. This proves the
assertion including the operations.

   This proposition does not apply to an internally graded finite algebra merely because its underlying
complex is bounded. Input degrees can cancel arity degrees; the next calculation exhibits exactly
that cancellation. Nor does it allow an arbitrary unbounded coefficient complex in place of D.


20.4      An entirely explicit complete brace algebra
Let A = R ⊕ Rϵ, |ϵ| = 1, ϵ2 = 0, d = 0. In the suspended normalized model put e = sϵ, of degree
zero. Define cochains
                           Fn (e⊗n ) = s1,    En (e⊗n ) = e,  n ≥ 0.
Their shifted degrees are −1 and 0; their actual Hochschild degrees are 0 and 1. Nullary terms are
included. The internal reduced multiplications are zero. The exterior terms on Fn cancel because
b2 (s1, e) = e and b2 (e, s1) = −e, and those on En vanish. Thus the entire cochain differential is
zero.
Theorem 20.3 (Every brace in the odd square-zero model). For p, q1 , . . . , qr ≥ 0,

The result is zero for r > p. Any brace with an inner Fq is zero.

Proof. Every inner Eq outputs e. Its shifted degree and all input degrees are zero, so each allowed
choice of r ordered distinct outer slots has the same sign and value. There are pr choices. The

20.5      Locality of infinite operations
Let L<N denote cochains in arities below N . A brace with outer arity p, inner arities q1 , . . . , qr ,
and output arity n obeys                 X
                                 n=p+        qi − r,  p ≥ r.
                                                i

Thus p ≤ n + r and every qi ≤ n. The correct window map is

                                     L<N +r ⊗ (L<N )⊗r −→ L<N .

It is not in general an operation on L<N alone. Every output coefficient uses finitely many input
components. Each finite brace identity can be checked in one sufficiently large common input window,
so these maps define the complete action. The bound is sharp: Fn+r {E0 , . . . , E0 } = n+r
                                                                                         r Fn .
   In characteristic zero the ideal (uN ) is not a bracket ideal. Therefore the cup quotient by that
ideal is not a quotient of the complete centre structure. The operations are continuous with an
arity-dependent shift, not levelwise operations on every fixed quotient.


20.6      Localization which passes every finite test and still fails
Take R = k[t]. The comparison on degree-zero cohomology in this model is

                                      R[[u]][t−1 ] −→ R[t−1 ][[u]].

It misses n≥0 t−n un . Any element on the left has a uniform lower bound on all powers of t
          P

appearing in its coefficients; this series does not. Every finite truncation is nevertheless in the image,
since
                                     N                    N
                                           t−n un = t−N         tN −n un .
                                     X                    X

                                     n=0                  n=0

The failure is not nonflatness of localization. It is failure to commute localization with the infinite
product which the centre actually uses.
   A local complete cochain sheaf can be constructed as the product of its operation sheaves, with
the arity-budget maps above. It need not be quasi-coherent. An internal quasi-coherent centre
uses the product in the quasi-coherent category instead. They agree under appropriate finiteness
conditions, not by definition. This qualification is part of Theorem 17.3, and prevents the order-chart
argument from creating a new localization error.


20.7      Morita transport preserves the centre only on the category
          transported
Let A, B be dg algebras over a field. Suppose that bimodules A PB and B QA are supplied with
compatible equivalences
                                P ⊗LB Q ≃ A,     Q ⊗LA P ≃ B.
These are hypotheses about actual bimodules and maps, not conclusions drawn from equality of
Hilbert series.


                                                    76
Chapter 20. Centres, complete braces, and change of coefficients


Theorem 20.4. The equivalence of bimodule categories

                                        F (X) = Q ⊗LA X ⊗LA P

is monoidal for relative tensor products and carries A to B. Consequently it induces an equivalence
of ordinary derived centres, including their E2 structures.

Proof. The inverse sends Y to P ⊗LB Y ⊗LB Q. In either composite the two adjacent inverse bimodules
cancel by the displayed equivalences. The monoidal comparison is the composite

                        F (X) ⊗LB F (Y ) ≃ Q ⊗LA X ⊗LA (P ⊗LB Q) ⊗LA Y ⊗LA P
                                         ≃ Q ⊗LA (X ⊗LA Y ) ⊗LA P.

The unit comparison is F (A) ≃ B. Associativity and unit coherence are the associativity of derived
balanced tensor and the compatible inverse-bimodule data. Endomorphisms of the monoidal unit are
therefore identified. The two coherent ways of composing these endomorphisms, whose interchange
gives the E2 structure, are carried to the corresponding compositions by the monoidal equivalence.
This identifies that structure, not only its cohomology vector space.

   For A = k × k with augmentation given by the first projection, the augmentation dual is
RHomA (k, k) = k. But the full centre of A is k × k, whereas the centre of the augmentation-
generated category is k. Thus an augmentation dual cannot be substituted into the theorem without
supplying the required category and inverse bimodules. The second direct factor is a nonzero sector
invisible to the selected augmentation.


20.8      Double centralization calculated with its topology
There is a one-variable calculation in which completion is forced rather than postulated. Let
A = k[x], with |x| = 0 and augmentation x 7→ 0. Its two-term augmentation resolution gives a dg
algebra model
                               A! ≃ Λ(η),    |η| = 1,     dη = 0.
                                                                         x
Here is a direct multiplicative verification. On the resolution K = [A −
                                                                       → A] in degrees −1, 0, let
the degree-one endomorphism η be the identity coefficient map from K −1 to K 0 . It is closed and
squares to zero. The unit and this endomorphism represent the two Ext classes, so Λ(η) → EndA (K)
is a quasi-isomorphism of dg algebras.
   The reduced bar of Λ(η) has basis (sη)⊗n , n ≥ 0, all in degree zero, and zero differential. The
bar resolution computes the second augmentation dual using Hom from the direct sum of these free
generators. Hence                                 Y
                                RHomΛ(η) (k, k) ≃     kxn = k[[x]]
                                                        n≥0

as an algebra concentrated in degree zero. Deconcatenation gives the power-series multiplication.
The double-centralizer map is the natural inclusion k[x] → k[[x]] under these choices of generator.
   No degreewise finiteness argument turns this product into a direct sum: every bar word has the
same total degree. Restricting to the polynomial dual would change the Hom object. This example
supports a derived augmentation-completion theorem under suitable general hypotheses, but is not
by itself such a theorem for every algebra or for an undeclared geometric completion.

21.1     A moving multiple root
Let R = k[t], with char k = 0, and let

                                  Am = R[x]/(xm − t),       m ≥ 2.

It is free over R with basis 1, x, . . . , xm−1 . As a k-algebra it is a polynomial algebra, but as an
R-algebra it is ramified. Absolute and relative Hochschild centres consequently answer different
questions.
  Write E = Am ⊗R Am . With variables X, Y ,
                                                              m−1
             E∼
                                                              X
              = k[X, Y ]/(X m − Y m ),     u = X − Y,    v=         X m−1−i Y i ,   νv = 0.
                                                              i=0

The diagonal module is Am = E/(ν).

Theorem 21.1 (The complete periodic diagonal resolution). The complex
                                     ν     v    ν
                                  ··· −
                                      →E−
                                        →E−
                                          → E −→ Am −→ 0

with d2r+1 = ν and d2r+2 = v is a free diagonal resolution. Applying bimodule Hom gives Am in
every nonnegative cochain degree, with

                                   d2r = 0,      d2r+1 = mxm−1 .

Thus
              HH0R (Am ) = Am ,    HH2r+1
                                     R    (Am ) = 0,    HH2r+2
                                                          R    (Am ) = Am /(mxm−1 ).

Proof. In the polynomial domain k[X, Y ], multiplication by the nonzero polynomial ν cancels. If
νf belongs to (νv), then f ∈ (v); hence annE (ν) = (v). Similarly annE (v) = (ν). These equalities
prove exactness at every positive resolution degree, and the augmentation has kernel (ν). On the
diagonal ν becomes zero and v becomes mxm−1 . The ring Am =      ∼ k[x] is a domain and this latter
element is a nonzero divisor. Its kernel and cokernel give the displayed groups.

                                                 78
Chapter 21. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


   A degree-two periodicity map, the identity on each free term after shifting by two, is a chain map.
Its Yoneda powers act by periodicity. If β denotes its degree-two class, the cup algebra is therefore

                             HH•R (Am ) = Am [β]/(mxm−1 β),                  |β| = 2.
All degrees are even, so its degree-−1 Gerstenhaber bracket is zero. This vanishing is a statement
about the family cohomology, not a proof that the family cochain algebra is commutative or
E2 -formal.


21.2      A bar representative with its normalization
On the fixed free basis, multiplication has the form
                                                µt = µ0 + tβ,
where                                  (
                            i    j       xi+j−m ,      i + j ≥ m,
                         β(x , x ) =                                     0 ≤ i, j < m.
                                         0,            i + j < m,
It is normalized, since inserting 1 cannot overflow. Associativity for the polynomial family µs implies
[µ0 , β] = 0 and [β, β] = 0 by comparing coefficients of s, s2 . Hence it is a Hochschild cocycle for
every t.
   Its class is exactly the periodicity class, not an unspecified nonzero multiple. Map the degree-one
generator of the periodic resolution to [x] in the normalized diagonal bar. Map the degree-two
generator to
                                              m−1
                                              X
                                                     [xi |x]xm−1−i .
                                              i=1

The bar differential telescopes to m−1  i    m−1−i , which is v applied to [x]. The potential last
                                  P
                                   i=0 x [x]x
term [xm ] = [t] vanishes in the normalized relative bar. Evaluating the carry cochain on this sum
leaves only i = m − 1, with value one. This fixes the generator and its product normalization.
   Define the normalized unary cochain
                                        δ(xi ) = ixi ,        0 ≤ i < m.
It is not an R-derivation of Am . Direct substitution in the Hochschild differential gives
                                                    bδ = mtβ.
When i + j < m the derivation defect is zero. When i + j ≥ m, the two input degrees sum to i + j,
while the output basis exponent is i + j − m. Their difference is precisely m, multiplied by tβ(xi , xj ).
This proves the identity on every basis input.


21.3      The special fibre and its full Gerstenhaber algebra
Set Bm = Am /tAm = k[x]/xm . The same periodic resolution applies, but multiplication by mxm−1
now has kernel (x). The unary cochain δ becomes the Euler derivation. Its class generates the odd
groups as a k[x]/xm−1 -module. Periodicity gives
                                        k[x, δ, β]
                HH• (Bm ) =                                   ,   |x| = 0,    |δ| = 1,   |β| = 2,
                                (xm , xm−1 δ, xm−1 β, δ 2 )

                                                         79
Chapter 21. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


where the algebra is graded commutative. The odd square is zero in characteristic zero. The
periodicity chain map identifies multiplication by β on each positive group; the degree-zero action
and the annihilators are read from the same resolution.
   The complete Gerstenhaber bracket is determined by

                    [δ, x] = x,     [δ, β] = −mβ,        [β, x] = [β, β] = [δ, δ] = 0,

and the biderivation identities. These formulas have direct cochain checks. The first is evaluation of
the derivation on x. For the second, on an overflowing pair δβ has output exponent i + j − m while
the two inserted derivations contribute i + j, giving −mβ; both sides vanish on nonoverflowing
pairs. For [β, x], the two insertions cancel because the carry function is symmetric. For [β, β], both
double-overflow composites are nonzero exactly when i + j + k ≥ 2m, and then have the same
value xi+j+k−2m . The self-bracket of a unary derivation vanishes directly. The formulas respect the
displayed relations, so give the bracket on the whole cohomology algebra.
   In particular this bracket is not zero even for m = 2. It is not recovered from the zero Gerstenhaber
bracket of the family cohomology by underived specialization.


21.4     Why derived base change is valid in this example
The base-change assertion has to be proved before it can be used as a formality obstruction. The
ordinary normalized cochains of Am are finite free R-modules in each nonnegative cochain degree,
since the reduced basis x, . . . , xm−1 is finite free. Multiplication by t is termwise injective. The
quotient cochains are exactly the normalized cochains of Bm , with the same insertion formulas.
   Resolve k = R/(t) by the commutative dg algebra K = R[η], |η| = −1, dη = t, η 2 = 0. It is
a bounded free R-complex. Proposition 18.2 identifies K ⊗R CR (Am ) with the derived coefficient
extension of the cochain centre, including the brace operations. The map killing t, η to Ck (Bm ) is
a quasi-isomorphism: on underlying complexes it is the map from the cone of termwise injective
multiplication by t to the quotient. It is a morphism of the cochain operation structures. Thus

                                      k ⊗LR ZR (Am ) ≃ Zk (Bm )

as E2 algebras. No assertion that every infinite diagonal satisfies arbitrary base change has been
used.

Theorem 21.2 (No relative commutative refinement). The internal E2 algebra ZR (Am ) is not
equivalent, over R, to the restriction of an E∞ algebra. In particular it is not relatively E2 -formal
to its zero-differential cohomology algebra with its zero Gerstenhaber bracket, under a formality
convention which factors that zero-bracket model through commutative algebras.

Proof. Derived scalar extension of an E∞ algebra is still E∞ . Its restricted E2 structure has
zero Gerstenhaber bracket on cohomology: the degree-one little-disks class becomes zero in the
commutative operad. An R-linear E2 equivalence to such a refinement would therefore, after the
proved base change, force the Gerstenhaber bracket of Zk (Bm ) to vanish. But [δ, x] = x ̸= 0 in Bm .
This contradiction proves the stronger first assertion, and hence the stated formality obstruction.

  The proof does not rely on an unproved identification of a chiral deformation object with ordinary
Hochschild cochains. It is an obstruction in a completely specified relative associative centre. Nor

                                                    80
Chapter 21. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


does it claim that every notion of a chosen higher-operation model on the cohomology module is
impossible: such a model may retain nontrivial higher operations and is not the formal zero-bracket
replacement excluded here.


21.5      The connecting map identifies the hidden class
The short exact sequence of cochains under multiplication by t gives a connecting map
                                        HH1 (Bm ) −→ HH2R (Am )[t].
By the explicit identity for bδ, it sends xa δ to mxa β for 0 ≤ a ≤ m − 2. These classes form bases
on both sides, so it is an isomorphism. This is the exact mechanism creating the odd special-fibre
classes from even torsion in the family centre.
  The cohomology algebra has the right torsion groups to produce the special-fibre dimensions
and cup groups by derived tensor. What it lacks is the higher operation whose boundary is the
parameter times the carry cocycle. The bracket test therefore distinguishes more than dimensions,
and more than the derived additive special fibre.


21.6      The entire two-root cochain algebra
For m = 2 the normalized bar has one reduced letter. Let Fn (x⊗n ) = 1 and En (x⊗n ) = x, in cochain
degree n. The complete normalized cochain differential is
                               (                                      (
                                 2En+1      n odd,                        2tFn+1      n odd,
                       bFn =                                  bEn =
                                 0          n even,                       0           n even.

All internal bar terms vanish after normalization because x2 = t is a scalar. The two exterior terms
give the displayed formulas. There is one cochain arity in each degree, so no hidden same-degree
infinite product occurs here.
   The cup products are
                       Fp Fq = Fp+q ,     Fp Eq = Ep+q = Eq Fp ,            Ep Eq = tFp+q .
Put a = F1 . As a strict dg associative algebra this is
                     R[x] ⊗ T (a)
                                  ,       |x| = 0,      |a| = 1,      dx = 0,      da = 2xa2 .
                      (x2 − t)
The odd generator is associative, not exterior: a2 ̸= 0. The differential is a derivation since
d(a2 ) = da a − a da = 0. Here δ = xa and β = a2 , so dδ = 2tβ.
  Every higher brace is also explicit. An inner Fq gives zero by normalization. For inner Eq1 , . . . , Eqr
and outer Fp or Ep , the output is the same type in arity p + qj − r, multiplied by
                                                                P

                                                   Pr                P            
                                   X                      (q −1) lj +
                                                       j=1 j
                                                                             (q −1)
                                                                          k<j k
                                                (−1)                                   .

This is the signed insertion formula: lj is the chosen outer slot, and the parenthesis counts the
expanded input positions preceding that inserted block. It fixes the sign even for nullary inputs.
Thus this is an all-arity cochain model, not only a periodic complex together with a guessed bracket.

                                                         81
Chapter 21. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


21.7     A strict Gerstenhaber shortcut is impossible
The two-root model has the strict cup relation x2 = t and the chain bracket [δ, x] = x, but δ is not
closed. A strict R-linear Gerstenhaber biderivation with these same data would give

                                  [δ, x2 ] = 2x2 = 2t,    [δ, t] = 0,

a contradiction. The actual chain bracket is not a strict derivation of the cup product on arbitrary
nonclosed cochains. Its defect is

                                δ(ab) − δ(a)b − aδ(b) = −(bδ)(a, b).

At a = b = x this equals −2t. The carry cochain supplies precisely that homotopy. On cohomology
the defect vanishes, which is why the Gerstenhaber biderivation law holds there.
   Replacing the family by a strict dg Gerstenhaber algebra with the same naive generators would
therefore introduce an error even before a chiral realization is attempted. The correct repair retains
the full brace operations and the nonclosed unary cochain. This finite example explains why
cohomological bracket calculations are insufficient to construct a mixed chain-level theory.




                                                  82
Chapter 22

The exact commutative-refinement
locus of a finite family

22.1     A criterion, rather than one more example
The root family shows that an even cohomology ring can hide an obstruction to commutative
higher structure. For finite locally free commutative algebras the same test gives an exact criterion.
Throughout this chapter R is a regular noetherian Q-algebra of finite Krull dimension and A is a
finite locally free commutative R-algebra. A refinement means an E∞ algebra whose restriction
along E2 → E∞ is equivalent to the given relative centre as an E2 algebra over R.


22.2     The base changes used in the proof are valid
Each unnormalized Hochschild cochain group HomR (A⊗n , A) is finite projective. For localization in
R, its termwise scalar extension is the corresponding cochain group of the localized algebra. There
is no infinite product in one degree: the input algebra is in degree zero and arity n contributes
cochain degree n.
   For a prime p, localize first and use a finite regular-parameter Koszul resolution of κ(p) over the
regular local ring Rp . Its tensor with the cochain complex computes derived scalar extension. The
resulting double complex has bounded Koszul width. Filtering it by that finite width justifies the
comparison with termwise reduction of the finite-projective cochains. It follows that

                                κ(p) ⊗LR ZR (A) ≃ Zκ(p) (A ⊗R κ(p)).                             (22.1)

The comparison preserves braces, cup products and the higher operations: each of their fixed-arity
formulas is a finite sum of scalar-compatible compositions. A further field extension is flat and gives
the same comparison. This argument does not apply to the graded odd square-zero example, whose
arities contribute an infinite product to a single total degree.


22.3     Every nonreduced finite local fibre has a nonzero derivation
Let B be a finite-dimensional commutative algebra over a characteristic-zero field. Decompose it
into local factors. In a nonreduced local factor let m be the maximal ideal and L the residue field.

                                                  83
Chapter 22. The exact commutative-refinement locus of a finite family


The finite extension L is separable. A coefficient copy of L inside B is obtained by lifting a primitive
element through its separable polynomial: if a lift has an error in mj , subtract that error divided by
the invertible derivative. The error moves to m2j ; finitely many steps suffice because m is nilpotent.
  Both m/m2 and Soc(B) ∩ m are nonzero. For the first, Nakayama would otherwise force m = 0.
For the second, take the last nonzero power of m. Choose a nonzero L-linear map

                                       f : m/m2 −→ Soc(B) ∩ m.

Define D(l + x) = f (x̄) for l ∈ L, x ∈ m. The product rule follows from f (m2 ) = 0 and m Soc(B) = 0.
Extend D by zero on the other local factors. This is a nonzero ground-field derivation. Its Hochschild
class is nonzero because all inner derivations of B vanish. For some b ∈ B,

                                  [D, b] = D(b) ̸= 0 in HH 0 (B) = B.

An E∞ refinement would force the Gerstenhaber bracket to vanish: the degree-one generator of the
two-disc operation space maps to zero in the contractible commutative operation space. Thus a
nonreduced finite fibre obstructs that refinement.

Theorem 22.1 (Finite-family refinement criterion). For A and R as above, the following are
equivalent:

  (i) A is finite etale over R;

 (ii) the canonical map A → ZR (A) is an E2 equivalence;

(iii) ZR (A) admits an E∞ refinement over R.

Proof. If (iii) holds, base change (??) and the preceding derivation test show that every geometric
fibre is reduced, hence a product of separable fields. Therefore ΩA/R has zero fibre at every prime
of R. It is a finite module, so localization and Nakayama give ΩA/R = 0.
   Let J be the kernel of A ⊗R A → A. The elementary universal-derivation map identifies J/J 2
with ΩA/R : a ⊗ 1 − 1 ⊗ a represents da, and multiplication modulo J 2 is the Leibniz rule. Thus
J = J 2 . This finitely generated ideal is generated by an idempotent. To see the assertion, choose
generators and express each as a linear combination of them with coefficients in J. The determinant
of one minus that coefficient matrix annihilates J and is congruent to one modulo J. If it is 1 − e,
then e ∈ J, (1 − e)J = 0, and e2 = e, giving J = (e). The diagonal is consequently open and closed.
With finite local freeness this is the etale condition, proving (i).
   Conversely, a finite etale algebra has that open-and-closed diagonal. The diagonal module is
a projective summand of A ⊗R A, so its derived endomorphisms are its ordinary endomorphisms,
namely A. The canonical central multiplication action is an E2 map and this underlying equivalence
proves (ii). Since A is commutative, (ii) supplies (iii). Empty rank-zero components cause no
difficulty and may be treated separately.


22.4      A general monic polynomial and its periodic diagonal
For A = R[x]/f (x), f monic of degree m, set

                                                       f (xL ) − f (xR )
                                  a = xL − xR ,   b=                     .
                                                           xL − xR

                                                  84
Chapter 22. The exact commutative-refinement locus of a finite family


Over (R[xR ]/f (xR ))[xL ], both a and b are monic polynomials and therefore non-zero-divisors, even
when R has zero divisors. Their product is f (xL ). In the quotient by that product their annihilator
ideals are respectively (b) and (a): lift an annihilation equation to the polynomial ring and cancel the
corresponding monic non-zero-divisor. This proves the periodic diagonal resolution with alternating
maps a, b.
   Applying diagonal Hom gives alternating differentials zero and f ′ (x). Hence
                                 2j+1                         2j
               0
             HHR (A) = A,      HHR    (A) = AnnA (f ′ ),    HHR  (A) = A/(f ′ )   (j ≥ 1).

These formulas specify groups, not an unsupported strict Gerstenhaber model. The derivative is
invertible exactly when the finite family is etale. Equivalently the norm of f ′ is a unit, since the
adjugate of its multiplication matrix then supplies its inverse. This is the discriminant criterion
with its actual algebraic reason.
   For f = xm − t the even torsion, divided Euler homotopy and singular-fibre bracket of Chapter 19
are recovered. For a product of coprime factors, Chinese remainders split the finite algebra and
the diagonal calculation into the corresponding factors; multiple-root factors retain the periodic
contribution. Neither a trace discriminant nor the dimensions of the generic centre alone replace
the centre structure at that factor.

Chapter 23

A centre with order and ramification
coupled

23.1      The coefficient and the root share a parameter
Take R = k[z] and let X be the line with doubled origin. Put C = RΓ(X, OX ) with its coherent
commutative structure. On X consider the algebra
                                      Am = OX [x]/(xm − z).
Its ramification parameter is the collision parameter itself. It is not a tensor product with a root
family whose parameter has been held independent of the collision. Its derived global algebra is
                            Aord    L
                             m = C ⊗R Am ,         Am = R[x]/(xm − z).
Because Am is finite free over R, this is also the derived global sections of the displayed algebra
sheaf, with its algebra structure.
Theorem 23.1 (The exact coupled centre). There is a canonical equivalence of internal associative
E2 algebras
                                    ZR (Aord      L
                                         m ) ≃ C ⊗R ZR (Am ).
The right side uses the actual root-family cochain centre, not its cohomology algebra. It retains the
coefficient algebra C, not H(C).

Proof. The coefficient-relative comparison, Theorem 17.3, gives
                                       ZR (Aord        ord
                                            m ) ≃ ZC (Am ).

For this two-chart coefficient a bounded flat commutative model is the endpoint polynomial-form
model of Chapter 10. Its degree-zero module is R2 ⊕ s(1 − s)K[s], and its degree-one module is
K[s]ds, both flat over R. The ordinary algebra Am is finite projective over R. Proposition 18.2
therefore identifies the last centre with C ⊗R ZR (Am ), preserving every cup and brace operation.
This proves the assertion and its derived interpretation.

   The proof has two genuinely different steps. The order-chart diagonal removes spurious relative
directions by a fully faithful monoidal localization. The finite-basis root calculation permits the
complete cochain base change. Neither step is a comparison of Hilbert series, and neither requires a
false perfect-diagonal hypothesis for the ramified root algebra.

                                                 86
Chapter 23. A centre with order and ramification coupled


23.2     Generic and special fibres
Away from z = 0, the two charts agree, so C[z −1 ] ≃ R[z −1 ]. The root algebra there is etale:
mxm−1 is a unit because xm = z is a unit. The periodic diagonal cochain complex has no positive
cohomology. Thus the generic localized centre is the root algebra in degree zero.
   At z = 0, the derived coefficient fibre is k × k. Hence

                            Aord L
                             m ⊗R k ≃ Bm × Bm ,             Bm = k[x]/xm .

The two branches are idempotent components, each with its own nilpotent root variable. They are
not two nilpotent directions on one local algebra.
   The centre base change in this particular family is also exact. Theorem 20.1, associativity of
derived tensor, and the root base-change proof give

                                k ⊗LR ZR (Aord
                                           m ) ≃ Zk (Bm ) × Zk (Bm ).

This is the centre of the actual special-fibre algebra. One can verify the last assertion directly: the
product idempotents split the diagonal resolution, the off-diagonal bimodule components vanish, and
derived endomorphisms of the diagonal are the product of the two centres. The scalar comparison
preserves the same summands and cochain operations.
   On either branch, the special-fibre bracket has [δ, x] = x and [δ, β] = −mβ. Thus the two
kinds of information are simultaneously visible: branch idempotents from the order geometry and
ramification brackets from the root relation.


23.3     Two independent invalid simplifications
Replacing C by H(C) changes the special-fibre algebra to

                                           k[θ, x]/(θ2 , xm ),

in degree zero, rather than Bm × Bm . The former is local, with nilpotent maximal ideal, while the
latter has two branch idempotents. This is an algebra-level failure before taking the centre.
   Replacing only the root centre by its zero-differential, zero-bracket cohomology algebra makes the
right side of Theorem 20.1 a restriction of a commutative derived algebra. Its derived special fibre
therefore has zero Gerstenhaber bracket. The true special-fibre centre has the displayed nonzero
bracket on both branches. This is a higher-operation failure even when the coefficient algebra is
retained.
   These two tests are independent. The first does not use the Gerstenhaber bracket. The second
does not identify branch count with a nilpotent dimension. A reconstruction which passes just one
of them has not retained the complete coupled object.


23.4     The all-arity internal action
The centre in Theorem 20.1 has the ordinary internal Deligne action on the boundary algebra Aord
                                                                                             m .
In a cochain model its mixed operations evaluate cochains on ordered boundary inputs, compose
their disjoint insertions, and multiply adjacent boundary entries. The cup and brace identities

                                                   87
Chapter 23. A centre with order and ramification coupled


establish their coherences; the usual brace-pair/Swiss-cheese comparison is the ordinary foundational
theorem from [5, 6, 11]. Coefficient extension and the fully faithful monoidal localization used above
preserve that action.
   For m = 2 there is no unspecified operation in its algebraic model. Use the basis Fn , En and the
signed slot sum in Chapter 19, tensor with the endpoint coefficient model, and use the coefficient
Koszul signs. The differential on the root factor is dF2r+1 = 2E2r+2 , dE2r+1 = 2zF2r+2 . The
coefficient differential is d(a, b) = b − a in the Cech presentation, or polynomial de Rham differential
in the commutative presentation. The total differential is their tensor sum. It squares to zero
because each factor does, and the two odd terms anticommute by the tensor convention.
  The evaluation of a fixed-arity operation uses only finitely many slots. With a bounded coefficient
model, only finitely many arities contribute in any total degree for this ordinary root algebra.
Consequently the formulas define actual operations, not formal series without a target. They
commute with chart restrictions and with the derived specialization maps already proved.
   This is a complete internal associative action parametrized by the specified collision geometry. It
is not an identification of that action with the native holomorphic–topological operation system
on Xcurve × R≥0 . The labels of an order-chart coefficient base are not, by definition, the spacetime
colours of that mixed operad. The final chapter records the additional comparison needed for that
identification.


23.5     A nonconstant Lie-bundle example
There is another useful form of coupling which does not rely on a multiple root. On the two-origin
coefficient scheme glue the Heisenberg Lie algebra with basis x, y, z0 by

                    x 7−→ x,      y 7−→ y + z −1 x,     z0 7−→ z0 ,     [x, y] = z0 .

The map preserves the bracket. Assign weights 1, 1, 2. The weight-one bundle V is the extension
of O by O with Cech class z −1 . Changing its regular splittings cannot remove that principal part.
A global section has coefficients satisfying b− = b+ = b and a+ − a− = z −1 b, so b = zc. At either
origin every global section lies in the x direction; it does not trivialize the second direction there.
  PBW and the exterior resolution are equivariant under the gluing and give a sheaf-level bar–
Chevalley comparison for the enveloping algebra. The underlying homology sheaves are

                    H0 = O,     H1 = V,    H2 = V ⊗ Oz0 ,     H3 = O(x ∧ y ∧ z0 ).

The canceled map is Λ2 V → Oz0 , an isomorphism. As in the earlier Heisenberg example, that
cancellation is not a strict coalgebra splitting. Applying the monoidal order-chart equivalence
computes the global associative bar with both the extension and bracket retained. This is a
nonconstant geometric algebra, not an unchanged ordinary algebra accompanied by a passive
character.




                                                  88
Chapter 24

A quantum family has a higher center
which its fibers do not determine

24.1     The first example has only two operators
Take a field k of characteristic zero, put R = k[t], and consider

                                         A = R⟨p, q⟩/(pq − qp − t).

The parameter t is central and has cohomological degree zero. Ordering every q before every p gives
the R-basis q i pj : an inversion removes one reversed pair, and its correction lowers the number of
letters by two. There is no overlapping ambiguity involving two distinct reducible adjacent pairs in
a word of length three. Disjoint reductions commute. Thus ordering is unique, rather than only a
spanning argument. In particular multiplication by t is injective.
   We study the relative Hochschild center. Its normalized cochains are
                             n
                            CR (A, A) = HomR ((A/R1)⊗R n , A),                       n ≥ 0.

A cochain in positive arity vanishes when an input is the unit; CR0 (A, A) = A. Use the ordinary

Hochschild differential and cup product, without changing the totalization convention:

                      (dϕ)(a1 , . . . , an+1 ) = a1 ϕ(a2 , . . . , an+1 )
                                                       n
                                                       X
                                                   +         (−1)i ϕ(a1 , . . . , ai ai+1 , . . . , an+1 )
                                                       i=1
                                                   + (−1)n+1 ϕ(a1 , . . . , an )an+1 ,
                  (ϕ ⌣ ψ)(a1 , . . . , ar+s ) = ϕ(a1 , . . . , ar )ψ(ar+1 , . . . , ar+s ).

In particular (da)(v) = va − av for a ∈ A. The bar identities proved earlier give d2 = 0 and the
cup Leibniz identity. Since A is R-free and R1 is a direct summand, the normalized two-sided bar
consists of projective AeR -modules and computes RHomAeR (A, A).
   There is an integral derivation

                            α(v) = t−1 (vq − qv),               α(p) = 1,        α(q) = 0.


                                                         89
Chapter 24. A quantum family has a higher center which its fibers do not determine


Here t−1 denotes division of a numerator already known to be divisible by t; the coefficient ring has
not been localized. The derivation rule follows by dividing the commutator rule in the torsion-free
module A. Consequently
                                       dα = 0,      dq = tα.
Thus [α] ∈ HHR  1 (A, A) is a t-torsion class. It is not zero: a Hochschild boundary reduces to the

zero derivation in the commutative fiber k[p, q], whereas α reduces to ∂p .
   The products of the three classes [α], [t], [α], of degrees 1, 0, 1, are zero in cohomology. Choose q
as the primitive of both adjacent products. Their triple Massey representative is

                          M = q ⌣ α + α ⌣ q,         M (p) = 2q,     M (q) = 0.                    (24.1)

It is a cocycle because
                                    d(qα + αq) = tαα − αtα = 0.
The representative is another divided inner derivation:

                                       M (v) = t−1 (vq 2 − q 2 v).

It is not enough to notice that M ̸= 0 as a cochain. A Massey operation is a coset, and a proof of
nonformality must exclude zero from the entire coset. We do that next, first in the convention used
here and then uniformly for quantum families.


24.2      The defining-system calculation, including indeterminacy
Let D be a unital cochain dg algebra over k. For closed x, y, z of degrees 1, 0, 1, choose u, v of degree
zero with
                                      du = xy,       dv = yz.
Then xv + uz is closed. Changing u, v by closed degree-zero elements changes its cohomology class
by
                                     [x]H 0 (D) + H 0 (D)[z].                              (24.2)
Every element of this subspace is obtained in this way, so it is the full indeterminacy for fixed input
representatives.
   The coset is independent of those representatives. Replacing x by x + ds, |s| = 0, and u by u + sy
changes the representative by
                                        ds v + syz = d(sv).
Replacing z by z + dr, |r| = 0, and v by v + yr changes it by

                                          xyr + u dr = d(ur).

Finally, when a degree-minus-one element w exists, replacing y by y + dw, u by u − xw, and v
by v + wz changes the representative by zero. Applying these replacements successively proves
independence under arbitrary choices of cocycle representatives.

Lemma 24.1 (Massey cosets along quasi-isomorphisms). A dg algebra quasi-isomorphism identifies
the defined cosets above through its induced cohomology isomorphism. In particular, if a coset
does not contain zero, the dg algebra admits no zigzag of dg algebra quasi-isomorphisms to its
zero-differential cohomology algebra.

                                                   90
Chapter 24. A quantum family has a higher center which its fibers do not determine


Proof. Images of a defining system are a defining system. Conversely, subtract an image primitive
from any target primitive. The difference is closed in degree zero. Surjectivity on H 0 expresses its
class as the image of a source cycle; its remaining boundary changes the Massey representative only
by a boundary, by the same Leibniz calculation. The cohomology isomorphism therefore identifies
the entire indeterminacy and the coset, not just one value. The preceding representative-change
identities allow the argument to be repeated along arrows in either direction in a zigzag. In an
algebra with zero differential, the adjacent cohomology products are literally zero, so the zero
primitives give a Massey value zero. That contradicts a coset excluding zero.

   For the Weyl family, the ordinary center is R. Indeed the commutators with q and p of an ordered
polynomial are respectively t∂p and −t∂q , interpreted in its ordered symbol. Torsion-freeness and
characteristic zero force a central polynomial to have no p or q dependence. Thus (21.2) is R[α],
and, since t[α] = 0, it is k[α]. If (21.1) were zero modulo that indeterminacy, some M + cα would be
a Hochschild boundary. Modulo t, its value on p is 2q + c, whereas every boundary has value zero.
This is impossible. The center of this two-operator family is therefore nonformal as an associative
dg algebra, even after forgetting that t came from the coefficient ring.


24.3     The general quantum-family theorem
The mechanism does not depend on an explicit presentation by two generators. The needed
hypotheses describe the first failure of commutativity.
   Let R be k[t], or let R = k[[t]] and use coefficientwise complete modules and continuous cochains.
Suppose A is a unital R-algebra, R-free in the polynomial case and topologically free in the formal
case, with commutative special fiber
                                              A0 = A/tA.
In the formal case the completed tensor powers and their continuous R-linear maps define the
continuous Hochschild complex; every series coefficient is an algebraic vector. The following proof
takes place in that explicit complex. In a derived completed-module interpretation, its bar terms
are understood as completed relative free resolutions, not as arbitrary algebraic tensor powers of a
completed space.
   Assume that, for an integer r ≥ 1,

                              [A, A] ⊂ tr A,        {ā, b̄}r = t−r (ab − ba)                  (24.3)

is nonzero. Division is performed in the torsion-free algebra. A bar denotes reduction modulo t.
The operation is well-defined: changing a lift by tu changes the divided expression by an element of
tA. The commutator Leibniz identity and Jacobi identity, divided respectively by tr and t2r , prove
that it is a Poisson bracket on A0 .
Theorem 24.2 (A family-level higher-center obstruction). Suppose A0 is an integral domain of
characteristic zero and the bracket in (21.3) is nonzero. Then the normalized relative Hochschild
cup dg algebra of A is not formal, even as a dg k-algebra after forgetting its R-structure. More
explicitly, choose a ∈ A such that {−, ā}r ̸= 0 and put

                                        α(v) = t−r (va − av).

The triple Massey coset
                                               ⟨[α], [tr ], [α]⟩

                                                     91
Chapter 24. A quantum family has a higher center which its fibers do not determine


is defined, is represented by a ⌣ α + α ⌣ a, and does not contain zero.

Proof. The divided commutator is an R-linear derivation, hence a normalized Hochschild one-cocycle.
In the formal case it is continuous: it preserves t-adic order on an input because every commutator
already contains the specified factor. The zero-cochain identity is
                                              da = tr α.
Both adjacent products therefore have the primitive a. Its Massey representative is closed and has
value
                           M (v) = aα(v) + α(v)a = t−r (va2 − a2 v).
These are exact cochain identities.
   Since the algebra is concentrated in degree zero, the degree-zero cohomology of its Hochschild
complex is exactly Z(A). If z ∈ Z(A), then z̄ is in the Poisson center of A0 : divide [z, v] = 0 by tr
and reduce modulo t. If zero belonged to the Massey coset, there would be z, z ′ ∈ Z(A) and b ∈ A
with
                                   M + α ⌣ z + z ′ ⌣ α = db.
Reduce this equality of maps modulo t. The right side is zero because A0 is commutative. The left
side becomes
                                   (2ā + z̄ + z̄ ′ ){−, ā}r = 0.                        (24.4)
Choose an input on which the indicated derivation is nonzero. The domain hypothesis forces
2ā + z̄ + z̄ ′ = 0. Applying any Hamiltonian derivation to this equality, and using Poisson centrality
of z̄, z̄ ′ , gives 2{−, ā}r = 0, a contradiction.
   In particular [α] ̸= 0: an exact one-cochain would reduce to zero, contrary to the chosen derivation.
The scalar [tr ] is also nonzero in H 0 , since the coefficient inclusion is injective and there are no
negative cochains. Lemma 21.1 proves nonformality, including after forgetting R-linearity: the
argument only used the three distinguished classes inside the cohomology of the underlying dg
algebra. A hypothetical formality zigzag need not preserve the name of the parameter to preserve
this Massey coset on its transported classes.
Remark 24.3 (Exactly where the domain hypothesis enters). The argument uses absence of zero
divisors only in (21.4). It does not assert the same universal conclusion for a reducible, nonreduced,
or disconnected classical algebra. A suitable faithful Hamiltonian component can sometimes replace
the domain hypothesis, but that is an additional argument, not part of this theorem. Likewise, a
nonzero deformation of a commutative product which remains commutative has zero commutator
bracket and is outside the theorem.
   For a separated formal deformation with commutative domain fiber, any genuine noncommu-
tativity has a least parameter order among its nonzero commutators. If that least order is r, all
commutators belong to tr A and at least one has nonzero image modulo tr+1 . The theorem applies
at that first order. It is not necessary to assume that the first-order Poisson tensor was already
nonzero.


24.4     The first noncommutative infinitesimal window suffices
A formal obstruction is more useful when its finite place of appearance is identified. Let N > r and
put
                                RN = R/(tN ),        AN = A/tN A.

                                                  92
Chapter 24. A quantum family has a higher center which its fibers do not determine


The divided derivation α just constructed descends to an RN -linear derivation αN . We do not
divide by t inside AN , where such division would be ambiguous.

Theorem 24.4 (Finite-window nonformality). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 21.2, for every
N > r the normalized relative Hochschild cup dg algebra of AN over RN has a Massey coset

                                           ⟨[αN ], [tr ], [αN ]⟩

which excludes zero. It is nonformal even over k. In particular the first window N = r + 1 already
detects the obstruction.

Proof. The identities daN = tr αN and MN = aN αN + αN aN specialize. The scalar tr remains
nonzero since N > r. Suppose zN is central and choose any lift z ∈ A. Then [z, v] ∈ tN A for every
lift v. Division in A by tr and reduction modulo t show that the reduction of zN is Poisson central,
because N − r ≥ 1. Thus the entire indeterminacy has the same reduction as in the preceding proof.
All Hochschild boundaries again reduce to zero. Equation (21.4) gives the same contradiction. The
cochains are the relative cochains of the free RN -algebra module AN , so its normalized bar still
computes the relative center. No preservation of cohomology under a nonflat base change has been
assumed.

   The conclusion concerns the relative center of the truncated algebra, not an arbitrary truncation
of the cohomology of the original center. These are different operations. The next chapter computes
this difference explicitly for enveloping families.


24.5      The first noncommutative order is recovered from the center
          tower
When the classical fiber is a polynomial algebra, the finite-window conclusion can be sharpened to
an exact threshold.

Theorem 24.5 (The first nonformal center window). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 21.2,
and additionally A0 ∼= k[x1 , . . . , xm ]. Let r be the first nonzero commutator order. The relative
Hochschild cup dg algebra of AN = A/tN A over RN is formal for every 1 ≤ N ≤ r and nonformal
for every N > r. Thus the labeled tower of higher centers detects the first noncommutative order
exactly:
               r = max{N ≥ 1 : ZRN (AN ) is formal as an associative dg algebra}.
The formality in this formula is of the relative cochain complex, also viewed as a dg k-algebra; it is
not absolute Hochschild cohomology over k of the nonreduced coefficient algebra.

Proof. For N ≤ r, all commutators are zero in AN . Choose lifts x
                                                                ei of the classical polynomial
generators. They define an RN -algebra map

                                      RN [x1 , . . . , xm ] −→ AN .

It reduces to an isomorphism modulo t. Both sides are flat over RN , so their associated graded
modules for the finite t-adic filtration are copies of A0 in each of the N parameter layers, and the
associated graded map is the identity under the chosen identification. Induction through the finite
filtration proves bijectivity. Explicitly, surjectivity is successive lifting of the residue polynomial

                                                   93
Chapter 24. A quantum family has a higher center which its fibers do not determine


and its t-divided remainder; injectivity follows by taking the first nonzero parameter coefficient of a
proposed kernel element. Each lifting uses finitely many polynomial terms and at most N steps.
    For a polynomial algebra over any characteristic-zero base, the relative diagonal Koszul resolution
is free on the regular sequence of coordinate differences. Restriction from the normalized bar gives
the cup dg algebra quasi-isomorphism to the zero-differential algebra of polynomial polyvectors over
that base. This proves the asserted formality over RN , and hence over k. For N > r, Theorem 21.4
proves nonformality even over k. The threshold follows.

   This recovers one definite piece of deformation data from the higher-center tower. It does not
claim that this integer reconstructs the quantum algebra. It also explains why the individual classical
fiber can look completely formal while the first genuine quantum infinitesimal neighborhood already
has an essential ternary operation.


24.6      The Weyl center in all cohomological degrees
The Massey argument used a small part of the center. Here we calculate its complete cochain model.
The diagonal has the free bimodule resolution

                    0 −→ A ⊗ Λ2 k2 ⊗ A −→ A ⊗ k2 ⊗ A −→ A ⊗ A −→ A −→ 0,

with generators ep , eq and differential

       ∂(ep ) = p ⊗ 1 − 1 ⊗ p, ∂(eq )      = q ⊗ 1 − 1 ⊗ q, ∂(ep ∧ eq ) = peq − eq p − qep + ep q.

Its square on the top generator is t ⊗ 1 − 1 ⊗ t = 0 over R. Filter by the sum of the two
polynomial degrees and the exterior degree. The associated graded is the ordinary polynomial
diagonal Koszul resolution. The leading-degree primitive argument terminates, proving exactness.
Antisymmetrization maps it to the normalized bar resolution: the extra internal term is the scalar t
in a barred slot and is zero there. The shuffle diagonal agrees with deconcatenation. Thus restriction
from Hochschild cochains is a quasi-isomorphism of cup dg algebras onto the following small model.
   Use the symmetric ordering symbol space P = k[p, q], so coefficient multiplication is the Moyal
product
                                         t
                                                              
                        f ⋆t g = m exp     (∂p ⊗ ∂q − ∂q ⊗ ∂p ) (f ⊗ g).
                                         2
The exponential terminates on a pair of polynomials. With exterior generators ξ = e∗p , η = −e∗q , the
small center is
                      D = P [t] ⊗ Λ(ξ, η),     d = t d0 , d0 f = fq ξ + fp η,                 (24.5)
with the coefficient star product and exterior multiplication. Constant derivatives are derivations of
⋆t , so the displayed differential is a derivation. The underlying d0 complex is the polynomial de
Rham complex in the coordinates (q, p).
   For completeness, contraction with the Euler field proves its cohomology. On a monomial form of
polynomial degree m and exterior degree j, the anticommutator of d0 with qιξ + pιη is multiplication
by m + j. Divide by this positive integer outside the constant zero-form. This leaves exactly k in
degree zero. Put
                               B 1 = d0 P ⊂ P ξ ⊕ P η,     B 2 = P ξη.



                                                   94
Chapter 24. A quantum family has a higher center which its fibers do not determine


Torsion-freeness then gives the complete relative Hochschild groups
                      0                    1
                    HHR (A, A) = R,      HHR (A, A) = B 1 ,      2
                                                               HHR (A, A) = B 2 ,                (24.6)

where t annihilates both positive-degree groups; higher groups vanish. In the formal version the
degree-zero group is k[[t]] and the same positive-degree groups are killed by t. Cycles are d0 -cycles
and boundaries are t times d0 -boundaries, which proves every assertion without a spectral-sequence
collapse guess.
    The positive-degree ring structure is equally explicit. For f, g ∈ P , the product of the classes
d0 f, d0 g is
                                   [d0 f ][d0 g] = (fq gp − fp gq )ξη.
The reason only the ordinary coefficient product appears here is that all positive-degree classes are
killed by t; every higher Moyal correction therefore vanishes in cohomology. Products whose exterior
degree exceeds two are zero. The action of R on these groups is evaluation at t = 0.
   In this model α = ξ, the primitive of tξ is q, and the Massey representative is 2qξ = d0 (q 2 ). The
full cohomology calculation agrees with the direct Hochschild-cochain obstruction, including its
nonzero value and indeterminacy.


24.7     Formality of fibers does not give formality of the family
At t = 0, (21.5) has zero differential and ordinary polynomial-exterior multiplication. It is already a
formal model for the center of k[p, q]. At a nonzero scalar t = a, the same Euler contraction shows
that inclusion of the scalar field is a dg algebra quasi-isomorphism into the specialized small center.
The center of the numerical Weyl algebra is therefore formal as an associative dg algebra as well.
   Nevertheless Theorem 21.2 proves that the center of the family is nonformal. What survives
between the fibers is an operation involving the parameter class itself. It cannot be seen by making
that parameter zero, and it cannot be seen after making it invertible, since the torsion class [α]
then disappears.
   This example corrects a possible inference, not either fiber calculation. It also gives a constraint
on a mixed comparison which is required to exist over a quantization parameter: whenever its
restriction is this relative center and preserves the cup algebra up to coherent equivalence, it must
preserve the displayed Massey coset. A comparison known only after inverting the parameter does
not establish that stronger assertion.

Chapter 25

The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a

25.1      The parameter belongs in the differential
The ordinary enveloping comparison of Chapter 5 must be changed when the Lie bracket is scaled.
Let g be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra over a characteristic-zero field. Put

                       R = k[t],       A = Ut (g) = TR (g ⊗ R)/(xy − yx − t[x, y]g ).

This is the enveloping algebra over R of the free R-Lie algebra with bracket t[−, −]g . The subscript
on the bracket will be retained when confusion with an associative commutator is possible.
   Ordered rewriting terminates by length and inversions. The two reductions of a decreasing
three-letter overlap differ by t2 times the Jacobi identity in g. Disjoint rewrites commute. Induction
gives an R-basis of ordered monomials. No parameter has been inverted, so this proves flatness
through t = 0.
   The diagonal resolution has

                                   Kn = A ⊗ Λn g ⊗ A,           0 ≤ n ≤ dim g,

and the differential
                                                X
                                                     (−1)i+1 uxi ⊗ xbi ⊗ v − u ⊗ xbi ⊗ xi v

                ∂(u ⊗ x1 ∧ · · · ∧ xn ⊗ v) =
                                                 i
                                                     X
                                                +t         (−1)i+j u ⊗ [xi , xj ]g ∧ xbibj ⊗ v.       (25.1)
                                                     i<j

The bracket is first in the last exterior word, with the remaining labels in their original order. The
factor t in the last line is mandatory.
   To check the square, terms removing disjoint labels cancel. The noncommuting left actions differ
by t[xi , xj ]g , exactly the next bracket-action term; right actions cancel in the same way and commute
with left actions. Nested bracket terms have coefficient t2 times Jacobi. These cases exhaust two
successive operations. Thus ∂ 2 = 0.
   Filter Kn by the two coefficient PBW lengths plus n. The last line strictly lowers this filtration.
Its associated graded augmented complex is the polynomial diagonal Koszul resolution over R,

                                                       96
Chapter 25. The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a formality classification


on the regular sequence xi ⊗ 1 − 1 ⊗ xi . This sequence is regular over an arbitrary coefficient
ring because its elements can successively be taken as polynomial coordinates. Exactness of the
one-variable two-term complex, followed by the finite tensor argument, proves exactness of that
Koszul resolution. Lifting a highest-degree primitive and subtracting its boundary lowers the degree.
Finite descent proves exactness of (22.1). Each term is finite free over AeR .

Theorem 25.1 (The complete relative Rees center). The relative center of Ut (g) is represented, as
a cup dg algebra, by
                                    Dt = Homk (Λ• g, A)
with shuffle cup multiplication in A and differential

After equivariant PBW symmetrization, its underlying complex is

                                                   C ∗ (g, Sym g)[t], tδ ,
                                                                                 
                                                                                                                             (25.2)

where δ is the usual Chevalley differential for the adjoint action on Sym g. The cup product in these
coordinates uses the PBW-transferred coefficient product, not ordinary symmetric multiplication.

Proof. Apply bimodule Hom into A to the free diagonal. This gives the printed formula, including
the factor on its bracket term. Antisymmetrization into the normalized associative bar is a chain
map: the two internal adjacent orders subtract to t[xi , xj ]g . Endpoint terms give the two coefficient
actions. Deconcatenation of the antisymmetrized word is the shuffle diagonal. Restriction of
Hochschild cochains is therefore a map of cup dg algebras into Dt . Both augmented complexes are
projective resolutions of the same diagonal. Lifting along their augmentations and then along their
kernels constructs inverse comparison maps and chain homotopies degree by degree. Restriction is
consequently a quasi-isomorphism, not just a comparison of cohomology dimensions.
   Let
                                                               1 X
                                  sym(x1 · · · xm ) =                 x    · · · xσ(m) .

Its leading ordered term is the original symmetric monomial, so it is an R-module isomorphism by
finite PBW-degree descent. Commuting x through each product and using the defining relations
gives the exact identity
                                    [x, sym(P )]A = t sym(ρ(x)P ),
where ρ is the ordinary adjoint derivation on the symmetric algebra. Every term of the Hom
differential thus has a common factor t, yielding (22.2).
   Define P ⋆t Q = sym−1 (sym(P ) sym(Q)). This is an explicitly computable associative product:
order the concatenated words and apply the finite triangular inverse. The small cup product is ⋆t
on coefficients and the exterior shuffle on forms. The equivariance identity makes the action terms
derivations, and the Chevalley identity gives the cup Leibniz rule. Replacing ⋆t by the ordinary
product would in general change the dg algebra, despite leaving the underlying module fixed.

  The same construction can be completed coefficientwise in t. Every finite diagonal term becomes
completed free; all comparison identities hold in each parameter quotient and in their coefficientwise

                                                                 97
Chapter 25. The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a formality classification


inverse limit. The resulting assertion concerns the continuous relative Hochschild complex with
completed tensor inputs. It is not a statement about discontinuous algebraic Hom on a completed
algebra.


25.2      An elementary specialization rejects the missing factor
Choose a linear x with nonzero adjoint action. At t = 0, the actual associative algebra is Sym g, so
the Hochschild differential of the zero-cochain x is zero. The unscaled expression δx is its nonzero
Hamiltonian vector field. A purported comparison preserving the linear coefficient x and using δ,
rather than tδ, therefore fails the chain-map equation already in degree zero.
   This is the Rees correction developed in the parallel Igusa and Core Synthesis calculations. It is
integrated here with its full diagonal and cup product. It does not say that a Poisson deformation
complex should always be scaled: that is a different complex with a different question. It says
precisely which differential computes this relative associative center.
   There is a related distinction in the literature. Weinstein–Xu’s star-product calculation uses
formal Laurent coefficients and a rescaled Hochschild differential in its Section 3 [31]. Such a result
cannot be used to erase the parameter-torsion classes in a complex over k[[t]] before localization.
Both descriptions can be correct in their specified coefficient categories.


25.3      The cohomology retains all ordinary boundaries as torsion
Let C = (C • , δ) be a complex of k-vector spaces and set
                      Z n = ker(δ : C n → C n+1 ),         B n = im(δ : C n−1 → C n ).
For r ≥ 1, write Dr = (C[t], tr δ). Then its cycles are Z n [t] and its boundaries are tr B n [t]. Thus
                                        H n (Dr ) = Z n [t]/tr B n [t],                            (25.3)
and there is a natural exact sequence
                     0 −→ B n ⊗k R/(tr ) −→ H n (Dr ) −→ H n (C, δ) ⊗k R −→ 0.                     (25.4)
A vector-space section of Z n → H n (C) splits it as modules, but this is not a canonical choice or an
automatic algebra splitting. In the formal case the exact sequence is explicitly
                  0 −→ B n [[t]]/tr B n [[t]] −→ H n (C[[t]], tr δ) −→ H n (C, δ)[[t]] −→ 0.
These are coefficientwise series, not ordinary tensor products with k[[t]] when a coefficient space is
infinite-dimensional. A series of boundaries has a coefficientwise choice of primitives, which proves
surjectivity onto the stated boundary module.
  For the Rees center take r = 1 and C = C ∗ (g, Sym g). Its parameter torsion is exactly B n . It is
not an unspecified extension detected only by an Euler characteristic. Both B n and the ordinary
Chevalley cohomology have explicit finite matrices in each homogeneous symmetric degree.
   The exact sequence also identifies an elementary class. For x ∈ g with nonzero adjoint action,
                                          αx (v) = t−1 (vx − xv)
is a nonzero Hochschild one-class killed by t. Its reduction is the Hamiltonian vector field of x on
Sym g. It would be lost by replacing tδ by δ.

                                                      98
Chapter 25. The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a formality classification


25.4      A classification of family formality
Theorem 25.2 (The Rees center is formal exactly in the abelian case). Let g be a finite-dimensional
Lie algebra over a characteristic-zero field. The normalized relative Hochschild cup dg algebra of Ut (g)
over k[t], and its coefficientwise continuous counterpart over k[[t]], is formal as a dg k-algebra if and
only if g is abelian. For nonabelian g, every relative center of the infinitesimal family Ut (g)/(tN ),
N ≥ 2, is likewise nonformal over k.

Proof. If g is nonabelian, its symmetric algebra is a domain and its linear Poisson bracket is
nonzero. PBW gives the torsion-free deformation required by Theorem 21.2, with first order r = 1.
The Massey coset on [αx ], [t], [αx ] excludes zero for any x with nonzero Hamiltonian action. The
finite-window assertion is Theorem 21.4.
   If g is abelian, the family is the commutative polynomial algebra R ⊗ Sym g. Its diagonal Koszul
complex and the antisymmetrization comparison above give a cup dg algebra quasi-isomorphism
from its Hochschild cochains to
                                          R ⊗ Sym g ⊗ Λg∗ ,
with zero differential and ordinary coefficient product. This is formality. The construction is finite
in exterior degree and extends coefficientwise to the formal case.

   The theorem concerns the associative multiplication of the center complex. It does not say that
the underlying algebra Ut (g), concentrated in degree zero, is a nonformal dg algebra. Nor is it a
statement about the Lie formality of an unrelated BRST complex. These distinctions are necessary
because all three objects occur elsewhere in this programme.


25.5      Perfect base change and the failure of a cohomology shortcut
The diagonal in (22.1) is finite free. Therefore for every commutative R-algebra S the derived
relative center base-change theorem gives

                                      S ⊗L
                                         R ZR (A) ≃ ZS (S ⊗R A).

It is also visible directly: the small Hom complex is R-free and its base change has the differential
and product obtained by substituting the image of t. At t = 0 this is the polynomial-exterior
zero-differential center of Sym g.
   Cohomology does not obey the same rule. Suppose one replaced ZR (A) by its cohomology algebra
with zero differential and then derived-tensored with k = R/(t). The even degree-zero algebra would
have the square-zero ideal
                                     ϵH 1 (ZR (A))[t],  |ϵ| = −1.
For a nonabelian g it is nonzero, because it contains the torsion class ϵ[αx ]. The actual degree-zero
center of the classical fiber is the domain Sym g and has no nonzero square-zero ideal. This gives a
second obstruction to R-linear formality. The Massey argument proves the stronger statement even
after forgetting R.
   The comparison must be derived on both sides. Flatness of the original family alone does not
make its center cohomology flat, and an equality of the two generic centers cannot detect the omitted
square-zero ideal.


                                                    99
Chapter 25. The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a formality classification


25.6      Every infinitesimal center can be calculated without an un-
          proved limit
For N > r choose a vector-space decomposition
                                                                   ∼
                               C n = Hn ⊕ B n ⊕ L n ,       δ : Ln −
                                                                   → B n+1 .

Then the complex Dr,N = (C ⊗ RN , tr δ) is the direct sum of its zero-differential H part and two-term
free blocks
                                               tr δ
                                 Ln ⊗ RN −−−→ B n+1 ⊗ RN .
Consequently
                     H n (Dr,N ) ∼
                                 = (Hn ⊗ RN ) ⊕ (B n ⊗ R/(tr )) ⊕ (Ln ⊗ tN −r RN )               (25.5)
as k-vector spaces, with the indicated RN -actions. The splitting depends on the chosen complements.
The third term is genuine new cohomology coming from torsion in the differential. It is absent from
the full polynomial and formal family before quotienting by tN .
   This formula follows by the kernel and cokernel of multiplication by tr on each block; no inverse
limit has been exchanged with cohomology. If N ≤ r, the differential is identically zero and the entire
complex survives. The next chapter equips these vector-space answers with the higher products
which a cohomology table omits.


25.7      The complete rank-two Lie calculation
Let g = sl2 with basis E, F, H and brackets

                           [H, E] = 2E,       [H, F ] = −2F,       [E, F ] = H.

Use the lower-case variables e, f, h for its symmetric coefficient algebra, to distinguish them from
the deformation parameter t. The invariant polynomial is

                                             q = 2ef + h2 /2.

The H-weight equation first makes an invariant polynomial a polynomial P (ef, h). The E-equation
is h∂ef P − 2∂h P = 0. In the coordinates (q, h) it becomes ∂h P |q = 0. Thus the invariant ring is
k[q].
   Here are the cohomological inputs, with a proof. On a nontrivial finite-dimensional irreducible
sl2 module M , put                            X
                                       hM =      ρ(xa )ιxa
                                                    a

for dual bases under an invariant form. Expanding the Chevalley differential as its trivial-coefficient
part plus θa ρ(xa ) gives
          P
                                                X
                                   δhM + hM δ =     ρ(xa )ρ(xa ).
                                                        a

The cross terms cancel by invariance of the dual-basis tensor. The right side is the nonzero Casimir
scalar on the nontrivial irreducible, so the complex contracts. Complete reducibility decomposes
every finite coefficient degree into such modules and trivial modules. For trivial coefficients the map



                                                   100
Chapter 25. The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a formality classification


from degree one to degree two is dual to the bracket isomorphism Λ2 sl2 → sl2 ; the next differential
is zero and the degree-three volume is nonzero. Hence

                                    H ∗ (sl2 , Sym sl2 ) = k[q] ⊗ Λ(ω3 ).

The ordinary Chevalley differential preserves symmetric coefficient degree m. Put
                                               !               (
                                        m+2                        1 m even,
                                dm =        ,           am =
                                         2                         0 m odd.

The complex in that degree has dimensions (dm , 3dm , 3dm , dm ) and cohomology dimensions
