                                dm − am ,        2dm + am ,         dm − am .                    (25.6)

These are exactly the parameter-torsion multiplicities in Hochschild degrees one, two and three of
the Rees family. At m = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 they are

                         (0, 3, 0), (3, 6, 3), (5, 13, 5), (10, 20, 10), (14, 31, 14).

The coefficient grading is a grading of the small complex, not an algebra grading of the PBW
product. The explicit ⋆t rule in Theorem 22.1 retains the product across coefficient degrees.


25.8      Semisimple fibers can all be formal while the Rees center is
          not
For a semisimple g over a characteristic-zero field, consider the ordinary enveloping small center
C ∗ (g, U (g)ad ). Its closed subalgebra

                                  Z(U (g)) ⊗ (Λg∗ )g −→ C ∗ (g, U (g)ad )                        (25.7)

is a quasi-isomorphism.
   To prove this, use equivariant PBW to decompose the coefficient module into finite-dimensional
semisimple modules degree by degree. The coefficient Casimir contraction just displayed, separately
for simple factors when necessary, contracts every nontrivial irreducible coefficient module. Trivial
coefficients are exactly Z(U (g)). In the remaining trivial-coefficient Chevalley complex, Cartan’s
identity contracts all noninvariant diagonal-action summands, and the differential is zero on invariant
alternating forms. The inclusion of those forms therefore computes its cohomology. It preserves
products: coefficients are central and their multiplication is the actual central multiplication, while
invariant forms are closed under exterior product. This proves (22.7) as a dg algebra statement.
   For t = a ̸= 0, rescaling the Lie generators identifies Ua (g) with the ordinary enveloping algebra.
The fiber center is therefore formal. At t = 0 the polynomial diagonal gives a formal center as well.
Yet Theorem 22.2 proves the nonformality of the entire family for every nonzero semisimple g. This
is a classification of the family obstruction, not a contradiction with either fiber theorem.




                                                     101
Chapter 25. The Rees diagonal, its torsion, and a formality classification


25.9      Source integration and what the theorem adds
The scaled Rees differential, its torsion exact sequence, the divided-inner-derivation test and the sl2
rank calculation were already present in the parallel Igusa and Core Synthesis developments. Their
signs and coefficient categories have been retained here. The new family-level conclusion uses the
complete Massey indeterminacy, including central corrections, and works for every nonabelian finite-
dimensional Lie algebra. Its finite-window version locates the obstruction already over k[t]/(t2 ). The
all-arity reconstruction below supplies the positive higher model rather than ending at nonformality.

Chapter 26

Constructing every higher operation of
the family center

26.1     The obstruction specifies what a small model must retain
The preceding theorem does not prohibit a small center model. It prohibits replacing that model
by its cohomology with only the binary product. There is a direct construction of all the missing
operations. In the Rees examples it uses finite linear algebra in each symmetric coefficient degree
and a parameter division with a precise bound.
   We first make the division visible on a single pair. Let δℓ = b, with |b| = |ℓ| + 1, and replace δ by
d = tr δ. In the polynomial or formal family, the cohomology of this pair is the span of

                                               b, tb, . . . , tr−1 b.

The homotopy is                            (
                                           tj−r ℓ      j ≥ r,
                               h(tj b) =                                h(tj ℓ) = 0.              (26.1)
                                           0           j < r,
It is a k-linear homotopy, not an R-linear one:

                                       h(tr b) = ℓ ̸= 0 = tr h(b).

That distinction is forced by the torsion. An R-linear inclusion of the torsion cohomology into the
torsion-free cycle module would have to be zero, so there cannot be an R-linear strong contraction
onto this cohomology module.
                                                                                       tr
   The correct R-linear small carrier keeps the two-term free block Rℓ −     → Rb and its unary
differential. A minimal model over k, with the parameter action retained through higher operations,
is a different valid construction. We now give both carriers explicitly and never identify them by
simply setting that unary differential to zero.


26.2     Finite linear algebra fixes the contraction
Let C = C ∗ (g, Sym g), with its unscaled Chevalley differential δ. Order the Lie basis, the symmetric
monomials, and the exterior basis. In each symmetric degree m, the complex is bounded and

                                                       103
Chapter 26. Constructing every higher operation of the family center


finite-dimensional. Compute its exact differential matrices over the field of structure constants.
Choose a basis of actual boundary columns B n , then cycle representatives Hn complementary to
those boundaries, and finally preimages Ln−1 of the chosen boundary columns. This yields
                                                                   ∼
                              C n = Hn ⊕ B n ⊕ L n ,        δ : Ln −
                                                                   → B n+1 .
Every choice is obtained by row reduction. To preserve a specified Massey defining system, begin
with a prescribed independent boundary such as α = δa and its prescribed preimage a, then extend
the bases. When necessary subtract the cohomology component of that preimage. The nonzero
boundary makes the prescribed pair independent.
    This procedure terminates in every m. It is a uniform algorithm even when no closed formula for
all Chevalley dimensions is known. Over a standard rational Lie basis all the operations are rational.
For an arbitrary field, only its given structure constants and invertible row-reduction pivots are
used.
   For Dr = (C[t], tr δ) define the zero-differential vector space
                                      Hr = H[t] ⊕ B ⊗k k[t]/(tr ).
The inclusion i uses the chosen representatives. The projection p keeps every H coefficient, keeps
the coefficients of tj b for j < r, and kills L and the remaining B coefficients. Define h blockwise by
(23.1). On H it is zero. On each of the three summands, direct substitution proves
                        pi = 1,       dh + hd = 1 − ip,        h2 = hi = ph = 0.                 (26.2)
For example, dh(tj b) = tj b when j ≥ r, while hd(tj ℓ) = tj ℓ for every j ≥ 0. On a retained torsion
representative, both sides of the contraction equation are zero.
   The same formulas extend coefficientwise to C[[t]] and
                                      b r = H[[t]] ⊕ B ⊗ k[t]/(tr ).
                                      H
The map h can lower t order by at most r, hence is continuous. Each output coefficient comes from
at most one coefficient in a fixed block. The direct sum over symmetric degrees means that each
coefficient remains an algebraic vector; no hidden completion in the Lie coordinates is introduced.
   For the ordinary Rees center r = 1. More generally, replacing its relation parameter by tr gives
the same construction with the indicated r. Its coefficient product is the correspondingly substituted
PBW product. The formulas also apply to the Weyl small center after a homogeneous polynomial
de Rham splitting.


26.3      The complete tensor construction with signs
We spell out how (23.2) constructs every operation, not just the first Massey representative. Work
on the coaugmented direct-sum tensor coalgebras T c (Dr [1]) and T c (Hr [1]). Put
                  b1 (sa) = −s(da),      b2 (sa, sb) = (−1)|a| s(ab),      k = −shs−1 .
Here the product ab is the actual small-center cup product, including its PBW coefficient multipli-
cation. The maps i, p are transported to suspension with degree zero. Let I, P denote their tensor
coalgebra maps. On a word of length n use
                                          n−1
                                                (ip)⊗j ⊗ k ⊗ 1⊗(n−j−1) ,
                                          X
                                  Kn =                                                           (26.3)
                                          j=0

Chapter 26. Constructing every higher operation of the family center


with the tensor Koszul rule. Telescoping gives

                       b1 K + Kb1 = 1 − IP,          ∆K = (K ⊗ 1 + IP ⊗ K)∆.

The second formula follows by cutting a word before or after the slot carrying its homotopy. The
strong side conditions follow from those of h.
   Treat multiplication, extended as the coderivation b2 , as a perturbation and put

                    I ′ = (1 + Kb2 )−1 I,                          P ′ = P (1 + b2 K)−1 ,
                   K ′ = K(1 + b2 K)−1 ,                        bH = P b2 (1 + Kb2 )−1 I.          (26.4)

Every inverse denotes its alternating geometric series. Since b2 lowers tensor length and K preserves
it, there are at most n − 1 perturbations on a word of length n. Thus every expression is finite
before any topology is used.

Theorem 26.1 (An all-arity higher center model). Equations (23.2)–(23.4) define a strictly unital
minimal A∞ algebra on Hr , two A∞ comparison maps with the small cup dg algebra Dr , and a
strong comparison homotopy on their tensor coalgebras. They satisfy every structure and comparison
identity in every input arity. In the formal version each fixed-arity operation is continuous in the
coefficientwise t-adic topology.

Proof. Let B = b1 + b2 . The three identities b21 = 0, b1 b2 + b2 b1 = 0, and b22 = 0 are respectively the
differential, Leibniz, and associativity identities in suspended form. Insert b1 K + Kb1 = 1 − IP into
the finite geometric series. Terms with one more Kb2 cancel with their adjacent geometric-series
term. The remaining terms give

                             BI ′ = I ′ bH ,    P ′ B = bH P ′ ,        P ′ I ′ = 1,

                                         BK ′ + K ′ B = 1 − I ′ P ′ .
This is the tensor perturbation calculation already proved in Chapter 3; here all its finiteness and
strong side conditions have been checked on the actual parameter-dependent carrier. Applying P ′
and I ′ to B 2 = 0 gives b2H = 0.
   The coalgebra identities follow from (23.3) and the coderivation identity for b2 by induction
on word length. Cutting a term of the series separates whether its last homotopy is before or
after the cut. Those cases give respectively the two terms in ∆K; the IP terms are canceled by
hi = ph = h2 = 0. The surviving terms are I ′ ⊗ I ′ for the inclusion, P ′ ⊗ P ′ for the projection, and
the coderivation identity for bH . This proves the A∞ and comparison assertions, not only their
linear terms.
   Choose the splitting with 1 ∈ H0 , h(1) = 0. At a higher operation with a unit input, the two
neighboring binary insertions cancel by the unit signs; endpoint cases cancel the unit-containing
subtree, and hi = 0 kills a homotopy immediately above a unit multiplication. The binary operation
has the given unit and higher operations on unit inputs vanish. This is strict unitality without
assuming an augmentation of the input algebra.
   The unary target operation is zero because the target of (23.2) has zero differential. Its first
comparison term is i, a quasi-isomorphism. Finally, each tree of fixed input arity contains only
finitely many h factors. Each lowers t order by at most r, and the PBW coefficient product
introduces no negative powers of t. Hence a fixed output coefficient depends on finitely many input
coefficients. This proves continuity and coefficientwise algebraicity in the formal version.

                                                    105
Chapter 26. Constructing every higher operation of the family center


   These formulas can be used directly as an implementation. Enumerate the finitely many word
cuts, multiply coefficients by PBW normal ordering, apply the previously fixed rational block
matrices and the shift in (23.1), and project. No “unknown higher correction” remains in a specified
arity. A sum over all arities on an arbitrary infinite input is not licensed: the tensor coalgebra is
conilpotent, or else a separate positive coupling filtration is required.


26.4      The ternary operation recovers the obstruction, with its sign
Choose the splitting so that h(tr α) = a for the divided-inner-derivation defining system and let
p identify cohomology classes with the chosen representatives. The two pair products [α][tr ] and
[tr ][α] vanish in Hr . On their suspended inputs, (23.4) gives

                                I2′ (sα, str ) = −sa,     I2′ (str , sα) = sa.

Indeed |α| = 1, |tr | = 0, k = −shs−1 and I2′ = −kb2 (i ⊗ i). At arity three, the two binary trees
therefore give
                                bH,3 (s[α], s[tr ], s[α]) = −s [aα + αa].                   (26.5)
Terms containing the zero pair products vanish. This matches the full Massey coset, including the
fixed suspended sign. In the Weyl case the value is −s[2qξ] modulo its indeterminacy.
   The operation contains a scalar-parameter input tr 1. Consequently this minimal model is not an
R-multilinear strictly unital A∞ structure on a strictly split R-module: such multilinearity would
force the operation to vanish from its unit input. This is not a defect. The impossibility of that
strict split model is exactly what the parameter torsion and the nonformality theorem detect. To
retain a strict R-module structure, keep the free two-term blocks and their differential instead.
   Changing the finite complements changes individual transferred coefficients. The invariant
conclusion is that the Massey coset of (23.5) does not contain zero. Thus no change of higher
coordinates can remove all the higher operations while retaining the quasi-isomorphism type.


26.5      Every truncated parameter base has its own complete small
          model
For N > r, the cohomology carrier is the three-summand space in (22.5). Keep all H coefficients,
the B coefficients of orders 0, . . . , r − 1, and the L coefficients of orders N − r, . . . , N − 1. On a B
coefficient of order j ≥ r, use exactly (23.1); on every other retained or complementary vector use
zero.
   On L of order less than N − r, hd is the identity. On its top r orders, d = 0 and the vector
is retained in cohomology. On B of order at least r, dh is the identity; the bottom r orders are
retained. These checks prove the strong contraction on every block. If N ≤ r, the differential is zero
and the identity contraction is used. The tensor construction therefore gives every higher operation
and both comparison maps on every infinitesimal fiber.
   These contractions are not obtained by naively reducing the full-family homotopy modulo tN .
That homotopy divides by tr on a boundary and does not preserve the ideal tN . The additional top
L classes are precisely the reason for the different formula.

It is not asserted to be a strict map of the cohomology rings before those higher components are

  For clarity, all the higher linear coherence identities of these bar comparisons can be printed.

its homotopies.


26.6     A new necessary test for a parameter-dependent mixed com-
         parison
Suppose a geometric or physical candidate center has a specified restriction equivalent, as an asso-
ciative homotopy algebra, to one of these relative centers over the parameter line. Its cohomological
image of the divided Hamiltonian action and the parameter must have the same nonzero Massey
coset. After localization that test disappears; after passing to a numerical fiber it can disappear
again. Neither calculation certifies the family-level comparison.
   This is a constraint on a particular proposed restriction functor and a particular action. It does
not identify the native chiral center with the Rees center by definition. The construction in this
chapter supplies the full positive higher replacement for the algebraic family, while leaving the
geometric representation and its comparison map as separate obligations.

Chapter 27

Relative Weyl vacua and the algebra
of their endomorphisms

A relation with a scalar term has no augmentation obtained by killing its generators. There is
nevertheless a distinguished module, and its endomorphisms can be calculated without inventing
such an augmentation. The calculation is also a useful test of the difference between a faithful
representation and a generator of a derived category.


27.1      An operator algebra over an arbitrary ring
Let R be a commutative ring and let M = (Mij ) be an a × b matrix over R. On R[q1 , . . . , qb ] define
the commuting derivations
                                                          b
                                                          X
                                                   δi =         Mij ∂qj .
                                                          j=1

Let WM be the iterated Ore extension with generators qj , pi and relations

                                 [qj , ql ] = [pi , ph ] = 0,         [pi , qj ] = Mij .

Commutativity of the derivations makes the iterated extension independent of the order in which
the pi are adjoined, with the indicated labelled identification. Its underlying R-module has basis
q α pβ . To verify the normal form, move a pi past a coefficient by pi f = f pi + δi (f ). Two such moves
across a product agree by Leibniz, and two moves involving different pi agree because [δi , δh ] = 0.
They decrease the number of out-of-order pairs, or decrease the number of operator letters. This
proves existence and uniqueness by terminating reduction. No entry of M is divided by.
   The polynomial vacuum is

                       VM = R[q1 , . . . , qb ],          qj · f = qj f,        pi · f = δi (f ).
                            P
Equivalently VM = WM / i WM pi . The quotient is a left module; it is not an algebra quotient
when the commutators are nonzero.

Chapter 27. Relative Weyl vacua and the algebra of their endomorphisms


27.2      A finite free resolution
Let E be free with basis e1 , . . . , ea , placed in degree −1, and put

                                    K −r = WM ⊗R Λr E,
                                                                                X

                                                                                  i

Here Rw means right multiplication by w on the coefficient of a free left module, and ιi is contraction
with ei . Thus
                                                         r
                                                         X
                     dK (w ⊗ ei1 ∧ · · · ∧ eir ) =            (−1)j−1 wpij ⊗ ei1 ∧ · · · ebij · · · ∧ eir .
                                                         j=1

The right multiplications commute and contractions anticommute, so d2K = 0, including in charac-
teristic two with the exterior squares imposed separately.

Theorem 27.1. The augmentation K → VM is a finite free left WM -module resolution over every
commutative ring R.

Proof. In the normal basis q α pβ , right multiplication by pi increases only βi . Consequently the
underlying complex of R[q]-modules is the tensor product of the one-variable augmented complexes
                                                     i    p
                                        0 −→ R[pi ] −→ R[pi ] −→ R −→ 0.

Each is split exact as a complex of R-modules: remove one pi from a nonconstant monomial to
define the contraction onto constants. Tensoring these explicit contractions gives exactness of the
augmented multivariable complex. The normal form identifies its quotient with VM , and each term
is a finite free left WM -module.


27.3      The strict differential graded model
Define
                                                                                                  X
      GM = R[q1 , . . . , qb ] ⊗R Λ(θ1 , . . . , θa ),     |qj | = 0,     |θi | = 1,      dqj =       Mij θi ,   dθi = 0.
                                                                                                  i

The products of the θi are exterior products, not unrestricted symmetric products in characteristic
two.

Theorem 27.2. There is a natural quasi-isomorphism of differential graded associative algebras

                                                 GM −→ EndWM (K).

In particular GM is a strict algebra model for RHomWM (VM , VM ), with its composition product.
This model commutes with arbitrary derived extension of the scalar ring.

Proof. Send qj to Rqj and θi to −ιi . The images of the qj commute, the images of the θi satisfy
the exterior relations, and the two families commute. There is a sign worth calculating rather than
suppressing:
                              [Rpi , Rqj ](w) = w(qj pi − pi qj ) = −Mij w.

                                                               109
Chapter 27. Relative Weyl vacua and the algebra of their endomorphisms


Therefore the endomorphism differential satisfies
                                                     X
                                   [dK , Rqj ] = −        Mij ιi ,       [dK , −ιi ] = 0.
                                                      i

This is exactly the differential prescribed on GM .
   Compose the map with postcomposition by the resolution augmentation:

                                       EndWM (K) −→ HomWM (K, VM ).

This second map is a quasi-isomorphism because K is bounded and termwise projective. One can
check it directly: applying HomWM (K, −) to the acyclic augmentation cone gives a finite total
complex whose columns are finite sums of acyclic complexes.
   The composite from GM is an isomorphism of underlying complexes. In exterior degree r, a
monomial q α θi1 · · · θir evaluates on the matching wedge to q α multiplied by a sign, and evaluates on
all other basis wedges of that degree to zero. With increasing index order, the sign is (−1)r(r+1)/2 .
Thus its matrix is diagonal with entries 1 or −1. Its compatibility with the differential was already
checked through the endomorphism algebra. The two-out-of-three property proves the first map is a
quasi-isomorphism of algebras.
   Finally GM has only a + 1 cohomological degrees, and each term is a free R-module. For R → S,
derived scalar extension is therefore computed by ordinary scalar extension, giving GM ⊗S . The
resolution and its endomorphism comparison are defined by the same formulas after extension. This
proves the assertion for this vacuum; it is not a general base-change theorem for arbitrary infinite
Hochschild centres.


27.4     Rank, torsion, and the failure of a field-only classification
Over a characteristic-zero field, elementary changes of the p and q bases put a matrix of rank r
into a block with r unit entries and all other entries zero. The model is then a tensor product of r
polynomial de Rham complexes and the zero-differential algebra

                                        k[qr+1 , . . . , qb ] ⊗ Λ(θr+1 , . . . , θa ).

For a unit pair, the contraction is

                         q n+1
            h(q n θ) =         ,       h(q n ) = 0,          dh + hd = 1 − projection to constants.
                         n+1
Tensoring gives a quasi-isomorphism onto the displayed zero-differential algebra. This proof uses
the indicated characteristic-zero divisions.
  Over a general ring, the matrix must remain in the differential. For M = (z r ) over R = Zp [z],
auxiliary weight n ≥ 1 is the two-term complex
                                                          nz r
                                                 Rq n −−→ Rq n−1 θ.

Its cokernel is R/(nz r ). Both the arithmetic factor n and the collision factor z r are present. The
mixed-cone calculation in the integral chapter compares these actual complexes and their maps; a
rank calculation at a generic field point would discard their interaction.

27.5     Faithfulness does not imply generation
Take the first Weyl algebra over a characteristic-zero field, with [p, q] = 1. Its polynomial vacuum
V = k[q] is faithful. Indeed if N              j
                                 P
                                    j=0 fj (q)∂q acts as zero, commuting it with multiplication by
q lowers its operator order and identifies N fN as the leading coefficient. Induction on N , and
characteristic zero, imply every fj vanishes.
   Nevertheless V is not a generator of all derived W -modules. Let

                     N = k[p, p−1 ],     p act by multiplication,        q = −∂p .

The commutator is [p, −∂p ] = 1, so this is a nonzero W -module. Applying HomW (−, N ) to
        Rp                         p
0 → W −−→ W → V → 0 gives N −
                            → N , which is an isomorphism. Thus

                                  RHomW (V, N ) = 0,         N ̸= 0.

The relative endomorphism algebra controls the subcategory generated by the vacuum, not every
representation of the operator algebra. A Morita theorem must specify its generator and its generated
subcategory.


27.6     BRST differentials and what a concentration theorem supplies
Suppose A is an augmented graded algebra with a degree-one derivation Q satisfying Q2 = 0 and
preserving the augmentation. On suspended letters the internal bar differential is bQ (sa) = −sQa.
Its coderivation extension obeys b2Q = 0. The binary bar coderivation obeys b2m = 0, and

                                         bQ bm + bm bQ = 0

is precisely the graded Leibniz identity for Q. Hence the bar of the dg algebra (A, Q) is the actual
                  c
                                 
total complex T (sA), bQ + bm .
   The assertion is not applicable to a claimed vacuum augmentation which is not an algebra map.
For an unaugmented operator algebra one must instead choose an actual module and compatible
differentials on the algebra–module resolution. A trace is not that choice.
   If the cohomology of a dg algebra A is concentrated in degree zero, there is a natural zigzag of
dg algebra quasi-isomorphisms
                                            alg
                                    A ←− τ≤0    A −→ H 0 (A).
The middle algebra has Ai in negative degrees, ker(d : A0 → A1 ) in degree zero, and zero in
positive degrees. Its multiplication is inherited from A. Inclusion and projection induce the stated
cohomology isomorphisms. For compatible augmentations, bar invariance therefore gives a zigzag
between BA and B(H 0 A). This proves an equivalence without declaring that a spectral-sequence
edge homomorphism is a canonical inverse chain map. When the concentration or augmentation
hypotheses fail, that argument is unavailable; neither can be supplied by the name of a reduction
procedure.

28.1     A free boundary with every mode retained
Fix a finite-dimensional vector space with basis indexed by a = 1, . . . , m, over a characteristic-zero
field k. The free commutative differential vertex superalgebra on odd generators η a has states

              V = Λk (ηra : a = 1, . . . , m, r ≥ 0),       |ηra | = 1,       T ηra = (r + 1)ηr+1
                                                                                              a
                                                                                                  .

Its generating fields have zero pairwise singular products. On its vacuum module they are Y (η a , z) =
        a r
   r≥0 ηr z . The grading |η| = 1 is a cohomological grading; the differential is zero. Independently,
P

give ηra energy r. This is an auxiliary nonnegative grading with T of energy one. It need not be
supplied by a conformal vector.
   The full mode algebra is the supercommutative exterior algebra

              U = Λk (ψna : a = 1, . . . , m, n ∈ Z),       |ψna | = 1,         wt(ψna ) = −n − 1.

Only finite exterior words occur in U . Put

                          U+ = Λ(ψna : n ≥ 0),                  a
                                                        U− = Λ(ψ−r−1 : r ≥ 0).

The vacuum is M = U ⊗U+ k = U− , with ψ−r−1  a     identified with ηra . Every positive mode kills its
vacuum vector and indeed kills all of M , since all modes supercommute.
   These are the mode conventions used throughout this chapter. The next chapter compares with
smooth vertex modules. For now the derived category is the category of dg left U -modules, with
homogeneous cochain maps; an energy-graded version will be specified separately. The resulting
distinction between a product and a sum is part of the answer.


28.2     The full mode resolution
Index pairs (a, n) with n ≥ 0 by i. Introduce a degree-zero divided-power variable ti , of energy
−n − 1. Write t[α] for the divided-power monomial for a finite-support multi-index α. Define
                                                        X
                      P = U ⊗k Γk (ti ),        dP =        ψi ∂i ,       ∂i t[α] = t[α−ei ] .
                                                        i

                                                    112
Chapter 28. An infinite boundary and its actual derived endomorphisms


A negative exponent means zero. A vector of P is a finite sum of finite exterior and divided-power
monomials. Consequently dP is locally a finite sum. The derivatives ∂i commute, while the ψi
anticommute, so d2P = 0. The augmentation ϵ : P → M sets every ti and every positive ψi to zero.

Lemma 28.1 (A contraction before taking Hom). The augmentation P → M is a quasi-isomorphism.
It is a semifree dg U -module resolution, with filtration by total divided-power degree.

Proof. For one pair, the complex Λ(ψ) ⊗ Γ(t) has differential

                                   d(t[r] ) = ψt[r−1] ,     d(ψt[r] ) = 0.

The homotopy h(ψt[r] ) = t[r+1] , h(t[r] ) = 0 gives dh + hd = 1 − iϵ. For finitely many pairs use the
tensor-product homotopy

                         h1 ⊗ 1 + i1 ϵ1 ⊗ h2 ⊗ 1 + i1 ϵ1 ⊗ i2 ϵ2 ⊗ h3 ⊗ 1 + · · · ,

with the cochain tensor signs. Its cross terms telescope, leaving identity minus projection to the
constant in all pairs. Every input in the infinite tensor product involves only finitely many pairs,
and all additional homotopy summands vanish on it. Thus the same formula defines an actual
contraction for the infinite direct union. Tensor with U− , using the tensor signs, to obtain the
asserted quasi-isomorphism.
   Let Fr P be spanned over U by t[α] with |α| ≤ r. The differential of a newly added generator lies in
Fr−1 P . The associated successive graded modules are direct sums of free U -modules on the indicated
generators. This is precisely a semifree filtration. Notice that the underlying cochain complex need
not be bounded above: semifreeness, rather than an inapplicable boundedness assertion, is the
property used below.

Lemma 28.2 (The required K-projectivity). For an acyclic dg U -module N , the complex
HomU (P, N ) is acyclic. This remains true for maps of any fixed energy when N is energy graded.

Proof. Let f : P → N be closed of degree q. We construct g of degree q −1 with dN g −(−1)q−1 gdP =
f . Suppose it is already defined on Fr−1 P . For a new basis generator v = t[α] of filtration degree r,
the equation requires
                                   dN g(v) = f (v) + (−1)q−1 g(dP v).
The expression on the right is closed: applying dN gives (−1)q f (dP v) + (−1)q−1 f (dP v) = 0, by
closedness of f , the previously solved equation, and d2P = 0. Choose a primitive in N , which exists
by acyclicity, and extend to the free U -summand by graded U -linearity. Start with r = 0 and
proceed through all nonnegative integers.
   Each generator is assigned once; no infinite sum of assigned values is formed. This proves the
assertion without commuting Hom with a filtered colimit. If f has fixed energy, the right side is
homogeneous of the required energy. Acyclicity holds in that energy component, so the primitive
may be chosen homogeneous. The same induction proves the graded version.


28.3     Computing the whole Hom complex
A degree-q U -linear map from P to M is determined by arbitrary values

                                            aα = f (t[α] ) ∈ M q .

                                                     113
Chapter 28. An infinite boundary and its actual derived endomorphisms


For each fixed α, this is an element of the algebraic exterior algebra M , hence a finite linear
combination of exterior words. There is no requirement that only finitely many α have nonzero
values. Since positive modes act by zero on M , the differential on this Hom complex is zero.
   Define the graded algebra
                                       Zq =
                                                       Y
                                                                  M q xα .                          (28.1)
                                               α∈N({1,...,m}×N)

Here xi has cohomological degree zero. This notation means a product over finite-support multi-
indices, with an algebraic exterior coefficient at each multi-index. It is not permission to replace M q
itself by a product over exterior words. Multiplication is the finite-divisor convolution
                                                        X
                                           (ab)γ =             aα bβ .
                                                       α+β=γ

Every finite-support γ has finitely many such decompositions. Each coefficient of the product is
again a finite exterior polynomial. The algebra is therefore well defined and graded commutative.


28.4      An operator-valued quasi-isomorphism
Let D = EndU (P ), with its full Hom differential and composition product. A series in (25.1) acts
by an actual endomorphism:                     !
                                           X                 X
                                       ρ       aα xα    =         Laα ∂ α .                         (28.2)
                                           α                 α
This is a locally finite operator series, not an equality between D and a ring of finite-order differential
operators. Applied to ut[β] , only α ≤ β contribute. There are finitely many of them. Each output
belongs to P .

Theorem 28.3 (The full algebraic mode answer). The map (25.2) is a unital dg algebra quasi-
isomorphism
                                  (Z , 0) −→ EndU (P ).
Consequently it computes the full derived endomorphism algebra of the vacuum in dg U -modules,
including its product, rather than only its graded vector space.

Proof. An exterior coefficient aα of degree q supercommutes with every u ∈ U , so the operator has
the graded U -linearity required of a degree-q map. The operators ∂i commute with one another
and with the exterior coefficients. The latter are closed; [dP , ∂i ] = 0. Thus ρ lands in closed
endomorphisms. Composition gives exactly the convolution product above, because the derivatives
do not differentiate the coefficients aα . Every composition is locally finite on every input, so this
calculation does not reorder an undefined infinite sum.
   Postcomposition with ϵ : P → M gives a quasi-isomorphism

                                    ϵ∗ : EndU (P ) −→ HomU (P, M ),

by Lemma 25.2 applied to the acyclic cone of the augmentation. For t[β] , the composite ϵ∗ ρ returns
exactly aβ : all terms with a remaining divided power are killed by ϵ. Hence this composite is the
coefficientwise isomorphism of complexes from (25.1) to the Hom complex just computed. The
two-out-of-three property proves that ρ is a quasi-isomorphism. Its unit is the constant operator
1P .

                                                       114
Chapter 28. An infinite boundary and its actual derived endomorphisms


   The proof is important for two separate reasons. It includes every mode without asserting that a
limit of finite Ext groups is already a derived endomorphism algebra. It also constructs a subalgebra
mapping quasi-isomorphically to the entire endomorphism complex, rather than claiming that every
endomorphism is a finite differential operator.


28.5     The energy-graded answer is polynomial
Let Dgr be the direct sum, over energy shifts, of homogeneous endomorphism complexes of P . The
input algebra and its differential preserve the energy grading. Give xa,n energy n + 1 and keep ηra of
energy r.
Theorem 28.4 (The positive-energy mode answer). There is a translation-equivariant dg algebra
quasi-isomorphism

             Z = k[xa,n : a = 1, . . . , m, n ≥ 0] ⊗ Λ(ηra : a = 1, . . . , m, r ≥ 0) −→ Dgr .

Every fixed energy component of Z is finite dimensional. Its elements are polynomials in the x
variables and finite exterior polynomials in the η variables.

Proof. A map of energy e sends t[α] , whose energy is −w(α), to Me−w(α) . Since M has no negative
energies, its coefficient is zero when w(α) > e. All t-dual weights are positive, with only finitely
many generators of any bounded weight, so there are only finitely many multi-indices with w(α) ≤ e.
In M , only finitely many exterior generators have weight at most e. The weight-zero exterior
generators are the finitely many η0a , each usable at most once. Thus M has finite-dimensional energy
components. The Hom complex of fixed energy is exactly the same component of the displayed
polynomial algebra. Repeat the proof of Theorem 25.3 with the homogeneous K-projectivity lemma.
  Define translation on the resolution by

                          T ψna = −nψn−1
                                     a
                                         ,        T ta,n = −nta,n−1    (n ≥ 0),

with the n = 0 term zero. Extend as a degree-zero derivation. Since dP ta,n = ψna , one has
T dP = dP T on generators and hence everywhere. Direct differentiation gives

                                       [T, ∂a,n ] = (n + 1)∂a,n+1 .

For negative modes it gives T ηra = (r + 1)ηr+1
                                            a . These are precisely the translation rules on Z,

proving equivariance of the operator map.

   This polynomial answer depends on the stated positive auxiliary grading. It is not an assertion
that every physical spin grading has the same finiteness property. A spin-zero even dual generator
immediately changes the completion problem.


28.6     A zero-weight mode and a genuine completion
For one odd degree-one generator ψ of weight zero, let U = Λ(ψ) and M = k. The same resolution
is U ⊗ Γ(t), |t| = 0, dt = ψ. All its free generators have the same cohomological degree and energy.
Hence                                 Y
                      HomU (P, k) =       k,     RHomU (k, k) ≃ k[[x]], |x| = 0.

   With infinitely many weight-zero modes, the corresponding completion is a product over all finite-

28.7     Finite differential operators are not the full endomorphism
         algebra
Already Endk (k[t]) contains evaluation at zero. It is not a finite-order polynomial differential
operator. Indeed an operator L = rj=0 aj (t)∂tj has an (r + 1)-fold iterated commutator with
                                          P

multiplication by t equal to zero. For evaluation E(f ) = f (0), one has [E, t] = −tE, and induction
gives
                                 [· · · [[E, t], t], . . . , t] = (−1)s ts E ̸= 0
                                 |        {z           }
                                     s commutators

for every s. Evaluation does have the locally finite expression
                                                 X (−t)j j
                                           E=                   ∂t
                                                 j≥0
                                                           j!

on polynomials, by the binomial identity. The distinction is between a locally finite infinite operator
and a finite-order one.
   Thus a useful differential-operator subcomplex may compute a derived endomorphism, but this
requires a quasi-isomorphism proof. Equation (25.2) and the augmentation comparison supply
exactly such a proof for the free boundary. They are the replacement for an unsupported identification
of the entire endomorphism algebra with a chosen differential-operator presentation.




                                                     116
Chapter 29

A smooth resolution and an
infinite-mode comparison

29.1      Why the free resolution is not already a vertex module
The resolution P of the preceding chapter is a useful free dg U -module, but its generator 1 is
not smooth: ψna 1 ̸= 0 for every n ≥ 0. Thus the formal field n ψna z −n−1 does not satisfy lower
                                                             P

truncation on P . Calling P a vertex-module resolution would silently change the category.
   There is a second resolution on which the fields do satisfy lower truncation. Its comparison with
P will be proved, not inferred from a limiting argument. This is also the resolution on which the
secondary operation has an elementary operator interpretation.


29.2      Smooth mode modules and free vertex modules
A mode module is smooth if, for every vector v, there is an N for which ψna v = 0 for all a and all
n ≥ N . A differential graded module is smooth when this holds on its underlying graded vectors.
We use homogeneous energy and cohomological maps when those gradings are present.

Proposition 29.1. Smooth modules of the mode algebra U are equivalent to weak modules of the
free vertex superalgebra V in Chapter 25. A module differential compatible with the mode action is
compatible with the vertex-module action.

Proof. On a smooth mode module define

                                                         ψna z −n−1 .
                                                   X
                                        Ψa (z) =
                                                   n∈Z

Smoothness is exactly the lower-truncation property for this field on each vector. The defining
exterior relations give Ψa (z)Ψb (w) + Ψb (w)Ψa (z) = 0 as formal distributions. In particular the fields
are mutually local, with no contraction term between two generating fields.
   Define the field of ηra to be ∂zr Ψa (z)/r!, and the field of an exterior monomial to be the normally
ordered product of the indicated derivative fields. These fields are well defined: when a positive
mode is moved to the right, it anticommutes with every other mode and gives no contraction term;
sufficiently large positive modes then kill the chosen vector. In a fixed coefficient only finitely many

                                                   117
Chapter 29. A smooth resolution and an infinite-mode comparison


remaining negative-mode indices can occur. The free exterior relations hold because identical odd
factors square to zero and all odd factors anticommute.
   The module vacuum is the identity field. Translation is the derivative by the displayed definition.
Associativity can be checked on the generating fields and extended to their normally ordered
derivatives: expansion of Ψa (w + z) in z has coefficients ∂w  r Ψa (w)/r!, and moving generating

factors into normal order produces no extra scalar or singular terms. Thus the iterate and product
expansions agree after the usual common lower-truncation factor. Iterating this argument proves
the module Jacobi identity on every exterior monomial. Equivalently, this is the mutually-local-field
reconstruction proof in the special case in which every generator contraction is zero.
   Conversely, the modes of a V -module obey the exterior relations by the mode commutator formula
and the zero generating λ-brackets. They are smooth by lower truncation. The reconstruction just
given recovers all fields, so the two functors are inverse and preserve morphisms. A differential
supercommuting with every ψna supercommutes with all derivative and normally ordered fields,
proving the last assertion.


29.3     The coinduced Koszul resolution
Introduce odd variables ζa,n of cohomological degree −1 and energy n + 1, and even variables xa,n
of degree zero and the same energy. Define
                                                                               X
            I = Λ(ηra : r ≥ 0) ⊗ Λ(ζa,n : n ≥ 0) ⊗ k[xa,n : n ≥ 0],     dI =         xa,n ∂ζa,n .
                                                                               a,n

All polynomials and exterior words are algebraic finite expressions. Let negative modes act by
multiplication by ηra , and positive modes by ∂ζa,n . These odd operators anticommute, so they give a
U -action. The differential supercommutes with them. Every vector involves only finitely many ζ’s,
so it is smooth.
  The map j : M → I includes the polynomials in the η variables. Let Nx,ζ count polynomial
degree in x plus exterior degree in ζ, and put
                    P
                     a,n ζa,n ∂xa,n
             hI =                     on the positive-count summands,      hI = 0 on M.
                        Nx,ζ
This is a degree-−1 linear map, not a U -module map.
Lemma 29.2. The inclusion j is a quasi-isomorphism of smooth dg vertex modules. The underlying
complexes satisfy
                                   dI hI + hI dI = 1 − jp,
where p sets x and ζ to zero.

Proof. For one pair (x, ζ), the anticommutator of x∂ζ and ζ∂x is x∂x + ζ∂ζ . For different pairs, the
cross terms cancel because the exterior multiplication and differentiation operators anticommute
and the even polynomial operators commute. Summing proves
                                                  X
                                          [dI ,       ζ∂x ]+ = Nx,ζ .
Both operators preserve the count. Division by the positive integer count gives the contraction;
characteristic zero is used here. Each vector has finite count and involves finitely many variables, so
the formula is locally finite. The mode action and smoothness were already checked; Proposition 26.1
gives the vertex-module statement.

                                                       118
Chapter 29. A smooth resolution and an infinite-mode comparison


   The lack of U -linearity of hI is not a defect to conceal. It is precisely why the new closed classes
will be nonzero in derived module endomorphisms even though they act by null-homotopic maps of
underlying vector-space complexes.


29.4      Coinduction and the category in which Ext is computed
We give the comparison argument with its finiteness condition. First regard the mode algebra
as an internally graded superalgebra, and compute extension degree separately. Smooth, energy-
graded modules form an abelian category. Kernels and cokernels are computed in graded vector
spaces and are smooth. It has enough injectives: the smooth part of the graded coinduced module
Homk (U, W ) is right adjoint to the exact forgetful functor. To verify this assertion, a graded linear
map f : N → W has adjoint n 7→ (u 7→ f (un)), with the usual graded signs. If n is killed by all
sufficiently large positive modes, so is this adjoint vector, since those modes supercommute with all
of U . The adjunction therefore restricts to smooth modules. Extension of a graded vector-space
map across an injection proves injectivity of the coinduced object, and the action map embeds every
smooth module into one with W its underlying vector space.
   There is an exact smooth induction functor

                                     F (N ) = U ⊗U+ N = U− ⊗ N.

It preserves smoothness because positive modes supercommute with every negative exterior word.
It is left adjoint to restriction, which is also exact. Consequently restriction preserves injectives.
Applying these adjunctions to injective resolutions gives

                                 ExtpU,sm (M, N ) ∼    p
                                                  = ExtU+ ,sm (k, N |U+ )                          (29.1)

with all internal and energy gradings retained. This is a derived adjunction, not a declaration that
arbitrary inverse limits commute with Ext.
   For U+ alone, a graded coinduced module of an energy-bounded-below space W is already smooth.
Indeed a homogeneous functional of energy e on an exterior input of energy −s takes values in We−s .
It vanishes when s exceeds e minus the lower bound for W . Thus only finitely many positive-mode
indices occur in that functional. The coinduced module is identified, by exterior duality, with
the algebraic exterior algebra in ζ tensored with W , read degree by degree in energy. The usual
dual-basis signs give the action by exterior contractions. This also proves that these terms are
injective in the smooth U+ -module category, by the preceding adjunction argument.


29.5      The two gradings of the resolution must not be collapsed
          prematurely
Let p be polynomial degree in x. Give I an internal grading by

                              |η|int = 1,    |ζ|int = −1,      |x|int = −1.

Its total degree is internal degree plus p, so that |x|tot = 0, as before. The differential increases p by
one and preserves internal degree. In resolution degree p, untotalize the mode action by multiplying



                                                   119
Chapter 29. A smooth resolution and an infinite-mode comparison


the action of a homogeneous u ∈ U by (−1)p|u| . Then the differential is an internally degree-zero
module map. This sign follows from

                    dI (uv) = (−1)|u| u dI v :   dI ((−1)p|u| uv) = (−1)(p+1)|u| u dI v.

Thus the total dg module above is the totalization of an ordinary bounded-below resolution in the
internally graded module category.
   The same rephasing applies to morphisms and their products. If a homogeneous morphism has
resolution degree r and internal degree q, its totalized component on resolution degree p is (−1)pq
times its untotalized component. Accordingly the totalized composition corresponds to

                                          f ⋆ g = (−1)qf rg f ◦ g

on the bigraded endomorphism complex. This convention is forced by applying the two component
signs in succession. In particular multiplication by x on the total complex is represented before
totalization by (−1)p Lx of bidegree (1, −1), and multiplication by η by (−1)p Lη of bidegree (0, 1).
These maps satisfy the ordinary resolution-degree chain identities. The signs must not be dropped
when the Yoneda algebra is regraded by total degree.
   The degree-p term, restricted to U+ , is a coinduced module with coefficient space M ⊗ Symp ⟨xa,n ⟩,
with the indicated internal shifts and harmless parity rephasing of the contraction action. This
coefficient space is energy bounded below. Hence every term is U+ -injective. By (26.1), every term
is acyclic for the functor HomU,sm (M, −). Lemma 26.2 proves that this is a resolution of M . It
therefore computes the derived Hom of this vacuum, although it is not asserted to be an injective
resolution for every other source module.
  A U -linear map from M into a term of I is determined by the image of its vacuum. Positive
modes must annihilate that image, so it is independent of ζ. Thus the resulting Hom complex is

                                        M ⊗ k[xa,n ],       d = 0.

The class xa,n has extension degree one, internal degree −1, and total degree zero. The class ηra has
extension degree zero and total degree one. Confusing extension degree with total cohomological
degree would change both the answer and its parity.

Theorem 29.3 (The smooth, graded vacuum comparison). In the smooth energy-graded category,
with the total grading just specified, the vacuum derived endomorphism cohomology is

                                          Z = k[xa,n ] ⊗ Λ(ηra ).

Its Yoneda product is the displayed graded-commutative product. The natural comparison to the
energy-graded algebraic mode calculation of Theorem 25.4 is an isomorphism on all homogeneous
extension groups and their products. The polynomial algebra Z acts by closed graded module
endomorphisms on the smooth dg resolution I. This last action is a strict chain-level construction;
the theorem about smooth Ext uses extension degree and internal degree separately before their
energywise finite totalization.

Proof. The acyclic resolution and Hom calculation prove the groups. At a fixed energy e, the
polynomial degree p of x is at most e, and only finitely many x and ζ indices and exterior η
monomials can occur. Thus the relevant totalization has finite diagonals in that energy. There is no
infinite product along an unbounded p diagonal in this comparison.

                                                   120
Chapter 29. A smooth resolution and an infinite-mode comparison


   One can compare the two calculations before taking cohomology. For the positive-mode algebra,
use the free divided-power resolution P+ = U+ ⊗ Γ(t) and the coinduced resolution I restricted to
U+ . The two maps into the double Hom complex

                        HomU+ (P+ , M ) −→ HomU+ (P+ , I) ←− HomU+ (k, I)

are induced by the two augmentations. In fixed energy, a generator t[α] can contribute only if
w(α) ≤ e, and x-degree is also bounded by e. Therefore the double-complex comparison has finite
filtrations. The first map is a quasi-isomorphism by the free resolution; the second is one by the
coinduced resolution. This gives the claimed natural Ext comparison, with induction (26.1) restoring
the negative-mode coefficient algebra.
   On the total complex I, multiplication by xa,n and by ηra defines closed, graded U -linear
endomorphisms. Before totalization use the component signs just specified, and on the totalized
Yoneda algebra use f ⋆ g = (−1)qf rg f ◦ g. The resulting operators have exactly the stated polynomial
and exterior relations. Postcomposition with j : M → I identifies their classes with the basis
elements in the computed Hom complex. Hence their compositions compute the Yoneda product
and give exactly the displayed algebra.
   The closed multiplication operators themselves give the stated strict dg action on I. We do not
identify the raw complex EndU,sm (I) with a derived endomorphism complex: I was proved acyclic
for the particular vacuum Hom functor, not K-injective against every unbounded smooth complex.
Likewise, the computation in the abelian internally graded category is not a license to identify an
arbitrary unbounded totalized smooth dg enhancement with the algebraic mode category. The
fixed-energy finite double-complex argument proves exactly the displayed smooth Ext comparison.
The strict dg algebra quasi-isomorphism for the energy-graded algebraic mode category is the
separate theorem of the preceding chapter.


29.6     The operator action is visible on the smooth resolution
The generating boundary field on I is

                                                                     ∂ζa,n z −n−1 .
                                             X                 X
                                  Ψa (z) =         ηra z r +                                           (29.2)
                                             r≥0               n≥0

For every vector the second sum is finite. Let Lζa,n denote multiplication by ζa,n . It has degree −1
and is not U -linear. Its exact identities are

                      [dI , Lζa,n ]+ = Lxa,n ,       [ψsb , Lζa,n ]+ = δab δsn        (s ≥ 0).         (29.3)

The first identity says that the action of xa,n is null-homotopic after forgetting the boundary action.
The second says why that null-homotopy is not a module homotopy. Thus xa,n is a nonzero derived
boundary endomorphism. It is not an ordinary endomorphism of the vacuum that happened to be
given an extra name.
   Translation on I is the degree-zero derivation

               T ηra = (r + 1)ηr+1
                               a
                                   ,   T ζa,n = (n + 1)ζa,n+1 ,             T xa,n = (n + 1)xa,n+1 .

It commutes with dI . For the positive modes, [T, ∂ζa,n ] = −n∂ζa,n−1 , while the negative modes have
the same coefficient-mode rule. Hence [T, Ψa (z)] = ∂z Ψa (z), including the n = 0 endpoint. The
resolution and its action therefore retain translation, unlike a finite last-mode cutoff.

30.1      An integral complex whose fibres conceal its multiplication
Over Zp let
                          D = Zp [q] ⊗ Λ(θ),        |q| = 0,     |θ| = 1,    dq = θ.
The variable is polynomial, not implicitly completed. In auxiliary weight n ≥ 1 the complex is
multiplication by n from Zp q n to Zp q n−1 θ. Therefore
                                                             M
                            H 0 (D) = Zp ,       H 1 (D) =         Zp /(n)[q n−1 θ].
                                                             n≥1

Put α = q p−1 θ. Since d(q p ) = pα, the triple Massey product ⟨[α], [p], [α]⟩ is defined. Its representative
from the two primitives q p is
                                                 2q 2p−1 θ.
The indeterminacy is Zp [α], in weight p. The displayed class is in weight 2p, whose cohomology is
Zp /(2p). It is nonzero: for odd p, 2 is a unit modulo p, and for p = 2, 2 ̸= 0 modulo 4. Thus the
coset does not contain zero.
   Over Qp , the homotopy h(q n−1 θ) = q n /n contracts all positive weights. Over Fp , the inclusion

                                       Fp [q p ] ⊗ Λ(q p−1 θ) −→ D ⊗ Fp

is a multiplicative cohomology isomorphism, by inspection of monomial derivatives. Both fibres
are formal. The family is not: in its zero-differential cohomology algebra the corresponding two
products are zero and zero primitives give a Massey value zero, contradicting invariance of the
nonzero coset under quasi-isomorphisms. This is a multiplication-level extension obstruction over
the base.


30.2      Finite differences retain a different product
Let T (f )(q) = f (q + 1) and define

                    DT = R[q] ⊕ R[q]ϵ,       ϵf = T (f )ϵ,     ϵ2 = 0,    df = (T f − f )ϵ.

                                                     122
Chapter 30. Integral extensions and divided operators


The identity T (f g) − f g = (T f − f )T g + f (T g − g) proves the dg Leibniz rule. The falling factorials
(q)n = q(q − 1) · · · (q − n + 1) satisfy (T − 1)(q)n = n(q)n−1 . Sending q n to (q)n identifies the
underlying complexes with the preceding differential complex, but is not multiplicative: (q)2 = q 2 − q.
   Over Zp use α′ = (q)p−1 ϵ and primitive (q)p . The analogous Massey representative is
                                                                     
                                      (q)p (q)p−1 + (q)p−1 (q + 1)p ϵ.

Its leading falling-factorial coefficient is 2 in coordinate 2p − 1. Exact elements in that coordinate
have coefficient divisible by 2p, and the indeterminacy has only coordinate p − 1. Thus the class is
again nonzero. An additive identification of two complexes did not identify their multiplication or
tensor law.


30.3      The divided-power replacement
Let ΓR (Q) have basis γn , n ≥ 0, and product
                                                  !
                                             a+b
                                   γa γb =       γa+b ,          γ0 = 1.
                                              a

The operator ∂γn = γn−1 , with γ−1 = 0, is a derivation. Pascal’s identity proves its Leibniz rule
over every ring. The dg algebra

                                 G = ΓR (Q) ⊗ Λ(θ),          dγn = γn−1 θ

has the denominator-free contraction h(γn θ) = γn+1 . Thus G ≃ R as a dg algebra via its augmenta-
tion.
   The factorial map q n 7→ n!γn is multiplicative and commutes with differentials because n(n−1)! =
n!. Over Zp it is not a quasi-isomorphism: the source has the torsion just computed and the target
does not. It changes the integral algebra rather than repairing the same algebra by an equivalence.
   The operator algebra changes as well. Form the Ore extension

                                WΓ = ΓR (Q)[P ; ∂],         [P, γn ] = γn−1 .

                      fj P j are unique; multiplication follows by repeatedly commuting P past a
                    P

coefficient. In characteristic p the binomial commutator formula gives

                                              [P p , γp ] = 1.

In the ordinary Weyl algebra, the corresponding pth powers of its two ordinary generators are
central. The two operator algebras must not be identified in characteristic p.


30.4      The full locally nilpotent module category
Theorem 30.1 (Divided Taylor reconstruction). For every commutative ring R, the category of
left WΓ -modules on which P acts locally nilpotently is equivalent to R-modules. The functors are

                           V 7−→ ΓR (Q) ⊗R V,         N 7−→ ker(P : N → N ).

Both are exact.

                                                   123
Chapter 30. Integral extensions and divided operators


Proof. On a locally nilpotent module the sum E = j≥0 (−1)j γj P j is finite on every vector. The
                                                             P

relation for P γj makes the two sums in P E cancel, so its image is horizontal, and it is the identity
on horizontal vectors. For such a vector v, P j γn v = γn−j v, and the divided-power product gives
                                                                 !
                                                 n
                                                 X               n 
                                    Eγn v =         (−1)j           γn v.
                                                 j=0
                                                                 j

It is v for n = 0 and zero otherwise. Expanding also gives
                                            X
                                                  γn EP n = 1,
                                            n≥0

since the coefficient of γr P r is the same alternating binomial sum. Hence the maps
                                                                 X
                              γn ⊗ v 7−→ γn v,         w 7−→          γn ⊗ EP n w
                                                                  n

are inverse. The first respects all divided multiplications and the P action, hence is a module
isomorphism. The constructions are natural. The first functor is exact because ΓR (Q) is free. For
a surjection of locally nilpotent modules, apply E to any lift of a horizontal vector to obtain a
horizontal lift; thus the kernel functor is exact too.

   At a nonunit level [P, γn ] = λγn−1 the conclusion fails. The nonzero module R/(λ) with P = 0
and positive divided creators zero is locally nilpotent but is not a free divided-power vacuum on its
horizontal part. The unit commutator is a hypothesis, not a normalization available over every base.


30.5      Supported differential operators and Frobenius digits
On R[x] the Hasse derivative satisfies ∂ [n] xm = m   m−n . The map
                                                         
                                                  n x

                                    P 7−→ x,           γn 7−→ (−1)n ∂ [n]

identifies WΓ with the ring of finite-order relative differential operators on R[x]. The Hasse product
and commutator identities verify the relations. Every operator of order at most m is uniquely a sum
Pm           [j]                                                 m
  j=0 aj (x)∂ : subtract its prescribed values on 1, x, . . . , x successively and use that commutation
with x lowers order. The triangular matrix of Hasse values has diagonal one, so no division is used.
   The vacuum is the supported module R[x, x−1 ]/R[x], under γn 7→ x−n−1 . Multiplication by
x lowers the index, while the negative Hasse derivatives give exactly the divided-power binomial
coefficients. This is not the ordinary polynomial vacuum with the same generator names.
   Over Fp , put σ(P ) = P p , σ(γj ) = γpj , X = γ1 , and D0 = P − γp−1 P p . The binomial identities
modulo p show that σ is an injective algebra map. On the vacuum basis γpj+i , 0 ≤ i < p, X raises
the first digit with coefficient i + 1, and D0 lowers it without borrowing from the next digit. Hence
X p = D0p = 0 and [D0 , X] = 1. They generate the full first-digit matrix algebra: XD0 has the
distinct eigenvalues 0, . . . , p − 1, so interpolation gives its diagonal projectors, and powers of X, D0
give all matrix units.
   These operators commute with σ(WΓ ), which acts on the remaining digits. They generate the
original algebra because γpj+i = γpj X i /i! and P = D0 + γp−1 P p . Faithfulness can be checked
without assumption: a finite normal sum acting on all large γn has coefficients which are finite linear

                                                    124
Chapter 30. Integral extensions and divided operators


combinations of n+d
                      
                    a . Consecutive values give a matrix of determinant one by finite differences,
forcing every coefficient to vanish only for the zero operator. Thus
                                         WΓ ∼
                                            = Matp (Fp ) ⊗ σ(WΓ ).
Keeping only the first matrix factor would discard all the remaining operator digits.


30.6     A scalar annihilator which detects an extension
Let R = Zp [z], a = b + r with b, r ≥ 1, n ≥ 1, and c = r(n − 1). Consider
                                              (z rn ,−nz a )t      (nz b ,z c )
                                  Cn :     R −−−−−−−→ R2 −−−−−→ R
in degrees −1, 0, 1. The composite is zero because a + c = b + rn. Put g = min(b, c). A direct kernel
computation gives
                          H −1 = 0,      H 0 = R/(z r+g ),          H 1 = R/(nz b , z c ).
For c ≥ b, the middle kernel is generated by (z c−b , −n), since n and z c−b are relatively prime in the
UFD R. The first image is z a times that generator. For c < b, the kernel is generated by (1, −nz b−c )
and the image is z rn times it. These are the stated exponents.
   The ideal of scalars null-homotopic on this entire complex is exactly
                                                 (z rn , nz a ).
For sufficiency use the homotopies with components H10 (u, w) = u, H11 (1) = (0, z r ), and H20 (u, w) =
−w, H21 (1) = (z r , 0). Matrix multiplication gives dH1 + H1 d = z rn id and dH2 + H2 d = nz a id.
Conversely the degree-−1 component of any scalar homotopy gives s = H 0 (z rn , −nz a ), so s lies in
this ideal.
   For p = 2, a = 3, b = 1, r = 2, n = 2, multiplication by z 3 kills both cohomology modules but is
not null-homotopic: z 3 ∈
                        / (z 4 , 2z 3 ). Thus an annihilator of cohomology is not an annihilator of the
derived object. When c > b, the exact sequence in H 1 has extension class 1 in R/(n, z min(b,c−b) ),
obtained by lifting 1 and multiplying by z b . The class is nonzero exactly when p divides n.


30.7     Tensor laws and integral intervals
The formal group laws x + y and x + y + hxy arise from additive and multiplicative composition of
operator data. Associativity of the second follows by expanding either threefold composition to
x + y + z + h(xy + xz + yz) + h2 xyz. Over Fp their p-series at h = 0, 1 are respectively zero and xp .
No invertible formal coordinate change conjugates them. A characteristic-zero logarithm is not an
integral tensor equivalence.
   Similarly ∂xp = 0 on Fp [x] does not make the truncated exponential multiplicative. It sends x to
x − t but sends xp to xp , not (x − t)p . Full translation retains all Hasse derivatives.
    An associative interval over any ring has basis e0 , e1 in degree zero and h in degree one, with
e2i = ei , e0 e1 = e1 e0 = 0, e0 h = h = he1 , e1 h = he0 = h2 = 0, and de0 = −h, de1 = h. The unit is
e0 + e1 . Direct checks give the Leibniz identity. The homotopy H(h) = e1 , H(ei ) = 0 contracts the
kernel of evaluation at endpoint zero. It is an associative, not strictly commutative, interval. The
integral polynomial de Rham interval has the torsion just calculated and cannot replace it by an
undeclared equivalence.

Chapter 31

A polarized boundary calculation

31.1      The two halves of a factorization
Let R = k[x, y] and let E = R1|1 be a free supermodule. Put
                                                 !
                                              0 x
                                   D=             ,        D2 = xy id .
                                              y 0

The endomorphism algebra is Z/2-graded and has differential dF = DF − (−1)|F | F D. Since D2 is
scalar, d2 F = [D2 , F ] = 0. This is a genuine dg endomorphism algebra of a matrix factorization,
although the factorization differential itself squares to the potential rather than zero.
   Let a = E12 and b = E21 . Then

                        a2 = b2 = 0,      ab + ba = 1,        da = y,     db = x.

These equations use no division by two. The two even idempotents are ab and ba. The odd
anticommutator is the nonzero unit, not a relation that can be replaced by an exterior product.
   For an even diagonal matrix F = diag(u, v),
                                                                   !
                                                  0     x(v − u)
                                       dF =                      .
                                               y(u − v)     0

For an odd matrix G = ( 0s 0r ),
                                          dG = (xs + yr) id .
These formulas give all differential components and their signs.

Theorem 31.1 (The node endomorphism algebra). The even cohomology is k id and the odd
cohomology is zero. The algebra is formal over k, but is not formal over R to that cohomology
algebra with the scalar action x = y = 0.

Proof. An even cycle has u = v because R is a domain. Even boundaries are scalar matrices in the
ideal (x, y), so even cohomology is k. An odd cycle satisfies xs + yr = 0, hence (r, s) = (xh, −yh)
by relative primality. These are exactly the even differentials. Thus odd cohomology is zero. The
scalar-unit map k → EndR (E) is a dg algebra quasi-isomorphism, proving formality over k.


                                                     126
Chapter 31. A polarized boundary calculation


   For the relative assertion, specialize derivedly along R → k. The original complex is finite free
in the two-periodic sense; tensor with the finite Koszul resolution of k and then compare with its
ordinary special fibre. Since x, y are termwise a regular sequence on the free modules, this gives the
supermatrix algebra Endk (k1|1 ) with zero differential. Its even part is k × k.
   For the zero-differential cohomology algebra k with its R action, the derived fibre is k ⊗LR k,
represented by the exterior algebra on two odd Koszul generators. Its even part has a nonzero
square-zero element and no two-branch idempotent decomposition. The two fibre algebras cannot
be isomorphic. An R-linear algebra quasi-isomorphism or zigzag would preserve them, so no relative
formality exists.

   The scalar quasi-isomorphism in the first part is not R-linear: multiplication by x, y is null-
homotopic in the target, not strictly zero there. This is precisely the datum that the relative
comparison must retain. The calculation therefore does not conflict with formality over a field; it
identifies which scalar structure was forgotten.


31.2      All hyperbolic ranks
Let R = k[x1 , . . . , xr , y1 , . . . , yr ] and E = ΛR (Rr ). Let ai be exterior multiplication by the ith basis
vector and bi its contraction. Then

                                 {ai , aj } = {bi , bj } = 0,    {ai , bj } = δij ,

with a2i = b2i = 0 also imposed in characteristic two. Put D = i (xi ai + yi bi ). The relations give
                                                                          P

D2 = i xi yi . The endomorphism differential has dai = yi , dbi = xi .
      P

    The ai , bi generate the full supermatrix endomorphism algebra. For one pair the four operators
1, a, b, ab are a basis; tensoring the independent factors proves the statement in all ranks. As
an underlying complex the differential is the folded Koszul complex on the regular sequence
x1 , y1 , . . . , xr , yr , with the signs from the ordered odd basis. Tensoring the one-pair exact calculation
shows its cohomology is k in even parity only.
   The ordinary special fibre is the full supermatrix algebra Endk (Λkr ), while the derived fibre
of its cohomology algebra is exterior on 2r odd generators. Both have dimension 4r , but their
multiplications differ. In particular the matrix even part has nontrivial idempotents, whereas the
even part of the finite exterior algebra is local with nilpotent augmentation ideal. Thus the relative
nonformality persists in every positive rank.
    This polarized calculation survives characteristic two. It never symmetrizes an operator by
dividing by two. A proposed normal algebra constructed from half an anticommutator with a
forbidden denominator would lose exactly this robustness. The characteristic-free polarized viewpoint
is consistent with the programme’s supplied normal-direction calculations; it is not a replacement of
their integral scalar data by a characteristic-zero shadow.


31.3      A trace on cohomology is not a cyclic chain trace
For the one-pair model define a degree-zero functional on even matrices by τ (diag(u, v)) = u(0, 0)
and let it vanish on odd matrices. It vanishes on boundaries because those are scalar matrices in
(x, y), and induces the nonzero trace on the one-dimensional cohomology algebra.


                                                        127
Chapter 31. A polarized boundary calculation


  It is not graded cyclic on all chains. For a = E12 and b = E21 ,

                                      τ (ab) = 1,         τ (ba) = 0.

Graded cyclicity for these two odd elements would require τ (ab) = −τ (ba). The failure is not a
contradiction with the trace on cohomology, since a, b are not closed in the family. A cyclic or
Calabi–Yau enhancement at chain level requires the additional homotopies or a cyclic model; it is
not supplied by this one scalar functional.
   The same distinction applies to residue pairings obtained from derivatives of a matrix-factorization
differential. Such a formula can induce the correct pairing on cohomology without being a strict
cyclic trace on the displayed endomorphism complex. The module, its curved differential, its
endomorphism dg algebra, and a chosen cyclic enhancement are four separate pieces of information.


31.4     Probes and what they generate
For a dg algebra A and a perfect module M , the functor RHomA (M, −) identifies the thick
subcategory generated by M with perfect modules over RHomA (M, M ), with the appropriate
right-module convention. This follows by the same finite-cell and retract argument used in the
compact-generator proof: the unit and counit are equivalences on the generating module and remain
so under shifts, cones and retracts.
   The conclusion concerns the subcategory generated by the probe. Extending it to the entire
module category requires a generator theorem. A faithful action on a vector space does not prove
that the corresponding derived probe detects every object. The matrix-factorization calculation
above computes the probe endomorphisms explicitly; no statement that it generates every matrix
factorization is inferred solely from that computation.

32.1      The monomial laboratory
Work over a characteristic-zero field and set S = k[x]. A finite free matrix factorization of xd is a
Z/2-graded free module with an odd matrix Q satisfying Q2 = xd . For homogeneous F : Qr → Qs ,
its differential is
                                     dF = Qs F − (−1)|F | F Qr .
The square is xd F − F xd = 0, and matrix composition satisfies the graded Leibniz rule. These facts
specify an actual dg category.
   Let                                                     !
                                            0 xd−r
                                    Qr =           ,                 0 ≤ r ≤ d.
                                            xr 0
Smith reduction of one off-diagonal matrix in an arbitrary factorization diagonalizes it. Each
invariant factor divides xd , since the other matrix is its inverse times xd . After absorbing units, the
factors are xr , and the other matrix is diagonal with entries xd−r . Therefore every object is a direct
sum of the Qr . For Q0 , the odd matrix E12 has differential one; for Qd , use E21 . These endpoint
objects are contractible.

They are closed. An even diagonal matrix is closed precisely when its two entries have the ratio

This determines every morphism group, not only the endomorphism dimensions.

32.5     The cyclic defect has an explicit primitive
For any one-variable potential W with W ′ ̸= 0, put

                                                            str(U ′ V )
                                     H(U, V ) = Res                     dx.
                                                               W′
Supertrace cyclicity gives

                        str(Q′ U V ) − (−1)|U ||V | str(Q′ V U ) = str([Q′ , U ]V ).

Differentiate dU = [Q, U ] to get [Q′ , U ] = (dU )′ − d(U ′ ). The supertrace of d(U ′ V ) is zero.
Substituting the graded Leibniz rule yields

                    τ (U V ) − (−1)|U ||V | τ (V U ) = H(dU, V ) + (−1)|U | H(U, dV ).

This proves the first chain cyclic homotopy exactly. It is not a statement that no higher cyclic
data are required. The next chapter constructs a strictly cyclic minimal boundary model, with a
specified odd pairing; comparison with geometric Calabi–Yau structures is kept distinct from this
first homotopy.


32.6     Periodization loses maps through a free module
For A = S/(xd ) and Mr = A/(xr ), ordinary endomorphisms form S/(xr ). A map Mr → A sends
one to a multiple of xd−r ; composing with A → Mr gives exactly the ideal (xd−r ). Therefore stable
endomorphisms are
                                    EndA (Mr ) = S/(xmin(r,d−r) ),
agreeing with the even endomorphisms of Qr . For d = 3, r = 2, multiplication by x is nonzero before
stabilization and zero afterwards. This is an explicit reason not to identify the ordinary module
problem with its matrix-factorization version.

The minimal boundary and all its

33.1       A two-state model retaining the potential
Let Ed = k1 ⊕ kθ with θ odd. Write v = s1 and w = sθ, so v is odd and w is even. Set the unary
differential to zero. The binary unit operations are

                                    b2 (v, v) = v,       b2 (v, w) = w,     b2 (w, v) = −w.

The only nonunit operation is
                                                     bd (w⊗d ) = (−1)d v.
For d = 2 this specifies the nonzero binary square; for d > 2 the binary square of θ is zero. Higher
coefficients vanish on any unit input.
   These formulas satisfy every A∞ identity. On a word of w’s, an inner higher operation outputs
the unit. The outer higher operation vanishes unless it is binary; its two endpoint contributions
cancel by the opposite unit signs. On a word containing v, the adjacent ways of multiplying the
unit away cancel, and endpoint terms cancel the outer unit operation. Multiple unit inputs reduce
to the associative unit identities. This exhausts all words, not merely the first d + 1 inputs.


33.2       A comparison with actual matrices
                       
