               x 0
                                             e = E12 ,       Θ = E21 − xd−2 E12 .
Then dΘ = 0, de = x, Θ2 = −xd−2 , and Θe + eΘ = 1. Define the A∞ comparison coefficients by

               F1 (v) = s1,          F1 (w) = sΘ,    Fn (wn ) = (−1)n s(xd−1−n e)      (2 ≤ n ≤ d − 1),

with every other higher coefficient zero and all unit-containing higher coefficients zero.
  For n = 2, the projected morphism equation is −d(xd−3 e) − Θ2 = 0 when d > 2. At each later
n < d, the two endpoint products involving Θ add to the tail polynomial whose differential is
removed by the next Fn . Products of two higher coefficients vanish because e2 = 0. At n = d,

                                                               132
Chapter 33. The minimal boundary and all its twisted objects


their surviving endpoint anticommutator is (−1)d 1, exactly the boundary operation. For n > d all
possible terms vanish. The unit-containing identities have the same adjacent cancellations as above.
This is the specialized tail computation proved in complete generality in Chapter 35.
   The first map sends the two basis states to the generators of H End(Q1 ) computed in the
preceding chapter. It is therefore a quasi-isomorphism. Thus Ed is a complete minimal model, not
just a two-dimensional cohomology space.


33.3      A strictly cyclic odd structure
On the shifted space choose the odd bilinear pairing

                      ω(v, w) = 1,    ω(w, v) = −1,                  ω(v, v) = ω(w, w) = 0.

It is nondegenerate. The cyclic tensor of an operation is obtained by pairing its output with the
last shifted input. For the nonunit higher operation all inputs are w and have even shifted parity,
so cyclic rotation has sign one and leaves its coefficient (−1)d unchanged. For the binary operation,
the nonzero profiles are permutations of two v’s and one w. The displayed unit rules and the two
pairing signs give exactly the Koszul cyclic signs; for example the tensors on (v, v, w) and (v, w, v)
are 1 and −1, and their cyclic permutation has sign −1. All other profiles vanish. This proves strict
cyclicity in every arity.
    The corresponding odd trace on cohomology has τ (θ) = 1 and τ (1) = 0, agreeing with the residue
trace on the generator Q1 . The cyclic minimal structure supplies its own coherent sewing data. An
identification of a prescribed geometric negative-cyclic Calabi–Yau class must retain that class and
its comparison; it is not determined solely by agreement of a trace on two cohomology states.

   The Smith invariants of xI − Jr are 1, . . . , 1, xr . To see this constructively, its columns give the

Theorem 33.1 (A generating minimal boundary). The enhanced idempotent-complete category
of finite matrix factorizations of xd is generated by Q1 . Its perfect twisted-complex presentation
is obtained from the explicit minimal algebra Ed above. In particular every Qr is realized by the
printed nilpotent twisting matrix, and no higher boundary multiplication has been discarded.

Proof. Smith reduction proves that the Qr exhaust the indecomposable summands of every finite
free object. The Jordan construction places each in the thick category generated by Q1 . The
A∞ comparison identifies Ed with its complete endomorphism algebra. Passing to finite twisted
complexes preserves quasi-isomorphisms on morphism complexes: filter each finite upper-triangular
twisting by its finite matrix length, so the associated graded comparison is a finite direct sum of the
original quasi-isomorphism. Finite induction proves quasi-full-faithfulness. Essential surjectivity
follows from the Jordan and Smith constructions; adjoining split idempotents gives the stated
perfect presentation. This is the usual derived Morita construction here with its generator explicitly
exhibited.


33.5     Why the Jacobi ring is not this boundary
For d > 2, the boundary has an odd class θ and a nonzero d-input operation. The categorical
Hochschild center, calculated next, has even cohomology k[z]/z d−1 and a different higher-operation
system. Putting the boundary’s odd operation on that even Jacobi space is not a change of notation.
In the cubic case it violates the degree of a ternary operation and the arity-four unit signs. The
six-input operation of the actual center arises only after its own diagonal calculation and second
transfer.

34.1     The idempotent that survives before cohomology
The monomial matrix-factorization category was calculated in Chapter 29. We retain two further
structures which its cohomology table alone does not determine: specialization over the polynomial
base, and coherent identifications of colliding probes.
               0 xd−r

                                                  P = P − εxn−1 θr
is closed and idempotent. Indeed ∂θr = 0, P 2 = P , P θr + θr P = θr , and ε2 = 0 give both identities

any putative k[x]-linear formality zigzag with Kx would identify these algebras, an impossibility. The
finite free scalar Koszul complex preserves quasi-isomorphisms without a boundedness hypothesis.
   This is relative nonformality. For d = 2, r = 1, the constant odd matrix θ has square −1 and
gives an absolute Clifford quasi-isomorphism over k. The two assertions are compatible: changing
the coefficient ring changes the formality question.

For f = x2 (x − u)(x − v) and labels (0, 1, 2), its upper-right linear exterior part is x(vε1 − uε2 ). It
is not the zero map. Pairwise inverses alone have not constructed a strict diagram.

Chapter 34. Boundary-changing maps through a collision


34.4      All ordered-list coherences by a finite gauge formula
Fix the probe labelled 0 as an anchor and put Eij = T0j Ti0 . Exact cancellation gives Ejk Eij = Eik .
The triangle formula supplies odd hij with

                                          dtot hij = Tij − Eij .

For distinct nonzero i, j, use the negative of the primitive in (??) for (i, 0, j); for equal labels or an
anchor endpoint put h = 0. The chosen anchor remains part of the comparison data.
   For any finite ordered list of positions, let S0 be its diagonal algebra and L the strictly upper-
triangular incidence ideal. The augmented algebra S0 ⊕ L has a reduced relative bar consisting of
composable strings of arrows in that ordered list. There are finitely many lengths. Its convolution
with the total probe endomorphisms is a nilpotently filtered dg algebra; the commutator is a dg Lie
bracket. Bar suspension makes a closed degree-zero arrow map a degree-one convolution coefficient.
A Maurer–Cartan element is precisely a coherent ordered diagram.
  The strict maps E give such an element αE . Let g have only its one-arrow component, equal to
−hij , so its convolution degree is zero. Define
                                                      X (ad g)r
                                    αT = ead g αE −                    dg.                         (34.3)
                                                      r≥0
                                                            (r + 1)!

This is a finite formula on every list, and on every particular composable word its answer involves
only finitely many lower-length data.

Theorem 34.1 (Coherence on every ordered probe list). Formula (??) is Maurer–Cartan, has
one-arrow components Tij , and is compatible with restriction to ordered sublists. Its coefficients give
every higher coherent-composition map on these lists.

Proof. For a formal scalar s, the expression es ad g αE − 0s eu ad g dg du has curvature whose derivative
                                                            R

is ad g applied to that curvature. This follows by differentiating dα + 12 [α, α] and using the derivation
and Jacobi identities. Its initial curvature is zero, so its finite polynomial coefficients all vanish.
Set s = 1. At length one every commutator raises length and hence the answer is Eij + dhij = Tij .
All higher coefficients are the explicit commutator sums above. Restriction to a sublist preserves
deconcatenation, composition, E and h, so preserves the entire formula.

    The theorem is about ordered lists and their face restrictions. It does not identify a matrix
algebra with an augmented diagonal algebra, and does not require an augmentation of the full
groupoid of mutually isomorphic probes. Such an augmentation would fail on a pair of inverse
off-diagonal arrows. The incidence algebra is the correct finite augmented source for the theorem as
stated.


34.5      The relative formality obstruction for the whole family
Let D = EndS ( i Qi ). It is termwise free over R. At the total collision all Qi become Q1 for xN ,
                L
     P
N = mi , so the even cohomology of its derived fibre is Matq (k).
  By (??), odd family cohomology contains R/(ti − tj ). Its first Tor at the total collision gives a
nonzero even class in the derived fibre of H(D). In H(H(D) ⊗R K), positive scalar-Koszul homology

                                                   137
Chapter 34. Boundary-changing maps through a collision


degree is a nilpotent ideal: multiplication adds that degree and the resolution has only q − 1 exterior
generators. This ideal has a nonzero even component. The even algebra therefore has a nonzero
nilpotent ideal, unlike Matq (k). A formality zigzag over R would identify them, a contradiction.
The distinction is in multiplication, not in their total dimensions.
   The subsequent cyclic chapters supply the full trace on these polynomial complexes and the
homotopies between its choices. The present maps and their higher corrections are consequently
concrete data for that trace to act on, rather than an undeclared identification after taking
cohomology.

35.1      A principal part in several variables
The coefficient of x−1 paired the polynomial vacuum with a supported derivative in one variable. In
several variables the same construction has a cohomological degree. Omitting that degree changes
both the parity of the boundary trace and the direction of duality.
  Let S = k[[x1 , . . . , xd ]], where d ≥ 1 and k has characteristic zero, and let m = (x1 , . . . , xd ).
Consider
                                                 d
                                                       [S −→ S[x−1
                                                 O
                                          Im =                  i ]],                                   (35.1)
                                                 i=1
with the left term of each factor in degree zero. Its only cohomology is in degree d, where it is
                                             S[(x1 · · · xd )−1 ]
                                          E=P      Q          −1

As a vector space, E has basis x−a1

linear combination of these monomials. In particular it is a direct sum, not a space of unrestricted
infinite negative Laurent series.
   Here is an elementary proof of the cohomology assertion. The complex (31.1) is the filtered
union of the cochain Koszul complexes on xN                     N
                                                   1 , . . . , xd . Each sequence is regular, so its cohomology
is S/(xN              N

by x1 · · · xd . Sending a polynomial class to its fraction with denominator (x1 · · · xd )N identifies
the direct limit with (31.2). A fraction with a nonnegative exponent of some xi lies in one of the
summands in its denominator; the remaining monomials give exactly the asserted basis. Exactness
of filtered unions proves the claim in every degree.
   The residue functional is
                                    res(e dx1 · · · dxd ) = [x−1       −1

Multiplication followed by residue identifies E with the continuous k-linear dual of S. Indeed a

                                          res(xα x−β−1 dx) = δα,β

checks the identification. It is an identification of S-modules when S acts on its dual by precompo-

35.2     Changing the generators of the support ideal
Let w1 , . . . , wd generate an m-primary ideal. The complement of their common zero is the same
punctured spectrum as for the coordinates. The two finite Cech covers and their common refinement
therefore give canonical identifications of their local-cohomology objects. In particular
                                        S[(w1 · · · wd )−1 ] ∼
                                       P      Q          −1 =
                                                               E.
                                         i S[( j̸=i wj ) ]

Composing with the coordinate residue defines the Grothendieck residue of a generalized fraction in
the wi . An expression which is regular in one of the wi directions represents zero in this quotient
and has zero residue. This vanishing is the ingredient needed in Stokes’ formula later; it is stronger
and more precise than treating residue as an arbitrary scalar projection.
                                                         m1           md
   For computation, choose N such that each xN

Two choices of the matrix induce the same map on the supported direct limit: their difference is
a Koszul homotopy after passing to a sufficiently high power. This can be checked by lifting the
identity successively through the free resolutions; the difference of two lifts has zero augmentation
and is lifted one degree at a time. Thus (31.3) is independent of the chosen expression. It is also a
finite algorithm for each fraction, involving one coefficient of a power series. No choice of a path to
a critical point or analytic contour enters the formal construction.

characteristic by cancelling a denominator which has become zero.


35.3     A finite-rank duality lemma
We will use two-periodic complexes because a matrix factorization is genuinely Z/2-graded. The
Cech direction remains an ordinary bounded integer grading. Totalization adds these degrees modulo
two and sums only finitely many Cech terms.
Lemma 35.1 (Supported duality for finite free two-periodic complexes). Let M be a two-periodic
complex of finite free S-modules. Suppose its cohomology has finite length. Then there is a natural
duality
                              H p̄ HomS (M, S) ∼
                                               = Homk (H d−p (M ), k).
The map is the supported comparison followed by the residue pairing. It is not the ordinary map
obtained by evaluating an S-linear functional at x = 0.

                                                  140
Chapter 35. Supported duality and the residue which remembers a boundary


Proof. First consider the continuous dual. A submodule of a finite free module over the complete
noetherian ring S is closed in its m-adic topology. One proof uses the Rees module: finite generation
gives a constant c with
                                    N ∩ mn S r ⊆ mn−c N (n ≥ c).
Thus the induced topology on N is equivalent to its adic topology. Completeness of finite modules
and separation of the quotient make N closed. Applied to kernels and images of the two differential
matrices, this shows that continuous duality preserves their exact sequences. More explicitly, a
continuous functional on a submodule vanishes on its intersection with mN S r for a sufficiently large
N ; it extends by ordinary finite-dimensional linear algebra from that subspace of S r /mN S r . This
proves the required exactness directly.
   By the residue identification, the continuous dual complex is HomS (M, E). The finite-length
cohomology is discrete and finite dimensional. Consequently

                                  H HomS (M, E) = Homk (HM, k),

with the usual contravariant degree and differential signs.
    Now tensor the triangle Im → S → C(Spec S \ {m}) with M . Localizing HM away from m gives
zero, so every term of the punctured Cech calculation is acyclic. Its bounded totalization is acyclic.
Therefore Im ⊗S M → M is a quasi-isomorphism. The same is true for HomS (M, S): its localization
is the dual of a finite free exact two-periodic complex on a regular localization. Alternatively, in the
matrix-factorization application below there is an explicit localized contraction on both complexes,
which proves this step without that general observation. For the general statement one may use the
following finite-projective argument over a regular ring of finite global dimension: dimension shifting
in the two-periodic exact localized complex makes its cycle modules projective, so the complex splits
and its dual is exact.
   Since Im ≃ E[−d] and M has finite free terms, the bounded Cech comparison yields

                                  HomS (M, S) ≃ HomS (M, E)[−d].

The bounded Cech width ensures that these are valid totalizations even though the matrix complex
is two-periodic. Combining the two identifications proves the formula.

   The proof deliberately exposes the completeness and support hypotheses. A finite-dimensional
vector-space pairing cannot replace them for an arbitrary family of infinite complexes. In the
applications, support and the localized contractions are visible from the defining matrices.


35.4     Why matrix-factorization morphisms are supported
Let W ∈ m2 have an isolated critical point, and let Q2 = W 1 be a finite free matrix factorization.
Set wi = ∂i W . Differentiating the curvature identity gives

                                      Q(∂i Q) + (∂i Q)Q = wi 1.                                  (35.4)

Thus hi = (∂i Q)/wi contracts the object on D(wi ). On a morphism complex with target Q, left
multiplication by hi satisfies
                                      δ(hi Φ) + hi δΦ = Φ.


                                                 141
Chapter 35. Supported duality and the residue which remembers a boundary


Before localization, the same formula says that multiplication by wi is null-homotopic. Hence
morphism cohomology is annihilated by the Jacobian ideal. It is finitely generated over S, because
the kernels and images of finite matrices are finitely generated, and therefore finite dimensional over
k.
   For two factorizations P, Q, supertrace and composition give a perfect S-linear pairing between
the underlying finite free morphism modules

                                  HomS (P, Q) ⊗S HomS (Q, P ) −→ S.

Matrix units show perfection, and the identity str[Q, F ] = 0 shows compatibility with the differentials.
Lemma 31.1 turns this S-linear pairing into a k-linear pairing of parity d. The residue and the
localized contractions are the explicit passage between the two. This proves why the dimension of
the normal complex, and not the size of a chosen matrix, determines the parity of its Calabi–Yau
trace.


35.5      A complete product example
Let W = di=1 xm
          P        i
                  i and take the external tensor product of the rank-one factorizations Qri , where
0 < ri < mi . Its differential is
                                   d
                                   X
                             Q=          Γ1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Γi−1 ⊗ Qri ⊗ 1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ 1,
                                   i=1

with Γi the parity operator. Distinct summands anticommute, so Q2 = W . Its morphism complex
to another such product is the graded tensor product of the one-variable morphism complexes.
Over a field the tensor contraction or the Kunneth argument identifies all its cohomology and all
compositions.
   Put ni = min(ri , mi − ri ). On the product of the odd generators the trace is

                          τ f (x)θ1 · · · θd = (−1)d(d−1)/2 [xn1 1 −1 · · · xnd d −1 ]f.
                                             


The sign is the Koszul sign of tensoring d odd trace maps. For opposite morphisms the product of
the one-variable anti-diagonal matrices is nondegenerate. This gives an entirely explicit multivariable
instance of the supported duality. It will also fix the normalization of the full cyclic construction.

An explicit cyclic trace in every

36.1      The first commutator homotopy is not the full answer
For a one-variable factorization, the residue functional

                                                       str(Q′ Φ)
                                        τ (Φ) = Res              dx
                                                          W′
is cyclic on cohomology, not on arbitrary polynomial matrices. The previously constructed functional
H(U, V ) = Res str(U ′ V )dx/W ′ gives a homotopy for its trace commutator. That homotopy need not
itself have the cyclic symmetry of a length-two cyclic cochain. For W = x3 and U = V = x diag(1, 0)
it gives 1/3, whereas cyclic antisymmetry requires zero on these equal even inputs.
   There are two tasks. One must give all cyclic lengths, not only another binary homotopy. One
must also glue the local contractions in several variables without asserting that their sum is already
a contraction for the total differential. We solve both by one finite formula. The existence and the
general residue interpretation of such proper Calabi–Yau structures are classical [17, 18]; the proof
here gives an algebraic simplex model, its signs, and its coherent dependence on the choices.


36.2      A commutative coefficient model on the puncture
Continue with S = k[[x1 , . . . , xd ]] and an isolated critical point of W . Cover the punctured
formal spectrum by D(wi ), where wi = ∂i W . For a nonempty subset J ⊆ {1, . . . , d} write

graded commutative and its differential is the polynomial-form differential. Integration over all

oriented simplexes compares it with the finite Cech complex. The comparison is a quasi-isomorphism:
filter the finite diagram by face dimension, and use the polynomial de Rham contraction on a
simplex relative to its already attached boundary. The relative complex has one top cohomology
class, detected by the simplex integral. Finite induction proves the assertion.
   Only the coefficient differential is present in T . The variables xi are holomorphic coefficients; dxi
in a residue denotes the fixed volume form and is not an extra differential in this algebra.
   Define a functional of degree 1 − d by

includes d = 1, where a regular formal power series has zero residue.


36.3      The finite contractor
For a factorization Q put hi = ∂i Q/wi on the ith chart. On a simplex set

                                 (αM )(βN ) = (−1)|M ||β| (α ∧ β)M N.

                              dQ h = 1,     Dh = 1 + u,       Du = 0.

Lemma 36.1 (Contracting the localized object). The family H belongs to the Thom–Whitney
matrix algebra, is compatible with every face, and satisfies DH = 1.

Proof. All entries on a face use only its allowed hi and denominators. The barycentric restriction
takes h and u to the corresponding smaller-simplex expressions. Thus their finite polynomial in
(32.2) respects every face. Since Du = 0, the graded derivation rule gives
                                     DH = (1 + u)(1 + u)−1 = 1.
There is no convergence assertion here: the geometric series terminates by exterior form degree.
In particular dQ h = 1 alone was not the desired total identity; the terms −h dh + h(dh)2 − · · · are
exactly its missing higher corrections.

   The formula is a contraction on the puncture. It does not contract the original polynomial or
formal boundary category, whose supported morphism classes are generally nonzero.

36.5      Every length has one formula
Set σd = (−1)d(d−1)/2 . Define
                             Θl (c) = −σd R str(Φl Hl Φl−1 Hl−1 · · · Φ1 H1 ).                           (36.3)
Here Hi is the contractor at the source of Φi . Composition therefore has exactly the displayed
type. At a fixed length, all expressions in the simplex variables are polynomials, all matrix products
are finite, and the residue of each rational formal expression is defined in the supported module.
Direct-sum cyclic chains contain only finitely many lengths. No sum of infinitely many residues is
used.
                                                                                         P
Theorem 36.2 (All-length residue trace). The functional Θ = l≥1 Θl has parity d, is cyclic, and
satisfies
                          Θl bδ + Θl−1 bµ = 0 (l ≥ 1),     Θ0 = 0.
For the isolated-critical-point category it induces a nondegenerate proper Calabi–Yau trace. Its
length-one component on cycles is the Kapustin–Li residue in the normalization below.

                                                               145
Chapter 36. An explicit cyclic trace in every dimension and every length


Proof. Every factor Φi Hi has parity |Φi | + 1. Supertrace cyclicity therefore gives exactly the
displayed rotation sign. To check the differential, apply D to their product. Its supertrace is the
form differential of the scalar supertrace, because the supertrace of the internal graded commutator
is zero. Applying R kills this differential.
   A term differentiating Φi has sign (−1)ϵi+1 from the preceding factors. These are the terms of
Θl bδ . A term differentiating Hi uses DHi = 1 and composes its neighbouring morphisms. The
interior signs are precisely those of bµ . At the cyclic end, moving the last morphism around the
supertrace gives (−1)(|Φ1 |+1)(ϵ2 +1) . Thus for l ≥ 2 the differentiated-product identity is the stated
cocycle equation. For l = 1, differentiating the final contractor leaves str Φ, a regular coefficient,
whose residue is zero. This proves that length as well.
  A nonzero supertrace has even matrix parity. Selecting form degree d − 1 then forces the cyclic
word to have parity d, proving the degree assertion.
   For nondegeneracy, the localized contraction on a morphism complex M gives a concrete inverse
to the supported comparison. In the model

                     Γm M = Cone(M → T ⊗ M )[−1],          d(a, c) = (δa, a − Dc),

the map a 7→ (a, Ha) is a chain map and a section of projection to M . The latter is a quasi-
isomorphism because the localized complex is contracted. Projection to the top Cech quotient
and the residue then gives the supported duality of Lemma 31.1. Composing with the perfect
matrix supertrace pairing gives, up to the single shift-orientation sign fixed by σd , exactly Θ1 of
the composed morphisms. The lemma proves that its pairing on cohomology is perfect. The sign
is fixed explicitly by the next computation. Thus nondegeneracy is not deduced merely from the
existence of a cyclic cocycle.

Only the form-degree-d − 1 component of H contributes. Expand h(dh)d−1 . Any summand missing
an index i is regular in the missing wi direction and has zero residue. The surviving terms use
each index once. For a permutation of these indices, integration of its barycentric monomial and
differentials is its orientation sign divided by d!. Moving the odd matrix factors past the form
factors contributes (−1)d(d−1)/2 at the indicated stage. Combining these signs with (32.3), and
using supertrace to move the final Φ to the right, gives

the suspension convention.

37.1     One trace class, many chain representatives
The formula of Chapter 32 used local contractors. Changing those contractors generally changes
individual cyclic components on nonclosed maps. The correct independence statement is therefore a
coherent homotopy, not literal equality of formulas. We construct the homotopy in every dimension
of the choice space.
                                                                   (0)       (q)
  Fix the cover and residue functional of that chapter. Let hi , . . . , hi be q + 1 systems of local
                        (a)
odd contractors, so dQ hi = 1 on the ith chart. Introduce barycentric coordinates s0 , . . . , sq on an
auxiliary simplex and put
                                X           (a)                            −1

                                i,a

This is finite because the form degree is bounded by d − 1 + q. Exactly the calculation of Lemma 32.1
gives
                                         (dQ + dt + ds )H = 1.
Every face of the choice simplex is the formula obtained by omitting its vertex. Thus the family
includes the originally chosen contractors at its vertices and supplies all their higher comparisons on
the same coefficient domain.


37.2     The homotopy signs
Let Cλ be the raw cyclic chain complex with the differential b of the preceding chapter. Its dual

                                   (δf )(c) = −(−1)|f | f (bc).

Chapter 37. Coherent choices, changes of basis, and collision families of traces

Theorem 37.1 (All simplex coherences of the trace). The functionals (33.2) are cyclic and obey
                                               q

for the closed element Θe by form degree. Its degree-j part says

Integrate over the oriented choice simplex and use polynomial Stokes. Multiplying successively

   The theorem does not use the claim that an arbitrary space of choices is contractible. The affine
interpolation was written down, its total contractor was calculated, and the face maps were retained.
These are the additional data needed to make the contractibility argument true.


37.3      Polynomial changes of basis
Let g be an even invertible change of basis and Qg = gQg −1 . Transporting every morphism and
every contractor by g leaves the raw trace formula unchanged, because supertrace is conjugation
invariant. However, the derivative contractor of the changed matrix is not just the conjugated old
contractor:
                                ∂i Qg    ∂i Q −1 [(∂i g)g −1 , Qg ]
                                      =g     g +                    .
                                 wi       wi              wi
Both expressions have internal differential one. The extra term is an internal boundary, but it
must not simply be deleted inside products of nonclosed maps. Interpolating between these two
contractors and applying Theorem 33.1 gives an explicit full cyclic comparison. It includes every
cyclic length and every finite chain of changes of basis.


                                                     149
Chapter 37. Coherent choices, changes of basis, and collision families of traces


   For two successive changes g1 , g2 , the two resulting edge homotopies need not be equal to the
edge homotopy for g2 g1 . Their difference is the boundary of the displayed two-simplex functional.
Higher chains of changes of basis are governed by the corresponding higher simplexes. This is the
required distinction between a cohomological trace, a chain trace, and a coherent trace on a category
whose objects have several presentations.
    A formal change of ambient coordinates transports the entire ring, cover, potential, matrices and
volume form. The residue transformation law of (31.3) makes the trace natural for that transported
datum. Reselecting a different gradient cover is a separate Cech refinement comparison. The
existence of the resulting intrinsic Calabi–Yau class is also supplied by the general theorem in [17];
the explicit fixed-cover homotopies proved here are not an assertion that arbitrary cover choices are
literally equal as chain formulas.


37.4      A trace through a whole monic family
A useful family version requires no separation of critical points. Let R be any commutative Q-algebra
and set
                                                                       d
                                                                       X
                                 S = R[x1 , . . . , xd ],        W =         fi (xi ),
                                                                       i=1

where each fi is monic of a fixed degree mi ≥ 2. Its derivative is
                                                  i −1
                                       wi = mi xm      1 + O(x−1
                                                                         
                                                i             i ) .

For a rational expression with denominators powers of the wi , define the relative residue by the
coefficient of x1−1 · · · x−1
                           d in its simultaneous expansion at infinity. This convention is the sum of the
finite residues in each variable, not the residue at infinity with its opposite orientation.
   The coefficient exists over R. For example
                                                              !
                                    −a(mi −1) X             −a               i −1
                                                                                         r
                         wi−a = m−a
                                 i xi                             wi /(mi xm
                                                                           i      )−1 .
                                              r≥0
                                                             r

Each coefficient at a fixed inverse power involves finitely many summands. A rational input has
finite numerator degree, so its required residue uses only finitely many coefficients. It is a polynomial
expression in the coefficients of the fi and inverses of the integers mi . No discriminant is inverted.
If one gradient denominator is absent, the corresponding variable has a polynomial dependence and
its residue is zero.

Theorem 37.2 (Relative all-length trace across critical collisions). For finite free matrix factor-
izations of the displayed W , the simplex-contractor formula defines an R-valued cyclic cocycle in
every length. It commutes with every coefficient homomorphism R → R′ , and its choice homotopies
satisfy the same all-simplex identities after that specialization. Over each field fibre, the induced
pairing on the supported matrix-factorization morphism cohomology is nondegenerate.

Proof. The contractor identity uses only differentiating Q2 = W and the inverses of the selected wi
on the puncture. All form and matrix products remain finite. The residue functional is closed by
Stokes and the missing-denominator vanishing just proved. Thus the proof of Theorem 32.2 works
over R for the cocycle and cyclicity identities. Its formulas use finitely many coefficients in each


                                                        150
Chapter 37. Coherent choices, changes of basis, and collision families of traces


cyclic length and simplex degree, so they commute with every ring homomorphism. The same is
true of (33.2).
   On a field fibre the critical scheme is finite, since the separate derivatives have fixed positive
degrees and invertible leading coefficients. Complete at each of its points. The supported-duality
proof applies there. A critical point at which W is a unit contributes a contractible factorization
category: Q/(2W ) is an odd contractor. Summing the local traces therefore gives the nondegenerate
pairing on the remaining supported category. This proves the fibre statement without assuming
that cohomology commutes with a nonflat change of R.

   The last qualification matters. The theorem gives a cyclic cocycle over the whole coefficient ring
and perfect field-fibre pairings. It does not identify the ordinary cohomology of the family with a
flat family of its fibre cohomology groups. The root-probe examples show explicitly why those are
different assertions.


37.5      What has been completed
The construction supplies the full proper cyclic trace on the monomial control category, extends it to
isolated multivariable factorizations, gives every fixed-cover change-of-contractor and change-of-basis
homotopy, and carries the trace through a monic collision family. Each of these is an operation-
bearing statement. It is not obtained by equating a scalar residue with an augmentation, or by
imposing a strict cyclic equality which fails on matrices.
   The native chiral bulk is still a different object. A cyclic trace gives closed-string operations in
the ordinary categorical theory only after its corresponding cyclic or modular construction has been
made. It does not by itself provide the missing morphism from a holomorphic–topological coloured
configuration operad. The point of this chapter is to finish one of the original boundary construction
problems completely on its stated carrier, so that a later comparison has actual maps to preserve.

the monomial boundary

38.1      The completed cochain space has two explicit coordinates
The two-dimensional boundary model is small enough that its entire Hochschild operation system
can be written down. This calculation supplies an independent route to the higher center and makes
the mixed action visible before any kernel interpretation.
  Fix d ≥ 2 and let Ed = k1 ⊕ kθ be the strictly unital A∞ algebra of Chapter 30. Put v = s1 and
w = sθ. Thus v is odd, w even, and the nonunit operation is

                                     bd (w⊗d ) = ϵv,           ϵ = (−1)d .

For d = 2 this is the ordinary nonzero binary square, not an additional operation superimposed on
it. The unit operations are b2 (v, v) = v, b2 (v, w) = w, and b2 (w, v) = −w.
  A normalized shifted Hochschild cochain is a family of maps on w⊗n with values in kv ⊕ kw.
Encode it by
                         φ = a(z)v + b(z)w,      a(z), b(z) ∈ k[[z]],
where the coefficient of z n is its value on w⊗n . Here z records arity; it is not introduced as a spatial
coordinate. The product over all arities is exactly the power-series completion in this description.
Unit-valued cochains are odd in the shifted complex and θ-valued ones are even.


38.2      All braces in a single formula
For r ≥ 1, write ψj = aj (z)v + bj (z)w. The normalized brace is
                                                                    r
                                                           1 dr φ Y
                                   φ{ψ1 , . . . , ψr } =               bj (z).                     (38.1)
                                                           r! dz r j=1

This is understood multilinearly on homogeneous components. A term with a unit-valued inner
cochain is zero, since the outer normalized cochain then receives a unit.
Proposition 38.1 (Complete brace calculation). Equation (34.1) is the ordinary insertion brace
on every normalized Hochschild cochain of Ed , in every arity.

                                                     152
Chapter 38. Every cochain and mixed evaluation of the monomial boundary


Proof. Consider an outer cochain supported in arity p and inner θ-valued cochains supported           in
                                                                                               p
arities n1 , . . . , nr . One chooses r of the p ordered slots of the outer cochain. There are r choices.
Each substituted block has the prescribed arity and the unfilled p − r slots remain single w inputs.
Therefore the output arity is p − r + nj . All shifted input letters and all nonzero inner cochains are
                                          P

even, so every such summand has sign +1. Its coefficient is exactly the coefficient of pr z p−r j z nj
                                                                                                  Q

in the right side. If an inner output is v, normalization makes the term zero. This proves the
formula on basis cochains. For a fixed output coefficient and fixed r, the equality involves finitely
many input coefficients; it therefore extends to the stated completion.

   For instance, on the shifted complex the commutator bracket is

             [b(z)w, g(z)w] = (b′ g − g ′ b)w,       [b(z)w, a(z)v] = −a′ b v,              [a(z)v, ã(z)v] = 0,

where ã is an independent coefficient function. More invariantly, all brackets are obtained by
antisymmetrizing (34.1) for r = 1; this avoids any ambiguity of notation.


38.3      The differential and all higher cup products
Let Cd denote the unshifted cochain complex, so Cd [1] is the space just described. The differential
on the shifted complex is D = [b, −], with b the entire boundary coderivation. It is

                                         D(a(z)v + b(z)w) = ϵd z d−1 b(z)v.                                        (38.2)

The terms from multiplying next to a unit cancel in pairs. The nonunit term has d possible slots for
a θ-valued cochain. Insertion of the boundary bd into a normalized cochain instead produces a unit
and vanishes. These observations prove (34.2) directly.
   The higher cup structure on Cd has suspended coefficients

                         B1 φ = [b, φ],       Br (φ1 , . . . , φr ) = b{φ1 , . . . , φr }    (r ≥ 2).

Its complete list is

               B1 (bw) = ϵdz d−1 b v,                                                                              (38.3)
                     ′           ′
          B2 (av, a v) = aa v,                        B2 (av, bw) = ab w,                   B2 (bw, av) = −ab w,   (38.4)
                                     !
                                 d d−r Y
   Br (b1 w, . . . , br w) = ϵ     z     bj v                       (2 ≤ r ≤ d).                                   (38.5)

For r > 2, a unit-valued input makes the operation zero. Operations with r > d vanish. The
binomial coefficient is obtained by choosing the r slots of the single nonunit boundary operation;
the remaining slots contain w. No infinite sum of rooted vertices is hidden in these formulas.

Theorem 38.2 (The explicit monomial cochain center). The braces (34.1), differential (34.2),
higher cups (34.3), and evaluation on boundary inputs are the complete normalized Hochschild
operation system of Ed . In particular the higher cups satisfy every A∞ identity. The cohomology is
even and is the algebra
                                        H(Cd ) = k[z]/(z d−1 ).



                                                           153
Chapter 38. Every cochain and mixed evaluation of the monomial boundary


Proof. The brace and differential calculations were proved above. The standard brace insertion
identity proves the higher-cup identities by expanding b{b} = 0: every substitution tree with two
boundary vertices occurs in exactly the two combinations defining the A∞ associator. Their signs
are the tensor signs of those two insertions. Equivalently, insert the explicit list into the suspended
square-zero equation. On inputs all of type w, a higher cup has output of type v, so the only nonzero
outer higher operation is binary. Its two endpoint terms cancel by B2 (v, w) = w and B2 (w, v) = −w.
The unit-containing identities cancel in the adjacent and endpoint pairs exactly as for the strictly
unital boundary operations. This proves the identities in every profile.
   Equation (34.2) is multiplication by the nonzero divisor ϵdz d−1 from the w row to the v row.
Its kernel on the w row is zero and its cokernel is the stated quotient. The product of unit-valued
cochains is ordinary multiplication of their power series by (34.3), so the assertion identifies the
cohomology algebra, not just its dimension.

    The usual identification of the Hochschild complex with the derived associative center, including
its universal coherent action, applies to this strictly unital A∞ model by homotopy invariance. Its
geometric Swiss-cheese interpretation is the ordinary Deligne comparison, not the native chiral
comparison. Here its entire algebraic operation system has been computed rather than inferred
from the Jacobi ring.


38.4     Mixed evaluations detect a class invisible in zeroth order
For the cochain a(z)v + b(z)w, the mixed operation with n suspended boundary inputs is simply

                                      εn (φ; w⊗n ) = an 1 + bn θ.

Every other mixed tree is evaluated by composing this rule, the boundary operations, the braces
and the higher cups. This is a complete algorithm on a finite labeled tree. The identities are the
insertion identities already proved; they are not extrapolated from finitely many tested inputs.
    The class z in the center has zeroth mixed component zero, but its first component sends the single
suspended boundary generator to 1. More generally z j has a nonzero component on j boundary
inputs. Thus the whole central class need not be detected by its action on the cohomology of one
boundary object through the zeroth component alone. The relation z d−1 = 0 in center cohomology
is implemented by the actual homotopy D(w) = ϵdz d−1 v. This ties the central nilpotence order
directly to the higher boundary multiplication.
   This observation is consistent with the fact that the boundary generator generates the matrix-
factorization category while nonconstant central classes may vanish on the ordinary cohomological
endomorphism ring of that generator. The coherent action, not that one ring map, retains the
missing information.

the transverse coordinate. Since a = de in the diagonal endomorphism complex, their actions are
homotopic rather than strictly identical. The coordinate change transfers higher operations; it
cannot be made by replacing their coefficients at a = 0 and discarding the comparison. In the cubic
example below the resulting change of minimal-model gauge is computed explicitly.

39.1     The diagonal is a field of operations, not only a ring
A useful calculation begins before taking the Jacobi quotient. The stabilized diagonal of a one-
variable potential has two odd matrix generators. One is contractible in the direction transverse to
the diagonal. Eliminating that generator transfers its products into higher multiplications on the
remaining two-row complex. The derivatives of the potential occur as their coefficients. This is the
mechanism by which a very small cohomology ring retains a much larger multiplication law.
  Let R be a commutative Q-algebra, let a be an indeterminate, and write
                                                     m

The scalar polynomial variable a has even parity. Everything in this chapter is Z/2-graded; the
coefficients in R are even and closed.

39.2     An explicit contraction of the underlying complex
Let CB = R1 ⊕ Rξ, with ξ odd and dξ = β0 . As an R[a]-module, DB has basis 1, e, θ, eθ. Define
                                               i(1) = 1,                                  i(ξ) = θ,
               p(F0 + F1 e + F2 θ + F3 eθ) = F0 (0) + F2 (0)ξ.
For a polynomial F , write ∇a F = (F (a) − F (0))/a. Define the odd map H by
                   H(F ) = ∇a F e,            H(F θ) = ∇a F eθ,             H(F e) = H(F eθ) = 0.
Lemma 39.1. The maps just defined are R-linear and satisfy
                            pi = 1,       dH + Hd = 1 − ip,          H 2 = Hi = pH = 0.
No regularity assumption on β0 is needed.

Proof. The maps i and p commute with the differentials by dθ = β0 . On F 1, dH is F − F (0) and
Hd = 0. On F e, Hd = H(aF ) = F e. On F θ, use
                                                   d(eθ) = aθ − β0 e.
The two contributions to (dH + Hd)(F θ) are ∇a F (aθ − β0 e) and β0 ∇a F e; their sum is (F − F (0))θ.
Finally, on F eθ only Hd contributes, and its value is F eθ. This proves the contraction on a basis
over R[a]. The three side conditions follow because the image of H is spanned by e, eθ, H kills that
span, and evaluation at a = 0 kills neither scalar nor θ terms only when such terms occur. In the
image of H they do not occur.

   This is only a contraction of complexes. The map i is generally not multiplicative: i(ξ)2 = −q1 (a)
need not be the image of any chosen product on CB . The missing terms will now be constructed,
rather than suppressed.

              b1 (w) = −β0 v,                                 b1 (v) = 0,                                 (39.3)

Theorem 39.2 (Relative Clifford–Taylor transfer). Equations (35.3)–(35.5) define a strictly unital
A∞ algebra over R. Equations (35.6)–(35.7) define a strictly unital A∞ quasi-isomorphism from
that algebra to DB . They specify every operation and every comparison coefficient.

Proof. It is useful to check the identities without appealing to an unspecified transfer algorithm.
On a word made entirely of w’s, every bn has output proportional to the unit v. In a composite of
two higher operations, the outer operation therefore vanishes unless it is binary. The two possible
binary endpoint terms cancel because b2 (v, w) = w and b2 (w, v) = −w. A unary operation on v is
zero, and an outer higher operation with a unary output as input is zero. This proves b2 = 0 on all
such words.
   On a word containing v, operations of arity greater than two cannot contain that input. If an
inner binary operation removes the unit, the two adjacent possibilities have opposite suspended
signs and the same remaining word. At an endpoint the remaining term cancels the binary operation
outside the inner block. Repeated unit inputs either reduce to the associative unit identities or
leave a unit in a normalized higher operation, making it zero. The unary differential kills the unit.
This proves b2 = 0 on every word, including all unit profiles.
   For the morphism, put f1 = θ and fn = (−1)n qn e for n ≥ 2. Each fn is odd. The projection of
the equation bD F = F bC on a word wn is
                                            X
                                  −dfn −           fi fj = (−1)n βn−1 1,                         (39.8)
                                           i+j=n

where at n = 1 the right side is −β0 . Higher components on the right involving an inner unit are
zero by normalization. The minus sign in each binary term on the left is bD,2 (sfi , sfj ) = −s(fi fj ),
since fi is odd.
   For n = 1, the equation is −dθ = −β0 . For n = 2, it is

                                           −aq2 + q1 = β1 .

For n ≥ 3, products with both indices at least two vanish because e2 = 0. The two remaining
products add to (−1)n−1 qn−1 by θe + eθ = 1. Thus the left side of (35.8) is

                                  (−1)n (qn−1 − aqn ) = (−1)n βn−1 .

This proves the equation in every arity. The unit-containing morphism identities reduce to the
same endpoint and adjacent-unit cancellations as above. Finally F1 = i is a quasi-isomorphism by
Lemma 35.1. A coalgebra morphism intertwining the differentials and with a quasi-isomorphic first
coefficient is precisely an A∞ quasi-isomorphism.

  The formula is valid over R even if β0 is a zero divisor. In that case the odd cohomology is
AnnR (β0 )ξ and must not be discarded. If β0 is regular, the cohomology is the even algebra R/(β0 ).
Neither assertion erases the higher operations.

39.5     When this is the Hochschild center
There is one geometric input to the center interpretation. For a germ with an isolated critical point
on its zero fiber, the stabilized diagonal represents the identity functor of the matrix-factorization
category. The derived kernel realization identifies its endomorphism algebra with the Hochschild
cochains of that category, including composition. This is the stabilized-diagonal and derived-Morita
theorem of Dyckerhoff and Toën; see [28, Proposition 6.3 and Corollary 6.4] and [26]. The categorical
identification is imported here with its hypotheses, not inferred from equality of Jacobi dimensions.
   For W = xd , d ≥ 2, on the formal germ at the origin, those hypotheses apply. The matrix Q
above is exactly the one-variable stabilized diagonal for W (x) − W (y). Its endomorphism differential
and composition are the explicit ones used throughout this chapter. Therefore the Clifford–Taylor
construction gives an A∞ , equivalently E1 , model of this categorical Hochschild center. The center
also has its canonical E2 structure by the ordinary categorical Deligne theorem; transferring that
structure is treated by the colored construction, not by declaring the displayed binary multiplication
to be all of it.
Corollary 39.4. For the isolated monomial germ W = xd , the complex CW together with (35.9)
is an explicit E1 model of the Hochschild center of MF(W ). Its cohomology is k[u]/(ud−1 ), but
the identification with that ring is not asserted to be an A∞ quasi-isomorphism with zero higher
operations.

Proof. Apply the stated kernel theorem, followed by the explicit quasi-isomorphism of Theorem 35.2.
The cohomology calculation uses injectivity of multiplication by dud−1 in k[[u]]. Completion at
the isolated point does not change the finite Jacobi quotient. The higher operations and their
comparison coefficients extend continuously in this completion.

                                                     159
Chapter 39. The relative Clifford–Taylor model


   The restriction to the appropriate critical locus matters. For a general global potential, critical
points need not lie on its zero fiber. A diagonal endomorphism algebra for W (x) − W (y) can see
those points while the category of matrix factorizations of the zero fiber of W does not. Chapter 39
constructs the zero-fiber support restriction and compares the other critical values.

40.1     A four-matrix calculation
The cubic is the first example in which the higher center can be seen without a large computation.
It also prevents a misleading inference: the center’s cohomology is concentrated in even parity, but
in a two-periodic category that does not force formality.
   Put                               !

The scalar u commutes with every matrix. The first identity provides a homotopy for u2 = 0 in

cannot be removed by taking one more homotopy, because the identity is not exact.
   The cohomology of D is B = k1 ⊕ ku, entirely even, with u2 = 0. One can see this from the

for a and 3u2 + a2 /4.

Theorem 40.1. The preceding operations make B a strictly unital minimal A∞ algebra, and the
displayed F is an A∞ quasi-isomorphism B → D. Together with the stabilized-diagonal theorem,
this is a complete minimal E1 model of the Hochschild center of MF(x3 ).

Proof. The square of the source coderivation has only three possible types of terms. The square
of b2 is zero by associativity. A composition of b6 with itself is zero because the inner output is a
unit. In the mixed terms, a binary multiplication of two u inputs is zero. The two binary products
with the unit output of b6 occur at opposite endpoints and cancel: the six shifted u inputs are odd,
so moving the degree-one operation past the first shifted input changes the sign. Unit-containing
words obey the strict-unit cancellation proved in Chapter 35. This proves every source identity.
   For the morphism equation on (su)n , write f1 = u1, f2 = t, f4 = v and all other fi = 0. Its left
side projected to one output is             X
                                  −dfn +         (−1)i+1 fi fj .
                                               i+j=n

The sign follows from |fi | ≡ 1 − i (mod 2) and the convention for the binary suspended product in
D. At n = 2 this is −dt + u2 = 0. At n = 3 it is ut − tu = 0. At n = 4 it is −dv − t2 = 0. At n = 5
it is uv − vu = 0. At n = 6 it is
                                                         1
                                        −(tv + vt) = −      1,
                                                       108
exactly F1 b6 . At n = 8 it is −v 2 = 0, and all other arities have no nonzero term. On the right,
any insertion of b6 into Fn , n > 1, vanishes because it inserts a unit. Thus these checks exhaust
all arities, rather than merely the arities tested by a program. The same normalization deals
with words containing units. Finally F1 represents 1, u, a basis of the cohomology, so it is a
quasi-isomorphism.


40.3      Why the sixth operation cannot be a coordinate artifact
A transferred higher operation can sometimes be removed by changing coordinates. Its nonzero
value alone is not a nonformality proof. In this example the obstruction to removal can be computed
completely.
Theorem 40.2 (Essentiality of the sixth multiplication). The minimal algebra of Theorem 36.1
is not strictly unital A∞ quasi-isomorphic to the ordinary dual-number algebra with all higher
operations zero. Consequently the diagonal endomorphism algebra D is not formal as a unital
two-periodic dg algebra over k.

Proof. Suppose such a quasi-isomorphism G existed. Since both algebras are minimal, its first
coefficient is an isomorphism of their binary cohomology algebras. It fixes the unit and sends u to
λu, with λ ̸= 0. No scalar term is possible: the image must square to zero.

                                                  162
Chapter 40. The cubic center has an essential six-input operation


    Every element of B is even. The unsuspended coefficient Gn has degree 1 − n, so Gn = 0 when n
is even. Strict unitality says that for odd n > 1 its only possible nonzero value is

                                      Gn (u, . . . , u) = pn 1 + qn u.

Use the morphism equation first at arity four. Every source binary product u2 is zero. Its target
side is 2λp3 u, so p3 = 0.
   At arity six, the target side is a sum of products G1 G5 , G3 G3 and G5 G1 , with the suspended
convention fixed above. Since p3 = 0 and u2 = 0, its scalar component is zero. The source side
                        1
includes G1 b6 = − 108    s1, whose scalar component is nonzero. This is a contradiction. Notice that
no choice of q3 , p5 , q5 can change the scalar comparison.
   A unital dg formality zigzag gives a strictly unital A∞ quasi-isomorphism between its minimal
models. This follows by the normalized transfer and comparison construction: choose contractions
preserving the unit line and use the normalized tensor coalgebra of Chapter 3.1. Between minimal
models an A∞ quasi-isomorphism has invertible first term and a recursively constructed inverse in
word length. Thus the contradiction excludes a unital dg formality zigzag as well.

   The conclusion is compatible with the small dimension of the cohomology. In an ordinary
Z-grading a minimal algebra concentrated in degree zero cannot have a degree 2 − n operation for
n ̸= 2. Here the category is two-periodic, and 2 − 6 has even parity. Forgetting that periodicity
would incorrectly prohibit the operation that has just been constructed.


40.4      What the Jacobi complex computes
The two-row complex
                                    KW = k[u] ⊗ Λ(ξ), dξ = 3u2
                                                                         

with only its ordinary graded-commutative product is a perfectly valid dg algebra. Projection to
B, sending ξ to zero and u to its residue class, is a multiplicative quasi-isomorphism. Therefore
that dg algebra is formal. It has the same underlying cohomology ring as D, but it cannot be a
multiplication-preserving model of D with all additional operations omitted, by Theorem 36.2.
   There is no conflict with the traditional computation of Hochschild cohomology as the Jacobi
ring. Nor does this refute a statement that the Hochschild complex is quasi-isomorphic to a Koszul
complex. The missing information is the transferred multiplication law. In the previous chapter
it appears as a ternary operation before taking the cohomology of dξ = 3u2 ; after this second
elimination it appears as the essential six-input operation. These are two stages of one resolution,
not two competing answers.


40.5      Normalization and the invariant assertion
Rescaling the cohomology generator u rescales the displayed coefficient. The number −1/108 belongs
to the normalization W = x3 , x = u + a/2, y = u − a/2, and to the bar signs of this volume. The
invariant assertion is that the corresponding arity-six class is nonzero and cannot be removed by
a strictly unital A∞ equivalence. The exact coefficient is still worth recording: it connects the
polynomial factorization, the two explicit homotopies t, v, and the normalized higher operation
without leaving a sign undetermined.

                 I ′ = (1 + Kb2 )−1 I,      P ′ = P (1 + b2 K)−1 ,         K ′ = K(1 + b2 K)−1 ,

telescoping with its order reversed. Applying P ′ and I ′ to (b1 + b2 )2 = 0 gives b2B = 0.

is also an executable algorithm involving only polynomial division and matrix multiplication. In
positive arity the normalized unit line is preserved by the contraction; the standard endpoint-unit
cancellations make the transferred operations strictly unital.

41.5     A periodic resolution detects the class
Let
                                                                                          n−1

                B e = k[uL , uR ]/(unL , unR ),            a0 = uL − uR ,          s0 =         uL uR   .

The periodic free resolution of the diagonal alternates multiplication by a0 and s0 . Their product
is zero. To prove exactness, the quotient by a0 has dimension n, so the kernel of multiplication

                                                             166
Chapter 41. The monomial center: a uniform essential six-input class


by a0 has dimension n. The ideal generated by s0 is spanned by urL s0 , 0 ≤ r < n: the relation
uL s0 = uR s0 reduces all monomials to these. They are nonzero and independent by their distinct
total degrees. Thus this ideal is exactly that kernel. The kernel of multiplication by s0 has the
complementary dimension n2 − n and contains the ideal generated by a0 , which has that dimension.
Equality follows. The augmentation kernel is the difference ideal. This proves the entire resolution.
  Applying bimodule Hom into B gives alternating maps zero and multiplication by nun−1 .

The interior bar faces telescope and the endpoint faces give s0 times the degree-one generator.
Evaluation by C leaves only j = 0, with value 1. Thus C is the degree-two periodic class. The chain
map shifting the periodic resolution by two places is the identity in every free term; its iterates show
that the powers of this class give the generator in each even degree. This identifies the normalization
of τ 3 in (37.3), not merely a one-dimensional subspace.
  The scalar multiplying ud−3 is nonzero in characteristic zero, and d − 3 = n − 2 < n − 1. It
remains nonzero modulo nun−1 . Hence (37.3) is a nonzero Hochschild class.


41.6      Why lower coordinate changes cannot remove it
Suppose the minimal algebra just constructed were strictly unital A∞ quasi-isomorphic to the binary
algebra B. Its first coefficient is a binary algebra automorphism. Postcomposing with its strict
inverse makes that coefficient the identity. Every element of B is even, so the higher morphism
coefficient Fj , of parity 1 − j, vanishes when j is even.
   The arity-four equation, since m3 = m4 = 0, says that F3 is a Hochschild three-cocycle. The
arity-six equation would then imply

                                        m6 = δF5 ± F3 ⌣ F3 .

The possible global sign depends on unsuspension and is immaterial to the class. Hochschild
cohomology is graded commutative for its cup product. Since [F3 ] has odd Hochschild degree, its
square is zero over a characteristic-zero field. The right side has zero cohomology class, contradicting
(37.3).
    A unital dg formality zigzag induces an equivalence of strictly unital minimal A∞ models over a
field: choose contractions preserving the unit and use the finite normalized tensor transfer. Between
minimal models the first term of a quasi-isomorphism is invertible, and its inverse is solved recursively
in tensor length. Thus the contradiction excludes a dg formality zigzag as well.

Theorem 41.1 (Uniform monomial nonformality). For every d ≥ 3 the monomial diagonal
endomorphism algebra, and hence the underlying E1 algebra of the isolated categorical center of
MF(xd ), is nonformal. Its minimal model has m3 = m4 = m5 = 0 and the nonzero six-input class
(37.3). All later operations and both comparison maps are determined by the explicit finite-on-each-
word transfer above.

                                                    167
Chapter 41. The monomial center: a uniform essential six-input class


   For d = 3 the coefficient is −1/108 and C(u, u) = 1, recovering the earlier four-matrix example
exactly. For d = 4 the class is −uτ 3 /64; for d = 5 it is −u2 τ 3 /50. At d > 3 evaluation on six copies
of u may vanish because some carries vanish. This does not make the cochain class zero: inputs
alternating u and un−1 make all three carries equal to one.
   The local stabilized-diagonal identification is the categorical input. The matrix algebra, contrac-
tion, higher-operation algorithm, periodic certificate and obstruction to removal have all been given
explicitly. A global polynomial potential with several critical values requires the separate support
theorem of Chapter 39.

42.1     The finite centre can retain infinitely many operations
This calculation is retained from the seventh core synthesis, with its complete recurrence and its
normalization. In this chapter the arity variable previously denoted z is called u. Write pf = f (u)v
and qg = g(u)w for the shifted cochains of Chapter 34. These symbols are not new state spaces.
The full cochain centre of Chapter 34 has two complete arity families. In characteristic zero its
cohomology has only d − 1 basis vectors. We now give every operation on that finite space and every
comparison component. This is not only the assertion that a minimal model exists. The recurrence
uses ordinary multiplication, polynomial division by one monomial, and finite ordered partitions. A
special family of its coefficients can then be summed exactly.
   Fix d ≥ 2 over a characteristic-zero field and write

                c = (−1)d ,     P (u) = cud ,      Hd = k[u]/(ud−1 ),    M = d − 1.

The space Hd is entirely even in the two-periodic grading. Each of its elements has the distinguished
polynomial representative of degree less than M . Denote remainder and quotient by remM and
quoM , so that
                                   f = remM (f ) + uM quoM (f ).
For formal series these are defined coefficientwise and are continuous; on polynomial inputs all
expressions below are polynomial.
  On the shifted full Hochschild complex define i(a) = pae, project pf to remM (f ) and qg to zero,
and put
                                        1
                             hC (pf ) = qquoM (f ) ,   hC (qg ) = 0.
                                       cd
Here hC is the suspended homotopy: it obeys

                         B1 hC + hC B1 = 1 − ip,       h2C = hC i = phC = 0,

by the exact formula B1 qg = cd puM g . These identities hold on the entire complete cochain space.
The homotopy lowers arity by the fixed amount M , so it is continuous with the declared arity
topology. It is not defined integrally when d is a nonunit; the integral centre remains the unreduced
two-family model of Chapter 34.

Theorem 42.1 (A full finite-dimensional ordinary centre). The complete strictly unital minimal
cup structure on Hd has suspended coefficients

                           b2m (sa2m , . . . , sa1 ) = pM2m (a2m ,...,a1 ) ,       b2m+1 = 0.

Here ph in the target denotes the suspended copy of the polynomial class h; the same symbol in the
following comparison denotes its full cochain representative. Every component of the comparison
with the complete Hochschild centre is

          f1 (sa) = pae,       f2m (sa2m , . . . , sa1 ) = qG2m (a2m ,...,a1 ) ,      f2m+1 = 0    (m ≥ 1).

These formulas satisfy all A∞ and all comparison equations. They are finite on each list of polynomial
representatives and specify all arities. No assertion is made that all but finitely many of them vanish.

Proof. Apply the simultaneous transfer construction to the explicit contraction above, with first
component i. The root expression at arity n is the sum of original cup operations on the comparison
components of its consecutive blocks. Higher components are −hC of that root expression, and the
transferred operations are its projection. The terms involving an already transferred operation and
a higher comparison component have zero projection and zero image under hC , since every such
comparison component lies in im hC . A term involving the first component is killed by hC i = 0.
Thus the root expression alone gives both recurrences.
   The first component has p output. Every higher comparison component can only have q output
because hC kills the q family. On odd input lengths greater than one, a nonzero original binary cup
pairs a p first component with an even-length q component. Its output is q, so both its projection
and its image under hC vanish. A higher original cup accepts only q inputs, whose comparison
lengths are all even, so it cannot contribute at odd total length. This proves the stated odd vanishing
inductively.
   At length two, the only root term is the product of the two p representatives. It gives (38.1). At
a larger even length, no binary mixed pair can contribute, and every contributing block has even
length and q output. Equation (34.3) gives its coefficient ∂ [k] P times the product of the G’s. This
is exactly (38.2).

                                                           170
Chapter 42. Every minimal monomial centre and an exact root series


  The simultaneous identity BE + Eb = −F b2 , for E = BF − F b, proves both the source and
comparison identities by the same contraction induction as in Theorem 3.3. Its use here requires
no unconstructed primitive: hC is the displayed monomial quotient. A fixed arity has finitely
many partitions, and all divisions and products are finite on polynomial representatives. The first
component represents every cohomology class, hence is a quasi-isomorphism.
   The unit has representative 1. In (38.1) a product with it already has degree less than M , so G2
vanishes. Every larger term partitions the inputs into smaller even blocks. The block containing a
unit has zero G by induction. Thus all higher unit inputs vanish, and M2 has the strict unit. This
proves the unit conditions directly, rather than by a choice of unital rectification.

   For monomial inputs ua1 , . . . , ua2m , every nonzero term of T2m has exponent

block. Negative exponents mean that the coefficient is zero, not that a Laurent monomial has been
introduced. These exponent tests are useful independent checks of every recursion value.

42.4     The cubic exception and the role of a coordinate change
For d = 3, the only nonunit cohomology input is u. The recursion gives
                        1                           1                                   1
             G2 (u, u) = ,       M4 (u, u, u, u) = − u,     M6 (u, u, u, u, u, u) = −      ,
                        3                           3                                   27
with Gn = 0 for every even n ≥ 4 and all other higher Mn zero. The fourth operation is a Hochschild
coboundary. Removing it changes the sixth coefficient: the complete comparison in Chapter 36
gives the corrected value −1/108, not −1/27. Thus the all-monomial recurrence and the finite cubic
normal form are compatible presentations of the same centre, not competing values of an invariant.
The ordinary Lie model remains homotopy abelian, while the cup structure retains its independent
higher operations.




                                                 172
Chapter 43

The zero fiber, the critical locus, and
the actual center

A difference of two potentials forgets an additive constant. A category of matrix factorizations of
a fixed potential does not: its objects live on the zero fiber. This simple distinction changes the
global center theorem. It is important precisely because all the familiar local monomial calculations
remain correct.
   Throughout this chapter k is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, S = k[x], and
W ∈ S is nonconstant. Matrix factorizations and their morphisms are genuinely two-periodic. We
take finite free factorizations, their finite twisted complexes, and their idempotent completion. The
notation MF(W ) refers to this enhanced category, not just to its list of homotopy classes of objects.


43.1     An additive constant is a decisive test
Consider W0 = x3 and W1 = x3 − 1. Their polynomial difference kernels are identical:
                                                         !
                                     0  x2 + xy + y 2

The endomorphism algebra of this factorization has cohomology k[x]/(3x2 ) in even parity and zero
odd cohomology. But MF(x3 − 1) is the zero category, whereas MF(x3 ) is not. Thus this unrestricted
polynomial kernel cannot represent the center of the zero-fiber category for both potentials.
   This is not an objection to the isolated local diagonal theorem. It identifies a missing support
condition when that theorem is used globally. It also shows why an equality of Jacobi rings, or
even an identity between polynomial difference matrices, is not by itself the desired categorical
comparison.

Proposition 43.1 (Squarefree potentials). If W is squarefree, every finite free matrix factorization
is contractible. The enhanced category MF(W ) is zero.

Proof. For a divisor g of a squarefree polynomial, g and W/g are relatively prime. Bezout’s identity
gives the preceding contraction. Sums, shifts, finite cones and retracts of zero objects remain zero.
Smith decomposition therefore proves the assertion on the entire specified enhancement.


43.3      Two homotopies locate the support
For factorizations Q, Q′ of W , the morphism differential is

                                        dF = Q′ F − (−1)|F | F Q.

Left multiplication by Q′ /2 is an odd homotopy for multiplication by W :

                                    d (Q′ /2)F + (Q′ /2)dF = W F.
                                                


Indeed the two terms containing Q′ F Q cancel and the remaining two terms are Q′2 F . Differentiating
Q′2 = W 1 gives Q′ Q′x + Q′x Q′ = W ′ 1. Hence left multiplication by Q′x similarly gives

                                        d(Q′x F ) + Q′x dF = W ′ F.

The cohomology of every morphism complex is therefore annihilated by (W, W ′ ).
   These complexes have finite free terms. Their cohomology is finitely generated over the Noetherian
ring S, so it has finite length and is supported exactly within the finite set of roots of W having
multiplicity at least two. This argument concerns morphism cohomology. It does not assert that
the two displayed homotopies are coherent natural homotopies on the whole center.


43.4      Completion gives a categorical product
Put ti = x − ai and let Sbi = k[[ti ]]. The polynomial-to-formal extension is flat: a torsion-free module
over a principal ideal domain is flat, and Sbi is torsion-free as an S-module. For our finite free
two-periodic morphism complexes, flatness commutes with kernels, images and cohomology. The
finite-length support calculation consequently gives
                                         ∼
                           H Hom(Q, Q′ ) =              H Hom(Q ⊗ Sbi , Q′ ⊗ Sbi ).
                                                 Y

                                                di ≥2

Theorem 43.2 (The zero-fiber decomposition). Extension of scalars induces a quasi-equivalence
                                                 Y                                  
                                MF(W ) ≃                MF k[[ti ]], W (ai + ti ) .
                                                di ≥2

Proof. The displayed map of morphism complexes is a quasi-isomorphism by the support and
flatness argument. This proves quasi-full-faithfulness.
   For essential surjectivity, Smith reduction over the discrete valuation ring k[[ti ]] expresses every
local object as a sum of factorizations with lower-left entry tsi , 0 ≤ s ≤ di . Each such object is
the completion of the global factorization Q(x−ai )s . At a different root aj , this lower-left entry
is a unit, so that completed object is contractible. Thus global objects supply each component
independently. Finite sums give all tuples. Passing to finite twisted complexes and retracts preserves
the quasi-equivalence and includes the prescribed enhancements.

   At a singular root, write W (ai + t) = tdi ui (t) with ui (0) ̸= 0. Choose a di th root of ui (0). The
binomial recursion in characteristic zero constructs ui (t)1/di . The coordinate

                                                 zi = t ui (t)1/di

has invertible linear term and a formal compositional inverse. It sends the potential to zidi . This is
a formal change of coordinate; no global polynomial automorphism is asserted. We obtain

                                                                  MF(zidi ).

43.5      The center of the zero fiber
For an enhanced category C, write Zcat (C) for its Hochschild center, with its derived composition
and higher center operations. We use the ordinary derived Morita identification of this center and,
at an isolated formal singularity, the stabilized-diagonal identification. These are categorical inputs,
not consequences of a cohomology dimension count.
   A finite disjoint union of dg categories has no morphisms between different components. Every
nonzero Hochschild string lies in one component; the Hochschild differential, insertions and cup
operations preserve that component. Thus its center is the product of the component centers,
including the higher operations. Morita invariance and the preceding quasi-equivalence prove

Theorem 43.3 (Zero-fiber center classification). There is an equivalence of centers

                                                                  Zcat MF(zidi ).
                                                           Y
                                    Zcat MF(W ) ≃
                                                          di ≥2

Its cohomology is zero in odd parity, and its even ring is

                                          k[zi ]/(zidi −1 ) ∼
                                                            = k[x]/(W, W ′ ).
                                   Y

                                  di ≥2

The last identification is the natural polynomial action, with local coordinates expressed through their
inverse formal series.



                                                         175
Chapter 43. The zero fiber, the critical locus, and the actual center


Proof. The categorical equivalence and the componentwise Hochschild construction give the first
assertion. The local monomial center computation gives the displayed factors. In one variable,
the ideal (W, W ′ ) is generated by their greatest common divisor, which is a nonzero scalar times
             di −1 . Chinese remainders give exactly these local rings. The formal change of variable has
  i (x − ai )
Q

invertible derivative and hence does not change their lengths or their unital algebra identification.
A simple root contributes the zero factor rather than an extra copy of the field.

   The empty product here denotes the center of the zero category, namely the zero complex. The
statement is specific to one variable. In several variables, replacing a support calculation by the
underived quotient (W, ∂W ) would require another proof.


43.6      What the unrestricted difference kernel computes
Let R = k[x, y], a = x − y, and
                                                                            !!
                              W (x) − W (y)                             0 b

                                  x−y                                   a 0

The pair (a, b) is a regular sequence: a is a nonzerodivisor and b modulo a is the nonzero polynomial
W ′ (x). An even closed matrix is scalar. Boundaries from odd matrices are the scalar ideal (a, b).
An odd cycle ( 0s 0r ) satisfies ar + bs = 0; regularity gives (r, s) = t(−b, a), which is a boundary from
an even diagonal matrix. Therefore

                               H 0 (DW ) = k[x]/(W ′ ),      H 1 (DW ) = 0.

This calculation retains every critical point, independently of its critical value. That is precisely
why it cannot always be the zero-fiber center.
