Theorem 43.4 (The supported diagonal model). The underlying E1 algebra of Zcat MF(W ) is
               (0)
equivalent to DW . Its cohomology is the zero-critical-value summand of the Jacobi ring k[x]/(W ′ ),
rather than that entire ring in general.

Proof. On every completed factor the explicit conjugation identifies the kernel with the isolated
monomial kernel, or the explicit unit homotopy contracts it. The isolated stabilized-diagonal theorem
identifies the former endomorphism algebra with the local categorical center. The finite product and
the categorical decomposition finish the comparison. All algebra multiplications in the kernel model
are composition of matrices. A cohomology idempotent has not been promoted to a chain-level
projector without construction.


43.8      A formality criterion for the whole zero fiber
The essential six-input operation for z d proved in the monomial chapter obstructs E1 formality for
every d ≥ 3. At d = 2 the local center cohomology is just k; its unit map is a quasi-isomorphism of
dg algebras, so that center is formal.
                                                                                         Q
   We need to ensure that a product cannot hide a nonformal component. If A = Ai is formal,
a formality equivalence identifies its regular derived module with that of H(A). The central
idempotent summand ei A is transported to the corresponding retract of the regular H(A)-module.
Since H(A) has zero differential, that retract is represented by a strict idempotent in H(A) and
its endomorphism algebra has zero differential. Derived endomorphisms are preserved under the
equivalence. Hence every Ai = RHomA (ei A, ei A) is formal. This argument uses split idempotents in
the module category; it does not assert a strict idempotent lift along an arbitrary chain-level map.

Corollary 43.5 (Morse criterion for the zero-fiber center). The one-variable zero-fiber center is
formal as an E1 algebra if and only if every singular root of W has multiplicity two. The squarefree
case is the zero category and is included.

Proof. If all relevant roots are double, every nonzero local center is the field and their finite product
is formal. If some root has multiplicity at least three, the essential local six-input class makes that
component nonformal. The product argument excludes formality of the whole center.


43.9      All critical values and an honest global interpretation
Let CritVal(W ) be the finite set of critical values and define
                                                    M
                                  Mall (W ) =                   MF(W − c),
                                                c∈CritVal(W )


                                                    177
Chapter 43. The zero fiber, the critical locus, and the actual center


with zero morphisms between the indicated summands. Every critical point is now a singular point
of exactly one zero fiber. Applying the preceding arguments at all critical points gives

                             HH 0 (Mall (W )) = k[x]/(W ′ ),       HH 1 = 0.

Its underlying center is modeled by the unrestricted DW . To see the last statement, complete
DW at its diagonal critical support. Its finite-length cohomology makes the completion map a
quasi-isomorphism. Each local factor is the local center for the appropriate W − c, by the formal
coordinate and conjugation argument. Thus the entire Jacobi ring has a categorical interpretation,
but it is the interpretation with all critical values retained.
    Adding a constant changes which summand is the zero-fiber summand. It does not change this
all-critical-values model. This explains the opening counterexample without discarding the correct
local computations.


43.10       A family in which the distinction cannot be suppressed
For Ws (x) = x3 − sx, every nonzero scalar fiber has three simple roots, so its zero-fiber category
vanishes. At s = 0 the category is the cubic singularity, with a nonformal center. In contrast,

                                             k[s, x]/(3x2 − s)

is free of rank two over k[s]. It is the family of Jacobi algebras, not a flat family of the zero-fiber
centers just computed.
   This example concerns the stated scalar fibers. It does not identify the center of an unspecified
relative matrix-factorization category, or commute a support completion with arbitrary base change.
Those are further derived comparison problems. Retaining that qualification is necessary: the
support correction should not create a new false base-change theorem.


43.11       Categorical inputs and audit boundary
The elementary parts above are Smith decomposition, explicit homotopies, flat completion of finite
complexes, Chinese remainders, and polynomial division. The identification of a local stabilized
diagonal with a categorical center uses the isolated-singularity matrix-factorization theorem of
Dyckerhoff, Compact generators in categories of matrix factorizations, arXiv:0904.4713. Derived
Morita invariance is used with the displayed quasi-equivalence. No theorem about arbitrary quantum
chiral centers is inferred from these algebraic identifications.

every even dimension

44.1      The geometry and the topology are both specified
Let d = 2s > 0, S = k[x1 , . . . , xd ], and let Yr be the scheme obtained from r ≥ 2 copies of Ad
by identifying their complements of the origin. The double branch at d = 1 was the elementary
collision coefficient. Here the normal dimension is even so that it carries a nondegenerate constant
symplectic matrix Π = (Πij ).
   On each coordinate localization define
                                                              
                                             ℏX
                              f ⋆ g = µ exp       Πij ∂i ⊗ ∂j  (f ⊗ g).
                                             2 i,j

The coefficient algebra is formed first, and only then completed in ℏ. Thus a series may have
pole order increasing with its ℏ-order, while its coefficient at each order is an ordinary localization
element. Every coefficient of the product involves finitely many derivatives. On three factors the
three constant bidifferential operators commute, so both associative composites are multiplication
after the exponential of their sum. This proves associativity to all orders. The unit is one.
   Restrictions commute with all coordinate derivatives and hence with the product. We obtain a
sheaf Aℏ of complete algebras on Yr . Its modules are complete module sheaves whose successive
ℏ-quotients are quasi-coherent on the underlying coefficient charts. Localization is constructed
order by order: a function invertible on a localization chart has a unique star inverse there, solved
recursively from its invertible leading coefficient. Exactness of restriction follows on each associated-
graded layer from ordinary flat localization, and then at finite ℏ-order by induction. In the complete
category the transition maps and inverse limits are part of this construction.
   Let Cℏ = RΓ(Yr , Aℏ ), calculated with a finite coordinate-localization cover and a coherent Cech
model. In a Thom–Whitney presentation the Moyal product is applied to the coordinate coefficients
and the usual wedge product to the auxiliary simplex forms. Those auxiliary forms must also be
included before completing in ℏ.




                                                  179
Chapter 44. Formal quantum normal centres in every even dimension


44.2      The diagonal is constructed before its answer is guessed
Work first modulo ℏN . On a matched pair of affine branch charts put
                                                                op
                                    ui = xL    R
                                          i − xi ∈ AN ⊗k[ℏ]/ℏN AN .
The ui commute: their left commutator is ℏΠij and their right opposite commutator is its negative.
Right multiplication by them therefore defines a length-d free Koszul complex resolving the diagonal
bimodule. Modulo ℏ it is the ordinary regular-diagonal resolution. At finite N , filter by powers
of ℏ; every associated-graded layer is that exact augmented resolution. Finite induction proves
exactness. Passing to the compatible complete finite free complexes gives the completed diagonal
resolution. Exactness in the limit follows because the finite complexes, their contractions in the
local comparison, and the quotient maps are compatible; equivalently the successive finite parameter
layers solve each boundary recursively in the complete free module.
   The construction is restricted to the matched open neighbourhood of the diagonal, as in Chap-
ter ??. Outside that neighbourhood the diagonal is zero. Open pushforward and its full faithfulness,
not an unjustified affineness assertion, transport the calculation to the full product.
   At finite parameter order the free algebra sheaf is a compact generator of its local-module category.
Indeed its reduction is the structure-sheaf generator, and the finite ℏ-filtration detects vanishing. Its
global section functor is computed by the finite localization cover and preserves direct sums. The
usual generator adjunction is therefore an equivalence, proved first on the free generator and then
on its localizing closure. The same argument on the product identifies diagonal endomorphisms with
the relative centre of the global coefficient algebra. For complete objects this statement means the
compatible system of these finite-order comparisons; no algebraic module category of unrestricted
power-series modules is substituted for it.

                                                     X

multiplications has the original, not opposite, Moyal order, because both the right-multiplication
convention and the opposite algebra reverse it. The contraction generators anticommute and square
to zero.
   Postcomposition with the augmentation of the finite free diagonal resolution identifies this

Descent gives the global model

                         Zk[[ℏ]] (Cℏ ) ≃ RΓ(Yr , Ω•Yr /k )[[ℏ]], dCech + ℏdhol , ⋆∧ .                (44.1)
Here the Cech and holomorphic degrees are totalled with the Koszul sign. The latter differential

44.4      A simultaneous contraction for coefficient and holomorphic
          differentials
Let
                                                             kx1−a1 · · · x−a
                                                   M
                                                                             d
                                      M=                                   d .
                                             a1 ,...,ad ≥1

The cohomology of the coefficient Cech complex of Yr is

                                                 S ⊕ (M [−d])r−1 .

This can be obtained without a spectral-sequence collapse guess. Cover the common puncture by
D(xi ). Decompose its localization Cech complex by Laurent monomials. For a monomial, record
the subset of coordinates with negative exponent. If this subset is proper and nonempty, contract
the index complex by inserting or removing a fixed coordinate outside it, with the usual Cech sign.
If it is empty, the surviving class is its regular polynomial. If it is the full set, only the top puncture
class survives. Gluing r branches retains the diagonal regular class and r − 1 differences of these
puncture classes, shifted once by the gluing cone. This proves the displayed degree d, rather than
the unshifted puncture degree d − 1.
   Coordinate differentiation preserves the negative-coordinate subset of each nonzero Laurent
derivative. Hence the index contractions commute with dhol . The same decomposition therefore
calculates the total complex in (??) using the two ordinary de Rham complexes

                            Ω•S [[ℏ]], ℏdhol ,       (Ω•M [[ℏ]], ℏdhol )[−d]⊕(r−1) .

This is a comparison of complexes. Their multiplication is still the multiplication transported from
the actual coefficient model, not an assumed multiplication on S ⊕ M [−d].
   The polynomial de Rham complex contracts onto k in degree zero. In one variable use h(xa dx) =
xa+1 /(a + 1), a ≥ 0, and combine the one-variable contractions by the tensor telescoping formula.
The negative-Laurent de Rham complex contracts onto the single class

                                        (x1 · · · xd )−1 dx1 ∧ · · · ∧ dxd

in degree d. In one variable its derivative is injective and its cokernel is kx−1 dx; the homotopy on
x−a dx, a > 1, is −x−a+1 /(a − 1). The tensor contraction proves the several-variable assertion with
no convergence issue, since each coefficient is a finite Laurent polynomial.

Theorem 44.1 (All degrees of the quantum normal centre). The cohomology of (??), as a k[[ℏ]]-
module, is the direct sum of

                           k[[ℏ]]   in degree 0,             k[[ℏ]]r−1   in degree 2d,

                              BSq ⊗ k[[ℏ]]/(ℏ) ⊕ (BM
                                                   q−d r−1
                                                      )    ⊗ k[[ℏ]]/(ℏ).

                                                        181
Chapter 44. Formal quantum normal centres in every even dimension


There is no torsion of higher parameter order. The free degree-2d classes are the branch differences
of the displayed volume-transgressed principal part.

Proof. The contractions give explicit vector-space decompositions into cohomology and pairs. Apply
them coefficientwise to each formal series; every coefficient remains an algebraic polynomial or
principal part. On a pair the map L[[ℏ]] → B[[ℏ]] is ℏ times a vector-space isomorphism, so its
kernel is zero and its cokernel is B. The cohomology summands have zero differential and remain
free. The coefficient shift adds d to the negative-Laurent degrees. This proves every degree and the
precise annihilator, not only the result after inverting ℏ.

   A bare principal part generally has a nonzero holomorphic derivative and does not lift as a closed
class through the first quantum order. The volume-transgressed class does lift. This is why the
surviving degree is 2d = 4s, not d or d − 1.
   After inverting ℏ, all printed torsion disappears. Choose a closed Cech representative of each
branch residue by the finite monomial contraction above and multiply it by the top holomorphic
volume form. These representatives and the unit form a subalgebra: the product of any two volume
representatives is zero because its holomorphic degree exceeds d. Its inclusion induces precisely the
free cohomology classes. Thus the generic underlying associative centre is formal with algebra

                           k((ℏ))[v1 , . . . , vr−1 ]/(vi vj : i, j),   |vi | = 2d.

This generic formality does not trivialize the formal family, remove its parameter torsion, or identify
this normal model with the full physical bulk of a chiral boundary.

All-state fields and independent
exchange

45.1      The differential-algebra field
For an associative unital algebra B with derivation T , define

                                           Y (a, z)b = (ezT a)b.

Over characteristic zero this is a power series, with vacuum 1 and translation T . The creation
identity is Y (a, z)1 = ezT a. Its associativity is an exact formal identity:

                            Y (a, z0 + z2 )Y (b, z2 )c = (e(z0 +z2 )T a)(ez2 T b)c
                                                       = Y (Y (a, z0 )b, z2 )c.

No commutativity was used. When B is noncommutative, the constant fields need not be local.
This is a nonlocal field algebra, not an ordinary vertex algebra by a change of name. In positive
characteristic, replacing ezT by a truncated exponential does not preserve this argument; one needs
an actual iterative Hasse translation action.


45.2      A full smash-product construction
Let V be a vertex algebra and H an ordinary Hopf algebra acting on V so that it commutes with
translation, sends the vacuum by its counit, and satisfies
                                                  X
                               h · YV (v, z)w =       YV (h(1) v, z)(h(2) w).

Every coproduct is a finite algebraic sum. Define on V ⊗ H
                                                   X
                           Y (v#h, z)(w#k) =           YV (v, z)(h(1) w)#h(2) k.

This is the usual smash-product field construction; we give its all-state verification rather than infer
it from a generator formula.
  The vacuum is 1#1, and translation is TV ⊗ 1. The counit and module-vacuum identities give
both creation and the vacuum field. Lower truncation follows from that of V and the finite coproduct
sum. Translation covariance follows because the H action commutes with TV .

                                                    183
Chapter 45. All-state fields and independent exchange


   For weak associativity, expand the product on a third state u#l. The Hopf module identity and
coassociativity give       X
                              YV (v, z1 )YV (h(1) w, z2 )(h(2) k(1) u)#h(3) k(2) l.
The iterate is             X
                               YV (YV (v, z0 )h(1) w, z2 )(h(2) k(1) u)#h(3) k(2) l.
After z1 = z0 + z2 , weak associativity in V identifies them after multiplying by a sufficiently high
power of z0 + z2 , in the stipulated expansion domain. There are finitely many coproduct terms, so
one exponent works for them all. This proves the identity for arbitrary states, not just generators. It
is a nonlocal vertex-algebra construction of the type used in the programme’s earlier smash-product
repair; no equivalence with an associative Ran algebra has been used.


45.3      A singular, genuinely interacting control
Use the rank-two Heisenberg vertex algebra with isotropic generators J + , J − and cross pairing κ.
Its Fock state space is the polynomial algebra on creation symbols Jr± , r ≥ 0, with
                                                           ±
                                           T Jr± = (r + 1)Jr+1 .

The generating operators are

                               J ± (z) =         Jr± z r + κ         n ∂J ∓ z −n−1 .
                                           X                   X
                                                                         n−1
                                           r≥0                 n≥1

The negative-power part acts locally finitely on each polynomial state. Its commutator with the
creation part gives the derivative of the formal delta function, hence the double-pole cross contraction.
Complete Wick normal ordering of divided derivatives supplies the field of every Fock monomial.
Each finite set of state factors has only finitely many Wick matchings, giving the standard creation,
translation and locality identities. Complete Wick ordering of oscillator monomials must not be
confused with arbitrary iterated normally ordered products, whose associators can contain derivative
terms.
   The charge operator Q with QJr+ = Jr+ and QJr− = −Jr− preserves every contraction and
commutes with translation. It is therefore a vertex derivation. Let H = k[p] with primitive
coproduct and action p = Q. The preceding construction gives a nonlocal algebra whose constant p
operation satisfies
                             Y (1#p, z)(J + #1) = J + #1 + J + #p,
whereas the reverse field has creation term ezT J + #p. Their constant terms differ by J + #1. At the
same time the J + , J − sector retains its double pole. Thus the ordered action interacts with the
singular sector through an actual derivation; it is not a tensor product in which the first factor is
inert.
   The noncommuting constant operation is a defect-type control. It cannot be identified without
comparison with a bulk-local raviolo field whose cohomological product has different commutativity
requirements. Nor can one apply the ordinary tensor bar to the vertex normally ordered product:
the latter is not the associative multiplication used in Chapters 1–2.




                                                       184
Chapter 45. All-state fields and independent exchange


45.4     The full two-constant Wick–Ore family
Work over R = k[τ, η] and put V = Hτ 2 ⊗ R[v], with T v = 0. Let Q be the charge derivation
of the Heisenberg factor. The derivation δ = ηQ + η 2 ∂v preserves every state-field product and
commutes with translation. Let the primitive generator u of R[u] act by δ. The smash-product
theorem applies over R: every coproduct is the finite binomial coproduct and no inverse of either
parameter is required.
   In this normal ordering the generating fields are

                 Ynew (u) = u + ηQ + η 2 ∂v ,    Ynew (v) = v,      Ynew (J ± , z) = J ± (z).

The entire state-field map, not just these generators, was constructed by the smash-product formula.
On its polynomial state module set

                        F = exp(η 2 ∂u ∂v ),     Yold (a, z) = F −1 Ynew (F a, z)F.

The exponential is finite on each polynomial, has the opposite-exponent inverse, fixes the vacuum
and generating states, and commutes with translation. Hence all state-field identities are transported.
The two nonzero elementary commutators with its exponent give

                            F −1 uF = u − η 2 ∂v ,         F −1 vF = v − η 2 ∂u .

The current operators and charge commute with F . Consequently

                Yold (u) = u + ηQ,       Yold (v) = v − η 2 ∂u ,       Yold (J ± , z) = J ± (z).   (45.1)

Thus the original two-constant formulas have a genuine all-state weakly associative realization.
Their commutators are [Y (u), Y (v)] = η 2 and [Y (u), J ± (z)] = ±ηJ ± (z). The constant normal
products are u ∗ v = uv and v ∗ u = uv − η 2 . On a current-neutral pair the charge terms cancel,
while its double-pole contraction remains τ 2 /(z − w)2 .
  This ordering transformation has no separation pole. The separate meromorphic contraction
which changes a regular collision lattice by τ 2 (z − w)−2 is not the same transformation and cannot
be removed without transporting the lattice and coproduct. The constant subalgebra has no
R-augmentation killing u, v, since its commutator would force η 2 = 0. Its vacuum is not an
augmentation. These statements prevent two different comparisons from being conflated.


45.5     Exchange needs its own data
Let V be finite-dimensional and R(u) an invertible exchange operator over a field of meromorphic
coefficients. Relations between distinct spectral labels are

                                A1 (u)A2 (v) = R12 (u − v)A2 (v)A1 (u).

Fix a chamber order of the labels and orient the relations toward it. Rewriting terminates by
the number of inverted labels. Disjoint adjacent exchanges commute. The unique overlapping
three-label ambiguity is equality of

            R12 (u − v)R13 (u − w)R23 (v − w)        and    R23 (v − w)R13 (u − w)R12 (u − v).


                                                     185
Chapter 45. All-state fields and independent exchange


Hence the spectral Yang–Baxter equation supplies confluence. If the relations are imposed in both
directions, the two-label loop also requires R21 (−u)R12 (u) = 1. Under these conditions the ordered
words with distinct labels are a basis: the terminating reductions define a normal-form map, and
the diamond check shows it is independent of choices. This does not address coincident-label limits,
which require separate extension data.
   For Rℏ (u) = 1 − ℏP/u with P the flip, direct multiplication gives

                                        Rℏ (u)Rℏ,21 (−u) = 1 − ℏ2 /u2 .

It is not unitary as written. The formal scalar factor f (u) = (1 − ℏ2 /u2 )−1/2 makes f (u)Rℏ (u)
unitary and does not change Yang–Baxter, since the same scalar product occurs on both sides. The
Yang–Baxter identity follows by expanding the three factors and using the transposition relations
and u − w = (u − v) + (v − w); the verification code checks its full rational matrix identity on a
two-dimensional test space, while the permutation-algebra argument gives the general identity.


45.6       The logarithmic connection
Let operators tij = tji satisfy [tij , tkl ] = 0 for disjoint pairs and [tij , tik + tjk ] = 0 for distinct triples.
The connection                                      X
                                         ∇=d−           tij d log(zi − zj )
                                                    i<j

is flat. The one-forms are closed. In the curvature, disjoint-pair terms vanish by the first relation.
Each three-label contribution is reduced by the second relation to a common commutator times the
Arnold relation among the three logarithmic wedges, which is zero. The Arnold identity itself is the
differential-form version of the elementary partial-fraction identity for three coordinate differences.
   A quadratic Casimir from an invariant bilinear form supplies the infinitesimal braid relations
on tensor representations: invariance says the diagonal Lie action commutes with the two-tensor
Casimir, and expanding this commutator on three factors gives [t12 , t13 + t23 ] = 0. Thus flat
meromorphic transport supplies braid data independently of ordered multiplication.
   The ordered chamber of points on a real interval is contractible. Its E1 multiplication alone
has no braid generator. Interval reversal, reversal of a path of exchange, and Verdier duality are
different operations with different domains. An R-matrix must come from the exchange data just
supplied, not from the existence of an ordered word.

Chapter 46

Formal coordinates, the descended
connection, and normal ordering

46.1     Changing a coordinate is an operation
A Laurent coefficient is not an invariant tensor simply because it occurs in an invariant distribution.
Let X be a smooth characteristic-zero curve. Two coordinates z, w = f (z) at a moving point are
related on their formal discs by
                                       gz (t) = f (z + t) − f (z).
The linear coefficient is a unit. The dependence on the point z is important: replacing gz by the
fixed series f (t) would forget the connection that translates the centre of the disc.
   We make the representation hypotheses explicit. Let w be the Lie algebra of formal vector fields
on a disc, with its usual derivation bracket, and let ρ : w → End(V ) be an action. For infinite series
this means a specified continuous action on a complete filtered space, or an action on a graded
space on which the relevant positive modes are locally finite. Let G = Aut k[[t]] be the group of
point-coordinate changes fixing zero. Assume an actual representation R(g) on V has been supplied,
satisfying
                                      R(g)ρ(v)R(g)−1 = ρ(g∗ v).
Our point-composition convention is g · h = g ◦ h. Its Lie algebra identifies with the negative of
the usual derivation bracket. Accordingly its infinitesimal action is −ρ(v) for v(0) = 0. The usual
derivation representation on functions is an example: R(g)a = a ◦ g −1 .
   An infinitesimal action alone does not automatically exponentiate on every completed vector
space. The existence, convergence, and composition identity of R are hypotheses of the following
descent construction. This prevents an unspecified formal coordinate torsor from being used to
prove its own equivariance.


46.2     The moving-disc identity fixes the sign
Differentiate gz while holding t fixed. Then

                      ġz (t) = f ′ (z + t) − f ′ (z),         ġz ◦ gz−1 = gz∗ ∂t − f ′ (z)∂t .


                                                         187
Chapter 46. Formal coordinates, the descended connection, and normal ordering


The difference on the right vanishes at the origin, so it belongs to the Lie algebra of G, even though
its two summands individually do not. Differentiating the representation and using equivariance
gives
                        (∂z R(gz ))R(gz )−1 = f ′ (z)ρ(∂t ) − R(gz )ρ(∂t )R(gz )−1 .             (46.1)
This identity can also be checked on functions by differentiating inverse substitution. It is the reason
the sign below is a minus sign.
Theorem 46.1 (Coordinate descent with its connection). Glue the local trivial coefficient spaces by
aw = R(gz )az . Their connection is locally

                                         ∇z a = da − dz ρ(∂t )a.

It is independent of the coordinate and flat. Algebraic operations on V which are equivariant for R
and horizontal for this connection descend, together with all equalities or homotopies they satisfy.

Proof. Apply ∂z − f ′ (z)ρ(∂t ) to R(gz )az and use (??). The result is

                                         R(gz )(∂z − ρ(∂t ))az .

Multiplying by dz, with dw = f ′ (z)dz, proves the transition identity. On triple coordinate overlaps,
gzh◦f = gfh(z) ◦ gzf , so the representation identity gives the cocycle condition. The curvature vanishes:
there is one base differential direction and the constant local operator has no z derivative. Horizontal
equivariant multilinear maps commute with the displayed change-of-trivialization maps, hence glue
by ordinary sheaf descent. The same statement applied to a complex of multilinear maps transports
its differential and its homotopies. Thus an entire already constructed operation system descends,
not merely its binary shadow.

   The associated left DX -module can be converted to a right one by tensoring with ωX . This
conversion also changes tensor and duality conventions in the usual way. It does not manufacture
chiral operations from the connection alone: their diagonal localization and pushforward maps must
be given before descent is applied.

For a formal coordinate change with invertible derivative put

calculation is not a construction of that representation for arbitrary c.


46.4      Every finite pole window transforms triangularly
A principal part of order at most N is a class in z −N k[[z]]/k[[z]]. Substitution ze = f (z) sends ze−m
to
                              a−m
                               1 z
                                   −m
                                      (1 + zq(z))−m ,        a1 = f ′ (0).
Modulo regular functions, only its first m terms remain. Consequently the change-of-coordinate
matrix on the pole window is triangular, with diagonal entries a−m 1 . Multiplication by the Jacobian
gives the corresponding matrix for one-forms. The residue of a one-form is invariant: for m ̸= 1,
f ′ (z)f (z)−m is the derivative of f (z)1−m /(1 − m) and has zero residue; for m = 1, it is z −1 plus a
regular series. Linearity proves the assertion for every finite principal part.
   These triangular maps preserve the filtration and its associated normal tensors, but do not
preserve a coordinate-dependent splitting of that filtration. The regular kernel in (??) records
exactly such a correction. A meromorphic conjugation which changes the regular lattice is therefore
a different comparison from an equivariant coordinate change of the filtered distribution.

by the 2−r normalization, and a common field label with N choices. It has two orientations, so it
is the sum of the two directed-cycle contributions N/2 each. Disconnected cycles multiply their
weights. This gives exactly the derangement formula.

same vacuum function for every positive integer N . Both the recursion result and the displayed
expression are polynomials in c with rational-function coefficients in the coordinates. Equality at
infinitely many integers proves the polynomial identity in characteristic zero, hence at every c. The
decomposition by graph connected components is precisely the moment–cumulant decomposition,
so the connected part is the single-cycle sum.

47.3      The complete five- and six-point organization
For five labelled vertices, fix vertex 1 as the start of a cycle and identify reversals. The twelve cycles
are

                           12345,
                           12354,        12435,        12453,      12534,       12543,
                           13245,        13254,        13425,      13524,       14235,        14325.


                                                               191
Chapter 47. Conformal calculations before scalar projections

                    Q                  −2
Each contributes c {i,j} in its cycle zij to the connected function. The remaining part consists of
the ten choices of a pair and its complementary triangle, each with coefficient c2 /2 and its indicated
edge denominator. This is all of W5 .
   For six vertices the connected part has sixty unoriented six-cycles, each weighted c. The
disconnected types are: forty-five pair–four-cycle graphs weighted c2 /2; ten pairs of triangles
weighted c2 ; and fifteen perfect matchings weighted c3 /8. These counts follow by choosing the
pair or partition and then its unoriented cycle, and sum to the derangement decomposition after
orientations are restored. The supplied enumeration code generates every graph without identifying
two inequivalent labelings.
   The formulas are rational in the marked coordinates but polynomial in c. Consequently a proposed
quantity with poles at 5c + 22 = 0 cannot simply be this raw connected five-point function. An
inverse-Gram contraction, a residue integral, a normalization or another projection could introduce
such poles, but the functional and its domain would have to be stated and evaluated. The all-arity
correlation formula does not validate an unspecified projected scalar tower.


47.4      What central-charge reflection proves
The affine scalar c(k) = k dim g/(k + h∨ ) satisfies
                                    c(k) + c(−k − 2h∨ ) = 2 dim g.
This is direct addition of rational functions. For the Virasoro expression c = 13 − 6(t + t−1 ), replacing
t by −t gives a sum 26. For the principal type-A expression
                             cN (t) = 2N 3 − N − 1 − N (N 2 − 1)(t + t−1 ),
                                           PN
the sum is 4N 3 − 2N − 2, also equal to      j=2 2(6j
                                                        2 − 6j + 1) by elementary summation.

   These identities do not identify a reflected-level algebra with an augmentation Koszul dual.
They compare scalars after a parameter transformation. A derived identification requires a map
of the relevant coefficient algebras, their completions and their module or diagonal resolutions.
Ghost-anomaly cancellation, determinant-line curvature and the Euler characteristic of a bar are
also values of different constructions. Equality of their scalar values alone is not a comparison
theorem.


47.5      Two sewing directions require two sets of maps
The associative bar and Hochschild chains describe an interval and a cyclic closure in their own
monoidal category. A conformal-block sewing construction on families of algebraic curves has a
different base, boundary strata and gluing maps. A two-colour graph complex can combine two
constructions only after the coefficient maps for its edge contractions have been supplied.
   The useful elementary lemma is conditional but exact: if every codimension-two contraction
square commutes before orientations, orient the two edge directions by the determinant of their
ordered edge set. The two paths to a codimension-two face then have opposite signs, so their
differentials anticommute. This proves the square-zero total differential for those supplied maps.
Orientation alone does not prove the coefficient square commutes, and a stratification does not assign
the coefficient maps. The distinction prevents a formal two-genus equation from being mistaken for
a constructed sewing theory.

48.1       Fields and the two expansions
A field on a graded vector space V is a series

                                                                 A(n) z −n−1
                                                           X

such that A(n) v = 0 for all sufficiently large n, for each fixed v. No uniform cutoff for all vectors is
assumed. A vertex superalgebra has a vacuum 1, an even translation operator ∂, and a state–field
map Y satisfying the vacuum, translation, and locality identities:

                   Y (1, z) = 1V ,    Y (a, z)1 ∈ a + zV [[z]],              [∂, Y (a, z)] = ∂z Y (a, z),

for some N depending on a, b. The corresponding product and iterate expansions satisfy the vertex
Jacobi identity. The operations a(n) b include all integers n, not only the singular ones.

is not determined by the singular polynomial alone without the additional vertex structure.

48.3     Free fields, with the full state space
For even currents ai with symmetric matrix G, define creation and annihilation modes on the
polynomial jet space by the oscillator relations

                          [ai,m , aj,n ] = mGij δm+n,0 ,             ai,n 1 = 0       (n ≥ 0).

For an odd charged pair b, c, the Clifford mode relations are

                    [b(m) , c(n) ]+ = δm+n,−1 ,            [b(m) , b(n) ]+ = [c(m) , c(n) ]+ = 0.

Negative modes act by exterior multiplication, and nonnegative modes by exterior contraction. For
an even first-order pair β, γ, use the analogous Weyl mode relations, so β(z)γ(w) ∼ (z − w)−1 and
γ(z)β(w) ∼ −(z − w)−1 . The sign in the reverse even first-order OPE is forced by locality.
   These formulas are actual operators on algebraic polynomial–exterior state spaces. Every state
uses finitely many creation modes. An annihilation series therefore has finitely many nonzero
terms on it. Generating fields create their states, satisfy translation, and are mutually local by the

                                                           194
Chapter 48. Full quantum fields: the regular terms matter


displayed scalar commutators. Their derivatives and successively normally ordered products in a
fixed PBW order reconstruct the field of every state.
   For completeness, reconstruction here has a finite algebraic proof. Move the nonnegative modes of
the leftmost generating field through the remaining fields. Each move produces its supercommuted
term plus a specified scalar contraction. A fixed coefficient on a fixed state involves only finitely
many nonzero mode actions. Multiplying by a sufficiently high power of every separation clears
the finitely many contraction poles. The regular expression then has identical product and iterate
expansions, because both use exactly the same mode commutations. Induction on the number
of generating factors proves their Jacobi identities. The vacuum and translation identities follow
from their generator versions. Thus this construction does not infer a vertex algebra from a list of
unrelated OPE coefficients.


48.4     Every Wick matching and every regular Taylor coefficient
Suppose two generating fields A, B have scalar OPE g(z −w)−p . Their derivative pair has contraction
                                                          (p + r + s − 1)!
               (∂ r A)(z)(∂ s B)(w) contraction = (−1)r                    g(z − w)−p−r−s .      (48.4)
                                                              (p − 1)!
For divided derivatives, divide this coefficient by r!s!. This distinction explains why factorial and
binomial normalizations in superficially similar jet formulas can differ.
    For a product of two fully Wick-normal monomials, sum over all partial matchings joining left
and right factors of nonzero scalar pairing. A matched edge contributes (42.4). Its sign is the Koszul
sign of moving its endpoints next to one another and removing the pair, in increasing order of the
left endpoint. Unpaired factors retain their original relative order before the final normal ordering.
For an odd pair, this sign is (−1) to the number of odd intervening factors. Disjoint even scalar
contractions commute, so changing their removal order does not change the answer.
   If a matching has total pole order P , denote its unpaired left and right normal monomials by
L, R. Its contribution to pole order s is
                                         ∂ P −s L
                                    C             R,       P − s ≥ 0,
                                        (P − s)!
where C includes its edge coefficients and Koszul sign. The same formula at s = 0 gives its regular
coefficient. The empty matching contributes only the no-contraction regular product. This is a
finite sum for fixed monomials; no graph integral or large-order approximation is involved.
    The proof is induction on the number of left annihilation factors. The first such factor either
passes all right factors, contributing the unpaired term, or contracts with one of them, contributing
one edge and leaving a smaller problem. Every matching occurs exactly once. Taylor expansion of
the remaining left factors gives the displayed coefficient. This proves the complete formula, including
all regular terms, and not only its singular part.


48.5     A regular product which tests the convention
Let B =: bc : for the odd charged pair. The contraction in : bc : (z)c(w) crosses one odd factor and
equals −c(z)/(z − w). Its regular coefficient is −c′ , so
                                              : Bc := −c′ .

                                                   195
Chapter 48. Full quantum fields: the regular terms matter


Similarly the scalar double contraction of B(z)B(w) is +(z − w)−2 and the regular coefficient gives

                                       : BB :=: b′ c : − : bc′ : .                               (48.5)

In particular the vertex-normal square is not the fully Wick-normal polynomial (bc)2 = 0. Both
expressions occur later, and their notation must be distinguished.
   For a free boson with level G, the field T =: a2 : /(2G) has central charge one when G is invertible.
At a polynomial level where G is not invertible, the raw quadratic field still exists; the normalized
conformal field need not. This is the simplest precursor of the raw critical reduction constructed
below.


48.6      An injective free-field realization for independent checks
For the rank-one affine algebra take a first-order even pair β, γ and an independent current a with

                                           1                         2(k + 2)
                             β(z)γ(w) ∼       ,     a(z)a(w) ∼                .
                                          z−w                        (z − w)2

The currents
                    e = β,      h = −2 : γβ : +a,       f = − : γ 2 β : +kγ ′ + : γa :
satisfy the affine sl2 OPEs. The matching formula proves this directly: h, h has double pole 2k, e, f
has poles k and h, h, e and h, f have coefficients 2e, −2f , and f, f has no pole. These are also the
Wakimoto current formulas; their use here is an explicit calculation, not an inference from their
name.
   The realization of the universal affine vertex algebra is injective. Give β, a polynomial degree
one and γ degree zero, with derivatives preserving degree. Contractions lower this filtration. The
leading current polynomials are

                             E = β,   H = −2γβ + a,         F = −γ 2 β + γa.

Their Jacobian with respect to (β, γ, a) is −a, nonzero. At every finite jet order the Jacobian
of the jet substitution is block triangular with this same invertible-after-localization matrix on
each diagonal block. Thus the leading jet polynomials are algebraically independent. Every finite
PBW relation would have a nonzero leading polynomial relation, a contradiction. The argument
is independent of k and remains valid at k = −2, where a is a commutative current rather than a
nondegenerate Heisenberg current.
   Adding the independent charged ghosts preserves injectivity. The accompanying exact checker
therefore verifies the raw reduction identities in an injective presentation, using every partial super-
Wick matching and the regular Taylor terms. It checks identities polynomial in k, not finitely
sampled levels. This supplements the analytic-free algebraic proofs of the following chapters.

A strict quantum rank-one reduction
map

49.1      The differential and the actual closed generator
Work first over Q[k]. The affine sl2 currents have brackets

                     [hλ h] = 2kλ,   [hλ e] = 2e,   [hλ f ] = −2f,    [eλ f ] = h + kλ.

The brackets e, e and f, f vanish. Adjoin odd ghosts b, c, of ghost degrees −1, 1, with [bλ c] = [cλ b] = 1.
Currents commute with ghosts. Let

                                      Q = Resz : c(z)(e(z) − 1) : .

A zero mode is a vertex derivation by the vertex commutator identity. The current defining Q
has zero bracket with itself, since both the e, e and c, c contractions vanish. Hence Q2 = 0. Direct
contractions give

                  Qb = e − 1,     Qh = −2 : ec :,    Qf =: hc : +kc′ ,    Qe = Qc = 0.

These are identities in the universal complex, not equations imposed only in cohomology.
   Put E = e − 1, B =: bc : and H = h + 2B. Then QH = −2c. Two regular-product identities are
essential:
                               : Bc := −c′ , : cH :=: Hc : +2c′ .
For the first, contract the b in : bc : (z)c(w) across the intervening odd c, obtaining −c(z)/(z − w),
and take its regular coefficient. For the second use quasi-commutativity and [cλ H] = 2c. Thus

                                         Qf =: Hc : +(k + 2)c′ .

It follows that
                                      1          k+1 ′
                              F =f+     : HH : +      H,      QF = 0.                       (49.1)
                                      4            2
Indeed Q(: HH : /4) = −(: cH : + : Hc :)/2 = − : Hc : −c′ , and the derivative term contributes
−(k + 1)c′ . The coefficient k + 1 in this formula cannot be replaced by k + 2: that replacement
leaves the nonzero differential −c′ .

                                                    197
Chapter 49. A strict quantum rank-one reduction map


49.2      A positive filtration, not an unproved quartet count
The new fields E, H, F, b, c and all their derivatives have the freely ordered PBW state basis. Here
is one way to verify the coordinate change. Use an auxiliary triangular filtration with f of degree
three, h, e of degree one, ghosts of degree zero, and derivatives preserving this degree. In that
associated graded, E has leading term e, H has leading term h, and F has leading term f . The
inverse formulas e = E + 1, h = H − 2 : bc : and f = F − : HH : /4 − (k + 1)H ′ /2 recover every
original generator. Successive finite descending triangular degree proves independence as well as
spanning of the new PBW monomials. This filtration is used only to establish the change of PBW
coordinates.
   For the differential use a different, positive filtration:
                   wtfil (E) = wtfil (b) = 1,       wtfil (H) = wtfil (c) = 3,   wtfil (F ) = 7.
Derivatives preserve filtration degree. These are not conformal weights. Every singular bracket of
two of the new generators has smaller filtration degree than the sum of their degrees. The potentially
nontrivial checks can be displayed explicitly:
   [Hλ H] = 2(k + 2)λ,                                                                     [Hλ E] = 2(E + 1),
    [Hλ b] = 2b,                                                                            [Hλ c] = −2c,
                                                ′
   [Eλ F ] = − : EH : −2 : bc : −(k + 2)E − kλE,
    [bλ F ] = − : bH : −(k + 2)b′ − kλb,
    [cλ F ] =: Hc : +(k + 2)(λ + ∂)c,
   [Hλ F ] = −2F + 12 : HH : +(k + 1)H ′ + (k + 2)λH + (k + 1)(k + 2)λ2 .
The bracket b, c is scalar, the brackets among E, b, c otherwise vanish, and reverse brackets follow by
skew-symmetry without increasing filtration. For F, F , expand (43.1). The subalgebra generated by
f, H has [Hλ f ] = −2f , [Hλ H] = 2(k + 2)λ and [fλ f ] = 0. Wick expansion shows that every term of
[Fλ F ] is a linear combination of derivatives of f H, f, H 2 , H and scalars. Its filtration degree is at
most ten, strictly below fourteen. Substitution of f = F − : HH : /4 − (k + 1)H ′ /2 does not raise
that bound.
   All displayed identities follow from the noncommutative Wick formula. As an illustrative
calculation,
                             [Eλ 41 : HH :] = − 12 (: eH : + : He :) + λe,
and : He : − : eH := 2e′ . Adding [eλ f ] = h + kλ and the derivative contribution −(k + 1)(λ + ∂)e
gives the displayed E, F bracket. The b, F and c, F equations are obtained by replacing [eλ H] = −2e
by [bλ H] = −2b and [cλ H] = 2c, respectively. The scalar terms in H, F come directly from [Hλ H].
   Counting quartet letters alone would not prove this property. In particular [cλ F ] contains : Hc :,
which has more quartet letters than c. The positive weighted filtration above is the replacement
that makes every reordering correction strictly smaller.


49.3      The entire ghost cohomology and its finite contraction
Order the new PBW fields and their derivatives once and for all. On the commutative exterior–
polynomial vector space of these monomials, the leading differential is
              Q0 b(r) = E (r) ,    Q0 H (r) = −2c(r) ,           Q0 E (r) = Q0 c(r) = Q0 F (r) = 0.

                                                         198
Chapter 49. A strict quantum rank-one reduction map


The actual differential is Q = Q0 + δ, where δ strictly lowers the positive filtration degree: it consists
precisely of the smaller reordering corrections just bounded. Define N to count letters in E, b, H, c,
including derivatives, and set
                                        1 X (r) ∂       1      ∂
                                                                          
                                 h0 =         b    (r)
                                                       − H (r) (r)
                                        N r≥0   ∂E      2     ∂c

on N > 0, with h0 = 0 at N = 0. Exterior derivatives are left derivatives. The numerator
anticommutes with Q0 to N , so
                               Q0 h0 + h0 Q0 = 1 − i0 p0 .
Here p0 kills quartet variables and i0 includes the fixed PBW words in F alone. The homotopy
preserves the positive weighted filtration and is locally finite on every state.
   Every series in

                  h = h0 (1 + δh0 )−1 ,        p = p0 (1 + δh0 )−1 ,    i = (1 + h0 δ)−1 i0

is finite on a state, because δ strictly lowers its nonnegative integral filtration degree. The perturba-
tion identities give Qh + hQ = 1 − ip and pi = 1. In fact i = i0 : products of the closed field F are
closed, so δi0 = 0. The transferred differential is zero, either for this reason or because all target
states have ghost degree zero.

Theorem 49.1 (Complete universal reduction cohomology). Over every commutative Q[k]-algebra,
the full universal reduction complex has zero cohomology in nonzero ghost degrees. Its degree-zero
cohomology is freely strongly generated by the class of F . The displayed polynomial-division and
finite-perturbation formulas give a contraction of the underlying complexes. No bound on the ghost
degrees of the original complex is assumed.

Proof. The contraction proves exactness outside the F -subspace and identifies that subspace with
all cohomology. The PBW coordinate argument proves that its ordered words are independent. The
only denominators used are positive integers and 2, so the construction is valid over the stated bases.
Each computation is finite in the positive filtration, even though derivatives and ghost degrees have
no global finite cutoff.


49.4      The stress tensor with its exact normal-ordering correction
At k + 2 invertible, set
                                                              S
           S = 21 : hh : + : ef : + : f e :,        T0 =            ,   TDS = T0 + 12 h′ + : b′ c : .

The current calculation determining the normalization is brief enough to include. Applying Wick in
the second input gives
                               [eλ 21 : hh :] = − : eh : − : he : +2λe,
                      [eλ : ef :] =: eh : +kλe,        [eλ : f e :] =: he : +kλe + 2λe.
Thus [eλ S] = 2(k + 2)λe. The h calculation has three respective contributions 2kλh, 2λh + kλ2 ,
and 2λh − kλ2 , giving the same coefficient; the f calculation follows by the current involution.
Skew-symmetry yields [Sλ J] = 2(k + 2)(∂ + λ)J for each current.

                                                      199
Chapter 49. A strict quantum rank-one reduction map


   Apply Wick once more to S, S. The leading product terms give 2(k + 2)(∂ + 2λ)S. The three
integral Wick corrections have central coefficients k(k + 2)λ3 /3 each; the two nonscalar λ2 h terms
cancel. Hence
                             [Sλ S] = 2(k + 2)(∂ + 2λ)S + k(k + 2)λ3 .
It follows that T0 has central charge 3k/(k + 2). The cross term with h′ /2 gives (∂ + 2λ)h′ /2, while

                                               [h′λ h′ ] = −2kλ3

subtracts 6k from the central charge. The ghost field : b′ c : has central charge −2, as follows from
its two fermionic contractions. Consequently

                                                     c(k) 3                     6(k + 1)2

   Closedness is fixed by an exact identity, rather than by naming this field:

                                        (k + 2)TDS − F = Q(: bf :).                                       (49.3)

To verify it, use : f e :=: ef : −h′ , : BB :=: b′ c : − : bc′ :, and B ′ =: b′ c : + : bc′ :. Expanding the left
side gives
                                          : Ef : − : hB : −k : bc′ :,
which is exactly Q(: bf :) =: Ef : − : b(: hc : +kc′ ) :, since currents commute with ghosts. This also
proves QTDS = 0.

Theorem 49.2 (A strict quantum vertex rectification). For every characteristic-zero level k ̸= −2,
sending the universal Virasoro generator to TDS defines a strict quasi-isomorphism of differential
graded vertex algebras
                                         Virc(k) −→ Kk .
The map preserves the vacuum and translation and is valid at special noncritical central charges as
well as at generic ones.

Proof. The exact OPE (43.2) supplies the vertex map from the universal Virasoro algebra. Equation
(43.3) identifies its cohomological generator with F/(k+2). Theorem 43.1 identifies every cohomology
state with a unique PBW word in the derivatives of F , and the corresponding universal Virasoro
PBW words map bijectively to them. This proves the quasi-isomorphism in all degrees. It is a map
to the actual reduction complex, not a formal arrow to its cohomology quotient.


49.5      The critical value and what is not divided out
The cohomology class of the raw field F has bracket

                                                         (k + 2)(−6k 2 − 11k − 4) 3
                        [Fλ F ] = (k + 2)(∂ + 2λ)F +                             λ .
                                                                   12
Indeed this follows after inverting k + 2 from (43.3) and (43.2). Both sides lie in the parameter-free
PBW cohomology module over Q[k], which is torsion-free by the explicit contraction. Equality after
localization therefore proves the polynomial identity before localization. At k = −2 it specializes to
zero, so the raw critical cohomology is a commutative differential vertex algebra.

                                                      200
Chapter 49. A strict quantum rank-one reduction map


   The stress-tensor formula itself does not extend through this pole. However, concentration
of cohomology in integer ghost degree zero gives a canonical zigzag of strict dg vertex quasi-
isomorphisms, including at the critical level, by Theorem 46.2. A strict section and a formality
zigzag are different requirements. The former is not supplied at the critical level by this formula;
the latter is supplied without dividing by k + 2.

The raw quantum reduction closes
before cohomology

50.1     The critical question has a stronger answer
The preceding chapter computed the bracket of the raw field F on cohomology by localizing the level
and using the genuine stress tensor. That argument was sufficient for the cohomological identity. It
left open whether the same raw representative closes on chains, and hence whether a strict vertex
map survives the critical level. We now calculate the bracket without taking cohomology. It does
close. This gives a polynomial, parameter-uniform strict quantum comparison, stronger than an
abstract formality zigzag.
   Work over R = Q[k] in the actual universal reduction complex. Put
                                                                k+1
             H = h + 2 : bc :,     S = 14 : HH :,          a=       ,          F = f + S + aH ′ .
                                                                 2
We have already proved QF = 0. The fields H, f generate a particularly small vertex subalgebra:

                  [Hλ H] = 2(k + 2)λ,     [Hλ f ] = −2f,        [fλ H] = 2f,     [fλ f ] = 0.       (50.1)

The ghost current contributes its nonzero double contraction to the first identity. It is this +2 which
must be retained. The mixed identities hold because affine currents and charged ghosts have zero
mutual contractions.


50.2     All mixed terms in the raw bracket
The noncommutative Wick formula gives
                                                                    Z λ
                          [fλ : HH :] = 2 : f H : +2 : Hf : +             [2fµ H]dµ
                                                                     0
                                      = 2 : f H : +2 : Hf : +4λf.

                                                     Z 0
                                 : f H : − : Hf :=         [fλ H]dλ = 2f ′ .
                                                      −∂

                                                 202
Chapter 50. The raw quantum reduction closes before cohomology


Consequently
                                        [fλ S] =: Hf : +f ′ + λf.
Skew-symmetry, with −λ − ∂ applied to the coefficient on its right, gives
                                            [Sλ f ] = − : Hf : +λf.
Their sum is (∂ + 2λ)f . Sesquilinearity likewise gives
                                   [fλ aH ′ ] + [aHλ′ f ] = 2a(∂ + 2λ)f.
Since 1 + 2a = k + 2, all terms involving f contribute exactly
                                              (k + 2)(∂ + 2λ)f.                                    (50.2)
There is no discarded BRST boundary in this calculation.

Temporarily write K = 2(k + 2). From [Hλ H] = Kλ, one contraction gives
                      [Hλ : HH :] = 2KλH,             [: HH :λ H] = 2K(∂ + λ)H.

                                            K               K2 3

Theorem 50.1 (A raw Virasoro identity on chains). In the universal dg vertex algebra KR , before

                      (k + 2)2            (k + 1)2   (k + 2)(−6k 2 − 11k − 4)
                               − 2(k + 2)          =                          .
                         12                  4                 12
The list includes the f, f term, which is zero, both mixed f, S + aH ′ terms, and the complete square
of S + aH ′ . It exhausts the bilinear expansion of [Fλ F ].
