50.4     A strict family over the polynomial level line
        raw to be the universal vertex algebra with one even generating state F and bracket (44.3),
Define VR
with F replaced by F. The vacuum is fixed and ∂1 = 0. This is a Lie conformal presentation: for a
bracket
                                    [vλ v] = α(∂ + 2λ)v + βλ3 1
with scalar α, β, sesquilinearity and skew-symmetry are immediate, and its generator Jacobi identity
is the usual Witt identity multiplied by α2 and its scalar cocycle identity multiplied by αβ. Direct
polynomial expansion proves both. No division by α is necessary.
   The universal mode construction and its ordered PBW basis consequently work over R itself.
Alternatively construct the induced vacuum of this central Lie conformal mode algebra and use
PBW before imposing the central vacuum scalar. Its state basis consists of ordered derivatives
of F. At k = −2 its generating singular bracket is zero, so it specializes to the free commutative
differential vertex algebra on F.
Theorem 50.2 (Strict quantum rank-one reduction at every level). There is a strict quasi-
isomorphism of unital dg vertex algebras over Q[k],
                                     raw
                                    VR   −→ KR ,          F 7−→ F.

It remains a quasi-isomorphism after every scalar extension R → S. In particular, at critical level,

                                      k[F, F′ , F′′ , . . .] −→ K−2                            (50.4)

is a strict differential graded vertex quasi-isomorphism. The source has zero differential and zero
singular products, but retains its commutative regular product and translation.

Proof. Closedness of F and Theorem 44.1 give the strict vertex map. The complete quartet
contraction of Theorem 43.1 proves, over S itself, that all cohomology is freely represented by the
ordered derivatives and normal products of this very F . PBW independence on the source and the
stated cohomology basis on the target show that the map is an isomorphism on all cohomology
groups. This proves the assertion without appealing to flatness of S over R. Specializing to k = −2
gives (44.4).

   This is a strict map from a constructed raw vertex family, not merely an equality between
the characters of two families. It corrects the previous edition’s statement that the critical strict
comparison had not been supplied. It does not turn the divergent expression F/(k + 2) into a critical
conformal vector.


50.5     Comparing the two noncritical stress-tensor maps
After inverting k + 2, F/(k + 2) is a Virasoro generator with central charge 1 − 6(k + 1)2 /(k + 2).
Thus there are two explicitly constructed strict maps to KR [(k + 2)−1 ]: one sends the generator to
F/(k + 2), and the other to TDS . Their difference is

                               TDS − F/(k + 2) = Q(: bf :)/(k + 2).

This does not assert that a linear interpolation is a vertex map. Both maps factor through the
good truncation in Chapter 46, and their composites with its cohomology quotient agree. Since that

                                                  204
Chapter 50. The raw quantum reduction closes before cohomology


quotient is a quasi-isomorphism, the two maps agree in the localized category of dg vertex algebras.
A particular strict cylinder homotopy would be additional data; the displayed exact difference is not
relabelled as one.


50.6     The two-parameter version
Use Rees current and ghost brackets, with every generating bracket multiplied by ℏ, retaining κ in
the affine central coefficient. Put
                                                                        ′
                         H = h + 2 : bc :,      F = f + 41 : HH : + κ+ℏ
                                                                     2 H .

Now [Hλ H] = 2ℏ(κ + 2ℏ)λ and [Hλ f ] = −2ℏf . The regular reordering identity is : f H : − : Hf :=
2ℏf ′ . Repeating the calculation gives, on chains,

                           [Fλ F ] = ℏ(κ + 2ℏ)(∂ + 2λ)F
                                         ℏ(κ + 2ℏ)(−6κ2 − 11κℏ − 4ℏ2 ) 3
                                     +                                λ .
                                                      12
The corresponding raw universal vertex algebra maps strictly to the Rees reduction complex. The
same positive-filtration quartet proof works over every Q[κ, ℏ]-algebra, since the generator equations
remain Qb = E, QH = −2c, QF = 0; the needed denominators are positive integers, not κ or ℏ. It
therefore gives the same uniform strict comparison before and after formal completion. The zero
mode defining Q is divisible by ℏ in the free PBW algebra, so this last assertion does not require ℏ
to be invertible.
   At κ = −2ℏ the raw singular bracket vanishes. At ℏ = 0 the singular bracket divided by ℏ is
the classical one. At a numerical specialization ℏ = 1 of this polynomial presentation one obtains
the ordinary quantum family. This permitted polynomial specialization must not be confused with
evaluating an arbitrary formal ℏ-adic series at 1.


50.7     What critical specialization does not manufacture
The state cohomology of this raw polynomial family is free, and its critical fiber is the commutative
differential algebra explicitly embedded above. There is no parameter torsion in that cohomology
from which an additional noncommutative or logarithmic boundary could be recovered by this
specialization alone. A different integral form, a singular quotient, a distinguished lattice, or
additional modules can define another problem. None of those data follows from dividing a stress
tensor by k + 2 and then ignoring the pole.




                                                 205
Chapter 51

The complete graded deformation
problem at the critical rank-one fiber

51.1     A strict critical comparison does not classify its deformations
The raw quantum reduction of the preceding chapter has a strict commutative vertex model at
k = −2. That is a formality statement about one differential graded vertex algebra. It does not
imply that changing the affine level gives every deformation of that commutative vertex algebra.
  We can solve the latter problem completely in a precise, useful class. Preserve the external
nonnegative weight grading, the vacuum, the translation, and the PBW state space freely generated
by one even field of weight two. The classification includes its nonlinear coordinate equivalences
and automorphisms. It does not assert a classification after dropping the grading or imposing an
additional null-vector ideal.
   The word external matters. A weight grading on the state space need not be generated by a
stress tensor inside the algebra. At the commutative critical point the weight-two generator has
no singular self-product and is not a conformal vector. Calling it one would incorrectly impose a
nonzero Witt term from the start.


51.2     Weight and skew-symmetry leave exactly two coefficients
Let S be a commutative k-algebra, with every element of S in weight zero. Consider a unital vertex
algebra V over S, freely strongly generated by an even field F of weight two, with ∂ raising weight
by one. Its ordered normally generated PBW states are a basis and its weights are nonnegative. In
particular
                            V0 = S1, V1 = 0, V2 = SF, V3 = S∂F.
The nth singular product F(n) F has weight 3 − n. Therefore
                                   [Fλ F ] = u∂F + vλF + bλ3 1
for uniquely determined u, v, b ∈ S. There is no weight-one term at λ2 and no negative-weight term
above λ3 .
  Skew-symmetry says [Fλ F ] = −[F−λ−∂ F ]. Its right side is
                                    (v − u)∂F + vλF + bλ3 1,

                                               206
Chapter 51. The complete graded deformation problem at the critical rank-one fiber


since ∂1 = 0. Consequently v = 2u. Put a = u. Every possible bracket is

                                        [Fλ F ] = a(∂ + 2λ)F + bλ3 1.                             (51.1)

This derivation has not assumed that a or b is invertible, or that S is reduced.
   Jacobi places no additional condition on a, b. Its coefficient of a2 F is the polynomial identity

                           (D + λ + 2µ)(D + 2λ) − (D + µ + 2λ)(D + 2µ)
                                           = (λ − µ)(D + 2λ + 2µ),

and its coefficient of ab1 is

                                (λ + 2µ)λ3 − (µ + 2λ)µ3 = (λ − µ)(λ + µ)3 .

Here D records translation on the field coefficient and acts as zero on a scalar. These are the
complete generator Jacobi equations. Sesquilinearity then extends them to all translated generators.


51.3      Constructing the universal family, including its regular prod-
          ucts
Work first over S0 = k[a, b]. Define a Lie algebra with S0 -basis Ln , n ∈ Z, and a central vector K,
by
                         [Lm , Ln ] = a(m − n)Lm+n + b(m3 − m)δm+n,0 K.                         (51.2)
The noncentral Jacobi equation is the Witt polynomial identity. In the central part m + n + ℓ = 0;
substitution of ℓ = −m − n makes the cyclic sum zero. Equivalently it is the scalar polynomial
identity just proved. Thus this is an actual Lie algebra over S0 .
   The submodule generated by K and all Ln with n ≥ −1 is a Lie subalgebra. Let it act on the
vacuum line by K1 = 1 and Ln 1 = 0. This is a representation: when m + n = 0 and both indices
are at least −1, the possible m are −1, 0, 1, where m3 − m = 0. Induce this module to the whole
mode algebra. PBW gives the basis

                                  L−n1 · · · L−ns 1,         n1 ≥ · · · ≥ ns ≥ 2.                 (51.3)

The PBW proof works over S0 : all its free Lie basis elements have a fixed total ordering, each word
has finite length, and the overlap calculation is exactly Jacobi. It does not require division by a or b.
   Define translation on the Lie algebra by

                                   DLn = −(n + 1)Ln−1 ,               DK = 0.

Direct substitution in (45.2) verifies the derivation rule. It preserves the vacuum relations and gives
an operator ∂ on the induced state space with ∂1 = 0. The generating field is

                                                             Ln z −n−2 .
                                                       X
                                            F (z) =
                                                       n∈Z

On every state its sufficiently positive modes vanish: commute such a mode successively to the right
through a finite word in (45.3); choose its index larger than the sum of that word’s creation indices.
All resulting modes still kill the vacuum and no scalar contraction is possible. Thus F (z) is a field.
It obeys translation covariance and the creation property.

                                                       207
Chapter 51. The complete graded deformation problem at the critical rank-one fiber


   The mode commutator is equivalent to
                                                                          3
                  [F (z), F (w)] = a∂F (w)δ(z, w) + 2aF (w)∂w δ(z, w) + b∂w δ(z, w).

Multiplication by (z −w)4 kills every term. The generating field is therefore mutually local with itself.
The vacuum-and-local-fields reconstruction theorem, used with its proof mechanism in the vertex
chapter, supplies the vertex algebra V (a, b). Concretely all its fields are obtained by derivatives and
iterated normal products of F (z) in the PBW order. Equivalently one repeatedly uses
                                             1
                        Y (u(−n−1) v, z) =     : ∂ n Y (u, z)Y (v, z) :,       n ≥ 0.
                                             n! z
This formula with the generator, the mode commutators, and the ordered basis determines every
regular product and every singular product. Finitely many mode commutations contribute to a
fixed state coefficient. Their coefficients are polynomials in a, b, so the construction base-changes to
every S and every pair a, b ∈ S.
Theorem 51.1 (The universal two-parameter family). For every commutative characteristic-zero
coefficient algebra S and a, b ∈ S, V (a, b) is a freely strongly generated graded vertex algebra with
generator of weight two and bracket (45.1). Every freely strongly generated graded vertex algebra
with that bracket is uniquely isomorphic to it by the map sending the named generator to the named
generator.

Proof. The mode construction proves existence, its PBW basis, and the generator bracket. For any
other such algebra, its modes satisfy (45.2) and its vacuum satisfies the inducing relations. Thus
the induced-module universal property gives a map from the constructed vacuum module. The field
recursion shows it preserves every vertex product, not only the generator bracket. It maps the PBW
basis bijectively onto the other algebra’s PBW basis, so it is an isomorphism. Uniqueness follows
since every state is built from the generator and vacuum by those products and translation.

  At a = b = 0, the modes commute, the field has no singular self-product, and the family is the
commutative differential vertex algebra

                                      V (0, 0) = k[F, F ′ , F ′′ , . . .].

At a = 0 and b ̸= 0 the sole singular product is central of order four in the OPE, or bλ3 in
the bracket. This family is allowed even though F is not a conformal vector. An independent
realization is the differential-field subalgebra generated by F = J ′ in the universal Heisenberg
algebra with [Jλ J] = −bλ: sesquilinearity gives [Jλ′ J ′ ] = bλ3 . Its derivative-current PBW states
remain independent, including over nonreduced bases. At a invertible, T = F/a has
                                                        b 3
                             [Tλ T ] = (∂ + 2λ)T +        λ ,         c = 12b/a2 .
                                                       a2
Thus the usual one-parameter Virasoro description is the chart a ̸= 0 of the two-parameter raw
description.


51.4      Every graded isomorphism and every automorphism
A vacuum- and translation-preserving graded isomorphism

                                          V (a, b) −→ V (a′ , b′ )

                                                     208
Chapter 51. The complete graded deformation problem at the critical rank-one fiber


must send F to sF ′ for a unit s ∈ S × : the weight-two module has rank one. Comparing the two
sides of the bracket identity gives
                                        a = sa′ ,   b = s2 b′ .                          (51.4)
Conversely these equations give an isomorphism by Theorem 45.1, with inverse scalar s−1 . There
are no additional graded coordinate changes hiding in higher weights, because the generator is
already determined in weight two.
   For free, trivialized weight-two lines, the global groupoid is therefore the action groupoid with
weights (1, 2) on the pair (a, b). Its stackification permits nontrivial line bundles and is the quotient by
Gm . The local formal deformation problem below uses trivial lines and identity-on-the-special-fiber
maps, so no assertion about arbitrary global line-bundle trivializations is needed.
   Let S now be a local Artinian k-algebra with maximal ideal m. A marked deformation of V (0, 0)
has a, b ∈ m and isomorphisms (45.4) with s ∈ 1 + m. For a fixed pair (a, b) its automorphism group
is                                                        
                                      1 + m ∩ AnnS (a, b) .                                   (51.5)
Indeed (s − 1)a = 0 and (s2 − 1)b = 0 are the stabilizer equations; s + 1 is a unit since its residue is
2, so the second equation is equivalent to (s − 1)b = 0.

Theorem 51.2 (The full marked graded critical deformation groupoid). For each local Artinian
characteristic-zero base S, the marked, vacuum-preserving, translation-preserving graded PBW
deformations of V (0, 0) form the groupoid of pairs (a, b) ∈ m × m with morphisms (45.4), s ∈ 1 + m.
The universal family over k[[a, b]] is versal and tangent-space minimal in this category. The tangent
space is two-dimensional and the automorphisms are exactly (45.5). The same description holds
coefficientwise over complete local bases.

Proof. Choose a lift of the weight-two special-fiber generator. Each weight space is finite free with
the stipulated PBW ranks. Its ordered normally generated monomials reduce to the special-fiber
basis, hence are a basis by the finite free Nakayama argument. For a complete base, invert the
corresponding matrix modulo each power of the maximal ideal; the inverse stabilizes coefficientwise.
The lifted field is therefore a free strong generator.
   Weight and skew-symmetry force its bracket to have the form (45.1), with a, b ∈ m. The universal-
family theorem constructs a unique isomorphism for that chosen lift. Every other lift relevant to a
graded isomorphism differs by the scalar described above, and the complete isomorphism calculation
gives the groupoid.
   For S = k[ϵ]/(ϵ2 ) write a = Aϵ, b = Bϵ and s = 1 + uϵ. Multiplication by s and s2 changes
neither coefficient, so the tangent is the entire two-dimensional space (A, B). Every pair lifts
across a surjection of local Artinian bases by lifting its two coefficients; a unit s lifts as a unit as
well. This proves versality and its tangent-space minimality, with stabilizers retained rather than
suppressing them in a false fine-moduli assertion. Passing through the parameter quotients proves
the complete-base statement.

   The statement deliberately fixes the external grading. Without it, arbitrary differential-polynomial
coordinate changes and additional terms of different weights may occur, and the classification proved
here would not address them. It also concerns universal PBW objects, not their simple quotients at
special central charge.




                                                    209
Chapter 51. The complete graded deformation problem at the critical rank-one fiber


51.5     The raw Drinfeld–Sokolov curve is not the full critical defor-
         mation
Put t = k + 2 in the strict raw reduction theorem. Its parameters in (45.1) are

                                           t(−6t2 + 13t − 6)    t 13   1
                    a(t) = t,     b(t) =                     = − + t2 − t3 .                    (51.6)
                                                  12            2 12   2
Thus its tangent at the critical point is the line (A, B) = (A, −A/2) in the two-dimensional tangent
space of Theorem 45.2.
   A concrete deformation outside that line is

                                  [Fλ F ] = ϵλ3 1,         S = k[ϵ]/(ϵ2 ).

It exists by the universal-family theorem and has (a, b) = (0, ϵ). If it came from a changed affine
level and an allowed graded isomorphism, (45.4) would give 0 = s t for a unit s, hence t = 0, forcing
b = 0. This contradicts b = ϵ. The example is not a numerical dimension guess; it is an explicitly
constructed vertex algebra and a complete obstruction to the proposed family equivalence.

Corollary 51.3 (Critical family versus regular chart). The one-parameter raw affine-level family is
not versal for graded PBW deformations of its commutative critical fiber. A complete replacement
is the two-parameter family V (a, b) with its scaling groupoid. On the regular chart a ̸= 0, the
normalized invariant 12b/a2 recovers the usual one-parameter Virasoro description.

   This does not withdraw the earlier theorem of formal quantum versality at a nonzero classical
parameter. That theorem explicitly excludes its singular parameter and uses a different regular
normalization. In the two-parameter quantum notation of the raw reduction, the critical line
is κ + 2ℏ = 0; it has classical limit κ = 0. It is outside the regular hypothesis κ0 ̸= 0. The
present theorem supplies a definite critical replacement in the graded rank-one category rather than
extending a regular proof through a forbidden division.


51.6     What the critical and center calculations say together
The raw reduction can be strictly formal at a critical fiber while its neighboring deformation problem
has more directions than the level curve records. Separately, a family of associative centers can be
nonformal even when its individual fibers are formal. Neither phenomenon is diagnosed by counting
the strong generators of a single fiber.
   The positive answer in both cases retains more data: here the two coefficients and their full
automorphism groupoid; in Chapters 21–23, the parameter class, the torsion class, their essential
ternary operation, and the complete transferred higher model. These are comparisons in explicitly
different categories. Their eventual role in a native mixed theorem must be established by actual
functors preserving the stated structures.




                                                     210
Chapter 52

Ghost-degree rectification, including
the critical level

52.1     The elementary construction which a strict section misses
A closed set of generators may fail to define a strict map from the cohomology algebra. That
failure does not, by itself, obstruct formality. When all cohomology lies in one integer degree, there
is a canonical intermediate algebra which supplies the missing zigzag. No choice of transferred
representatives is needed.
   This observation applies to vertex algebras because every vertex product has ghost degree zero.
It does not apply to the two-periodic matrix-factorization centers of Part II: even parity is not
concentration in integer degree zero. Distinguishing these two grading statements removes a false
conflict between critical quantum formality and the monomial six-input obstruction.


52.2     The good truncation preserves all vertex products
Let (A, d) be a unital differential graded vertex algebra, genuinely graded by Z, with |d| = 1. The
vacuum has degree zero, translation has degree zero, and

                     |a(n) b| = |a| + |b|,      d(a(n) b) = (da)(n) b + (−1)|a| a(n) db.

Define a subcomplex A≤0 by
                                     
                                       j
                                     A ,                                j < 0,
                                     
                                     
                            Aj≤0 = Z 0 (A) = ker(d : A0 → A1 ),          j = 0,
                                    
                                     0,                                 j > 0.

The differential from degree minus one lands in Z 0 (A) because d2 = 0.
Lemma 52.1 (Closure before taking cohomology). A≤0 is a unital dg vertex subalgebra. Its map

                                             π : A≤0 −→ H 0 (A)

which is the cohomology projection in degree zero and zero in negative degree is a strict dg vertex
map, with the ordinary induced vertex structure on H 0 (A).

                                                     211
Chapter 52. Ghost-degree rectification, including the critical level


Proof. For homogeneous inputs of degrees i, j ≤ 0, their product has degree i + j. If i + j < 0, it
belongs to the indicated negative component. If i + j = 0, both inputs have degree zero and are
closed, so every product is closed by the derivation rule. This checks every n ∈ Z, including the
singular and regular products. Translation preserves the subcomplex because it commutes with d.
The closed vacuum belongs to it. Every vertex identity in the subalgebra is the restriction of the
corresponding identity in A.
  To check the projection, if either input has negative degree, its image is zero and the product
has negative degree, so both sides of multiplicativity are zero. If both inputs have degree zero, the
equation is the definition of the product on cohomology. It is well defined: for closed z and x ∈ A−1 ,

                               (dx)(n) z = d(x(n) z),     z(n) dx = d(z(n) x).

Thus degree-zero boundaries form an ideal in the cycle vertex algebra. The same argument treats
translation and the differential, and the vacuum projects to the vacuum. This proves the strict
map.

Theorem 52.2 (Canonical rectification in ghost degree zero). If H j (A) = 0 for every j ̸= 0, the
canonical zigzag
                                 A ←− A≤0 −→ H 0 (A)
consists of strict quasi-isomorphisms of unital dg vertex algebras. No upper or lower bound on the
ghost degrees of A is required. The construction is functorial for strict dg vertex maps and preserves
every additional degree-zero multilinear operation which is part of such maps.

Proof. In negative degree the inclusion induces the identity on cohomology: its cycles and boundaries
there are unchanged. In degree zero its cohomology is Z 0 (A)/dA−1 = H 0 (A). In positive degree its
cohomology is zero, agreeing with the hypothesis. Therefore the inclusion is a quasi-isomorphism.
The projection induces the identity in degree zero and has zero cohomology in every other degree,
so it too is a quasi-isomorphism. Lemma 46.1 supplies their complete vertex compatibility. A
strict map sends cycles to cycles, restricts in every degree, and induces its cohomology map, giving
functoriality. The same degree argument applies to any specified degree-zero operation on any finite
list of inputs.

   In particular this is a formality theorem for any dg algebra over an ordinary degree-zero operad,
with a closed degree-zero unit where appropriate. It is a theorem about the existence of a zigzag,
not a theorem that there is a strict section H 0 (A) → A. These are different demands. The
endomorphism dg algebra of a projective resolution often illustrates exactly that distinction.


52.3      Why two other truncations do not prove this theorem
The brutal truncation retaining all of A0 and deleting A1 has changed the differential on degree
zero and can create spurious cohomology. The good truncation retains cycles in degree zero. For
                          d
example, the complex kx − → ky in degrees 0, 1 with dx = y is acyclic, while its brutal truncation
has a nonzero degree-zero class.
    The increasing word filtration in a chiral Chevalley complex is another different construction. Its
projections are not chain maps because the bracket lowers word length. Good truncation by ghost
degree is not such a word projection. The two conclusions therefore coexist: the ghost-degree zigzag
is valid, and the naive inverse tower of finite word truncations is invalid.

                                                    212
Chapter 52. Ghost-degree rectification, including the critical level


   Nor may degree-minus-one Poisson operations be included automatically. A bracket of two
degree-zero cycles may have degree minus one and be exact, but replacing its homotopy coherences
by zero is an additional comparison. The theorem only preserves operations of degree zero; the
higher mixed operations of the full programme are not erased by its proof.


52.4      Critical rank-one quantum reduction is formal
Chapter 43 proves that the full universal rank-one reduction complex Kk has cohomology only in
ghost degree zero, including at k = −2. At the critical level its cohomological raw generator satisfies
                                                       [Fλ F ] = 0.
Its cohomology vertex algebra is therefore the freely generated commutative differential algebra
                                k[F, F ′ , F ′′ , . . .],     Y (f, z)g = (ez∂ f )g.
Corollary 52.3 (Critical dg vertex formality). There is a canonical zigzag
                                K−2 ←− (K−2 )≤0 −→ k[F, F ′ , F ′′ , . . .]
of strict dg vertex quasi-isomorphisms. The pole of the noncritical stress-tensor formula is not an
obstruction to this formality statement.

Proof. Apply Theorem 46.2 to the all-level cohomology calculation. The raw critical bracket and
PBW generation identify the right endpoint. No occurrence of (k + 2)−1 enters the zigzag.

    This corrects the seventh edition’s assertion that the critical formality question was left open by
the absence of a strict stress-tensor section. The earlier computation of F , the noncritical stress
tensor, and their exact difference is unchanged. The corrected deduction uses their already proved
all-level cohomology concentration.


52.5      Uniform coefficients and specialization
The good truncation exists over every commutative base ring. Its preservation of products does not
require division. However, tensoring a cycle kernel with a nonflat base change need not preserve
that kernel. A uniform claim therefore needs more than the abstract truncation.
   For the universal rank-one complex over R = Q[k], the explicit contraction onto the PBW space
in F gives the necessary splitting. In degree zero,
                                             eZ = 1 − hd : A0 → A0
is a projection onto Z 0 (A): from dh + hd = 1 − ip and dH = 0, one obtains dhd = d, hence deZ = 0;
and eZ z = z on a cycle. Thus the kernel defining the good truncation is the image of a split
idempotent. Its formation commutes with arbitrary scalar extension. Moreover H 0 (A) is the free
PBW module supplied by the contraction.
   It follows that the rank-one good-truncation zigzag over Q[k] remains a quasi-isomorphism
after every commutative scalar extension. At k + 2 invertible it can be compared with the direct
stress-tensor map; at k = −2 it specializes to the critical zigzag. This is an actual family theorem,
not the claim that cohomology commutes with every base change for every complex.

                                                            213
Chapter 52. Ghost-degree rectification, including the critical level


52.6      Quantum reductions in every rank: the precise formal state-
          ment
The formal quantum construction in Part VI supplies a dg vertex BRST algebra Kℏ,κ (g, f, ℓ) over
k[[ℏ]], with all its cohomology in ghost degree zero. Its complete PBW cohomology is the constructed
quantum slice algebra Wℏ,κ . Consequently the same theorem gives

                                     Kℏ,κ ←− (Kℏ,κ )≤0 −→ Wℏ,κ .

Every coefficient modulo a fixed power of ℏ is an algebraic state. Cycles are closed in the complete
topology, and the maps are continuous. The contraction in Part VI proves the required cohomology
concentration and completeness directly. No specialization of an arbitrary formal series at ℏ = 1 is
part of this assertion.
    At a numerical quantum level, the same deduction applies to any universal DS complex for which
the usual ghost-degree vanishing theorem is supplied, including critical level; the vanishing theorem
is then an imported representation-theoretic input, not a consequence of evaluating a formal power
series at one. This separates the formal construction proved here from its additional numerical-level
realization.




                                                    214
Chapter 53

Quantum reduction and the genuine
chiral bar construction

53.1     The comparison is a map, not a spectral-sequence slogan
A reduction complex with cohomology in ghost degree zero need not come with a map from its
cohomology algebra. In the quantum rank-one reduction that map has now been constructed: the
Virasoro generator is sent to the actual closed stress tensor. This is enough to prove a chiral bar
comparison, provided the bar is the genuine Lie–chiral construction and its direct-sum totalization
is retained. It is not enough to justify an associative bar made from the normally ordered product.
   Let X = A1 with its fixed coordinate. Write DRan for the derived category of right D-modules on
the Ran space, with its chiral symmetric monoidal product. In this chapter every external product,
open extension, and pushforward occurring in that product is derived. A map called an equivalence
is a quasi-isomorphism in this derived category. These conventions are necessary for unbounded
differential graded inputs.
   We use the Beilinson–Drinfeld and Francis–Gaitsgory construction of this chiral monoidal category
as a named geometric input [20, 21]. On a finite configuration it uses the disjointness open, the
external product, and the union of finite sets; its restriction to each finite configuration is a finite
sum of these operations. None of these functors is replaced by an operation on bare vector spaces.
The following homological comparison has a proof independent of a finiteness hypothesis on the
cohomological degrees of the input.


53.2     A finite filtration proves homotopy invariance
Suppose A is a differential graded Lie algebra object for the chiral product. Adjoin the empty
configuration to the Ran indexing category when writing a coaugmentation. Its formal tensor unit
is distinct from the distinguished vertex vacuum; alternatively omit the word-zero summand and
work with the reduced positive-word coalgebra on the nonempty Ran space. With this convention
the coaugmented Chevalley construction has underlying graded object
                                                   M
                                  CEch (A) = 1 ⊕         Symrch (A[1]).
                                                   r≥1




                                                  215
Chapter 53. Quantum reduction and the genuine chiral bar construction


Here the internal differential preserves r, and the component induced by the chiral Lie bracket lowers
r by one. The latter contracts each unordered pair of inputs, applies the suspended chiral bracket,
and uses the Koszul sign for moving the selected inputs together. Coassociativity is given by all
unshuffles. The internal–bracket anticommutator is zero because the bracket is a chain map; the
square of the pair-contraction differential is zero because disjoint contractions cancel with opposite
signs and the three overlapping contractions sum to Jacobi. This proves that the displayed object is
a complex and a conilpotent coalgebra.
   The distinguished vertex vacuum is not quotiented out of A. Coaugmentation of the Chevalley
coalgebra is a different operation from an augmentation of the input vertex algebra. In particular a
vertex product which has a nonzero vacuum output creates no obstruction to this definition.
Theorem 53.1 (Unbounded chiral Chevalley comparison). A quasi-isomorphism f : A → B of
differential graded chiral Lie algebras induces a quasi-isomorphism

                                   CEch (f ) : CEch (A) −→ CEch (B)

of conilpotent chiral coalgebras. The assertion includes all word lengths. It does not require either
input complex to be bounded in ghost degree.

Proof. Set Fr CEch (A) = 0≤j≤r Symjch (A[1]). This is a subcomplex because pair contraction lowers
                          L

word length. Its successive quotient is Symrch (A[1]) with its internal differential.
   The derived chiral product preserves equivalences in each input. Taking homotopy coinvariants
for the finite group Sr also preserves equivalences; over a characteristic-zero coefficient field ordinary
coinvariants in a compatible complex model are exact. Consequently Symrch (f [1]) is an equivalence
for each r. Induction on r, using the exact triangle from Fr−1 to Fr and its associated quotient,
proves the assertion on every finite word window. There is no infinite spectral sequence in this
argument.
   Finally the full displayed Chevalley complex is the filtered colimit of these subcomplexes. Filtered
colimits are exact in the present derived setting, so the equivalences on finite windows give the
asserted equivalence. The map is a coalgebra map because applying f before or after an unshuffle
gives the same tensor of copies of f . It commutes with the bracket differential because f preserves
the chiral bracket. Thus the result is an equivalence of the specified coalgebras, not merely of their
cohomology objects.

   One may equivalently perform this proof in a homotopically flat model and then pass to the
derived category. The use of derived products is essential: termwise tensoring arbitrary nonflat
resolutions over an auxiliary parameter ring would require a separate argument.

Put

                           R = Q[k, (k + 2)−1 ],           c(k) = 1 −    .

Let KR be the universal affine sl2 reduction complex with the b, c ghosts and the differential of the
preceding chapter. Its genuine stress tensor is
                                    1
                                        : hh : + : ef : + : f e : 1 ′
                            TDS = 2                              + h + : b′ c : .
                                               2(k + 2)           2

                                                     216
Chapter 53. Quantum reduction and the genuine chiral bar construction


The previously proved operator calculation gives a strict differential graded vertex map
                                         c(k)
                                 jR : VirR      −→ KR ,    T 7−→ TDS .
It preserves the vacuum and translation. Its source has zero ghost differential.
Theorem 53.2 (Uniform quantum rank-one rectification). The map jR is a quasi-isomorphism.
For every commutative R-algebra S, its scalar extension to S is again a quasi-isomorphism of
differential graded vertex algebras. In particular specialization to every characteristic-zero level
k ̸= −2 is covered by the same proof; there is no generic-level exclusion.

Proof. Use the PBW coordinates E = e − 1, H = h + 2 : bc :, the ghosts, and the closed raw field
                                             1           k+1 ′
                                    F =f+      : HH : +        H.
                                             4             2
The differential on their generators is Qb = E, QH = −2c, QE = Qc = QF = 0. Reordering the
differential into the fixed PBW order gives a linear quartet differential plus terms strictly lowering
the positive PBW filtration of Section 43.2. Each state has finite filtration degree. The linear quartet
complex contracts by polynomial division by positive integers and by 2; all these denominators
are invertible in every S under consideration. The finite perturbation of that filtration-preserving
quartet contraction therefore gives cohomology freely generated by the derivatives of F , with all
other ghost degrees zero, over S itself.
   The exact identity
                                     (k + 2)TDS − F = Q(: bf :)
identifies the image of T with F/(k + 2) on cohomology. Normally ordered products and translations
                                                                 c(k)
descend through the differential. The PBW monomials of VirS therefore map to the freely
generating PBW monomials of this cohomology, with the invertible scalar (k + 2)−1 on each
generator. This proves bijectivity on cohomology in every ghost degree and every PBW word. The
proof was over S throughout, so it does not assume that a nonflat scalar extension preserves an
unspecified acyclic complex.

   At k = −2 the raw cohomology computation remains available, but the displayed stress tensor
and jR do not specialize. This particular normalized map does not address the critical value. The
strict raw map of Chapter 44 does address it and supplies the all-level consequence below.


53.4      The native chiral bar comparison which follows
For a differential graded vertex algebra V , its coordinate chiral algebra on A1 is the corresponding
right D-module with the full chiral operation. The underlying module construction is exact over
the ground field, and a strict differential graded vertex map induces a strict differential graded
chiral map. The chiral operation contains the regular product as well as the singular information;
replacing it by the Lie conformal bracket is not this construction.
Corollary 53.3 (Quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov comparison for the chiral bar). Over a characteristic-
zero field and at every k ̸= −2, the map constructed above induces
                                                      ∼
                                    CEch (Virc(k) ) −−→ CEch (Kk ).
It is a quasi-isomorphism of the genuine conilpotent Lie–chiral bar objects in all word lengths, and
it is induced by the actual closed stress-tensor map.

                                                   217
Chapter 53. Quantum reduction and the genuine chiral bar construction


Proof. Apply Theorem 47.1 to the chiral map induced by Theorem 47.2. The latter theorem supplies
the underlying quasi-isomorphism without a bounded-ghost assumption. The finite-word exact
triangles then prove every bar window, and the exact direct union proves the full stated bar.

   This is a precise replacement for the original rank-one bar–reduction assertion. It does not use the
nonassociative normally ordered product as an associative bar differential. It does not assert that
an ordinary ghost-degree-zero truncation of the reduction complex is already a quasi-isomorphism.
It also does not compare an ordered associative bar from another monoidal structure with this
Lie–chiral bar.


53.5     Balanced BRST and the same comparison
The anomaly-balanced Virasoro complex in this volume has the strict vertex zigzag
                                    Kbal ←− C −→ E ←− Λ(θ3 ),
where C is the commutative differential vertex algebra on all c jets, E is its three-jet quotient, and
θ3 7→ cc′ c′′ . Every arrow was proved to be a quasi-isomorphism by an actual weight contraction
or the finite residual calculation. Applying the preceding theorem to each arrow gives a zigzag of
quasi-isomorphisms of the corresponding chiral Chevalley objects.
   The right endpoint has zero translation, but its associated chiral operation is not obtained by
discarding its commutative product. Thus this consequence is not an identification with the ordinary
Chevalley complex of a zero-bracket vector space. Nor does the zigzag preserve a matter embedding
or every secondary operation which was not included in the vertex-algebra maps.


53.6     Why no inverse-limit assertion has been smuggled in
The filtration used in the proof is increasing. Although Fr is a subcomplex, the naive word projection
Fr+1 → Fr is not a chain map: the bracket of a word of length r + 1 can have length r. Consequently
these finite windows do not supply a tower of chain projections defining a completed bar. An
independent complete filtration would be required for a product completion.
   Likewise the statement is not a theorem that an arbitrary boundary vertex algebra has a universal
holomorphic–topological mixed center. A chiral bar comparison is one constructed ingredient, on
its actual Lie–chiral carrier. Its significance is that a central rank-one comparison in the original
programme is now realized by a strict quantum map and an all-word-length proof, rather than left
as the instruction to rectify a BRST complex.


53.7     The strict raw comparison includes critical level
The calculation in Chapter 44 supplies a stronger application of the same theorem. Over the
polynomial level line there is a strict vertex quasi-isomorphism
                                            raw
                                           VQ[k] −→ KQ[k] .
At every characteristic-zero numerical level, including k = −2, it induces a quasi-isomorphism of
chiral Lie algebras and hence
                                                  ∼
                                   CEch (Vkraw ) −−→ CEch (Kk )

                                                 218
Chapter 53. Quantum reduction and the genuine chiral bar construction


in every word length. No inverse of k + 2 is used. The critical source is a commutative differential
vertex algebra, but its full chiral operation still retains its regular multiplication. It must not be
replaced by the ordinary Lie algebra with zero bracket on the same vector space.
   The direct raw section constructed here is rank-one information. A general nilpotent reduction
requires its own all-ghost cohomology proof before a good-truncation zigzag can be used. No all-rank
quantum comparison is assumed in this volume.

Chapter 54

Anomaly balance and a complete
quantum BRST theorem

54.1     Exactness can annihilate the whole theory
Theorem 54.1 (The unit test for an exact stress tensor). Let (V, Q) be a differential graded vertex
algebra. Suppose T is closed, even, and T(3) T = (c/2)1 with c ̸= 0. If T = QG, then H ∗ (V, Q) = 0.

Proof. The derivation identity gives Q(G(3) T ) = T(3) T = (c/2)1. Thus 1 = Qa for an odd a. For
any closed state v, Q(a(−1) v) = v. Every closed state is exact.

   This test applies to the same vertex algebra with the same singular products. A bulk raviolo
operation of a different degree is not an embedding preserving those products. The other correct
way to avoid the contradiction is to construct an anomaly-balanced total stress tensor. We now do
so and compute its full quantum cohomology.

Theorem 54.2 (Nilpotency for the stated matter family). For M = Virc1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Vircr with its

                                                                               P
Proof. The sum of the independent stress tensors is Virasoro of charge i ci . Formula (48.2) proves
necessity, since c′′′ is a nonzero free-ghost state. It proves sufficiency on the ghost generators. For
each Ti , QTi = cTi′ + 2c′ Ti + (ci /12)c′′′ , whose square cancels as above. The entire tensor product is
generated by these fields, so the square of the derivation is zero everywhere. The mode identity is
the coefficient of the derivation equation [Q, Y (b, z)]+ = Y (Qb, z).

  The following cohomology argument applies more generally to anomaly-balanced matter for
which these BRST identities hold, provided its weights are nonnegative, M0 = k1, and M1 = 0.


54.3      Every weight and every ghost degree
The ghost vacuum is the exterior PBW space on b(r) , c(r) , r ≥ 0. Their weights are 2 + r and −1 + r.
The only negative-weight generator is the odd c, usable at most once. At fixed total weight there are
finitely many ghost monomials, and the same is true of matter monomials for the universal Virasoro
family. Thus the algebraic state space is the direct sum of its finite-dimensional weight spaces.
    On a nonzero weight w, the operator b(1) /w is a contracting homotopy by the preceding theorem.
It is defined on each state and does not require summing infinitely many modes. The weight-zero
space has precisely
                                   1,      c′ ,    cc′′ ,   cc′ c′′ .
No b can occur, and no positive matter weight can occur: its least possible value is two and the
ghosts can lower weight by at most one. The differential is

                                Qc′ = cc′′ ,     Q(cc′′ ) = Q(cc′ c′′ ) = 0.

                                                   221
Chapter 54. Anomaly balance and a complete quantum BRST theorem


Theorem 54.3 (The full balanced vacuum cohomology). For every c ∈ k,
                                                            
                            H ∗ Virc ⊗ Vir26−c ⊗bc, Q = Λ(ϑ),                    |ϑ| = 3,

with ϑ represented by cc′ c′′ . All surviving classes have weight zero. The product and the entire
cohomological vertex structure are those of the constant exterior vertex algebra.

Proof. The contraction kills every nonzero weight; the four-state complex computes weight zero.
The product of the degree-three representative with itself has no singular contractions and vanishes
in regular degree by the exterior relations. Translation is exact by the same L0 contraction in weight
one. The stronger chain-level statement below identifies all vertex operations, rather than inferring
them solely from these dimensions.


54.4      An explicit zigzag of differential graded vertex algebras
Let
                           C = Λ(c(r) : r ≥ 0),         Qc = cc′ ,          Q∂ = ∂Q.
This is the commutative differential vertex subalgebra generated by c inside the full BRST complex.
Define K = ∂/∂c′ as a left exterior derivative. On generators,

                                       (QK + KQ)c(r) = (r − 1)c(r) .

Indeed the terms of ∂ r (cc′ ) containing c′ have coefficients r and −1. Both sides are derivations,
so their equality on generators proves equality everywhere. The same nonzero-weight contraction
and the same four weight-zero states compute H(C); its inclusion into the full BRST complex is
therefore a quasi-isomorphism.
   Kill c(r) for r ≥ 3. This is a differential and translation-stable ideal: at r = 3 the possible low-jet
term is c′′ c′′ = 0, and for larger r at least one derivative index is at least three. The quotient is

                     E = Λ(c0 , c1 , c2 ),   Qc0 = c0 c1 ,       Qc1 = c0 c2 ,     Qc2 = c1 c2 ,

with ∂c0 = c1 , ∂c1 = c2 , ∂c2 = 0. This is a Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra of sl2 in a fixed basis. The
quotient map C → E is a quasi-isomorphism by the same weight contraction. Finally

                                       Λ(ϑ) −→ E,            ϑ 7−→ c0 c1 c2

is a strict dg vertex map: its image is closed, has square zero, and is annihilated by translation. It
induces both surviving cohomology classes and is a quasi-isomorphism.
Theorem 54.4 (Vertex-algebra formality of the balanced vacuum). There is a zigzag of strict
differential graded vertex quasi-isomorphisms

                          Virc ⊗ Vir26−c ⊗bc, Q
                                                   
                                                       ←− C −→ E ←− Λ(ϑ3 ).

Every algebra, differential, map, and translation in the zigzag is specified above.

   This is a complete quantum statement for the balanced vacuum. It is not a proof that Vir26−c is a
Koszul dual of Virc . In fact the resulting unmarked dg vertex object has the same quasi-isomorphism
type for every c. Recovering the separate matter factors would require their marked embeddings
and actions, information this unmarked object does not retain.

54.5      The relative complex and the degree-two class
The relative BRST complex imposes Ltot     0 = 0 and b(1) = 0. On the four weight-zero states, b(1) is
the exterior derivative with respect to c . Its kernel is exactly k1 ⊕ kcc′′ , with zero differential. Thus
                                         ′


                                       ∗
                                      Hrel = k[ω]/ω 2 ,     |ω| = 2.

The absolute weight-zero complex is (k[ω]/ω 2 ) ⊗ Λ(θ) with Qθ = ω, after the sign of θω is fixed
by c, c′ , c′′ . This explains why the absolute degree-three answer and the relative degree-two answer
differ. The relative restriction is not a vertex subalgebra closed under translation; its computed
product is the displayed zero-weight exterior-state product. No category change is hidden in the
notation.

55.1      The coefficient identity and the geometric identity
Let g be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra with a nondegenerate invariant symmetric form. Its Casimir
Ω = a xa ⊗ xa is independent of a dual basis. In U (g)⊗n ,
     P

                    [Ωij , Ωkl ] = 0   ({i, j} ∩ {k, l} = ∅),            [Ωij , Ωik + Ωjk ] = 0.
For the second identity, invariance of the pairing says Ω is invariant under the diagonal adjoint
action. Apply that statement in two positions and sum against the dual basis in the third. This is
the entire coefficient calculation.
   On distinct complex coordinates let ωij = d log(zi − zj ). With a = zi − zj , b = zj − zk , the
coefficient of da ∧ db in
                               ωij ∧ ωjk + ωjk ∧ ωki + ωki ∧ ωij
is 1/(ab) − 1/(b(a + b)) − 1/(a(a + b)) = 0. Thus
                                                       X

is flat. The closedness of the forms removes dω; disjoint commutators vanish; each triple cancels by
the two displayed identities. The same grouping of terms proves that the Gaudin operators

commute. No claim about their spectrum is needed for this identity.


55.2      Finite transport and a retained base point
For three complex points use coordinates z = z1 , t = z2 − z1 , u = (z3 − z1 )/(z2 − z1 ). On a finite
filtered coefficient space take strictly filtration-lowering R0 , R1 , R2 with [R0 , R1 ] = [R0 , R2 ] = 0.
The flat connection has form d − A, where
                                                dt      du       du
                                       A = R0      + R1    + R2     .
                                                t       u       u−1

Chapter 55. Spectral exchange, PBW, and reconstructed quantum symmetry


The finite algebra Λ(σ) ⊗ (k ⊕ kα ⊕ kβ), αβ = 0, computes its de Rham complex. For scalars this
follows by partial fractions: every pole of order greater than one integrates rationally, while the two
logarithmic residues remain. The multiplicative t factor contributes dt/t. The finite filtration of the
coefficient space reduces the assertion to this scalar calculation by successive exact triangles.
   Transport along a path is
                                      XZ
                           Hγ = 1 +                         A(γ̇(sr )) · · · A(γ̇(s1 )).

The sum is finite because long products of the residues vanish. Endpoint differentiation verifies

concatenation. A fixed-endpoint variation differentiates the transport by the integral of curvature,
which is zero. Applying uniqueness to A ⊗ 1 + 1 ⊗ A proves tensor compatibility.
   For R1 = E12 , R2 = E23 , R0 = 0, and base point b ∈ (0, 1), the full solution is

Its derivative verifies the formula, since the last coefficient differentiates to (log(1 − u) − log(1 − b))/u.
Positive based meridians have

             G0 = 1 + 2πiE12 − 2πi log(1 − b)E13 ,            G1 = 1 + 2πiE23 + 2πi log bE13 .

These are not bare residue exponentials in the chosen frame. The single matrix exp(− log bE12 −
log(1 − b)E23 ) simultaneously conjugates them to those exponentials. Thus the lost datum is the
based trivialization, not an asserted impossibility of conjugating the pair.
    With residues X, Y in a nilpotent ideal J 3 = 0, the two endpoint-normalized solutions of

                                         F1−1 F0 = 1 − ζ(2)[X, Y ].

Indeed their XY coefficients differ by −ζ(2) and their Y X coefficients by +ζ(2), from the two
integrals of log(1 − u)/u and log u/(u − 1). With X = A/(2πi), Y = B/(2πi) the coefficient is
[A, B]/24. It is an actual comparison coefficient, not an associator assigned to a nonassociative
binary product.


55.3      The rational matrix and ordered confluence
Let P flip V ⊗ V and put Rh (u) = 1 − hP/u. The spectral Yang–Baxter equation follows by
expansion in h. The linear terms agree. The quadratic terms reduce to the three permutation
products and the identity 1/(uv) = 1/(u(u + v)) + 1/(v(u + v)). The cubic terms agree because
P12 P13 P23 = P23 P13 P12 . Also
                                                             u − hP
              Rh (u)R−h (u) = 1 − h2 /u2 ,       Sh (u) =           ,         Sh21 (−u)Sh (u) = 1.
                                                              u−h
Thus R−h is not the unnormalized inverse. The scalar correction does not change Yang–Baxter.


                                                    225
Chapter 55. Spectral exchange, PBW, and reconstructed quantum symmetry


   For pairwise distinct spectral labels, orient exchange toward a chosen total order. Every swap
lowers the inversion count. Disjoint swaps commute, the overlapping three-label ambiguity is
exactly Yang–Baxter, and reversing a two-label swap is consistent by the displayed unitarity. The
least-unresolved-word argument proves a unique normal form in each multilinear distinct-label
sector. Repeated labels and collision specializations are not covered by that confluence assertion;
they require their own diagonal relations.

algebra. Ordinary Lie PBW shows that the top word-degree symbol of the chosen element cannot
vanish. Therefore the graded surjection has no kernel. Ordered PBW monomials then lift to a basis

is finite, so a highest filtration part exists.

   At N = 1 this is the polynomial algebra on all t(r) , and its augmentation Tor is exterior on their
suspensions. For N > 1, loop degree zero does not bound bar length. PBW alone therefore does
not justify an unrestricted completed bar spectral sequence or any proposed Koszul self-duality of
the Yangian.


55.5      A coend constructs the object before it is recognized
For a small rigid k-linear tensor category C with a tensor functor F to finite-dimensional vector
spaces, form                                  !
                                                       .
                                   F (X)∗ ⊗ F (X)          ⟨F (f )∗ ϕ ⊗ v − ϕ ⊗ F (f )v⟩.
                             M
                      H=
                             X∈C

The relation is imposed for each morphism f : X → Y with ϕ ∈ F (Y )∗ and v ∈ F (X). Let [ϕ ⊗ v]X
denote a class. For a basis ea of F (X), define
                                   X
                    ∆[ϕ ⊗ v]X =         [ϕ ⊗ ea ]X ⊗ [ea ⊗ v]X ,       ε[ϕ ⊗ v]X = ϕ(v).
                                    a

The basis-independent identity tensor makes these formulas independent of the basis, and the
defining relation makes them independent of the presentation. Applying that identity twice proves
coassociativity. Tensor products give multiplication [ϕ ⊗ v]X [ψ ⊗ w]Y = [(ϕ ⊗ ψ) ⊗ (v ⊗ w)]X⊗Y with
the tensor constraints understood. Their coherence proves associativity. Evaluation and coevaluation
in the rigid category define the antipode; their zigzag identities are its two inverse identities.
    Thus one has constructed a Hopf algebra and its universal coactions. A braiding supplies a
coquasitriangular pairing by evaluating F (cX,Y ) on v ⊗ w against ψ ⊗ ϕ. The two hexagons become
its multiplicativity identities by the same basis expansion. A recognition of this object as a particular
RTT algebra still needs generators, relations, a comparison map, and injectivity. The preceding
evaluation argument is an example of precisely that last, indispensable step. A general equivalence
with a full comodule category additionally requires the usual exact faithful fibre-functor hypotheses;
it is not asserted for an arbitrary small tensor category in this construction.

56.1      The extension product as a counted map
A representation of 1 → 2 over Fq is a linear map. Its dimension vector and rank determine it up to
                                                                                  1
isomorphism, so its indecomposables are S1 = (Fq , 0), S2 = (0, Fq ), and E = (Fq −
                                                                                  → Fq ). Define
                                                       X
                                                             L
                                          [M ][N ] =        FM N [L],
                                                        L

where FM L
           N counts subrepresentations isomorphic to N whose quotient is isomorphic to M . The
count is finite. Associativity counts the same flags 0 ⊂ U ⊂ V ⊂ L first by U , then by V , or in the
reverse order. The identity on the flag is the bijection proving equality, including multiplicities. The
zero representation is the unit.
   Put ui = [Si ], w = [E]. The exact binary relations are

                        u1 u2 = u2 u1 + w,         u1 w = qwu1 ,         wu2 = qu2 w.              (56.1)

In dimension (1, 1), both rank-zero and rank-one maps have one S2 subobject with quotient S1 ,
while only rank zero has an S1 subobject with quotient S2 . In S1 ⊕ E, the E subobject is a line
complementary to the kernel of a map F2q → Fq , giving q choices; the reverse S1 subobject is the
unique kernel. The dual image calculation in E ⊕ S2 gives the third relation. Thus every coefficient
in (??) is a counted subspace, not a guessed deformation parameter.
Theorem 56.1 (The whole A2 Hall algebra). The relations (??) present the rational Hall algebra.
With [a]q ! = aj=1 (1 + q + · · · + q j−1 ),
             Q


                              ua2 wb uc1 = [a]q ![b]q ![c]q ![S2⊕a ⊕ E ⊕b ⊕ S1⊕c ].

These monomials form a basis.

Proof. Repeated copies of one indecomposable give complete flags in its multiplicity space. Counting
the first line and continuing on its quotient gives the displayed factorial. In [S2a ][E b ], the required
E b subobject contains the entire source and its image in the target, hence is unique. In the following
product with [S1c ], the required subobject is the full kernel, again unique. The middle objects are

                                                      228
Chapter 56. Extension flags, cyclic words, and trace reconstruction


the displayed direct sums by elementary splittings of vector spaces. This proves the formula and
independence.
   For the presentation, order generators u2 < w < u1 and orient the relations toward that order.
Swapping an inverted adjacent pair lowers inversion count; the correction w in u1 u2 lowers word
length. Termination follows by these two successive orders. The only overlapping inverted triple is
u1 wu2 . Both reductions give q 2 u2 wu1 + qw2 . Disjoint reductions commute. Induction on the least
unresolved word proves unique normal forms. Their independent Hall images prove there are no
additional relations. Finally w = u1 u2 − u2 u1 , so the simple generators suffice.

   Eliminating w gives

             u21 u2 − (q + 1)u1 u2 u1 + qu2 u21 = 0,                u1 u22 − (q + 1)u2 u1 u2 + qu22 u1 = 0.

For q = v 2 , twist multiplication by v ⟨dim M,dim N ⟩ , where ⟨(a, b), (c, d)⟩ = ac + bd − ad. Bilinearity
makes the twist associative. Its exponents on the three words 112, 121, 211 are −1, 0, 1, so the first
Serre coefficient becomes v + v −1 and the outer coefficients become one. This derives the positive
A2 quantum-group presentation with its actual normalization.
   The Hilbert series (1 − t1 )−1 (1 − t1 t2 )−1 (1 − t2 )−1 follows from the basis. It could not have
determined the extension term w or its q-commutations in reverse. A compact Calabi–Yau Hall
comparison still needs its moduli correspondences, finiteness, orientation, and coefficient functors;
this finite quiver calculation does not supply those by analogy.


56.2      Cyclic differentiation and a Lie bracket
Let F = k⟨x, y⟩ and Fcyc = F/[F, F ], where the denominator is the linear span of commutators, not
their two-sided ideal. For a cyclic word define
                                                         X
                               ∂xcyc [a1 · · · ad ] =             ai+1 · · · ad a1 · · · ai−1 ,
                                                        i:ai =x

and similarly for y. Rotation permutes the summands and proves well-definedness. Set

                                 {f, g}cyc = [∂xcyc f ∂ycyc g − ∂ycyc f ∂xcyc g].                             (56.2)

The cyclic quotient is not generally an associative quotient algebra. Formula (??) defines a different
operation on it.
   For pairs of N × N matrices use {Xij , Ykl } = δil δjk , with the other coordinate brackets zero.
Differentiating each matrix occurrence proves

                d Tr f = Tr (∂xcyc f )dX + (∂ycyc f )dY ,
                                                                   
                                                                            {Tr f, Tr g} = Tr{f, g}cyc .

The coordinate bracket satisfies Jacobi: its Jacobiator is a derivation in every input and vanishes on
coordinate generators.

Lemma 56.2 (Traces jointly detect necklaces). Over characteristic zero, the trace maps for all
finite N jointly inject Fcyc into the product of the matrix-coordinate rings.

Proof. Separate word lengths by polynomial degree. For a word a1 · · · ad , choose N = d and the
directed cycle 1 → 2 → · · · → d → 1. On its ith edge use an X or Y coordinate according to ai . The

                                                            229
Chapter 56. Extension flags, cyclic words, and trace reconstruction


product of these entries occurs in a degree-d trace word precisely when its necklace is the chosen
one: a closed traversal using those edges must follow the cycle. Its coefficient is a positive integer,
recording possible starts, and is nonzero in characteristic zero. This isolates each necklace coefficient
in a finite sum. Length one uses a diagonal entry, and the empty word is detected by Tr I = N .

   The trace of the Jacobiator of (??) is consequently zero for every N and hence is itself zero.
Skew-symmetry follows from [ab] = [ba]. Thus (??) is a Lie bracket and every displayed trace is a
Lie map. This proves the all-word identity, not only its value at one rank.


56.3      A rank restriction has a kernel
On diagonal matrices write pab =
                                    P     a b
                                       i xi yi . Direct differentiation gives

                                  {pab , pcd } = (ad − bc)pa+c−1,b+d−1 .

Terms with a zero coefficient are zero, regardless of a formal negative exponent. For one pair,
A = x2 , B = xy, C = y 2 obey B 2 = AC and brackets {A, B} = 2A, {B, C} = 2C, {A, C} = 4B.
For two pairs the same sums instead have

                                     B 2 − AC = −(x1 y2 − x2 y1 )2 .

A one-particle relation is not therefore a stable trace relation. Restricting ranks or loci must retain
this kernel, rather than declaring the source algebras identical.


56.4      A derived trace before passing to commuting matrices
There is an associative dg resolution

                          Q = T (x, y, ψ),      |ψ| = −1,       dψ = xy − yx.

Its map to k[x, y] is a quasi-isomorphism. To prove it, expand each ψ into the placeholder xy,
and filter by expanded length and the number of x-before-y inversions. The leading differential
replaces ψ by xy; the other replacement yx lowers inversions. Occurrences of the leading subword
xy do not overlap. For each fixed expanded word, marking or unmarking these occurrences gives
a tensor product of two-term identity complexes. It is acyclic if any occurrence is present. The
unmarked survivors are exactly y a xb . The filtration is finite at each expanded length, so this proves
the assertion without an infinite spectral-sequence convergence assumption.
   For matrix-valued representations of Q, put dΨ = [X, Y ]. Evaluation of a word is a chain map
because it respects this one differential and extends by the derivation rule. Taking trace kills graded
commutators and gives a chain map from cyclic words to invariant polynomial functions on the
derived commuting locus. In particular

                                d Tr(Ψvu) = Tr(uXY v) − Tr(uY Xv).

Adjacent exchanges in a trace are boundaries. This is a chain-level statement; it does not promote
an unextended necklace Poisson bracket to a differential-compatible bracket on all words involving ψ.
A full gauge or BV extension must include its conjugate ghost and verify its Hamiltonian equation.
The trace map just constructed needs no such unproved identification.

57.1     An adjoint coproduct is not an augmentation
Let B be finite-dimensional with a symmetric Frobenius functional τB . If ei , ei are dual bases, its
Casimir i ei ⊗ ei is basis-independent and satisfies
       P
                                                                                !
                                       X                 X
                                                  i                         i
                             (b ⊗ 1)       ei ⊗ e =                ei ⊗ e           (1 ⊗ b).
                                       i                     i

Pair both sides with a ⊗ c using the trace pairing. Associativity and cyclicity give the same scalar,
and nondegeneracy proves equality. This identity permits an open cut to slide across multiplication.
It makes no assertion that B is commutative.
   For a commutative Frobenius state algebra (C, τC ), the handle is hC = i ci ci . Cutting a handle
                                                                                               P

inserts its adjoint coproduct followed by multiplication, so it multiplies the residual state by hC .
The connected closed genus-g amplitude is
                                               Zg = τC (hgC ).
In particular g = 0 is calibrated by τC (1). Substituting an arbitrary noncommutative open algebra
into this closed formula changes the sewing theory.

57.3      Two independent genus variables
A rank-one commutative Frobenius algebra with trace µ has amplitudes µ1−gX . Its external tensor
product with the previous sewing datum has coefficient
                                                      X
                                              µ1−gX       λ2−2g
                                                           α
                                                                ⊥
                                                                  .
                                                      α

The two genera belong to two supplied sewing parameter systems. In a matrix-index ribbon model
with ’t Hooft vertex weight N , edge weight N −1 , and closed index-face weight N , the coefficient is
                                           N V −E+Fint = N 2−2g⊥ −b .
The second equality is the Euler characteristic of the thickened ribbon surface with its b distinguished
boundaries unfilled. It does not identify the matrix-index model with every chiral conformal-block
theory.
   For a product of two actual chain complexes, the total differential is dX ⊗ 1 + (−1)degX 1 ⊗ d⊥ ;
expanding its square proves anticommutation of the two summands. In an incidence model of
boundaries, this is the product-orientation sign. It cancels equal coefficient composites. It cannot
prove that two unspecified coefficient composites are equal. Chapter 8 gives the correct criterion
when the boundary maps are not independent tensor factors.

Chapter 57. Open and closed sewing with all normalizations fixed


57.4      Formal sewing and analytic sewing
         L
Let V = n≥0 Vn have finite-dimensional graded pieces and a nondegenerate graded pairing. The
formal sewing tensor                X      X
                                        qn    vn,a ⊗ vna
                                          n≥0     a

is well-defined in the displayed product completion, independently of the chosen dual bases. At
each coefficient of q it is a finite tensor. Composition with supplied multilinear functionals therefore
gives a formal sewing series. On several edges the same argument works coefficient by coefficient
when the number of edge decorations of each total weight is finite.
   Convergence is not part of that proof. The series n!q n is a perfectly defined formal series with
                                                        P

zero analytic radius. To obtain an analytic conformal block, one must supply growth or energy bounds
or an analytic sewing theorem for the representation category at hand. Likewise the cylindrical
factor q −c/24 belongs to the chosen anomaly-line trivialization and coordinate normalization; it is
not obtained by changing the exponent in an unrelated ribbon contraction.
    For the rank-one Heisenberg vacuum, the direct occupation basis gives n≥1 (1 − q n )−1 . On
                                                                                 Q

|q| < 1 the logarithm is absolutely convergent on compact subdiscs, since n,k |q|nk /k < ∞. Thus
                                                                             P

this particular oscillator determinant is analytic there. Zero modes, lattice sums, and higher-genus
determinant lines require their own data. This elementary example shows how a formal coefficient
construction is strengthened by a real analytic estimate, rather than by its name.
