Virasoro conformal cohomology in
every arity

58.1      A polynomial complex rather than a guessed amplitude
Let Vir = k[T ]L, [Lλ L] = (T + 2λ)L, and let k be the trivial module. A basic conformal n-cochain
is an alternating polynomial P (λ1 , . . . , λn ). Its values on all translates of the inputs are fixed by
conformal antilinearity: inserting T L in the ith place multiplies by −λi . Put Ce 0 = k. This is the
centreless Lie conformal algebra with trivial coefficients; it is not the vertex envelope with adjoint
coefficients.
   With a merged argument put first, the differential is
                                              X
                (dP )(λ1 , . . . , λn+1 ) =         (−1)i+j (λi − λj )                                                (58.1)
                                              i<j
                                                      · P (λi + λj , λ1 , . . . , λ
                                                                                  bi, . . . , λ
                                                                                              b j , . . . , λn+1 ).

Indeed the bracket in the merged slot gives −(λi + λj ) + 2λi = λi − λj . The action terms disappear
for trivial coefficients. The square is zero: disjoint pair mergers cancel in the two orders; the
three overlapping mergers give conformal Jacobi. The latter is the elementary identity obtained by
expanding

              (T + λ + 2µ)(T + 2λ) − (T + µ + 2λ)(T + 2µ) = (λ − µ)(T + 2λ + 2µ).

Thus the complex is defined before its cohomology is counted.


58.2      The Euler contraction
For n ≥ 1 define

                (τn P )(λ1 , . . . , λn−1 ) = (−1)n−1 ∂t P (λ1 , . . . , λn−1 , t)|t=0 ;                τ0 = 0.

Lemma 58.1. For P ∈ Ce n homogeneous of polynomial degree w,

                                              (dτ + τ d)P = (w − n)P.


                                                             234
Chapter 58. Virasoro conformal cohomology in every arity


Proof. Expand τ dP with the last variable equal to t. The mergers of two original variables occur
also in dτ P , with opposite signs (−1)n and (−1)n−1 . For the pair λi , t, move the merged first input
back to position i. Its sign is (−1)i−1 , which combines with (−1)i+n+1 and (−1)n to give +1. The
remaining sum is
                   X                                                                X
                         ∂t (λi − t)P (λ1 , . . . , λi + t, . . . , λn )   t=0
                                                                                 =       λi ∂i P − nP.
                     i                                                               i

Euler’s identity proves the formula. The degree-zero case is the identity 0 = 0 on constants.

   The differential raises both arity and polynomial degree by one. Consequently w − n is constant
on each homogeneous subcomplex. When it is nonzero, (w − n)−1 τ contracts that subcomplex.
Every polynomial has finitely many homogeneous components, so no completed totalization is
needed.


58.3      All basic groups
Every alternating polynomial is divisible by
                                       Y
                               ∆n =          (λi − λj ),       deg ∆n = n(n − 1)/2.
                                       i<j

This follows by setting each pair equal and dividing by the corresponding prime polynomial; the
distinct factors are nonassociated in the polynomial unique factorization domain. Only polynomial
degree n can survive by Lemma ??. Hence n(n − 1)/2 ≤ n, leaving n = 0, 1, 2, 3.
  The constants survive in degree zero. The remaining resonant spaces in degrees one and two are
kλ1 and k(λ21 − λ22 ), with
                                       dλ1 = −(λ21 − λ22 ).
They cancel. Degree three is k∆3 . Its next differential is alternating of degree four in four variables,
and is zero since deg ∆4 = 6. It is not a boundary because the entire resonant degree-two space was
already an image. Therefore
Theorem 58.2. The basic conformal cohomology is one-dimensional in degrees 0 and 3, represented
by 1 and ∆3 , and vanishes in all other degrees. Its positive generator has square zero for the unshuffle
cup product.

   This is the classical basic calculation, reproduced here with its all-degree contraction. The
vanishing is not inferred from a finite matrix table.


58.4      Reduction and the exceptional degree zero
Let ∂ act on Ce n by multiplication by λ1 + · · · + λn . It commutes with d, since every merger preserves
the total sum. Define C = C/∂e C. e Multiplication by the total sum is injective in every positive arity
but is zero in arity zero. Thus the correct exact sequence is

                                         0 −→ ∂ Ce −→ Ce −→ C −→ 0,

where the first term is isomorphic to the positive-degree truncation of the basic complex, not to
that complex including its degree-zero constants.

                                                           235
Chapter 58. Virasoro conformal cohomology in every arity


   Its only cohomology is in degree three, represented by (λ1 + λ2 + λ3 )∆3 . Its map into basic
cohomology is zero, either by the Euler contraction or by the explicit identity

                                  d(λ31 − λ32 ) = −(λ1 + λ2 + λ3 )∆3 .                           (58.2)

Theorem 58.3. The reduced conformal cohomology is one-dimensional in degrees 0, 2, 3 and zero
otherwise. Representatives are 1, the class of λ31 − λ32 on the total-sum hyperplane, and the class of
∆3 . The degree-two connecting map is minus the displayed degree-three generator of H(∂ C).  e

Proof. The long exact sequence and the zero map just proved give the two positive-degree groups
and all the vanishings. In degree zero the quotient still equals k. Identity (??) identifies the
connecting map and proves non-exactness of the proposed representative. The quotient induces
an isomorphism in degree three. On the two-input hyperplane, the central class is 2λ31 , fixing its
normalization relative to the usual cubic extension.


58.5     A native comparison must change the coefficients correctly
The positive Witt algebra, annihilation Lie algebra, Lie conformal algebra, universal Virasoro vertex
algebra, and chiral deformation complex have different operation spaces. A repeated amplitude is not
a map between them. Chapter 49 separately records the published bounded vertex chiral-cohomology
theorem with adjoint coefficients for every central charge. That positive result is stronger than the
trivial-coefficient calculation above, but it is still not a construction of the full mixed centralizer.
Both calculations are retained under their actual names.




                                                  236
Chapter 59

A constant-field obstruction to the
proposed centre identification

59.1      The smallest example which distinguishes two deformation
          theories
A chiral algebra in the Beilinson–Drinfeld sense is not an associative algebra with a parameter
attached. The distinction remains visible when translation and every singular product vanish. Let
                           A = k[x1 , . . . , xr ],   T = 0,     Y (a, z)b = ab.
This is a commutative vertex algebra. Regard it as an input to the chiral deformation operad,
rather than replacing that operad by the ordinary associative endomorphism operad. The resulting
cohomology is Harrison cohomology, not associative Hochschild cohomology. The all-arity comparison
is Theorem 7.1 of Bakalov–De Sole–Kac [19]. We recall its decisive low-arity content and give the
obstruction independently.
   For a translation-zero module the two-input chiral operation is determined by its value on the
simple-pole coefficient (z1 − z2 )−1 . Its value on a regular coefficient is zero: the first sesquilinearity
equation makes it annihilated by the spectral variable, which is torsion-free in the polynomial
target. The second sesquilinearity equation makes the simple-pole value independent of that variable.
Higher-pole values are then determined recursively by the first equation. On parity-reversed even
states, the sign of interchange of the two inputs cancels the sign of the simple pole. The resulting
bilinear map is therefore symmetric. The linearized three-input chiral identity is the ordinary
associative cocycle equation on that symmetric map. These are precisely the two-input Harrison
cocycles. Unary coboundaries are changes of the underlying linear coordinates.
Proposition 59.1 (The low-degree constant-field calculation). In characteristic zero, the chiral
deformation cohomology of the displayed polynomial vertex algebra has
                    0                         1                         2
                   Hch (A, A) = A,           Hch (A, A) = Derk (A),    Hch (A, A) = 0.
The natural bracket of a derivation and a nullary element is evaluation.

Proof. Degree zero is A/T A = A. A unary cocycle is precisely a map satisfying D(ab) = D(a)b +
aD(b); inner derivations vanish because A is commutative. This proves the degree-one statement
and the evaluation formula directly.

                                                      237
Chapter 59. A constant-field obstruction to the proposed centre identification


   For degree two, let ϕ be a normalized symmetric Hochschild cocycle. On A ⊕ εA, ε2 = 0, define

                            (a + εa′ )(b + εb′ ) = ab + ε(ab′ + a′ b + ϕ(a, b)).

Symmetry and the cocycle equation make this a commutative associative deformation, with the
same unit after normalization. Choose the lifts xi of the polynomial generators. The universal
property of the polynomial algebra gives a unital algebra map from the undeformed A to this
deformed algebra, reducing to the identity modulo ε. It has the form a 7→ a + εD(a) as a linear
map. Multiplicativity gives
                               ϕ(a, b) = D(ab) − aD(b) − D(a)b.
Thus every symmetric cocycle is a coboundary. Normalizing a non-normalized unital cocycle removes
only a unit coboundary and gives the same result. The chiral/Harrison correspondence just described
transfers this calculation to the stated complex.

   For comparison, ordinary associative Hochschild cohomology has

                             HH• (A, A) = A ⊗ Λ(ξ1 , . . . , ξr ),                  |ξi | = 1.

A finite diagonal resolution proves this formula: resolve A over Ae = k[xL       R
                                                                            i , xi ] by the regular
sequence xL      R
           i − xi . Diagonal Hom makes the differential zero. Antisymmetrization sends an exterior
generator to its derivation cocycle and preserves the product on cohomology. In particular the two
theories already disagree in degree two when r ≥ 2.
   There is a proof of nonvanishing requiring no general Hochschild theorem. The two-cochain

                                          α(a, b) = ∂1 a ∂2 b − ∂2 a ∂1 b

is a cocycle by the two derivation rules. Every associative coboundary of a unary cochain on a
commutative algebra is symmetric in a, b, whereas α is antisymmetric and α(x1 , x2 ) = 1. Hence its
class is nonzero. It cannot be the image of a class in the zero Harrison degree-two group.


59.2      A Gerstenhaber identity forces the missing exterior classes
The discrepancy is not just a difference of dimensions. The missing classes are forced by the
interaction of products and insertions.

Theorem 59.2 (Canonical pairs force exterior products). Let G be a unital graded-commutative
algebra with a degree-−1 Gerstenhaber bracket. Suppose it contains a degree-zero subalgebra A =
k[x1 , . . . , xr ], degree-one elements ξ1 , . . . , ξr , and

                                  [ξi , xj ] = δij ,         [a, xj ] = 0      (a ∈ A).

Then, for each q ≤ r, the ordered products ξi1 · · · ξiq with i1 < · · · < iq are linearly independent over
                  q                                             r
A. In particular G contains a free A-submodule of rank q .

Proof. Bracket on the right with xj is an odd contraction on the subalgebra generated by A and
the ξi . The biderivation identity gives
                                                      q
                                                      X
                            [ξi1 · · · ξiq , xj ] =       (−1)q−a δia j ξi1 · · · ξc
                                                                                   ia · · · ξiq .
                                                      a=1

                                                            238
Chapter 59. A constant-field obstruction to the proposed centre identification


The sign is fixed by moving a degree-−1 derivation past the factors to its right. Coefficients in A
have zero contraction. Given a relation among products of one fixed length q, contract successively
with the coordinates indexed by a chosen subset J of size q. Every term except the one indexed by
J vanishes, and that term becomes its coefficient times 1, up to a sign. The inclusion of A and the
nonzero unit force that coefficient to vanish. Doing this for every J proves independence.

   For r = 2 the contradiction can be written in one line. If degree two were zero, then ξ1 ξ2 = 0, but

                                          [ξ1 ξ2 , x1 ] = −ξ2 ̸= 0.

Therefore the groups of Proposition 49.1 cannot carry an ordinary Gerstenhaber structure which
simultaneously extends the polynomial product, the natural A-module structure on derivations, and
their evaluation bracket. This is a proved obstruction to that particular proposed enhancement.


59.3      The corrected theorem has to say which centre it means
There are three distinct objects. The ordinary associative centre retains the exterior classes and its
usual E2 structure. The native chiral deformation complex has the Harrison specialization above and
retains its own operadic Lie deformation theory. A geometric holomorphic–topological centralizer is
an additional object whose comparison with either complex has to specify the operations and the
meaning of specialization.
   The obstruction does not say that no native mixed algebra can exist. A chiral or star-pseudotensor
bracket need not become the ordinary bracket used in Theorem 49.2 under an arbitrary forgetful
functor. Its supported coefficient object, translation action or degree shift can change that comparison.
What is excluded is declaring all of these identifications simultaneously while keeping the ordinary
product and the evaluation bracket unchanged. The missing exterior degree is an exact test on any
such declaration.
   In particular, the ordinary Deligne theorem applied to an associative envelope does not compute
the original chiral deformation complex without an envelope comparison. Nor does the theorem that
a constant differential algebra defines a commutative vertex algebra make its associative Hochschild
cochains the chiral ones. The polynomial case is already a counterexample.


59.4      Bounded native cohomology is stronger than a mode calcula-
          tion
The literature supplies a positive native result which should not be understated. For the universal
Virasoro vertex algebra, the bounded chiral cohomology in the filtered chiral-operadic sense has
dimension one in degrees 0, 2, 3 and zero elsewhere, for every central charge [19, Theorem 7.2]. This
is an imported theorem about the full vertex algebra, not the calculation of only the Virasoro Lie
conformal algebra with trivial coefficients. Its degree-two and degree-three classes are characterized
on generator inputs and regular rational coefficient by

                  (L, L) 7−→ λ31 1,     (L, L, L) 7−→ (λ1 − λ2 )(λ1 − λ3 )(λ2 − λ3 )1,

modulo the total translation relation. They extend, up to coboundary, to bounded chiral cocycles
by the cited filtered comparison.

                                                    239
Chapter 59. A constant-field obstruction to the proposed centre identification


   The word “bounded” is structural. It means the union of the filtration-bounded operation spaces,
not a truncation in cohomological degree. Identifying that union with every unrestricted chiral
operation requires a further exhaustiveness statement. The paper explicitly distinguishes these
complexes. No universal mixed-centre action, native cup computation, or unrestricted cohomology
assertion is obtained by deleting this adjective. This restores a genuine known result to the
programme while preserving its actual scope.




                                                   240
Chapter 60

Native chiral operations and the
remaining comparison

60.1      The operation spaces have different types
For right DX -modules on a smooth characteristic-zero curve, let jI denote the complement of the
pair diagonals in X I and ∆I the small diagonal. The native chiral operation space is

                            PIch (Mi ; N ) = HomDX I (jI∗ jI∗ ⊠i Mi , ∆I∗ N ) .

The star operation has source ⊠i Mi without that localization. The natural restriction from chiral
to star operations comes from the map of those sources. Pointwise tensor operations are a third
construction. No identification of these spaces follows from their having the same number of inputs.
   A chiral algebra is a Lie algebra for the chiral pseudotensor operations, with its specified unit. A
multiplication for an internal associative centralizer must therefore be supplied separately. For the
ordinary Lie algebra [h, e] = e, the bracket gives [h, [h, e]] = e but [[h, h], e] = 0. Jacobi is not the
associative identity. The same failure of type would occur if a chiral bracket were inserted as the
multiplication of the ordinary tensor bar.


60.2      The fixed-module deformation problem
Fix the right DX -module and unit of a chiral algebra. The chiral endomorphism operad composes
by the specified nested diagonal maps and localization operations. Use its operadic suspension and
alternating invariants to form the positive-arity deformation complex. For a degree-zero underlying
module the binary bracket has degree one in this suspended complex. The commutator of insertion
is the Nijenhuis–Richardson bracket; the nested/disjoint insertion proof gives its graded Jacobi
identity.
   If µ is the original chiral operation, [µ, µ] = 0 is chiral Jacobi. Deformations ν which vanish when
the fixed unit is inserted satisfy
                                            [µ, ν] + 12 [ν, ν] = 0.
The original unit operation is not itself a normalized zero-residue operation. The differential
preserves normalized deformations because the two remaining unit-residue insertions cancel by
D-linearity; it is not correct to delete the original unit term first. Over a formal parameter the

                                                   241
Chapter 60. Native chiral operations and the remaining comparison


obstruction proof of Theorem 3.2 applies to this actual convolution object. It classifies and tests
lifts; it does not assert that every obstruction vanishes.
   Tamarkin’s chiral deformation construction and cohomological chiral-Gerstenhaber theorem
provide related native inputs [9]. Their product, star bracket, pro-objects and unit conventions must
be retained. A cohomological product whose restriction is null-homotopic is not a strict chain-level
product with zero restriction. In the present cohomological convention a bracket of degree −1 on C
becomes degree zero on C[1], not C[−1].


60.3     A complete central conformal deformation
Let L generate a free k[T ]-module and let C be central with T C = 0. Consider
                                    [Lλ L] = (T + 2λ)L + f (λ)C.
Sesquilinearity defines the bracket on derivatives. Skew symmetry makes f odd. The noncentral
Jacobi identity is the polynomial identity for (T + 2λ)L. The central part is
                          (λ + 2µ)f (λ) − (µ + 2λ)f (µ) = (λ − µ)f (λ + µ).
Differentiate in µ and put µ = 0 to obtain 3f (λ) − λf ′ (λ) = 2λf ′ (0). Coefficient comparison forces
                                          f (λ) = aλ + bλ3 .
Substitution verifies both monomials, so the condition is sufficient. Replacing L by L + cC changes
f by −2cλ. With the quotient generator and central element fixed, the cubic coefficient is the
extension class. The argument applies coefficientwise to formal series a(t), b(t) and gives the all-
orders deformation in this ansatz. It does not classify every deformation of the full Virasoro vertex
algebra.
   For currents, [xλ y] = [x, y] + λκ(x, y)C satisfies skew symmetry exactly when κ is symmetric. Its
central Jacobi equation is
                          λκ(x, [y, z]) − µκ(y, [x, z]) = (λ + µ)κ([x, y], z).
This is exactly invariance of κ, with the second coefficient following by skew symmetry of the Lie
bracket. Thus the invariant pairing and level are genuine operation data, not values inferred from a
character.


60.4     What the new diagonal theorem supplies
Theorems 17.2 and 17.3 supply an actual higher centre comparison in an explicitly constructed
coefficient category. Theorem 20.1 computes a nonconstant ramified family there, with all internal
associative cochain operations retained. The acyclic reconstruction and support complexes of
Chapter 11 supply all-arity collision coefficients and their nontrivial derived fibres. The boundary
kernel of Chapter 10 supplies the degree-sensitive supported target. These are substantive interfaces
with explicit maps.
   They do not identify an arbitrary Beilinson–Drinfeld chiral algebra with an associative algebra
in this coefficient category. They also do not identify a fixed order-chart parameter space with
the entire bulk and boundary configuration system on a three-manifold. The complete internal
associative action is therefore not relabelled as the native mixed theorem.

                                                  242
Chapter 60. Native chiral operations and the remaining comparison


60.5     The exact mixed theorem still requiring a construction
A native version needs a two-coloured geometric operation system SX whose boundary restriction
recovers the given chiral pseudotensor operations and whose interior coefficient system retains
the derived holomorphic–topological collision coefficients. It needs an actual object Zch (A) and a
comparison with the intended native chiral deformation or Hochschild object. Finally it needs a
morphism from a cofibrant resolution of SX to

                                              End(Zch (A), A),

including every unit, boundary transgression, composition homotopy and descent map, together
with the centralizing universal property in that same category.
   Defining SX to be the endomorphism operad of a preselected pair would make its action
tautological and would not compare it to the geometric source. Defining a centre by an ordinary
Hochschild formula without specifying the associative input would change the object. Naming
a Maurer–Cartan element without constructing its components would leave the action unproved.
None of those substitutions is used in this edition.
   The most immediate tests on a candidate comparison are now independent and explicit. It must
preserve the two branch idempotents in the coefficient fibre, remove cyclic tournament branches
by the support correction, retain the degree shift of the supported boundary module, obey the
arity-dependent input budget for complete operations, and carry the root relation’s homotopy
bδ = mtβ. The strict Gerstenhaber shortcut in Chapter 19 fails even before these geometric
requirements are combined.


60.6     A finite quantum obstruction
There is a parallel danger in passing from a classical construction to a quantum one. Let

             O = k[ξ1 , ξ2 ] ⊗ Λ(x1 , x2 ),   |ξi | = −2,   |xi | = 1,   ∆ = ∂x1 ∂ξ1 + ∂x2 ∂ξ2 .

Use left odd derivatives and the BV bracket generated by this degree-one square-zero operator. For
S = ξ2 x1 x2 , one has S 2 = 0, ∆S = −x1 , and (∆S)S = 0. Hence the classical master equation holds.
    Every degree-zero candidate first correction is S1 = c + (aξ1 + bξ2 )x1 x2 . Direct multiplication
gives SS1 = cS; the terms in the generated bracket cancel, so {S, S1 } = 0 for every candidate. The
first quantum equation would require 0 + ∆S = −x1 = 0, impossible in this algebra. The obstruction
is an actual nonzero cohomology class for the supplied field content. Changing that content or the
coefficient algebra is new data, not an automatic consequence of the classical equation.


60.7     The logical endpoint of this edition
The reconstructed mathematical core consists of the proved word adjunction and reconstruction, the
complete operation models, the actual order-chart geometry and diagonal centre, the ramification
and integral extension calculations, and the named native chiral interfaces. It is a single coherent
development, rather than a claim that all its objects are equivalent.
  The full native mixed comparison and the original all-genus, automorphic, Hall-specialization
and gravity identifications are not proved here. Their source hypotheses and scalar observations are

                                                     243
Chapter 60. Native chiral operations and the remaining comparison


not inserted as conclusions. Appendix A records these dependencies so that later volumes cannot
silently cite this edition as a proof of them. This is a statement about the specific missing maps,
not a claim that their construction is impossible or that the programme should abandon them.

A.2      Corrections with exact locations
The supplied 121-page draft has an empty contents page and unresolved cross-references throughout;
the present source is rebuilt and cross-referenced independently rather than silently concatenated
with those pages. Its substantive limits in the preface and Chapter 19 are retained. In particular
its final pages state that the native mixed action has not been constructed; that limitation is not
turned into a theorem in this edition.
   In the supplied 281-page synthesis, Section 10.1, pp. 40–53, and Section 12.1, pp. 246–249,
several generalizations require correction. The factorizing subcategory is not a stable ambient for
pointwise sums. The singleton-supported Cauchy square has two nonempty summands at arity two.
A degreewise dual of the dual-number bar is not wrong in each positive weight; the issue is the
topology used to assemble weights. The overview’s common Gram determinant is replaced by the
distinct weight-four and weight-five determinants. The nonisomorphism of quantum and Jordan
algebras does not imply inequivalence of their graded module categories. The displayed affine-current
residue calculation with an omitted eleven-term operator is not certified by any ordinary tensor-bar
check.
   The synthesis’s normally ordered bar differential is not repaired by assigning an associator to
a minimal m3 . Chapter 3 gives the actual transfer and its hypotheses. A scalar augmentation
of a Weyl algebra or matrix algebra is impossible; Chapter 4 replaces it by actual modules or a
nonaugmented resolution. A scalar trace is not used as an augmentation. A level-reflection identity
is not used as a Koszul-dual identification.
   The later arithmetic reconstruction distinguishes native chiral operations, star operations and
pointwise tensor operations, and retains the degree shift in its boundary discussion. Those corrections
remain in Chapter 50. Its all-tree scale construction is retained only with a common simultaneous
Laurent bound; an arbitrary intermediate completion or a substitution of one for a formal scale is
not permitted.


A.3      New corrections to the tempting centre argument
The compact-generator theorem for order-chart coefficients must use the coherent commutative
algebra C, not only an associative Cech multiplication with an undeclared symmetric structure. Its
proof must establish generation for unbounded complexes; the residue-field argument does so. The
relative diagonal is open, not generally closed. Its multiplication C ⊗R C → C is a homological
epimorphism, while R → C is not asserted to be one. These distinctions are used in every step of

                                                 246
Appendix A. Source-to-result register


the new centre comparison.
   The resulting internal centre is not automatically the naive sheaf of cochains. The odd square-zero
example shows that scalar finiteness alone does not commute the centre with localization. The
bounded-coefficient theorem requires an ordinary finite-projective algebra and bounded coefficient
complex; it does not apply merely because a graded algebra is proper. The root family’s special base
change is proved with the Koszul coefficient model, not imported from a false general statement.
   The root family has even cohomology but its cochains do not have a strict Gerstenhaber product
compatible with all the naive generator relations. The explicit defect bδ = mtβ replaces that false
shortcut. Its special-fibre bracket proves the absence of an E∞ refinement of the relative centre.
This obstruction is not a claim that no higher model on cohomology can exist; any valid model
must retain higher operations.


A.4      Continuity with the preceding progress
The following distinctions and constructions are preserved, not retracted: the algebraic bar–cobar
counit without a quadratic hypothesis; the positive-weight unit and completion conventions; the
failure of cobar to preserve all underlying coalgebra quasi-isomorphisms; the split alignment projector
without a global interchange isomorphism; the three separate bar constructions; the difference
between augmentation dual, probe endomorphisms and diagonal centre; the two-chart idempo-
tent test; the cyclic tournament correction; complete brace input budgets; the perfect-diagonal
base-change criterion; parameter-scaled Chevalley differentials and the diagonal-centre Bockstein;
integral Massey torsion; divided Hasse operators and their digits; polarized matrix-factorization
endomorphisms; and the polynomial stress-tensor correlation formula.
   Some additional computations in prior sources, such as particular high-rank Lie-homology
windows, specialised matrix-factorization comparisons, detailed Yangian representation claims, or
automorphic normalizations, are not re-proved in this edition. They are not silently declared false
or deleted from the research programme, but they cannot be cited as checked here. Their original
source files, when present in the runtime, are recorded by the source manifest. The manifest is
evidence of file continuity, not a proof of their mathematical claims.


A.5      Dependencies of the principal new theorems
The relative order-chart centre uses finite affine descent, residue-field detection, compact-generator
Morita, the affine open diagonal, and the internal associative centre structure. It does not use the
native mixed conjecture. The root-family nonformality theorem uses the explicit periodic resolution,
carry cochain, proved special base change and the nonzero special-fibre bracket. It does not use the
order-chart result. The coupled centre theorem uses these two independent constructions and the
bounded-coefficient comparison. The polarized boundary calculation uses only its matrix differential
and regular-sequence Koszul complex. These separate dependencies make the tests noncircular.




                                                 247
Appendix B

Reproducible finite verification

The accompanying Python program uses exact integer, rational and symbolic arithmetic. It checks
finite instances of formulas proved in the text. It is not a formal proof assistant certificate and
does not verify every theorem of the book. Its report records actual parameter ranges and assertion
counts; no unperformed calculation is marked passed.
   The root tests check multiplication, the periodic factorization, the carry cocycle, the Euler defect,
and the displayed cochain brackets for multiple root orders. The two-root tests check the complete
differential’s square and the cup Leibniz identity on bounded cochain degrees. The order tests
enumerate tournaments and compare acyclic counts with factorials, and verify equality-block fibre
counts. The support tests check finite Koszul windows together with the maps that kill the spurious
lower-degree classes in the next window. The latter transition is essential: a finite window alone has
different homology from the infinite principal-part module.
   The boundary tests verify the polarized Clifford relations in several ranks, including characteristic
two. The bar tests check graded associativity cancellation and cut contractions on bounded words.
The conformal tests recompute the two Gram matrices and check Ward recursion against the cycle
formula at distinct rational points, including a degenerate Gram parameter. The exchange test
verifies the polynomial form of the rational Yang–Baxter identity and its scalar unitarity defect.


B.1     Executed report
The machine-readable report is delivered with the source. No report was embedded during this
build.


B.2     Reading the bounds correctly
Successful checks at finitely many arities do not establish a complete operad action. In the text, the
brace and cut identities are proved by their all-arity insertion or tensor formulas. The checks test
those formulas and their signs. Similarly the cycle formula is proved through Wick contractions and
polynomial interpolation in the central charge; numerical marked-point checks are regressions, not
that proof.
   A finite truncation of an infinite cochain product can commute with localization even when the
full object does not. That counterexample is retained explicitly. A finite principal-part window has

                                                  248
Appendix B. Reproducible finite verification


lower Koszul classes which vanish on passage to a larger window; recording only its ranks would
misstate the colimit. A finite family fibre can have the correct dimensions and still have the wrong
multiplication, as both the order and boundary examples show. The verification suite therefore
includes maps and algebra products, not only dimension checks.
